# Appendix A: Standards research, raw

> Every standards claim gathered by the 14 area-research agents, with its source URL, access date,
> stated confidence, and the adversarial verifier's verdict on that specific claim.
>
> Produced 2026-08-28. Subject: 2026 industry standards for a public-facing, HIPAA-regulated,
> multi-tenant .NET 10 / ABP Commercial / Angular 20 / SQL Server application maintained by two
> SDE 1 developers at roughly one developer-week per month.
>
> **How to read the verdicts.** Each claim was re-checked by a second agent instructed to open the
> URL and default to "does not support the claim" where the page did not clearly settle it.
>
> - `[yes]` the page carries the claim as stated.
> - `[partially]` the substance is right but the citation is imprecise, incomplete, or the number
>   lives on a different page. The correction says how.
> - `[no]` the claim is wrong as stated. **These were not used in the deliverables.**
> - `[could-not-check]` the URL was unreachable from the research sandbox.
>
> Verdicts are matched to claims by position: each verification agent was given the area's claims
> as a numbered list and returned one result per claim, in order. Counts reconcile exactly for all
> fourteen areas (290 claims, 290 results).
>
> **Nothing in this appendix should be quoted without reading its verdict.**

---


## 1. Merge gates and branch protection

**Verification verdict for this area: `material-errors`** (19 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- [6] MATERIAL: There is no merge-queue setting called "Require all queue entries to pass required checks". The actual setting on the cited page is "Only merge non-failing pull requests". Additionally the page documents "Require merge queue" as a BRANCH PROTECTION setting ("Repository administrators can require a merge queue by enabling the branch protection setting 'Require merge queue'"), not a ruleset rule; "Require merge queue" does not appear among the 16 branch ruleset rules on the available-rules-for-rulesets page. The 'consumes additional CI minutes' assertion is also not stated by the page.
- [19] MATERIAL: The backport scope is wrong. The changelog says enforcement was backported to ALL currently supported major versions except v1 (it names actions/checkout@v4 as an example), not "v5/v6". Editor's note: "V1 of actions/checkout will not receive this change. The security update will be backported to all other supported versions."
- [19] MATERIAL: The entire first half of the claim (2025-12-08 pull_request_target change, the quote "The workflow file and checkout commit will always be taken from the repository's default branch, regardless of the pull request's base branch", GITHUB_REF resolution, and environment branch protection evaluating refs/pull/{number}/merge) appears nowhere on the cited URL. No 2025-12-08 changelog was verifiable. Current docs describe pull_request_target running "in the context of the default branch of the base repository" as longstanding documented behaviour, with GITHUB_REF = Default branch and GITHUB_SHA = Last commit on default branch.
- [14] The 180-day compliance period in the THRESHOLD is not supported by the cited HHS factsheet. Also the factsheet dates the NPRM as issued December 27, 2024 and never states a Federal Register publication date, so 2025-01-06 is not verifiable from this URL.
- [12] Internal inconsistency: the claim says SSDF places the obligations in "three places" but then cites four (PS.1.1, PO.4.1, PO.4.2, PO.3.3), while the THRESHOLD field lists PO.3.2 -- a practice the claim body never uses and which is about deploying/operating toolchains, not merge-gate evidence.
- [9] The threshold values in this claim (code scanning alert levels, CodeQL severities, code quality merge thresholds, dependency review private-repo wording) are all absent from the cited billing URL. The values themselves are correct but the citation does not carry them. Note also that the page many would cite for the code quality thresholds, /en/code-security/code-quality/setting-code-quality-thresholds-for-pull-requests, 404s; the live path is /en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/set-pr-thresholds.
- [15] All four DORA operating thresholds (daily trunk merge, <=3 active branches, ~10 minute test upper limit, broken-build revert) are absent from the cited streamlining-change-approval page. Only the two review-process quotes are on it.

### Standard, in summary

The 2026 standard is: one protected default branch, changes only via pull request, a small number of REQUIRED status checks that always report, and no silent bypass path. GitHub has consolidated this into repository rulesets (an automatic branch-protection-to-ruleset migration tool shipped 2026-08-11) and classic branch protection still works with no announced sunset. NIST SSDF PW.7.1 explicitly leaves the human-vs-automated review mix to the organization, so a defensible minimum is one human approval plus an automated gate, not a fixed reviewer count; CIS Software Supply Chain Security Guide 1.1.3 asks for two approvers, which is arithmetically impossible for two developers because GitHub forbids self-approval. HIPAA names no gate at all: 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B) requires "security measures sufficient to reduce risks and vulnerabilities to a reasonable and appropriate level", 164.312(b) requires audit controls, and 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires the resulting documentation be retained six years -- so what matters for compliance is that the gate config is versioned, its bypasses are reviewable, and the record outlives GitHub's 180-day audit log. The evidence on gate count is one-directional: DORA found no evidence that heavier external approval lowers change failure rate, while the CI Theater study of 1,270 projects and 534,417 builds found 85% had at least one build broken for more than four days, with the smallest projects averaging 40 days to fix -- i.e. gates that are slow or ignorable decay into theatre. For this team the defensible set is roughly five required things (one aggregated CI check, one approval, most-recent-push approval, conversation resolution, no force-push/delete) plus a monthly bypass review, and the single highest-leverage engineering decision is to expose exactly ONE required check name backed by an `if: always()` aggregator job.

### Claims and sources

**A1.1** `[partially]` GitHub repository rulesets are the direction of travel and can now be auto-migrated from classic branch protection, but classic branch protection is not deprecated and has no announced sunset date. Rulesets differ operationally in that multiple rulesets can target the same branch and are aggregated with the most restrictive version of a rule winning, and they layer with any classic branch protection rules still in place (all applicable rules are enforced).

- Threshold: `Migration tool shipped 2026-08-11; no deprecation date announced`
- Source: GitHub Changelog - Automatically migrate branch protection rules to repository rulesets -- https://github.blog/changelog/2026-08-11-automatically-migrate-branch-protection-rules-to-repository-rulesets/
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/about-rulesets
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Changelog wording is strategic, not terminal: "As we continue investing in rulesets as the foundation for repository governance on GitHub, this tool makes it easy to bring your existing policies forward." Practical consequence for this team: pick ONE mechanism. Running a classic rule and a ruleset simultaneously means both are enforced and you will spend an afternoon working out which one is blocking you. Recommend rulesets, because they are readable by anyone with repo read access, have a REST API with version history, and default to nobody bypassing.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The cited changelog (dated August 11, 2026) supports only the migration-tool half and is silent on deprecation (silence, not an affirmative statement that there is no sunset). It does NOT support the aggregation or layering mechanics -- it only says rulesets let you 'Layer multiple rulesets for granular policy control' with no detail. The aggregation/layering facts ARE correct but must be cited to https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/about-rulesets, which states: 'If multiple rulesets target the same branch or tag in a repository, the rules in each of these rulesets are aggregated'; 'If the same rule is defined in different ways across the aggregated rulesets, the most restrictive version of the rule applies'; 'As well as layering with each other, rulesets also layer with protection rules targeting the same branch or tag.'
- Verifier read from the page: "You can now convert your existing branch protection rules to repository rulesets directly from your repository settings."

**A1.2** `[yes]` The branch ruleset rules available in 2026 are: Restrict creations, Restrict updates, Restrict deletions (selected by default), Require linear history, Require deployments to succeed before merging, Require signed commits, Require a pull request before merging, Require status checks to pass before merging, Block force pushes (enabled by default), Require code scanning results, Require code quality results, Restrict code coverage (public preview), Restrict file paths, Restrict file path length, Restrict file extensions, Restrict file size.

- Threshold: `Required approvals configurable 0-10; required reviewers up to 15 teams; Restrict file paths and Restrict file extensions limited to 200 entries each`
- Source: GitHub Docs - Available rules for rulesets -- https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/available-rules-for-rulesets
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud@latest/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/available-rules-for-rulesets
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Sub-options under "Require a pull request before merging": required approvals, "Dismiss stale pull request approvals when new commits are pushed", "Require approval of the most recent reviewable push", "Require review from code owners", restrict review dismissal, required reviewers, "Require all comments on the pull request to be resolved before it can be merged", allowed merge methods. Sub-option under status checks: "Require branches to be up to date before merging" and "Do not require status checks on creation". Note that "Require code scanning results" and "Require code quality results" exist in the UI but are unusable on a private repo without a paid licence (see separate entry) - the UI will show you rules you cannot actually turn on.

**A1.3** `[partially]` Classic branch protection does NOT apply to repository admins unless you explicitly enable "Do not allow bypassing the above settings". Rulesets invert this: enforcement is the default and bypass is an explicit list you populate (repository admins, organization owners, enterprise owners, the maintain or write role, custom roles based on write, teams excluding secret teams, GitHub Apps, and Dependabot), with each bypass actor set to either "Always allow" (the default) or "For pull requests only", the latter meaning the actor "is now required to open a pull request to make changes to a repository, creating a clear trail of their changes".

- Threshold: `Classic BP: admin exemption is the DEFAULT. Rulesets: bypass list is empty unless populated.`
- Source: GitHub Docs - About protected branches -- https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-protected-branches/about-protected-branches
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/creating-rulesets-for-a-repository
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim from the docs: "By default, the restrictions of a branch protection rule do not apply to people with admin permissions to the repository or custom roles with the 'bypass branch protections' permission." On a two-person repo both developers are almost certainly admins, so classic branch protection configured without that checkbox protects nobody. CIS makes this its own control (1.1.14). This is the single most common false sense of security in small-team GitHub setups.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The cited URL supports only the classic-branch-protection half. It says nothing about ruleset bypass actor types or the Always allow / For pull requests only modes -- it only notes 'This restriction does not apply to rulesets.' The ruleset half is factually correct but must be cited to https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/creating-rulesets-for-a-repository, which lists the actors (repository admins, organization owners, enterprise owners; the maintain or write role or custom roles based on write; teams excluding secret teams; GitHub Apps; Dependabot), names 'Always allow' as the default, and states: 'The selected actor is now required to open a pull request to make changes to a repository, creating a clear trail of their changes in the pull request and audit log.'
- Verifier read from the page: "By default, the restrictions of a branch protection rule don't apply to people with admin permissions to the repository or custom roles with the 'bypass branch protections' permission."

**A1.4** `[yes]` A pull request author cannot approve their own pull request, and repository owners/administrators can merge a pull request even if it has not received an approving review. Both facts constrain what a two-person team can promise.

- Threshold: `Self-approval: prohibited outright. Admin merge without approval: permitted unless bypass is closed off.`
- Source: GitHub Docs - Approving a pull request with required reviews -- https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/reviewing-changes-in-pull-requests/approving-a-pull-request-with-required-reviews
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/reviewing-changes-in-pull-requests/about-pull-request-reviews
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim: "Pull request authors cannot approve their own pull requests." and "Repository owners and administrators can merge a pull request even if it hasn't received an approving review, or if a reviewer who requested changes has left the organization or is unavailable." Consequence: with 2 devs, "1 required approval" already means genuine second-person review 100% of the time, and "2 required approvals" is unsatisfiable. Also: the admin-merge escape hatch is exactly why the ruleset bypass list must be empty and reviewed.

**A1.5** `[yes]` A required status check behaves differently depending on WHERE you skip it, and one of the two behaviours silently disables your gate. If a workflow is skipped due to path filtering, branch filtering or a commit message, the associated checks remain in a Pending state and block merging forever. If a JOB inside a workflow is skipped by a conditional, it reports its status as Success and does NOT prevent merging even when it is a required check.

- Threshold: `Workflow-level skip => Pending => PR permanently blocked. Job-level `if:` skip => Success => required check silently satisfied.`
- Source: GitHub Docs - Troubleshooting required status checks -- https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/collaborating-on-repositories-with-code-quality-features/troubleshooting-required-status-checks
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/collaborating-on-repositories-with-code-quality-features/about-status-checks
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim from About status checks: "A job that is skipped will report its status as 'Success'. It will not prevent a pull request from merging, even if it is a required check." GitHub's own remediation is to avoid requiring workflows that can be skipped, and to use `always()` with `needs` for aggregating jobs. Also verbatim: "If a check and a commit status have the same name, both must pass when that name is required" - so name collisions between an Action and an external app both block. A GitHub staff member acknowledged the path-filter papercut on 2024-09-19 in community discussion 44490 with no timeline; it is still open. This is the technical justification for exposing exactly one required check name.

**A1.6** `[partially]` GitHub merge queue is not available for private repositories except on GitHub Enterprise Cloud. Where available it is configured through the ruleset rule "Require merge queue" with build concurrency between 1 and 100, minimum and maximum pull requests to merge between 1 and 100, a wait time to meet the minimum group size, a status check timeout, and a "Require all queue entries to pass required checks" toggle. CI workflows must add the `merge_group` event, and it creates temporary `gh-readonly-queue/{base_branch}` branches that consume additional CI minutes.

- Threshold: `Private repos: GitHub Enterprise Cloud only. Concurrency 1-100; group size 1-100.`
- Source: GitHub Docs - Managing a merge queue -- https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/configuring-pull-request-merges/managing-a-merge-queue
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud@latest/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/available-rules-for-rulesets
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: The numeric ranges and settings are verified from pages I opened. The plan-availability sentence ("available in any public repository owned by an organization, or in private repositories owned by organizations using GitHub Enterprise Cloud") appeared consistently in docs.github.com search result snippets across the github.com, Enterprise Cloud and Enterprise Server variants of the page, but the availability banner did not render in the page bodies I fetched, so I could not quote it from a page I opened. Verify in your own repo settings before planning around it. Docs also warn that jumping to the front of the queue triggers full rebuilds of in-progress PRs.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Availability, 1-100 concurrency, 1-100 merge limits, the merge_group event requirement, gh-readonly-queue/{base_branch} branches, wait timeout and status check timeout are all confirmed. Three corrections: (a) the toggle is 'Only merge non-failing pull requests', not 'Require all queue entries to pass required checks'; (b) the docs describe 'Require merge queue' as a BRANCH PROTECTION setting, not a ruleset rule -- and it is not one of the 16 branch ruleset rules listed on available-rules-for-rulesets; (c) the page does not say the temporary branches 'consume additional CI minutes' -- that is an inference, not documented text.
- Verifier read from the page: "Pull request merge queues are available in any public repository owned by an organization, or in private repositories owned by organizations using GitHub Enterprise Cloud. ... Repository administrators can require a merge queue by enabling the branch protection setting "Require merge queue". ... Build concurrency: The maximum number of merge_group webhooks to dispatch (between 1 and 100). ... Only merge non-failing pull requests: This setting determines how a merge queue forms groups of pull req"

**A1.7** `[yes]` GitHub auto-merge is the correct latency mitigation for a small team: it is offered only on pull requests that cannot be merged immediately - specifically when a rule enforces required pull request reviews or required status checks - and it merges automatically once all required reviews and checks are satisfied. It is disabled automatically if someone without write access pushes to the branch or the base branch changes.

- Source: GitHub Docs - Automatically merging a pull request -- https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/incorporating-changes-from-a-pull-request/automatically-merging-a-pull-request
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/available-rules-for-rulesets
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the feature that makes a real gate survivable for two people: the reviewer approves, walks away, and the merge happens when CI finishes. Without it, every gate adds a context switch at the end of a 10-minute CI run, which is precisely the friction that produces admin merges. Enable "Allow auto-merge" in repository settings at the same time you create the ruleset - it is one checkbox and it is the difference between a gate people use and a gate people route around.

**A1.8** `[partially]` Rulesets are addressable as configuration-as-code and carry their own version history and bypass telemetry, which is the mechanism that turns a merge gate into retainable audit evidence: GET/POST /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets, GET/PUT/DELETE /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}, GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}/history and .../history/{version_id}, plus GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rules/branches/{branch}. Rule Insights shows actions that passed, failed, or "where someone has bypassed one or more rulesets", with a dashboard of successes/failures/bypasses over time and "Top bypassers" for GitHub Team and Enterprise Cloud, and an "Evaluate" enforcement mode that shows what would have passed or failed.

- Source: GitHub REST API - Repository rules -- https://docs.github.com/en/rest/repos/rules
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/managing-rulesets-for-a-repository
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This directly satisfies SSDF PO.3.2 Example 3 ("Use code-based configuration for toolchains (e.g., pipelines-as-code, toolchains-as-code)") and PO.3.3 Example 1 (use tooling "to create an audit trail of the secure development-related actions that are performed"). The ruleset history endpoint is the under-advertised one - it means you can answer "who loosened the gate and when" without an enterprise audit log. "Top bypassers" being available on Team is the cheapest possible implementation of CIS 1.1.19.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** All eight REST endpoints are confirmed on the cited URL, including the PUT (not PATCH) update verb. But Rule Insights, the bypass dashboard, 'Top bypassers' and the Evaluate mode are NOT on the REST reference page. Those are documented at https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/managing-rulesets-for-a-repository, which does confirm 'Actions where someone has bypassed one or more rulesets', the Evaluate mode ('If a ruleset is running in "Evaluate" mode, you can see actions that would have passed or failed if the ruleset had been active'), and the dashboard's availability on GitHub Team and GitHub Enterprise Cloud. Note the docs phrase is 'the most active bypassers for your rulesets' rather than 'Top bypassers'.
- Verifier read from the page: "GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rules/branches/{branch}; GET|POST /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets; GET|PUT|DELETE /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}; GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}/history; GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}/history/{version_id}"

**A1.9** `[partially]` GitHub's own security merge gates are paywalled on private repositories: "You need to pay to use Advanced Security features in private repositories on GitHub.com." This covers CodeQL code scanning (and therefore the "Require code scanning results" ruleset rule) and dependency review, which is available for "all public repositories" and "private repositories that have GitHub Code Security or GitHub Advanced Security enabled". Code scanning alerts carry a level of Error, Warning or Note, and CodeQL security alerts additionally carry Critical/High/Medium/Low; the newer "Require code quality results" ruleset rule blocks on thresholds of "Errors", "Warnings and higher", "Notes and higher" or "All".

- Threshold: `Code quality merge-gate thresholds: Errors / Warnings and higher / Notes and higher / All. Alert levels: Error, Warning, Note. CodeQL security severities: Critical, High, Medium, Low.`
- Source: GitHub Docs - GitHub Advanced Security license billing -- https://docs.github.com/en/billing/concepts/product-billing/github-advanced-security
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/set-pr-thresholds
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Also opened: https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/supply-chain-security/understanding-your-software-supply-chain/about-dependency-review (dependency review action "will fail if it discovers any vulnerable packages" and supports fail-on-severity plus license allow/deny lists) and https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/code-scanning/managing-code-scanning-alerts/about-code-scanning-alerts (severity levels). Cost is out of scope for this exercise, but FEATURE AVAILABILITY is not: half of the standard 2026 merge-gate advice assumes CodeQL and dependency review are free, and on your private repo they are not. The free substitutes below (NuGet audit, yarn npm audit, gitleaks) cover the dependency and secret cases well; SAST is the genuine gap.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The cited billing page supports only the paywall sentence and the public-repo access statement. It does NOT contain the dependency-review private-repo wording ('private repositories that have GitHub Code Security or GitHub Advanced Security enabled'), the Error/Warning/Note alert levels, the CodeQL Critical/High/Medium/Low severities, or the code quality merge thresholds. The four code quality threshold values ARE correct but live at https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/set-pr-thresholds: 'Set "Errors" to block pull requests with unresolved code quality errors being merged. Set "Warnings and higher" ... Set "Notes and higher" ... Set "All" to block pull requests with any unresolved code quality results being merged.' The plausible-looking URL /en/code-security/code-quality/setting-code-quality-thresholds-for-pull-requests returns 404.
- Verifier read from the page: "You need to pay to use Advanced Security features in private repositories on GitHub.com. ... All public repositories have access to code scanning, secret scanning, and dependency review."

**A1.10** `[yes]` CIS Software Supply Chain Security Guide section 1.1 (Code Changes) is the most prescriptive branch-protection checklist an auditor is likely to use, and its controls are: 1.1.1 changes tracked in a version control platform; 1.1.2 every change traceable to its associated task; 1.1.3 approval of two strongly authenticated users (Automated); 1.1.4 previous approvals dismissed when updates are introduced; 1.1.5 restrictions on who can dismiss reviews; 1.1.6 code owners set for extra sensitive code or configuration; 1.1.7 code owner review required when a change affects owned code; 1.1.8 inactive branches periodically reviewed and removed; 1.1.9 all checks passed before merging; 1.1.10 branches up to date before merge; 1.1.11 all open comments resolved before merging; 1.1.12 signed commits verified before merging; 1.1.13 linear history required; 1.1.14 branch protection rules enforced for administrators; 1.1.15 pushing/merging restricted to specific individuals or teams; 1.1.16 force push denied; 1.1.17 branch deletions denied; 1.1.18 any merging of code automatically scanned for risks; 1.1.19 any changes to branch protection rules are audited.

- Threshold: `1.1.3 requires TWO approving reviewers. 19 controls in section 1.1.`
- Source: CIS Software Supply Chain Security Guide v1.0, June 2022 (PDF copy distributed with aquasecurity/chain-bench) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aquasecurity/chain-bench/main/docs/CIS-Software-Supply-Chain-Security-Guide-v1.0.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://www.cisecurity.org/benchmark/software-supply-chain-security
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: I downloaded and extracted the v1.0 PDF text directly, so the control IDs and titles are quoted from the document, not from memory. CIS now also publishes platform-specific benchmarks - the cisecurity.org page I opened lists "GitHub (1.2.0)" and "GitLab (1.0.1)" as current - but those downloads are form-gated and I could not read v1.2.0, so control numbering may have shifted; treat the v1.0 numbering as indicative. 1.1.14 rationale verbatim: "Administrators by default are excluded from any branch protection rules. This means these privileged users ... are not subject to protections meant to prevent untrusted code insertion, including malicious code." 1.1.19 asks only for "a tracking system ... that logs changes in branch protection rules" - GitHub's ruleset history API satisfies it.

**A1.11** `[yes]` NIST SSDF (SP 800-218 v1.1) does NOT mandate human code review. PW.7.1 says to "Determine whether code review (a person looks directly at the code to find issues) and/or code analysis (tools are used to find issues in code...) should be used, as defined by the organization." PW.7.2 then says to "Perform the code review and/or code analysis based on the organization's secure coding standards, and record and triage all discovered issues and recommended remediations in the development team's workflow or issue tracking system", with Example 4 being a static analysis tool with a human reviewing the reported issues, and Example 6 being "Use automated tools to identify and remediate documented and verified unsafe software practices on a continuous basis as human-readable code is checked into the code repository."

- Threshold: `PW.7.1, PW.7.2 - no reviewer count specified; the mix is explicitly the organization's decision`
- Source: NIST SP 800-218, Secure Software Development Framework (SSDF) Version 1.1, February 2022 -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/218/final
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Text extracted from the PDF itself. This is the load-bearing citation for a two-person team: anyone who says "NIST requires two reviewers" is wrong, and PW.7.1's phrasing ("as defined by the organization") is the language to quote back. PW.8.1/PW.8.2 apply the same risk-based framing to executable-code testing. SP 800-218 v1.1 remains the current final version as of 2026-08-28; SP 800-218A is a companion part on generative AI, not a successor.

**A1.12** `[partially]` SSDF places the merge-gate obligations in three places that a two-person team can actually evidence: PS.1.1 (store all forms of code by least privilege) with Example 2 "Use version control features of the repository to track all changes made to the code with accountability to the individual account", Example 3 "Use commit signing for code repositories", and Example 4 "Have the code owner review and approve all changes made to the code by others"; PO.4.1 (define criteria for software security checks, with Example 3 "Add software security criteria to existing checks (e.g., the Definition of Done...)" and Example 5 recording "security check approvals, rejections, and exception requests"); PO.4.2 Example 4 "Only allow authorized personnel to access the gathered information, and prevent any alteration or deletion of the information"; and PO.3.3 (configure tools to generate artifacts of secure development practices) Example 1 "Use existing tooling ... to create an audit trail of the secure development-related actions that are performed".

- Threshold: `PS.1.1, PO.3.2, PO.3.3, PO.4.1, PO.4.2`
- Source: NIST SP 800-218, SSDF v1.1 -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/218/final
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: PO.3.2 Example 3 is "Use code-based configuration for toolchains (e.g., pipelines-as-code, toolchains-as-code)" - this is the SSDF hook for committing your ruleset JSON to the repo. PS.1.1 cross-maps to OWASPSAMM OE3-B, OWASPASVS 1.10 / 10.3.2, and SP800-53 SA-11 in the SSDF's own reference column, which is convenient if a customer questionnaire asks for a control mapping.

But the claim says 'three places' while naming FOUR practice tasks (PS.1.1, PO.4.1, PO.4.2, PO.3.3), and the THRESHOLD field lists PO.3.2, which the claim body never cites -- PO.3.2 is 'Follow recommended security practices to deploy, operate, and maintain tools and toolchains', which is not a merge-gate evidence obligation. **A1.13** `[partially]` HIPAA's Security Rule names no merge gate, no code review and no CI check. What it requires is 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B) Risk management (Required): "Implement security measures sufficient to reduce risks and vulnerabilities to a reasonable and appropriate level"; 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) Information system activity review (Required): "Implement procedures to regularly review records of information system activity"; 164.312(b) Audit controls (Required): "Implement hardware, software, and/or procedural mechanisms that record and examine activity in" systems containing ePHI; and 164.316(b)(2)(i) Time limit (Required): "Retain the documentation required by paragraph (b)(1) of this section for 6 years from the date of its creation or the date when it last was in effect, whichever is later."

- Threshold: `164.316(b)(2)(i): 6 years retention. 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(A)-(D) are all Required, not Addressable.`
- Source: 45 CFR 164.308 / 164.312 / 164.316 (Cornell Legal Information Institute) -- https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.308
- Cross-reference: https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.316
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Also opened https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.312 - 164.312(a)(1), (a)(2)(i), (b) and (c)(1) are Required; 164.312(c)(2) Mechanism to authenticate ePHI is Addressable. The practical translation for merge gates: the gate itself is a security measure you selected under (a)(1)(ii)(B) and must be able to justify; the PR record and ruleset history are your (b) audit controls and (a)(1)(ii)(D) activity review; and the six-year clock in 164.316 is why you should not rely on GitHub's audit log as your record. Note that eCFR blocked automated fetching (302 to unblock.federalregister.gov), which is why I used Cornell LII.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The cited page covers 45 CFR 164.308 only, and does confirm that (A) Risk analysis, (B) Risk management, (C) Sanction policy and (D) Information system activity review are all marked (Required). It does NOT contain 164.312(b) (Audit controls) or 164.316(b)(2)(i) (the 6-year retention rule) -- those need separate citations to https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.312 and https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.316. The 6-year threshold is therefore unverified against the URL supplied.
- Verifier read from the page: "(B) Risk management (Required). Implement security measures sufficient to reduce risks and vulnerabilities to a reasonable and appropriate level ... (D) Information system activity review (Required). Implement procedures to regularly review records of information system activity, such as audit logs"

**A1.14** `[partially]` A proposed rule published in the Federal Register on 2025-01-06 would materially tighten this: it proposes to "Remove the distinction between 'required' and 'addressable' implementation specifications and make all implementation specifications required with specific, limited exceptions", to "Require written documentation of all Security Rule policies, procedures, plans, and analyses", to require vulnerability scanning "at least every six months", penetration testing "at least once every 12 months", a technology asset inventory refreshed "at least once every 12 months and in response to a change", and a compliance audit "at least once every 12 months". As of 2026-08-28 I found no evidence it has been finalised.

- Threshold: `Vulnerability scanning every 6 months; pen test every 12 months; asset inventory every 12 months; compliance audit every 12 months; proposed 180-day compliance period after any final rule`
- Source: HHS - HIPAA Security Rule NPRM Fact Sheet -- https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/security/hipaa-security-rule-nprm/factsheet/index.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/regulatory-initiatives/index.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: The proposed content is verified from the HHS fact sheet. The NON-finalisation is where I am only partial: the HHS Regulatory Initiatives page I opened was last reviewed 2025-06-27 and still describes it as a proposed rule, and the January 2026 OCR cybersecurity newsletter (on system hardening) I opened makes no statement about it. I could not find an affirmative 2026 HHS statement either way. Check https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/security/hipaa-security-rule-nprm/index.html before you rely on this. Why it matters for merge gates: if finalised, the removal of "addressable" plus the written-documentation requirement means your gate configuration and your bypass log stop being nice-to-have and become documentation you must produce - which is a strong argument for committing the ruleset JSON now rather than later.

Two problems: (a) the factsheet dates the NPRM as issued December 27, 2024 and never states a Federal Register publication date, so '2025-01-06' is not verifiable from this URL and needs a federalregister.gov citation; (b) the '180-day compliance period after any final rule' in the THRESHOLD does not appear on this page at all -- drop it or cite the NPRM text directly.
