Require regulated entities to conduct a compliance audit at least once every 12 months."

**A1.15** `[partially]` The evidence does not support adding approval weight. DORA found "no evidence was found to support the hypothesis that a more formal, external review process was associated with lower change fail rates" and that heavyweight approval "tend[s] to slow down the delivery process leading to the release of larger batches less frequently, with an accompanying higher impact on the production system"; its recommendation is peer review during development captured at code check-in plus automation. DORA's associated operating thresholds are: merge to trunk at least once a day, three or fewer active branches, and tests that "should not take more than a few minutes to run, with an upper limit of about 10 minutes", with a broken build taking priority over other work and being reverted if not fixable in a few minutes.

- Threshold: `Build/test feedback upper limit ~10 minutes; merge to trunk >= daily; <= 3 active branches`
- Source: DORA - Streamlining change approval -- https://dora.dev/capabilities/streamlining-change-approval/
- Cross-reference: https://dora.dev/capabilities/continuous-integration/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Also opened https://dora.dev/capabilities/trunk-based-development/ (three or fewer active branches; branches lasting no more than a few hours) and https://dora.dev/research/ (most recent State of DevOps report listed is 2025). The 10-minute number is the single most useful design constraint here: it is the budget your entire required-check set has to fit inside. If your aggregated gate exceeds it, the gate will be routed around regardless of what the ruleset says.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The two review-process quotes are verbatim-accurate on this page. But none of the four operating thresholds (merge to trunk at least once a day, three or fewer active branches, tests under ~10 minutes, broken build takes priority / is reverted) appear on the streamlining-change-approval page. Those belong to DORA's trunk-based development and continuous integration capability pages and must be cited there separately.
- Verifier read from the page: "no evidence was found to support the hypothesis that a more formal, external review process was associated with lower change fail rates ... Such heavyweight approaches tend to slow down the delivery process leading to the release of larger batches less frequently, with an accompanying higher impact on the production system"

**A1.16** `[yes]` Gates decay when they are slow or ignorable, and small projects decay worst. The "Continuous Integration Theater" study (Felidre, Furtado, da Costa, Cartaxo, Pinto; ESEM 2019; arXiv 1907.01602, 2019-07-02) analysed 1,270 open-source projects and 534,417 builds and found that 85% of the analysed projects had at least one build that took more than four days to be fixed, that these long-to-be-fixed builds on very small projects are fixed on average in 40 days, and that 60% of projects had fewer than 2.36 commits per weekday. Build duration was NOT the problem - almost all projects built in under 10 minutes.

- Threshold: `85% of projects had a build broken >4 days; smallest projects averaged 40 days to fix; 60% below 2.36 commits/weekday`
- Source: Continuous Integration Theater (arXiv 1907.01602 / ESEM 2019) -- https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.01602
- Cross-reference: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.01602
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: I extracted the numbers from the PDF text directly rather than the abstract. This is the strongest available empirical answer to "how many gates before people start bypassing": the literature does not give a gate-count threshold (I looked and could not find one - treat any specific number as unsourced), but it does show that the failure mode in small projects is a red gate that stays red, not a bypassed gate. The design implication is the opposite of what teams assume: the risk is not that two devs will click admin-merge, it is that they will leave main broken for weeks and stop trusting the signal. Hence: few checks, fast, and a rule that a red main is the top priority.

**A1.17** `[yes]` OWASP SAMM v2 puts the merge gate at maturity level 3 of Secure Build Stream A - "Define mandatory security checks in the build process and ensure that building non-compliant artifacts fails" - with level 2 being "Automate your build pipeline and secure the used tooling. Add security checks in the build pipeline" and level 1 merely "Create a formal definition of the build process so that it becomes consistent and repeatable". Security Testing Stream A is level 1 "Utilize automated security testing tools", level 2 "Employ application-specific security testing automation", level 3 "Integrate automated security testing into the build and deploy process".

- Threshold: `Secure Build A: L1 formal definition, L2 automated + checks added, L3 mandatory checks that fail the build`
- Source: OWASP SAMM v2 - Secure Build -- https://owaspsamm.org/model/implementation/secure-build/
- Cross-reference: https://owaspsamm.org/model/verification/security-testing/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Useful for calibrating ambition honestly: a two-person team that gets to Secure Build A2 and Security Testing A1 before public launch is doing well, and SAMM says so. Secure Build Stream B (Software Dependencies) L1 is "Create records with Bill of Materials of your applications and opportunistically analyze these" - the free NuGet/Yarn audit gates below already exceed that.

**A1.18** `[partially]` Free, first-party dependency gates exist for both stacks and do not require GitHub Advanced Security. For .NET, NuGetAudit defaults to true, NuGetAuditLevel defaults to low, and NuGetAuditMode "defaults to `all` when a project targets `net10.0` or higher. Otherwise NuGetAuditMode defaults to `direct`" - meaning a .NET 10 project already audits transitive dependencies. Findings surface as warnings NU1901 (low), NU1902 (moderate), NU1903 (high), NU1904 (critical), and Microsoft documents a CI-only pattern using `<WarningsAsErrors Condition=" '$(AuditPipeline)' == 'true' ">` with `dotnet restore -p:AuditPipeline=true`. For Yarn, `yarn npm audit` accepts `--severity` with values info/low/moderate/high/critical plus `-A/--all` and `-R/--recursive`, and "the process will exit with a non-zero exit code if a report is found for the selected packages".

- Threshold: `NuGetAuditMode default `all` on net10.0+; NuGetAuditLevel default `low`; NU1903 = high, NU1904 = critical; yarn npm audit --severity high`
- Source: Microsoft Learn - Auditing package dependencies for security vulnerabilities -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages
- Cross-reference: https://yarnpkg.com/cli/npm/audit
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Microsoft Learn page updated 2026-07-07, and the feature table lists NuGet 7.0 / .NET 10 SDK (10.0.100) / Visual Studio 2026 for the NuGetAuditMode default change and `dotnet package update --vulnerable`. This is the single best effort-to-value gate available to this team: the audit already runs, you are just choosing to fail on it. The CI-only conditional pattern matters because it means a new advisory published overnight breaks your audit gate but not the other developer's local build.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Every .NET/NuGet element is verified verbatim on the cited page. The Yarn half is not on this page and needs its own citation: https://yarnpkg.com/cli/npm/audit confirms --severity accepts info/low/moderate/high/critical, that -A,--all and -R,--recursive exist, and that 'Regardless of this flag, the process will exit with a non-zero exit code if a report is found for the selected packages.'
- Verifier read from the page: "NuGetAuditMode defaults to `all` when a project targets `net10.0` or higher. Otherwise NuGetAuditMode defaults to `direct`. ... NuGetAuditLevel | low ... NuGetAudit | true ... NU1901 Package with low severity detected; NU1902 moderate; NU1903 high; NU1904 critical ... <WarningsAsErrors Condition=" '$(AuditPipeline)' == 'true' ">$(WarningsAsErrors);$(NuGetAuditCodes)</WarningsAsErrors> ... run: dotnet restore -p:AuditPipeline=true"

**A1.19** `[partially]` The trust model for workflow files changed twice in the last year and both changes affect merge gates. From 2025-12-08, for pull_request_target "The workflow file and checkout commit will always be taken from the repository's default branch, regardless of the pull request's base branch", GITHUB_REF resolves to the default branch, and environment branch protection rules evaluate against refs/pull/{number}/merge for pull_request events and against the default branch for pull_request_target. From 2026-06-18, actions/checkout v7 is GA and "refuses common pwn request patterns by default", blocking fork-PR checkouts of fork refs, PR head SHAs and merge commit refs unless you opt out with `allow-unsafe-pr-checkout`; enforcement was backported to v5/v6 on 2026-07-20.

- Threshold: `pull_request_target default-branch resolution effective 2025-12-08; actions/checkout v7 GA 2026-06-18; backport enforcement 2026-07-20`
- Source: GitHub Changelog - Safer pull_request_target defaults for GitHub Actions checkout -- https://github.blog/changelog/2026-06-18-safer-pull_request_target-defaults-for-github-actions-checkout/
- Cross-reference: https://github.blog/changelog/2025-11-07-actions-pull_request_target-and-environment-branch-protections-changes/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Tool-maintained evidence: actions/checkout latest release is v7.0.1, published_at 2026-07-20T15:10:05Z (https://api.github.com/repos/actions/checkout/releases/latest). If your workflows pin `@v4` or `@v5` you received behaviour changes on 2026-07-20 without editing anything. Note the residual gap this does NOT close: for ordinary `pull_request` runs the workflow file is still read from the PR branch, so a PR that edits .github/workflows/ci.yml runs the edited workflow as its own check while the required check name stays green.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Corrected: 'actions/checkout v7 went GA on 2026-06-18 and refuses common pwn request patterns by default (blocking refs/pull/{n}/head, refs/pull/{n}/merge and fork SHA checkouts) unless you opt out with the allow-unsafe-pr-checkout input; enforcement was backported on 2026-07-20 to ALL currently supported major versions except v1 (v4 included), affecting workflows pinned to a floating major tag but not those pinned to a SHA, minor or patch.' The entire 2025-12-08 pull_request_target portion (workflow file/checkout commit always from the default branch regardless of the PR's base branch, GITHUB_REF resolution, and environment branch protection evaluating refs/pull/{number}/merge vs the default branch) is absent from this URL and could not be verified -- do not attribute it to this changelog. GitHub's current events-that-trigger-workflows reference presents default-branch context as longstanding pull_request_target behaviour ('This event runs in the context of the default branch of the base repository', GITHUB_REF = Default branch), with no 2025-12-08 date.
- Verifier read from the page: "Starting today, actions/checkout v7 is generally available and refuses common pwn request patterns by default. On July 16, 2026, we'll backport the enforcement to all currently supported major versions. ... Editor's note (July 15, 2026): ... Enforcement for backported versions of actions/checkout has been moved from July 16, 2026 to Monday, July 20, 2026. ... V1 of actions/checkout will not receive this change. The security update will be backported to all other supported versions of actions/che"

### Where sources disagree

- Reviewer count: CIS Software Supply Chain Security Guide 1.1.3 requires 'approval of two strongly authenticated users', while NIST SSDF PW.7.1 explicitly declines to specify - 'Determine whether code review ... and/or code analysis ... should be used, as defined by the organization' - and DORA reports 'no evidence was found to support the hypothesis that a more formal, external review process was associated with lower change fail rates'. CIS is a prescriptive configuration checklist written for organisations with headcount; SSDF is risk-based; DORA is outcome-based research. For two developers the CIS control is not expensive, it is unsatisfiable, because GitHub forbids self-approval and two approvals therefore needs three people. I recommend one approval plus 'Require approval of the most recent reviewable push', and I recommend writing down that CIS 1.1.3 is a known, documented deviation rather than pretending it is met.
- Branch currency: CIS 1.1.10 requires branches be up to date before merging, unconditionally. GitHub built merge queue precisely because that requirement serialises merges on active repositories, and DORA's trunk-based-development guidance points the other way (three or fewer active branches, branches lasting hours, merge to trunk daily) - at which point the requirement is largely moot. Since merge queue is unavailable on private repos outside Enterprise Cloud, following CIS 1.1.10 literally would cost you a full CI cycle per merge with no queue to absorb it. I recommend not enabling it and noting the deviation.
- Scope of 'audit controls': HIPAA 164.312(b) is written about systems that contain ePHI - it requires mechanisms that 'record and examine activity in information systems that contain or use electronic protected health information'. A source-code repository containing no PHI is arguably outside it, and the honest reading is that your merge gate is a security measure selected under 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B) risk management, with the PR and ruleset history as supporting documentation under 164.316. Vendors and consultancies frequently assert that 'HIPAA requires code review' or 'HIPAA requires change control'; no provision I read says either. Be careful not to over-claim in the other direction: the pending NPRM would remove the required/addressable distinction and require written documentation of all policies, procedures, plans and analyses, which would tighten the documentation obligation without ever naming a merge gate.
- Feature availability: GitHub's documentation is internally inconsistent about where the plan-availability banners appear. Search snippets from docs.github.com consistently state that merge queue is available 'in any public repository owned by an organization, or in private repositories owned by organizations using GitHub Enterprise Cloud', and that rulesets are available on private repos only with Pro, Team or Enterprise Cloud - but neither banner rendered in the page bodies I fetched, so I could not quote them from a page I opened. I have marked both claims partial. Confirm against your own repository settings before designing around either, because whether merge queue exists for you changes the shape of the recommendation.
- CI Theater vs the intuition that gates get bypassed: the empirical literature I could find does not establish a gate-count threshold at which teams begin bypassing - I searched for one and did not find it, so treat any specific number ('more than five checks and people start bypassing') as unsourced folklore. What the literature does establish is that small projects tolerate long-broken builds far worse than large ones. If someone cites a specific gate-count threshold at you, ask for the source.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The same 'only run this when relevant files change' optimisation either deadlocks you or silently disables your gate, depending on which line you put it on. GitHub's docs are explicit: a workflow skipped by path filtering, branch filtering or a commit message leaves its checks Pending and blocks merging forever, while a JOB skipped by a conditional 'will report its status as Success. It will not prevent a pull request from merging, even if it is a required check.' So `on: pull_request: paths:` wedges every PR until an admin force-merges, and `jobs.x.if:` makes your required security check green while it does nothing. Both are things a team adds in week two to save CI minutes. GitHub acknowledged the path-filter case as a papercut in community discussion 44490 on 2024-09-19 with no timeline, and it is still open in 2026.
- Classic branch protection does not apply to you. Verbatim from GitHub's docs: 'By default, the restrictions of a branch protection rule do not apply to people with admin permissions to the repository.' On a two-person repo both developers are admins. If you configure classic branch protection and do not tick 'Do not allow bypassing the above settings', you have built a gate that constrains nobody and will pass a superficial audit anyway. CIS considers this important enough to be its own control (1.1.14). Rulesets reverse the default - enforcement applies and bypass is an explicit list - which is on its own a sufficient reason to use rulesets rather than classic protection.
- Your merge-gate evidence expires roughly twelve times sooner than HIPAA requires you to keep it. GitHub's organization audit log 'lists events triggered by activities that affect your organization within the last 180 days', while 45 CFR 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires documentation be retained 'for 6 years from the date of its creation or the date when it last was in effect, whichever is later'. Almost nobody notices this until an auditor asks for change-control evidence covering a period that has already aged out. The durable records are the pull requests themselves, the ruleset history endpoint (GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}/history), and a committed ruleset JSON plus a dated review log in your own repository - none of which are the audit log.
- A pull request can weaken its own gate. For ordinary `pull_request` runs the workflow file is read from the PR branch, so a PR that edits .github/workflows/ci.yml runs the edited workflow as its own check - the required check name stays green while doing less. Two SDE 1s reviewing each other are unlikely to read a workflow diff as adversarially as they read application code. Relatedly and very recently: since 2025-12-08 pull_request_target takes its workflow file and checkout commit from the default branch regardless of the PR's base, and actions/checkout v7 (GA 2026-06-18, enforcement backported to v5/v6 on 2026-07-20) now 'refuses common pwn request patterns by default'. If your workflows pin @v4 or @v5 you received a behaviour change on 2026-07-20 without editing anything.
- Most of the security merge gates GitHub advertises are unavailable to you on a private repository. 'You need to pay to use Advanced Security features in private repositories on GitHub.com' - that covers CodeQL code scanning and therefore the 'Require code scanning results' ruleset rule, secret scanning push protection, and dependency review. The ruleset editor will happily show you rules you cannot turn on. The free substitutes are genuinely good (NuGet Audit, yarn npm audit, gitleaks v8.30.1 released 2026-03-21) but you have to know to wire them yourself, and nothing in the GitHub UI tells you that.
- .NET 10 silently changed your dependency-audit posture. NuGetAuditMode 'defaults to `all` when a project targets `net10.0` or higher. Otherwise NuGetAuditMode defaults to `direct`.' Your build is already auditing transitive dependencies and reporting NU1901-NU1904 as warnings you are almost certainly scrolling past. Turning the top two severities into errors in CI only - via Microsoft's documented `<WarningsAsErrors Condition=" '$(AuditPipeline)' == 'true' ">` pattern and `dotnet restore -p:AuditPipeline=true` - is about an hour of work and converts existing noise into a real gate without breaking anyone's local build.
- The realistic decay mode for a two-person team is not bypass, it is a red main that stays red. The CI Theater study (1,270 projects, 534,417 builds) found 85% of projects had at least one build broken for more than four days, and that on the smallest projects those builds took an average of 40 days to fix. Nobody plans for this. The mitigations are cultural and cheap - a written rule that a red default branch outranks feature work, revert rather than fix-forward if it is not a few minutes' work, and keeping the whole required chain inside DORA's roughly 10-minute upper limit - and they matter more than any additional check you could add.
- GitHub shipped a code-quality merge gate you have probably not seen. The 'Require code quality results' ruleset rule blocks merges at thresholds of 'Errors', 'Warnings and higher', 'Notes and higher' or 'All', and there is a separate 'Restrict code coverage' rule in public preview. Worth knowing these exist before you spend a week building your own coverage-threshold action - and worth knowing they sit behind the same private-repo licensing as the rest of code scanning.
- Required status checks are matched by literal string, and the failure is asymmetric. Rename a job and the required check name never reports, which blocks every pull request repository-wide until someone with admin rights notices and edits the ruleset - and the fastest fix in the moment is an admin merge, which is precisely the bypass you built the gate to prevent. GitHub also warns that 'If a check and a commit status have the same name, both must pass when that name is required', so a name collision between an Action and a third-party app blocks silently. One required name, backed by an aggregator job, makes this entire class of incident impossible.
- The cheapest gate on the list has the strongest compliance story and zero CI cost. 'Require all comments on the pull request to be resolved before it can be merged' is one checkbox, and it is the mechanism that makes SSDF PW.7.2 - 'record and triage all discovered issues and recommended remediations in the development team's workflow or issue tracking system' - true by construction rather than by assertion. Teams routinely skip it as trivial and then have no answer when asked how review findings are tracked to closure.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- GitHub merge queue. It is not available on private repositories outside GitHub Enterprise Cloud, and even if it were, it exists to solve semantic conflicts between many concurrent PRs landing on a busy branch. DORA's own guidance for a healthy team is three or fewer active branches and merging to trunk at least daily; with two developers you will rarely have two mergeable PRs in flight simultaneously. It also multiplies CI minutes (every queue entry rebuilds on a gh-readonly-queue branch) and GitHub's docs warn that jumping the queue triggers full rebuilds of in-progress entries. The thing merge queue buys you - main is never broken by a combination that individually passed - is worth real money at 50 engineers and roughly nothing at two.
- Two required approving reviews (CIS Software Supply Chain Security Guide 1.1.3, 'approval of two strongly authenticated users'). This is not merely expensive here, it is arithmetically impossible: GitHub states plainly that 'Pull request authors cannot approve their own pull requests', so two approvals requires three humans on a two-person team. Do not attempt a workaround. Instead write a one-paragraph compensating-control note now - one approval, plus author-cannot-approve, plus 'Require approval of the most recent reviewable push', plus an automated gate - and keep it with your risk analysis. You will be asked for it by a customer security questionnaire before you are asked for it by OCR.
- 'Require branches to be up to date before merging' (the strict status-check option; CIS 1.1.10). Without a merge queue this serialises every merge: dev A merges, dev B must rebase and re-run the full CI suite, and with two people alternating you burn a 10-minute build per merge for a conflict class that a fast test suite would catch anyway. Enable it later if you ever see a semantic conflict reach main; do not pay for it prospectively.
- Required signed commits (CIS 1.1.12, SSDF PS.1.1 Example 3). Genuine supply-chain value, but the setup cost lands on both machines, both IDEs, any bot account, and every rebase, and CIS's own note is that pull requests with unsigned commits simply cannot be merged - so a misconfigured key becomes a hard stop on a Friday afternoon. This is a good month-six item once the rest is stable and habitual, or a good item to add the week a third developer joins. Not a launch blocker.
- CODEOWNERS with required code-owner review across the whole repository (CIS 1.1.6/1.1.7 applied broadly). With two people, repo-wide code-owner review means one named person is a single point of failure for every merge; when they are on leave the team's only options are to wait or to bypass, and bypass wins. Scope CODEOWNERS narrowly to `.github/**` (so nobody can quietly weaken the pipeline) and to the tenancy-resolution and multi-tenant data-access code. That is two lines in the file and it targets the two things where a second pair of eyes is actually load-bearing.
- A separate required check per analysis type - SAST, DAST, IaC scanning, licence compliance, container scanning, coverage threshold, bundle-size budget, each with its own name in the required list. This is the shape most enterprise pipelines take and it is the specific failure this brief warns about. Every additional required name is another string that can be orphaned by a rename, another workflow that can sit Pending after a path filter, and another negotiation about whether it may be bypassed. Consolidate behind one `ci / gate` name and manage the contents in code.
- A formal change advisory board, release sign-off tickets, or any approval step outside the pull request. DORA's finding is direct: 'no evidence was found to support the hypothesis that a more formal, external review process was associated with lower change fail rates', and heavier processes correlate with larger, less frequent batches and higher production impact. For a HIPAA system the temptation is to add ceremony because the data is sensitive; the evidence says the sensitivity argues for faster, smaller, better-tested changes, not slower ones.
- Organization-level rulesets, enterprise policies, 'Evaluate' enforcement rollouts, required workflows sourced from a central repository, and the whole governance-at-scale layer. These are GitHub Enterprise features designed to enforce policy consistently across dozens of repositories. You have one repository and two people who talk to each other every day. Revisit only if the codebase splits into several repos.
- Full build provenance, SLSA attestation, reproducible builds and signed release artefacts (SSDF PS.2, PS.3, PO.3.2 Example 4). These matter when third parties consume your build output and need to verify it independently. You are operating a hosted multi-tenant application for eleven offices; nobody downloads your artefacts. The archive-and-retain obligation in PS.3.1 is worth satisfying cheaply (keep tagged releases and their CI run records), but signing infrastructure is not.
- Coverage percentage as a merge gate (including GitHub's new 'Restrict code coverage' rule, currently public preview). A coverage floor on a 53k-line C# plus 27k-line TypeScript codebase written without one will either be set so low it certifies nothing or so high it blocks every PR. Report coverage as a comment, watch the trend, and spend the effort on the tenant-isolation suite instead - that is coverage that maps to your actual worst-case failure.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`ci / gate`** -- tool: GitHub Actions (first-party)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 8-16 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A single aggregator job named `gate` that declares `needs: [backend, frontend, deps-audit, secrets, tenant-isolation]` and runs with `if: always()`, failing unless every needed job result is success or skipped. This is the ONLY check name you add to the ruleset's required list. Everything else - build, unit tests, lint, audits - hangs off it. Adding, renaming or splitting a job never requires touching branch protection again, and a job legitimately skipped by an `if:` condition cannot silently satisfy a required check because `gate` inspects `needs.*.result` explicitly rather than relying on GitHub's skipped-equals-success behaviour.
- Why at this team size: This is the highest-leverage decision in the whole area and it costs one extra job. It removes three failure modes at once: (1) required-check names orphaned by a job rename, which blocks every PR until an admin notices; (2) path-filtered workflows sitting in Pending forever, which GitHub's own docs tell you to avoid by not requiring skippable workflows; (3) the political cost of asking two people to agree on which of fifteen check names should be required. From the outside the team sees exactly one green tick. Keep the whole chain inside DORA's ~10 minute upper limit - if it exceeds that, split the slow parts into a scheduled run rather than adding a second required name.
- Tool maintenance evidence: actions/checkout v7.0.1, published_at 2026-07-20T15:10:05Z per https://api.github.com/repos/actions/checkout/releases/latest ; GitHub documents the `always()` + `needs` aggregation pattern at https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/collaborating-on-repositories-with-code-quality-features/troubleshooting-required-status-checks

**`ci / tenant-isolation`** -- tool: GitHub Actions + your existing xUnit/integration test harness

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 12-24 to author the suite; ~1 to wire it into the gate | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A job feeding `ci / gate` that runs the cross-tenant test suite against at least two seeded tenant databases and asserts that a request carrying office-a's Host header cannot read, write or enumerate office-b data through any anonymous-reachable endpoint (registration, login, password reset, document upload) or any authenticated endpoint. It should fail closed: if the harness cannot prove it exercised both tenants, the job fails.
- Why at this team size: You have stated that tenancy resolves from the Host header only and that cross-tenant leakage is the highest-consequence failure mode. A gate is worth its friction in proportion to the consequence it prevents, and this is the only check on this list whose failure mode is a reportable breach across eleven offices. It is also the check most likely to catch a regression that two SDE 1s reviewing each other's PRs would not spot by reading. Everything is synthetic data today, so build and break this now while destructive testing is free. If you only fund one thing on this list beyond the branch ruleset, fund this.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Runs on the same first-party GitHub Actions runner as ci / gate; no third-party dependency introduced. Content of the suite is the testing area's remit, not this one.

**`ci / deps-audit`** -- tool: NuGet Audit (built into the .NET SDK) + `yarn npm audit` (Yarn 4)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 3-5 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Fails the build on known-vulnerable dependencies in both stacks without any paid licence: for .NET, `dotnet restore -p:AuditPipeline=true` with an MSBuild property group that adds NU1903 and NU1904 (high and critical) to WarningsAsErrors only when AuditPipeline is true; for the frontend, `yarn npm audit --all --recursive --severity high`, which exits non-zero when a report is found. Both feed ci / gate.
- Why at this team size: Nearly free: on .NET 10 NuGetAuditMode already defaults to `all`, so your build is already computing transitive vulnerability data and discarding it as warnings. You are only choosing to fail on the top two severities. Microsoft's `AuditPipeline` conditional is specifically designed so a newly published advisory breaks CI without breaking a teammate's local build at 11pm - which is exactly the property that stops this gate from becoming the reason someone starts using admin merge. Cap it at high+critical: auditing at the default `low` level on an ABP Commercial dependency tree will produce noise you cannot action and the gate will be disabled within a month.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Microsoft Learn 'Auditing package dependencies for security vulnerabilities' updated 2026-07-07 and documents NuGet 7.0 / .NET 10 SDK 10.0.100 / Visual Studio 2026 behaviour including the NuGetAuditMode default change and `dotnet package update --vulnerable` (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages). Yarn CLI docs for `yarn npm audit` at https://yarnpkg.com/cli/npm/audit document --severity values and non-zero exit.

**`ci / secrets`** -- tool: gitleaks

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-4 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs gitleaks over the PR's commit range and fails on any finding, feeding ci / gate. Add a committed `.gitleaks.toml` with allowlist entries for known synthetic test fixtures so that false positives are fixed once, in version control, rather than by re-running the job.
- Why at this team size: GitHub secret scanning push protection is an Advanced Security feature and 'You need to pay to use Advanced Security features in private repositories on GitHub.com', so the built-in option is not available to you by default. A leaked OpenIddict signing key, SQL Server connection string or MinIO credential in a repo that is about to face the public internet is a materially worse outcome than a leaked API key in a hobby project, because the connection strings are per-tenant database credentials. This is a ten-line workflow step with a well-maintained single binary and no service to run.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Latest release v8.30.1 dated 2026-03-21, previous v8.30.0 dated 2025-11-26, per https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases - actively maintained within the last 12 months.

**`CodeQL / Analyze (csharp, javascript-typescript)`** -- tool: GitHub CodeQL (paid on private repos) or .NET analyzers + ESLint (free)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-8 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Static analysis on PRs. On a private repo this requires a paid GitHub Code Security licence; if that is not available, the free substitute is to enable the .NET built-in code-quality and security analyzers with `<EnableNETAnalyzers>`/`<AnalysisMode>` plus ESLint security rules for the Angular side, run inside ci / gate at warning level and reviewed rather than enforced.
- Why at this team size: Advisory, not a merge gate, and deliberately so: SAST on a 53k-line ABP codebase will produce a first-run backlog that two people cannot triage in a week, and a required check with an unclearable backlog is the fastest route to bypass culture. But it must EXIST and have been triaged once before you face anonymous internet traffic, which is why launch_blocking is yes while gate_or_advisory is advisory. Promote it to a required gate at the 'Errors' threshold only after the initial backlog is at zero - which is a decision for month three, not week one.
- Tool maintenance evidence: GitHub documents the 'Require code scanning results' and 'Require code quality results' ruleset rules with thresholds Errors / Warnings and higher / Notes and higher / All (https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/set-pr-thresholds), and the paid-on-private-repos constraint at https://docs.github.com/en/billing/concepts/product-billing/github-advanced-security

**`Ruleset: main-protected`** -- tool: GitHub repository rulesets

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-4 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: One branch ruleset targeting the default branch, enforcement Active, bypass list EMPTY. Rules: Require a pull request before merging with 1 required approval, 'Dismiss stale pull request approvals when new commits are pushed', 'Require approval of the most recent reviewable push', and 'Require all comments on the pull request to be resolved before it can be merged'; Require status checks to pass with exactly one entry, `ci / gate`; Block force pushes; Restrict deletions; Require linear history. Do NOT enable 'Require branches to be up to date before merging'. Also enable repository-level 'Allow auto-merge'.
- Why at this team size: This is the whole defensible minimum in one screen, and it is five rules, not thirty. 'Require approval of the most recent reviewable push' is the one people skip and the one that matters most here: it means the person who last pushed cannot be the approver, which is the only mechanical separation of duties available to a two-person team. 'Require conversation resolution' costs zero CI minutes and is what makes SSDF PW.7.2's 'record and triage all discovered issues' true by construction rather than by promise. Leaving the bypass list empty is what distinguishes this from classic branch protection, where admins are exempt by default and both of you are admins. Auto-merge is what keeps the approval from becoming a scheduling problem.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Rule names and options verified at https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/available-rules-for-rulesets ; GitHub shipped automatic branch-protection-to-ruleset migration on 2026-08-11 (https://github.blog/changelog/2026-08-11-automatically-migrate-branch-protection-rules-to-repository-rulesets/), confirming active investment.

**`ci / ruleset-drift`** -- tool: GitHub Actions + GitHub REST API (repository rules endpoints)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 3-4 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A scheduled workflow (weekly) that calls `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets` and `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{id}`, normalises the JSON, and diffs it against a committed baseline at `.github/rulesets/main.json`. On any difference it opens an issue naming the changed fields. The baseline file is itself covered by CODEOWNERS so changing it requires the other developer's approval.
- Why at this team size: CIS 1.1.19 is a control entirely about auditing changes to branch protection rules, and it exists because in a small team the people who can loosen the gate are the same people the gate constrains. This is the cheapest possible compensating control for having no separation of duties: the config becomes a reviewed artefact in the repo, and any out-of-band change produces an issue rather than passing unnoticed. It also produces the SSDF PO.3.2 'pipelines-as-code' and PO.3.3 'audit trail' artefact for free, and gives you something concrete to hand an auditor asking how change control is enforced.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Endpoints documented at https://docs.github.com/en/rest/repos/rules including GET/POST /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets, PUT/DELETE /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}, and GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/rulesets/{ruleset_id}/history for versioned history.

**`Monthly ruleset bypass and gate-health review`** -- tool: GitHub Rule Insights + ruleset history API + a markdown file in the repo

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 1 to set up, ~20 minutes per month | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A recurring 20-minute review: open Rule Insights filtered to bypasses (the dashboard shows successes, failures and bypasses over time plus 'Top bypassers' on GitHub Team), check the ruleset history endpoint for config changes since last month, and record the result - including 'no bypasses' - in a dated CHANGE-CONTROL.md committed to the repo. Also note any period main was red for more than 24 hours.
- Why at this team size: This is the only item here that is compliance-shaped rather than engineering-shaped, and it is the one that turns everything above into evidence. 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) requires you to 'regularly review records of information system activity' and 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires the documentation be retained six years - GitHub's audit log gives you 180 days, so the durable record has to be a file in your own repo. Twenty minutes a month is affordable at one developer-week per month, and 'we reviewed bypasses monthly and here are 24 dated entries' is a materially different conversation with an auditor than 'branch protection is on'. It also gives you an early signal if the gates are being routed around, which is the failure mode you cannot see from inside.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Rule Insights behaviour verified at https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/managing-rulesets-for-a-repository ; the organization audit log is documented as retaining 180 days at https://docs.github.com/en/organizations/keeping-your-organization-secure/managing-security-settings-for-your-organization/reviewing-the-audit-log-for-your-organization



## 2. Test strategy and depth

**Verification verdict for this area: `minor-corrections`** (22 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- Claim 17: the Thoughtworks Radar volume number is wrong. The 'Broad integration tests' blip page shows 'Published: Apr 03, 2024' and blipUpdatedDate 'Apr 03, 2024' -- the April 2024 edition is Volume 30, not Volume 34. 'Component testing' (Adopt) is 'Published: Oct 23, 2024' (Vol 31). The blip pages do not print volume numbers at all, so 'Vol 34' is unsupported by the cited source and is a superseded/incorrect version label.
- Claim 19: the cited ABP multi-tenancy page does NOT say the IMultiTenant filter provides no cross-tenant protection under database-per-tenant. It says the opposite about the filter in general ('ABP automatically filters entities for the current tenant', 'a tenant can not access to data of another tenant by default'). The sentence the claim leans on -- 'this approach won't work if your tenants have separate databases' -- refers to the preceding example of DISABLING the filter (_dataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>()) to query across tenants, not to the filter's protective effect. The architectural conclusion is an inference the page does not state.
- Claim 21: the cited devblog supports only the global.json snippet. It contains nothing about testconfig.json replacing runsettings, and nothing about zero-test assemblies failing or exit code 8. Both are documented elsewhere, and the exit-code-8 framing is misleading: 'test session ran zero tests' = exit code 8 is a Microsoft.Testing.Platform behavior (documented on the MTP troubleshooting page), not a .NET 10 SDK change; and .runsettings is not wholesale 'replaced' -- the docs give a partial mapping table with several settings moving to CLI options instead, and VSTest mode still honors runsettings.
- Claim 11: the conclusion 'Vitest 4.x is not compatible with Angular 20.3' overstates what the registry shows. @angular/build 20.3.35 declares vitest ^3.1.1 as an OPTIONAL peer dependency -- that makes Vitest 4.x an undeclared/unsupported combination (npm peer warning or ERESOLVE), not a demonstrated runtime incompatibility. Also, the '[EXPERIMENTAL] Run application unit tests' description and the runner enum ["karma","vitest"] are in the package's builders.json / unit-test schema.json, not in the cited registry metadata document.

### Standard, in summary

The 2026 standard is still pyramid-shaped, not trophy-shaped, and the two most authoritative published mixes agree: Software Engineering at Google targets "80% of our tests being narrow-scoped unit tests... 15% medium-scoped integration tests... and 5% end-to-end tests", and Microsoft's ASP.NET Core guidance says outright "If a behavior can be tested using either a unit test or an integration test, choose the unit test." What has genuinely changed since ~2022 is the database question: Microsoft's EF Core team now explicitly recommends "good test coverage of their application running against their actual production database system" and warns that SQLite-as-a-fake produces false passes (SQL Server is case-insensitive by default, SQLite is not; provider-specific translations and raw SQL do not survive the swap). This puts EF Core's own docs in direct conflict with the ASP.NET Core integration-testing page (which recommends SQLite in-memory) and with the ABP startup template (which ships SQLite in-memory). "Enough" is defined by credible sources as a *ratio and a rate of change*, not an absolute number: Sonar's default gate is 80% coverage on **new** code and <=3% duplication on new code, while Microsoft explicitly refuses to name a number and warns "A high code coverage percentage isn't an indicator of success". For this specific stack the binding constraint is not proportion but *shape*: ABP's shipped integration tests resolve application services straight out of DI with `AddAlwaysAllowAuthorization()` pre-wired, so authorization, ASP.NET Core filters, model binding, routing and the multi-tenancy middleware -- i.e. every mechanism that stops cross-tenant leakage on a Host-header-resolved tenancy model -- are not exercised by the test shape the framework hands you.

### Claims and sources

**A2.1** `[yes]` Google's published target test mix is 80% narrow-scoped unit tests, 15% medium-scoped integration tests, and 5% end-to-end tests -- not the 70/20/10 figure that circulates in blog posts.

- Threshold: `80% small / 15% medium / 5% large`
- Source: Software Engineering at Google, Chapter 11 (Testing Overview), O'Reilly/abseil.io -- https://abseil.io/resources/swe-book/html/ch11.html
- Cross-reference: https://martinfowler.com/articles/practical-test-pyramid.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim: "80% of our tests being narrow-scoped unit tests that validate the majority of our business logic; 15% medium-scoped integration tests that validate the interactions between two or more components; and 5% end-to-end tests". Same chapter defines small tests as single-process, no I/O, no sleeps; medium as multi-process, localhost network allowed; large as multi-machine. The often-quoted 70/20/10 traces to Google's 2010-era blog framing (https://testing.googleblog.com/2010/12/test-sizes.html, which I opened and which gives runtime targets of <60s / <300s / <900s but NO percentage split). Cite the book number, not the blog number. Fowler/Vocke corroborate the shape: "Write tests with different granularity. The more high-level you get the fewer tests you should have."

**A2.2** `[yes]` Microsoft's official ASP.NET Core guidance is to prefer the unit test whenever a behavior could be covered by either a unit or an integration test, and to limit integration tests to the most important infrastructure scenarios.

- Threshold: `n/a (explicit preference rule, no number)`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Integration tests in ASP.NET Core -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/test/integration-tests
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/unit-testing-best-practices
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Page metadata: applies to ASP.NET Core 3.1-11.0, default moniker aspnetcore-10.0, last updated 2026-08-26. Verbatim: "Therefore, limit the use of integration tests to the most important infrastructure scenarios. If a behavior can be tested using either a unit test or an integration test, choose the unit test." Also verbatim: "Don't write integration tests for every permutation of data and file access with databases and file systems... a focused set of read, write, update, and delete integration tests are usually capable of adequately testing database and file system components." And: "Separate unit tests from integration tests into different projects."

**A2.3** `[yes]` Microsoft's EF Core team recommends testing against the real production database system, and states that SQLite used as a database fake produces divergent results versus SQL Server (case sensitivity, unsupported queries, provider-specific methods, raw SQL dialect).

- Threshold: `n/a (recommendation); EF Core's own suite is cited as >30,000 tests against SQL Server completing "in a few minutes"`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Choosing a testing strategy (EF Core) -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/testing/choosing-a-testing-strategy
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/testing/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Page metadata updated_at 2026-06-26. Verbatim summary bullet: "We recommend that developers have good test coverage of their application running against their actual production database system." On SQLite: "SQL Server does case-insensitive string comparison by default, whereas SQLite is case-sensitive. This can make your tests pass against SQLite where they would fail against SQL Server (or vice versa)." And: "we recommend either writing your tests against your real database, or if using a test double is an absolute necessity, taking onboard the cost of a repository pattern". Names Testcontainers and SQL Server LocalDB as setup options. THIS CONTRADICTS the ASP.NET Core page -- see the disagreements field.

**A2.4** `[yes]` The ASP.NET Core integration-testing page recommends the SQLite provider (not the EF Core in-memory provider) for in-memory testing, which is a weaker position than the EF Core team's own guidance on the same question.

- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Integration tests in ASP.NET Core -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/test/integration-tests
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/testing/choosing-a-testing-strategy
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim: "The EF-Core in-memory database provider can be used for limited and basic testing, however the SQLite provider is the recommended choice for in-memory testing." Sample code on that page (ASP.NET Core 10.0 sample) registers a singleton open SqliteConnection with DataSource=:memory: and UseSqlite. Both Microsoft pages agree on rejecting the EF in-memory provider; they disagree on whether SQLite is an acceptable destination or a trap.

**A2.5** `[yes]` EF Core's in-memory provider is explicitly discouraged by Microsoft for testing purposes and is described as supported only for legacy applications.

Listed limitations: transactions not supported, raw SQL completely unsupported, fewer query types than SQLite, slower than SQLite. ABP independently reaches the same conclusion: its docs say the EF in-memory provider "doesn't support transaction and rollback scenarios" whereas SQLite is "a real DBMS and supports the fundamental SQL database features."

**A2.6** `[yes]` EF Core ships a first-class way to assert that no migration has been forgotten, and Microsoft explicitly recommends turning it into a test: `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` / `context.Database.HasPendingModelChanges()`.

This can be used to write a unit test that fails when you forget to add a migration." This is the single cheapest high-value gate available to this team and it is Microsoft's own suggestion.

**A2.7** `[yes]` Microsoft publishes concrete isolation techniques for real-database tests: an xUnit class fixture that creates/seeds once, transaction-rollback for write tests, a separate database + collection fixture (parallelism disabled) for tests that manage their own transactions, and Respawn for efficient cleanup.

Write tests are more problematic, but transactions can be used to make sure they're properly isolated. Transactional tests are the most problematic, requiring logic to reset the database back to its original state, as well as disabling parallelization." This is the single highest-consequence finding in this area for an app about to accept anonymous internet traffic with four self-registering external roles.

- Notes: Page metadata: default moniker aspnetcore-10.0, updated_at 2026-07-22. Verbatim: "A controller unit test avoids scenarios such as filters, routing, and model binding. Tests that cover the interactions among components that collectively respond to a request are handled by integration tests." Directly load-bearing here: ABP implements authorization as filters and tenancy as middleware, so neither ABP unit tests nor ABP DI-level integration tests can cover them. Only a WebApplicationFactory/TestServer test can.

**A2.15** `[yes]` The default credible definition of "enough" test coverage is a gate on NEW code, not on the whole codebase: Sonar's out-of-the-box quality gate requires >=80.0% coverage on new code and <=3.0% duplication on new code.

- Threshold: `Coverage on new code >= 80.0%; duplicated lines on new code <= 3.0%; no new issues; all new Security Hotspots reviewed`
- Source: SonarSource Documentation -- Understanding quality gates (SonarQube Server) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The four Sonar way conditions verbatim: "No new issues are introduced", "All new Security Hotspots are reviewed", "New code test coverage is greater than or equal to 80.0%", "Duplication in the new code is less than or equal to 3.0%". Rationale verbatim: it "focuses on keeping high quality standards for new code, rather than spending a lot of effort remediating old code." Contrast with Microsoft, which names NO number and warns: "A high code coverage percentage isn't an indicator of success, and it doesn't imply high code quality" and "Setting an overly ambitious code coverage percentage goal can be counterproductive." Angular's v20 coverage page uses 80/80/80/80 only as an illustration ("suppose you want the code base to have a minimum of 80% code coverage"), not as a recommendation.

**A2.16** `[yes]` Flakiness has a published tolerance threshold: value degrades as a suite approaches 1% flaky tests, and Google operates at roughly 0.15%.

- Source: Software Engineering at Google, Chapter 11 (Testing Overview) -- https://abseil.io/resources/swe-book/html/ch11.html

Same chapter states the Beyonce Rule ("If you liked it, then you shoulda put a test on it") and warns coverage "only measures that a line was invoked, not what happened as a result" and becomes "a goal unto itself". This is the number to hold a two-person team's E2E suite to -- it is the reason to cap E2E at single digits rather than let it grow.

Recommended alternative: service virtualization plus contract tests. Component testing (Adopt) verbatim rationale: "test suites should be stable and run quickly"; browser-based tests are "slow, more flaky and harder to debug"; jsdom-based in-memory component tests are the balance point. Secondary-tier source (a consultancy radar) but a widely-cited industry one, and it independently corroborates the pyramid over the trophy. **A2.19** `[no]` In multi-tenant ABP with database-per-tenant, the IMultiTenant global query filter provides no cross-tenant protection -- isolation comes entirely from connection-string resolution driven by ICurrentTenant.

- Threshold: `n/a (architectural constraint)`
- Source: ABP.IO Documentation -- Multi-Tenancy -- https://abp.io/docs/latest/framework/architecture/multi-tenancy
- Cross-reference: https://abp.io/docs/latest/testing/integration-tests
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: ABP verbatim: "ABP automatically filters entities for the current tenant when you query from database" -- but also, regarding disabling the filter: "Note that this approach won't work if your tenants have separate databases since there is no built-in way to query from multiple database in a single database query." ABP documents built-in tenant resolvers reading user claims, query string, route, HTTP header and cookie (all `__tenant` by default) plus a domain/subdomain resolver. `using (CurrentTenant.Change(tenantId)) { ... }` is the documented scoping API and is the hook a tenant-isolation test uses. Consequence for testing: since isolation is connection-string-based, a test that asserts "tenant A cannot read tenant B's row" must go through tenant resolution (host header -> tenant -> connection string), which ABP's DI-level integration tests do not do.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The page does not state this. "This approach" in the quoted note refers to DISABLING the IMultiTenant filter (_dataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>()) in order to query across tenants -- i.e. cross-tenant querying is what breaks under separate databases, not the filter's protection. Supportable restatement: "ABP applies an automatic IMultiTenant query filter so a tenant cannot read another tenant's rows by default; under database-per-tenant the effective boundary is which connection string ICurrentTenant resolves, and ABP provides no built-in way to query across tenant databases in one query." Do not assert "the filter provides no protection" from this page.
- Verifier read from the page: "ABP automatically filters entities for the current tenant when you query from database ... Note that this approach won't work if your tenants have separate databases since there is no built-in way to query from multiple database in a single database query. You should handle it yourself if you need it."

Horizontal section tests "whether a user can access resources or conduct operations belonging to users with equivalent roles" -- that is the cross-tenant / cross-patient case for this app. WSTG does not use the phrase multi-tenancy, so mapping tenant isolation to the horizontal-escalation case is my inference, not OWASP's wording. Useful because it gives the negative-test suite a citable requirement ID for HIPAA documentation without dragging in a full ASVS assessment.

**A2.21** `[partially]` `dotnet test` behaviour changed materially in the .NET 10 SDK: Microsoft.Testing.Platform is selected via global.json, runsettings is replaced by testconfig.json, and a test assembly containing zero tests now fails rather than passes.

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: I opened and read the .NET Blog post (published 2025-08-21, author Mariam Abdullah) and confirmed the global.json snippet verbatim and the statement "MTP is designed to be the future of test execution in .NET". The exit-code-8 behaviour and the testconfig.json replacement for --settings came from the Microsoft Learn VSTest->MTP migration guide surfaced in search; I did NOT open that Learn page directly, so I am marking this entry partial rather than verified. Package maintenance is verified: Microsoft.Testing.Platform 2.3.3 published 2026-07-28, and xunit.v3 4.0.0 published 2026-08-15 (both via api.nuget.org registration index). Relevant because a CI job that passes today can begin failing for a non-test reason after an SDK bump.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Only the global.json part is on the cited page. Corrected and re-sourced: (a) MTP mode of dotnet test is new in the .NET 10 SDK and enabled by that global.json block -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/unit-testing-with-dotnet-test; (b) .runsettings is not simply "replaced": MTP uses [appname].testconfig.json and the docs give a partial migration mapping in which several runsettings features (loggers, blame/dump, coverage, MaxCpuCount) map to CLI options rather than JSON -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/microsoft-testing-platform-config; VSTest mode still consumes runsettings; (c) exit code 8 = "the test session ran zero tests" is a Microsoft.Testing.Platform exit code, not a .NET 10 SDK behavior change -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/microsoft-testing-platform-troubleshooting -- and it can be suppressed with --ignore-exit-code 8.
- Verifier read from the page: "{ "test": { "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform" } } -- "In a solution with MTP enabled via global.json, every test project must use Microsoft.Testing.Platform.""

**A2.22** `[yes]` NIST SP 800-218 (SSDF v1.1) is the current federal secure-development framework and locates executable-code testing under practice PW.8, but I could not extract the exact PW.8/PW.8.2 wording from the primary PDF.
