- Cross-reference: https://csrc.nist.gov/Projects/ssdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: unverified
- Notes: What I verified: the publication record confirms title "Secure Software Development Framework (SSDF) Version 1.1: Recommendations for Mitigating the Risk of Software Vulnerabilities", version 1.1, February 2022, DOI 10.6028/NIST.SP.800-218. The CSRC project page confirms 1.1 is current, that there is no v1.2, and that SP 800-218A exists as a GenAI community profile. What I could NOT verify: the exact wording of PW.8, PW.8.1 and PW.8.2 and their implementation examples. I downloaded https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf (739,891 bytes) but this environment has no working pdftotext/pypdf/poppler, and WebFetch could not extract the practice table from the PDF. The PW.8 title ("Test Executable Code to Identify Vulnerabilities and Verify Compliance with Security Requirements") appeared only in secondary search summaries. Do not quote a PW.8 requirement ID in a compliance document without opening the PDF yourself.

### Where sources disagree

- Microsoft vs Microsoft on the test database. The ASP.NET Core integration-testing page (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/test/integration-tests, updated 2026-08-26) recommends SQLite in-memory: "the SQLite provider is the recommended choice for in-memory testing." The EF Core testing pages (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/testing/choosing-a-testing-strategy, updated 2026-06-26) recommend the opposite: "We recommend that developers have good test coverage of their application running against their actual production database system", and describe SQLite-as-fake limitations that "tend to eventually become problematic when testing EF Core applications, even if they don't seem to be at the beginning." Both are first-party, both current. I lean to the EF Core team here because they own the divergence and because SQL Server collation behaviour is directly load-bearing for a registration endpoint -- but the disagreement is real and a reviewer can cite either page.
- Microsoft vs ABP on which test type is the default. Microsoft: "If a behavior can be tested using either a unit test or an integration test, choose the unit test" and "limit the use of integration tests to the most important infrastructure scenarios." ABP: integration tests are "Easier to write since you don't work to establish mocking" and run "much closer to the real application test", with the caveat "Write unit or integration test where it is necessary and you find effective to write and maintain it." ABP's template embodies its preference by giving you five layer-mapped projects with an integration harness ready to go.
- Pyramid vs trophy. The pyramid position has two strong sources: Google's book (80/15/5) and Fowler/Vocke ("the more high-level you get the fewer tests you should have"). The testing trophy -- 'write tests, not too many, mostly integration' -- originates in blog writing (Kent C. Dodds, Guillermo Rauch) and I found no primary-source empirical study supporting it over the pyramid; the 2025-26 commentary I saw was secondary (consultancy blogs) and converged on 'neither shape is universally correct; aim testing effort where your bugs come from.' Thoughtworks lands in between: component testing in Adopt (a trophy-ish middle layer) but broad integration tests in Hold (anti-trophy). I am labelling the trophy a secondary-source position, not an evidenced one.
- The Google mix itself is quoted two ways. Software Engineering at Google (the book) states 80% small / 15% medium / 5% large. The widely-repeated 70/20/10 traces to Google's 2010-era blog-and-comment era; the Test Sizes post I opened gives runtime targets (<60s / <300s / <900s) but no percentage split at all, and secondary accounts of the 70/20/10 figure say the numbers were "essentially pulled out of a hat." Use 80/15/5 and cite the book.
- Karma's status. karma-runner/karma's own README: "Karma is deprecated and is not accepting new features or general bug fixes." Angular's current Karma guide: "While Vitest is the default test runner for new Angular projects, Karma is still a supported and widely used test runner." Both first-party to their respective projects. Compounding it, Karma's stated security-support window runs "until 12 months after Angular CLI's Web Test Runner support is marked stable" -- a milestone Angular abandoned in favour of Vitest, so the end date is undefined.
- Coverage thresholds. Sonar ships a default gate of >=80.0% coverage on new code and <=3.0% duplication on new code. Microsoft names no number at all and warns against ambitious targets ("A high code coverage percentage isn't an indicator of success"). Angular's v20 coverage page shows 80/80/80/80 but frames it as an illustration ("suppose you want the code base to have a minimum of 80%"), not a recommendation. Google's book warns coverage "only measures that a line was invoked, not what happened as a result" and that targets get treated "like a ceiling." These reconcile only if you distinguish new-code coverage (where 80% is a defensible default) from whole-codebase coverage (where no credible source names a number).

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The ABP startup template ships integration tests with authorization switched off. `AddAlwaysAllowAuthorization()` is pre-wired into the test module's ConfigureServices -- ABP's own docs say verbatim "This is already done for the startup template integration tests." So every green integration test in the solution today proves nothing whatsoever about whether a permission check exists, is correct, or was accidentally deleted. For an app that is about to let four external roles self-register over the public internet, this is the gap that matters most and it is invisible unless you go read ABP's Authorization page rather than its Testing page.
- ABP's integration tests are not HTTP tests. `AbpIntegratedTest<T>` resolves application services straight out of the DI container. That means ASP.NET Core filters, model binding, routing and -- decisively -- ABP's multi-tenancy middleware never execute. Since tenancy on this system resolves from the Host header only, the mechanism that separates eleven offices' databases is literally not reachable from the test shape the framework provides. Microsoft states the same boundary from the other direction: "A controller unit test avoids scenarios such as filters, routing, and model binding."
- Two first-party Microsoft doc sets give opposite answers on the same question, and both were updated in 2026. The ASP.NET Core integration-testing page (updated 2026-08-26) says "the SQLite provider is the recommended choice for in-memory testing" and "If a behavior can be tested using either a unit test or an integration test, choose the unit test." The EF Core testing pages (updated 2026-06-26) say "We recommend that developers have good test coverage of their application running against their actual production database system" and treat SQLite-as-a-fake as a trap that "tend[s] to eventually become problematic... even if they don't seem to be at the beginning." A team that reads only one page will build the wrong suite and have a Microsoft URL to justify it.
- The SQLite/SQL Server divergence has a concrete, likely failure mode for this app. SQL Server's default collation is case-insensitive; SQLite is case-sensitive. A test asserting that a duplicate email or username is rejected can pass on SQLite and behave differently on SQL Server -- and the direction of the error is the dangerous one for a registration endpoint open to anonymous traffic. Microsoft names this exact example: "This can make your tests pass against SQLite where they would fail against SQL Server (or vice versa)."
- Migrations are never executed by ABP's test suite. The test database is created and seeded, not migrated. With database-per-tenant across 11 databases (headroom 33), the first execution of a new migration against SQL Server happens at deploy, against production data, eleven times. Meanwhile EF Core hands you the cheap half of the fix for free: Microsoft's Managing Migrations page says of `HasPendingModelChanges()`, verbatim, "This can be used to write a unit test that fails when you forget to add a migration" -- and from EF Core 9, calling Migrate() with pending model changes throws at runtime rather than warning.
- Angular 20 is about three months from end of support. Per Angular's own release policy page, v20 was released 2025-05-28, active support ended 2025-11-19, and LTS ends 2026-11-28. This is not a test-strategy fact on its face, but it determines the answer: any large Karma/Jasmine suite written this quarter is written against a runner Angular has already replaced, on a version that stops receiving security patches this year. It is the reason to cap frontend test investment rather than expand it.
- Karma's own stated support horizon is anchored to an event that never happened. The karma-runner README says "Karma is deprecated and is not accepting new features or general bug fixes" and that critical security fixes continue "until 12 months after Angular CLI's Web Test Runner support is marked stable." Angular evaluated Web Test Runner and shipped Vitest instead -- so the clock on Karma's security support has no defined start. Angular's own docs meanwhile still say "Karma is still a supported and widely used test runner." Two first-party sources, no clear answer.
- The Vitest escape hatch already exists in 20.3.x, and the obvious way to reach for it breaks. `@angular/build@20.3.35` (published 2026-08-26) contains a builder described verbatim as "[EXPERIMENTAL] Run application unit tests" whose runner enum is exactly ["karma","vitest"], and its optional peer dependency is `vitest ^3.1.1`. Installing Vitest today gets you 4.1.11 (published 2026-08-18), which does not satisfy that range. A team that follows a 2026 blog post about 'Angular + Vitest' without checking the peer range spends an afternoon on an unexplained failure. The same builder schema, incidentally, already supports `setupFiles` and defaults `watch` to false in non-TTY environments -- both limitations that early-v20 write-ups claim are missing.
- The 70/20/10 test-mix number that everyone quotes is not the number Google publishes. Software Engineering at Google states "80% of our tests being narrow-scoped unit tests... 15% medium-scoped integration tests... and 5% end-to-end tests." The 70/20/10 figure comes from the 2010-era blog framing and was, by the account in circulation, roughly made up. Citing 70/20/10 pushes a team to build twice the integration layer the evidence supports -- which for a two-person team is the difference between a suite they maintain and one they abandon.
- Flakiness has a published tolerance and it is far tighter than intuition. Software Engineering at Google: "as you approach 1% flakiness, the tests begin to lose value"; Google runs at ~0.15% and still sees "thousands of flakes every day." A two-person team with an eight-test E2E suite gets exactly zero flaky-test budget in practice. This is the concrete argument for capping E2E and for quarantining rather than retrying -- retry loops hide the 1% until it is 10%.
- `dotnet test` semantics changed in the .NET 10 SDK. Microsoft.Testing.Platform is selected through a `global.json` `"test": {"runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform"}` block, `--settings`/runsettings is superseded by `testconfig.json`, and -- the one that bites -- a test assembly containing zero tests now fails with exit code 8, where VSTest exited 0. A CI job that is green today can turn red on an SDK bump for a reason that has nothing to do with the code. (Partly verified: I confirmed the global.json snippet on the .NET Blog directly; the exit-code and testconfig.json details came from search summaries of the Learn migration guide, which I did not open.)
- The industry's most-cited radar has moved in the opposite direction from small-team instinct. Thoughtworks puts 'broad integration tests' in Hold and in-memory 'component testing' (jsdom) in Adopt. A team with no QA reasons 'we can't test manually, so test end-to-end.' The published position is the reverse: push toward fast in-memory component tests and away from anything requiring live run-time dependencies.
- In database-per-tenant, ABP's multi-tenancy query filter gives you no protection at all -- and ABP says so. `IMultiTenant` filtering is a shared-database mechanism; ABP's docs note "this approach won't work if your tenants have separate databases since there is no built-in way to query from multiple database in a single database query." Isolation is entirely a function of tenant resolution feeding connection-string selection. So the test that matters is not 'does the filter work' but 'does a request bearing tenant A's Host header ever obtain tenant B's connection string, and what happens when the host is unrecognised' -- including the case where an unresolved tenant silently falls back to the host (null-tenant) context.
- ABP quietly recommends the opposite of Microsoft on the unit-vs-integration question. ABP's docs say integration tests are "Easier to write since you don't work to establish mocking and dealing with the dependencies" and its unit-test page nudges you away from mocking ("It gets harder to setup the test objects and mock all the dependencies. See the Integration Tests section"). Microsoft says choose the unit test whenever either would do. Following ABP's grain gets you a suite that is integration-heavy -- which is defensible on its own, except that ABP's integration tests are the ones with authorization disabled and no HTTP pipeline. The two facts compound.
- ABP's Angular testing modules also grant all permissions by default. `CoreTestingModule.withConfig()` swaps `PermissionService` for a `MockPermissionService` that grants every policy. So the frontend mirror of the backend problem exists too: a component test cannot tell you whether a permission-gated element is correctly hidden from a role that should not see it. If any UI element gates PHI visibility by permission, that assertion has to be written against the real service or asserted in the E2E layer.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Consumer-driven contract testing (Pact) between the Angular app and the .NET API. ABP already generates the TypeScript client proxies from the backend service signatures via `abp generate-proxy`. A CI step that regenerates the proxies and fails if `git diff` is non-empty gives you drift detection for roughly one hour of work; Pact would cost days and duplicate what the code generator already guarantees.
- A whole-codebase coverage threshold (the classic '80% or the build fails'). Microsoft explicitly warns that "Setting an overly ambitious code coverage percentage goal can be counterproductive" and that a high percentage "doesn't imply high code quality". Applied retroactively to 53k lines of C# and 27k lines of TypeScript that were written without tests, this gate is unreachable, so it gets bypassed within a sprint -- and the bypass habit then spreads to the gates that matter. Use new-code coverage advisory instead.
- Broad integration tests that stand up SQL Server + Redis + MinIO + OpenIddict together on every PR. Thoughtworks places exactly this in Hold, defining it as tests "that require live versions of all run-time dependencies" and calling them "often fragile and unhelpful" because you cannot tell whether a failure came from your code, a version mismatch, or the environment. Two people cannot afford to triage that. Fake Redis and MinIO (ABP's own microservice guidance says "in the test environment, it's sufficient to use in-memory cache and in-memory event bus") and use a real database only.
- Migrating the Angular suite from Karma/Jasmine to Vitest right now. It is genuinely the right destination -- ABP's current template already ships Vitest, and Angular's default for new projects is Vitest -- but on 20.3.x the `unit-test` builder is still labelled EXPERIMENTAL, the `refactor-jasmine-vitest` schematic is a v22 artifact that Angular itself calls experimental, and Angular's own migration page says "Migrating an existing project to Vitest is considered experimental." Do it as one deliberate task inside the v21/v22 upgrade, not as a separate project competing with the authorization tests.
- Running the test suite once per tenant (an 11- or 33-way CI matrix). Tenant isolation in a database-per-tenant model is a property of connection-string resolution, not of per-tenant business logic. Two tenants plus one host-context case in the authz-contract-tests project proves the mechanism; eleven proves the same thing eleven times and multiplies your CI minutes and your flake surface by eleven.
- Mutation testing or a mutation-score threshold as a merge gate. Even at Google scale this is a reporting tool, and Microsoft's own page says not to chase the score. As a required check on a two-person team it is a multi-hour PR wait for a signal nobody has time to act on.
- Visual regression / screenshot-diff testing. Highest false-positive rate of any automated check, requires baseline curation every time a theme or ABP module updates, and this is an internal-facing LOB portal where a shifted button is not the failure mode anyone is worried about.
- Load and performance tests as a merge gate. Performance budgets belong in a separate area, and a scheduling portal for eleven offices is not going to fall over on throughput before it falls over on a tenancy bug. Revisit after the first year of real traffic.
- A formal test-plan / test-documentation regime (ISO/IEC 29119-style test specifications, traceability matrices, manual regression scripts). Real audit value exists, but for two SDE 1s it converts test-writing hours into document-writing hours. The cheaper equivalent: name the authz-contract-tests cases after their WSTG-ATHZ-02 objectives so the test names themselves are the traceability artifact.
- Property-based testing (FsCheck / CsCheck) and fuzzing of the domain layer. High-quality technique, but it pays off in algorithmic or parsing-heavy code. A scheduling domain's invariants are better served by a handful of explicit [Theory] cases that a second developer can read in ten seconds.
- Cross-browser E2E matrices. One Chromium project in Playwright. Adding Firefox and WebKit triples the runtime and the flake rate to defend against rendering differences that this application's user base will not encounter.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`backend-tests`** -- tool: dotnet test (.NET 10 SDK) + xUnit + Shouldly + NSubstitute, as shipped by the ABP 10.0.2 template; GitHub Actions ubuntu-latest

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 3-6 (workflow YAML, caching, results summary; the test projects already exist) | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs the ABP-shipped xUnit projects (Domain.Tests, Application.Tests, EntityFrameworkCore.Tests) on every PR against SQLite in-memory, exactly as the template configures them. Publishes a test-results summary. No coverage threshold attached.
- Why at this team size: This is the zero-argument baseline: the projects already exist because ABP generated them, so the only work is CI wiring. It is also the fastest gate, so it should run first and fail fast. Deliberately no coverage gate here -- Microsoft's own guidance is that "a high code coverage percentage isn't an indicator of success", and a percentage gate on 53k existing lines is exactly the kind of rule two people start bypassing.
- Tool maintenance evidence: xunit.v3 4.0.0 published 2026-08-15 and Microsoft.Testing.Platform 2.3.3 published 2026-07-28 (https://api.nuget.org/v3/registration5-gz-semver2/xunit.v3/index.json, .../microsoft.testing.platform/index.json). NSubstitute 6.2.0 published 2026-08-11; Shouldly 4.3.0 stable published 2025-01-23 with 5.0.0-preview.2 on 2026-07-18 (.../nsubstitute/index.json, .../shouldly/index.json) -- all released within 12 months.

**`migration-drift`** -- tool: EF Core 10 CLI / DbContext API -- `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes`, `context.Database.HasPendingModelChanges()`

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 1-3 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Fails the build if the EF Core model has changed without a corresponding migration, using Microsoft's own recommended pattern: an xUnit test asserting `context.Database.HasPendingModelChanges()` is false (or the `dotnet ef migrations has-pending-model-changes` CLI equivalent).
- Why at this team size: Highest value per hour of anything in this list. From EF Core 9 onward, Migrate() with pending model changes throws PendingModelChangesWarning at runtime -- so a forgotten migration is a startup failure, and with database-per-tenant it is a startup failure repeated across 11 (headroom 33) databases at deploy time. One test file, no infrastructure, and Microsoft explicitly suggests writing it as a test.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party EF Core feature, added in EF Core 8.0 and documented on a page last updated 2026-08-11 (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/managing-schemas/migrations/managing). Ships with the SDK -- no third-party dependency to maintain.

**`authz-contract-tests`** -- tool: Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing (WebApplicationFactory) + xUnit, layered over the ABP host project; ICurrentTenant.Change for seeding fixtures

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 20-32 initial (new project, factory that overrides tenancy + auth wiring, a token/role helper, first ~10 endpoint groups); then ~1 per new endpoint group | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: An HTTP-pipeline test project built on WebApplicationFactory/TestServer that deliberately does NOT call AddAlwaysAllowAuthorization(). For each externally reachable endpoint group it asserts: anonymous request returns 401 (or 200 only where anonymous access is intended -- registration, login, password reset, upload); a caller in the wrong external role returns 403; the correct role returns 2xx. Includes tenant-resolution cases: a request with Host header for tenant A must not read data seeded for tenant B, and a request with an unknown/absent host must not silently resolve to the host (null-tenant) context.
- Why at this team size: This is the one thing on the list I would refuse to launch without, and it is the one thing the ABP template actively hides. ABP pre-wires AddAlwaysAllowAuthorization() into the startup template's integration tests, and ABP integration tests resolve application services from DI rather than over HTTP -- so filters, model binding, routing and the multi-tenancy middleware never execute. Microsoft says the same thing from the other side: "A controller unit test avoids scenarios such as filters, routing, and model binding." On a portal where tenancy resolves from the Host header only and cross-tenant leakage is the stated top failure mode, no existing test in the solution can catch a tenancy or permission regression. Budget this as the single biggest item and cut something else -- a table-driven [Theory] over (endpoint, role, expected status) keeps the marginal cost near zero afterwards. Maps to OWASP WSTG-ATHZ-02 for HIPAA documentation.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing is first-party and ships with ASP.NET Core 10; the integration-testing guidance page carries an updated date of 2026-08-26 and an applies-to list that includes aspnetcore-10.0 and 11.0 (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/test/integration-tests).

**`db-integration-tests-sqlserver`** -- tool: GitHub Actions service container (mcr.microsoft.com/mssql/server, e.g. 2025-latest) + EF Core; Respawn only if transaction rollback proves insufficient. Testcontainers for .NET is the alternative if the team wants the same setup to run locally.

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 12-20 initial; ~15 min per added repository test | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs the migrations end-to-end against a real SQL Server container in CI, then executes a focused set of repository/query tests (read, write, update, delete plus every custom repository method and any raw SQL) against it. Uses transaction-rollback isolation for write tests per Microsoft's documented pattern.
- Why at this team size: ABP's shipped EntityFrameworkCore.Tests run on SQLite in-memory, and Microsoft's EF Core team is explicit that this produces false results against SQL Server -- most dangerously on case sensitivity (SQL Server default collation is case-insensitive, SQLite is case-sensitive), which is exactly how a duplicate-email or duplicate-username check passes in CI and fails in production. It also means migrations are never actually executed before deploy. Keep this suite small on purpose: Microsoft's own rule is "limit the use of integration tests to the most important infrastructure scenarios", which for this app means the migration run itself plus every custom repository method and anything with raw SQL or provider-specific EF.Functions. Prefer the GitHub Actions `services:` block over Testcontainers initially -- it is ~10 lines of YAML versus a fixture abstraction to maintain. One line of infrastructure dependency, as permitted: whether you also get a pre-prod database to run these against is an infra decision outside this scope.
- Tool maintenance evidence: mcr.microsoft.com/mssql/server currently publishes 2025-latest and 2025-CU8-ubuntu-24.04 tags (https://mcr.microsoft.com/v2/mssql/server/tags/list). Testcontainers for .NET 4.14.0 published 2026-08-14 and Respawn 7.0.0 published 2025-11-30 (api.nuget.org registration indexes) -- both released within 12 months. GitHub Actions service containers are documented and Linux-runner-only (https://docs.github.com/en/actions/tutorials/use-containerized-services/use-docker-service-containers).

**`frontend-tests`** -- tool: Angular CLI 20.3.x. Stay on the shipped Karma/Jasmine setup for now; when the team upgrades past v20, switch to `@angular/build:unit-test` with runner "vitest" (which ABP's current template already uses).

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 4-8 (CI wiring plus fixing whatever specs the template left broken) | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Runs the Angular unit/component suite headlessly on every PR with `ng test --no-watch --code-coverage`, producing a coverage report artifact but not enforcing a threshold. Component tests run in jsdom, not a real browser.
- Why at this team size: Cheap and it stops obvious breakage, but do not build a large Angular suite in the next quarter. Angular 20's LTS ends 2026-11-28 and the runner underneath these specs is deprecated, so specs written now are written twice. Thoughtworks puts in-memory component testing (jsdom) in Adopt and browser-based testing on the slow/flaky side of the ledger, so keep tests in jsdom. Concentrate the specs you do write on the four self-registration flows and any component that renders PHI, and let everything else wait for the Vitest migration.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Angular CLI 20.3.35 published 2026-08-26 (https://github.com/angular/angular-cli/releases). The `unit-test` builder in @angular/build@20.3.35 is labelled "[EXPERIMENTAL] Run application unit tests" with runner enum ["karma","vitest"] and optional peer vitest ^3.1.1 (verified by reading the published tarball). Karma itself is deprecated by its maintainers (https://github.com/karma-runner/karma) -- hence the deliberate do-not-migrate-yet stance.

**`e2e-smoke`** -- tool: Playwright (@playwright/test for the Angular app, or Microsoft.Playwright from .NET if the team prefers one language)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 16-24 initial; treat the cap as a hard budget | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A hard-capped Playwright suite of 6-8 journeys run on PR to main and on deploy: register (each external role), login, password reset, document upload, view own record, and one negative cross-tenant journey (authenticate against tenant A's host, attempt to reach a tenant B resource, assert refusal). Uses getByRole/test-id locators and web-first assertions.
- Why at this team size: The hard cap is the recommendation. Google's published mix puts end-to-end at 5% and warns that value collapses as flakiness approaches 1%; Thoughtworks puts broad integration tests in Hold precisely because small teams cannot afford to debug them. Two SDE 1s with no QA and no DevOps will abandon a 40-test E2E suite within two months, and an abandoned suite is worse than none because it teaches the team to ignore red. Write the six journeys that are anonymous-reachable from the public internet, put a written rule in the repo that the suite may not exceed eight tests without deleting one, and quarantine (do not retry-loop) anything that flakes twice.
- Tool maintenance evidence: @playwright/test 1.62.1 published 2026-07-30 (https://registry.npmjs.org/@playwright/test); Microsoft.Playwright 1.62.0 published 2026-08-11 (api.nuget.org). Best-practice guidance confirmed at https://playwright.dev/docs/best-practices -- "Automated tests should verify that the application code works for the end users, and avoid relying on implementation details".

**`coverage-new-code`** -- tool: coverlet.collector + dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool (ReportGenerator), rendered as a PR summary via the ReportGenerator GitHub Action or a markdown summary report

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-8 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Collects Coverlet/Cobertura output from backend-tests and Angular coverage from frontend-tests, merges, and posts a PR comment showing coverage of lines changed in that PR. Advisory only -- it informs the reviewer, it does not block.
- Why at this team size: Coverage on new code is the only coverage metric a credible source actually gates on -- Sonar's default is >=80.0% on new code, explicitly because it avoids "spending a lot of effort remediating old code". But start it advisory. Turn it into a required gate only after three months of data shows the team routinely clears the number without gaming it; flipping a gate on before then is how you get [ExcludeFromCodeCoverage] sprinkled through the domain layer. Note the alternative: SonarQube Cloud gives you this gate turnkey, but the repo is private, so that is a paid decision rather than a technical one.
- Tool maintenance evidence: coverlet.collector 10.0.1 published 2026-05-18; dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool 5.5.11 published 2026-07-27 (api.nuget.org registration indexes) -- both within 12 months. Microsoft documents this exact pairing at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/unit-testing-code-coverage.

**`mutation-scan-critical`** -- tool: dotnet-stryker with a stryker-config.json restricting `mutate` to the relevant namespaces and excluding Migrations and generated files

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 4-8 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A weekly scheduled Stryker.NET run scoped to just the tenancy, authorization, and domain-invariant code paths -- not the whole solution. Publishes an HTML report as an artifact for the team to read; no threshold enforced.
- Why at this team size: This answers the question coverage cannot: are the assertions in the tenancy and permission tests actually strong enough to fail when the logic is wrong. Scoped to a few namespaces it runs in minutes rather than hours, and Microsoft's own instruction is "Don't chase a 100% mutation score. Instead, focus on high risk or business critical areas." Weekly and advisory, read over coffee, never a gate -- a mutation gate on a two-person team is a guaranteed bypass. Defer this until after authz-contract-tests exists; mutating code that has no tests tells you nothing you don't already know.
- Tool maintenance evidence: dotnet-stryker 4.16.0 published 2026-07-03 (https://api.nuget.org/v3/registration5-gz-semver2/dotnet-stryker/index.json). Microsoft publishes first-party guidance for it at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/mutation-testing (updated 2026-04-03), including the config-file exclusion pattern.



## 3. End-to-end browser testing

**Verification verdict for this area: `minor-corrections`** (19 claims checked)

### Standard, in summary

In 2026 the defensible default for a new E2E suite on an Angular SPA is Playwright (@playwright/test 1.62.1, published 2026-07-30), and the margin over Cypress has widened rather than narrowed: 57.9M weekly npm downloads vs 7.6M, free built-in sharding vs sharding that Cypress's own GitHub Actions doc gates behind Cypress Cloud, and native cross-origin navigation vs Cypress's mandatory cy.origin() wrapper -- which matters here because ABP's Angular client redirects to a separate OpenIddict auth-server host for the authorization-code+PKCE flow. Cypress is emphatically not dead (15.21.1, 2026-08-25) and Angular lists it first among five integrated e2e packages, but nothing about it wins on this app's specific shape. On count, no authority gives an absolute number: Google's canonical guidance is a 70/20/10 unit/integration/E2E mix, Fowler says "reduce the number of end-to-end tests to a bare minimum" around "one or two more crucial user journeys," and Microsoft Learn goes further -- "Discourage the use of UI tests, because they tend to be unreliable." The binding constraint for two developers is DORA's "feedback from automated tests in less than ten minutes," which on a private-repo GitHub runner (2 vCPU / 8 GB, half the public-repo machine) caps a PR gate at roughly 8-14 tests; everything else belongs in a nightly job. Authentication follows Playwright's documented setup-project + storageState + dependencies pattern, never the OAuth password grant, which RFC 9700 (BCP 240) 2.4 now prohibits outright: "The resource owner password credentials grant MUST NOT be used." Accessibility assertions inside E2E are a first-party, actively maintained pattern -- Playwright's own accessibility-testing doc is built on @axe-core/playwright (4.13.0, published 2026-08-11, 9.5M weekly downloads).

### Claims and sources

**A3.1** `[partially]` Playwright is actively maintained and is the current-generation default E2E runner: @playwright/test stable is 1.62.1, published 2026-07-30, with a steady ~5-6 week stable cadence through 2026 (1.59.0 2026-04-01, 1.60.0 2026-05-11, 1.61.0 2026-06-15, 1.62.0 2026-07-24) and nightly 1.63.0-alpha builds still shipping as of 2026-08-28.

- Threshold: `@playwright/test 1.62.1 (2026-07-30); next alpha 1.63.0-alpha-2026-08-28`
- Source: npm registry API (registry.npmjs.org, first-party npm metadata) -- https://registry.npmjs.org/@playwright/test
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/release-notes
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Fetched the full packument and sorted by publish time. Cross-checked against playwright.dev/docs/release-notes, whose newest documented release is Version 1.62 (new component-testing stories/galleries model, AbortSignal on operations, WebP screenshots, Reporter.preprocess(), retryStrategy). The GitHub API was blocked by this session's proxy, so release dates come from npm rather than GitHub Releases.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Every version and date is exact, and the next dist-tag is literally 1.63.0-alpha-2026-08-28. The overstatement is 'steady ~5-6 week stable cadence THROUGH 2026'. The registry shows a ~10-week gap earlier in 2026: 1.58.0 (2026-01-23) and 1.58.2 (2026-02-06) to 1.59.0 (2026-04-01) is 7.8 weeks from the last patch and 9.9 weeks from 1.58.0 -- no stable minor shipped in all of March. The 5-6 week cadence is only true from 1.59.0 onward. Restate as: '~5-6 week stable cadence since April 2026 (1.59.0 onward), following a ~10-week gap over Feb-Mar 2026.'
- Verifier read from the page: "dist-tags: {'latest': '1.62.1', 'next': '1.63.0-alpha-2026-08-28'}; 1.62.1 2026-07-30T16:36:55.324Z; 1.59.0 2026-04-01; 1.60.0 2026-05-11; 1.61.0 2026-06-15; 1.62.0 2026-07-24; 1.63.0-alpha-2026-08-28 2026-08-28T05:33:36.527Z"

**A3.2** `[yes]` Cypress is also actively maintained -- 15.21.1 published 2026-08-25, on a roughly two-week cadence (15.17.0 2026-06-09 through 15.21.1 2026-08-25) -- but adoption has diverged sharply: @playwright/test draws 57,911,488 weekly npm downloads and the playwright package 86,996,936, against 7,569,177 for cypress, for the week 2026-08-21 to 2026-08-27.

- Threshold: `cypress 15.21.1 (2026-08-25); 57.9M vs 7.6M weekly downloads, week ending 2026-08-27`
- Source: npm registry API + npm downloads API (api.npmjs.org, first-party) -- https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/cypress
- Cross-reference: https://registry.npmjs.org/cypress
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Pulled both packuments and the weekly download counts directly from npm's own APIs rather than relying on the 2026 comparison blog posts, which are secondary and disagree with each other (one claimed 33M weekly for Playwright, another 78M). Do not read the download gap as a quality verdict -- Playwright's number is inflated by transitive use as a scraping/automation library. The maintenance evidence is what matters: both tools clear the 12-month bar comfortably.

**A3.3** `[yes]` Angular's official CLI documentation lists exactly five integrated E2E packages, offered by `ng e2e` when no e2e target exists: Cypress (ng add @cypress/schematic), Nightwatch (ng add @nightwatch/schematics), WebdriverIO (ng add @wdio/schematics), Playwright (ng add playwright-ng-schematics), Puppeteer (ng add @puppeteer/ng-schematics). Protractor is not mentioned anywhere on the page. Angular states there is nothing special about any of them: "The ng e2e command is really just running the e2e builder under the hood."

- Threshold: `5 packages; ng e2e is a thin builder wrapper -- no Angular-specific E2E coupling`
- Source: Angular (angular.dev) -- CLI End-to-End Testing -- https://angular.dev/tools/cli/end-to-end
- Cross-reference: https://registry.npmjs.org/playwright-ng-schematics
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Because ng e2e is just a builder shim, the Angular schematic is optional for Playwright. That matters: playwright-ng-schematics is a community package (not Microsoft), its current 22.1.0 pins @angular-devkit/core ^22.0.0, and the last release on the Angular-20 line was 20.0.2 on 2025-12-11. On Angular 20.3.19 you should skip the schematic and run `yarn create playwright` -- Playwright's own installer, which also offers to write the GitHub Actions workflow. By contrast Cypress's @cypress/schematic 6.0.0 shipped 2026-08-26 and @cypress/angular 5.0.0 the same day, so Cypress's Angular integration is the better-maintained one; it just isn't needed for E2E.

**A3.4** `[yes]` Playwright's official GitHub Actions CI guidance is a single ubuntu-latest job with `timeout-minutes: 60`, actions/checkout@v6, actions/setup-node@v6 at `node-version: lts/*`, `npm ci`, `npx playwright install --with-deps`, `npx playwright test`, and actions/upload-artifact@v5 uploading `playwright-report/` with `retention-days: 30` under `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}`. A container variant uses `image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.62.0-noble` with `options: --user 1001`.

- Threshold: `timeout-minutes: 60; checkout@v6; setup-node@v6; upload-artifact@v5; retention-days: 30; container mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.62.0-noble`
- Source: Playwright -- Continuous Integration -- https://playwright.dev/docs/ci
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/intro
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Fetched and text-stripped the page directly to read the literal YAML rather than trusting a summariser. The same page documents `--only-changed=origin/$GITHUB_BASE_REF` as a fail-fast preliminary run on pull requests, with the explicit caveat that it is a heuristic and "it's important that you always run the full test suite after the preliminary test run." Playwright's system requirements are Node.js latest 22.x, 24.x or 26.x -- check this against whatever Node the Angular 20 build pins.

**A3.5** `[yes]` Playwright's own documentation contradicts itself on CI worker count. /docs/ci states: "We recommend setting workers to '1' in CI environments to prioritize stability and reproducibility" with `workers: process.env.CI ? 1 : undefined`. /docs/test-parallel shows `workers: process.env.CI ? 2 : undefined` under "Limit the number of workers on CI, use default locally." The generated default config in /docs/test-configuration uses 1.

- Threshold: `workers: process.env.CI ? 1 : undefined (CI doc + generated config) vs ? 2 : undefined (parallelism doc)`
- Source: Playwright -- Continuous Integration / Parallelism / Configuration -- https://playwright.dev/docs/ci
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/test-parallel
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: I opened all three pages and read the literal code blocks. Resolve it in favour of 1 for this app: GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest is 2 vCPU / 8 GB for private repositories (4 vCPU / 16 GB only for public), and that budget is already shared with a .NET 10 API, SQL Server, Redis and MinIO. `workers` also accepts a percentage of logical cores, e.g. '50%'. Note separately that every Playwright benchmark blog post you will read assumes the public-repo runner.

**A3.6** `[yes]` Playwright's generated default configuration encodes the CI hardening defaults: `fullyParallel: true`, `forbidOnly: !!process.env.CI`, `retries: process.env.CI ? 2 : 0`, `workers: process.env.CI ? 1 : undefined`, `use.trace: 'on-first-retry'`. Per-test timeout defaults to 30000 ms and web-first `expect()` assertions to 5000 ms.

- Threshold: `retries 2 on CI, 0 locally; trace 'on-first-retry'; test timeout 30000 ms; expect timeout 5000 ms`
- Source: Playwright -- Configuration -- https://playwright.dev/docs/test-configuration
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/test-retries
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Retries plus trace-on-first-retry is the whole flakiness strategy for a small team: the HTML reporter categorises results as passed / flaky (failed then passed on retry) / failed, so 'flaky' becomes a first-class signal you can triage weekly without building any tooling. `forbidOnly` on CI stops a stray `test.only` from silently reducing your gate to one test -- a failure mode that is invisible in a green check.

**A3.7** `[yes]` Playwright 1.62 added `testConfig.retryStrategy`. The default 'immediate' retries a failed test as soon as a worker is free; 'isolated' "runs all retries at the end, one by one in a single worker, to minimize interference with the rest of the suite."

- Threshold: `retryStrategy: 'isolated' -- new in Playwright 1.62 (2026-07-24)`
- Source: Playwright -- Release notes, Version 1.62 -- https://playwright.dev/docs/release-notes
- Cross-reference: https://registry.npmjs.org/@playwright/test
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the single highest-leverage flakiness knob for a resource-starved runner, and it is two months old, so it appears in no tutorial. On a 2 vCPU box shared with SQL Server, most flakes are contention, not app bugs; retrying in an otherwise idle worker at the end of the run converts a large share of them into passes without any test changes. Combine with `retries: 2`.

**A3.8** `[yes]` Playwright's documented authentication pattern is a `setup` project matched by `testMatch: /.*\.setup\.ts/` that authenticates once and calls `page.context().storageState({ path: authFile })`, with test projects declaring `dependencies: ['setup']` and `use.storageState: 'playwright/.auth/user.json'`. The docs call this "the recommended approach for tests without server-side state", require the auth directory be gitignored, and warn: "The browser state file may contain sensitive cookies and headers that could be used to impersonate you or your test account. We strongly discourage checking them into private or public repositories." For tests that mutate shared server state, the documented pattern is one account per parallel worker keyed on `testInfo.parallelIndex`; API-based auth uses `APIRequestContext` and `request.storageState({ path: authFile })`.

- Threshold: `setup project + storageState + testProject.dependencies; playwright/.auth in .gitignore; per-worker accounts via testInfo.parallelIndex when state is mutated`
- Source: Playwright -- Authentication -- https://playwright.dev/docs/auth
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/test-projects
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: For an ABP booking wizard that writes appointments, you are in the state-mutating case: either use the per-worker account fixture or keep workers at 1 on CI (recommended here -- it solves both problems at once). The docs also warn that UI Mode does not run the setup project by default, so local runs go stale until you manually re-run auth.setup.ts.

**A3.9** `[yes]` Playwright's saved authentication state covers cookies, local storage, IndexedDB and WebAuthn passkeys, but NOT sessionStorage: "Playwright does not provide API to persist session storage" -- the docs give a manual `page.evaluate(() => JSON.stringify(sessionStorage))` save plus `context.addInitScript()` restore snippet instead.

- Threshold: `storageState = cookies + localStorage + IndexedDB + credentials; sessionStorage excluded, manual workaround only`
- Source: Playwright -- Authentication, "Session storage" section -- https://playwright.dev/docs/auth
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/release-notes
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Load-bearing for this stack specifically. ABP's Angular client uses angular-oauth2-oidc, whose default OAuthStorage implementation is sessionStorage (see abpframework/abp issue #13073, which is titled exactly that; ABP also ships an AbpLocalStorageService alternative). If your app is on the sessionStorage default, the canonical setup-project pattern will hand your tests a browser holding the auth cookie but no access token, and the app will look logged-out or silently re-run the code flow. Verify OAuthStorage in your own Angular providers before writing a single test -- 10 minutes now, a lost day later. I could not confirm ABP 10.0.2's shipped default from first-party docs, so treat that half as unverified.

**A3.10** `[yes]` The OAuth 2.0 resource owner password credentials (ROPC / password) grant is prohibited by current IETF Best Current Practice. RFC 9700, "Best Current Practice for OAuth 2.0 Security" (BCP 240, January 2025), 2.4: "The resource owner password credentials grant [RFC6749] MUST NOT be used." 2.1.2 additionally states clients "SHOULD NOT use the implicit grant (response type token)."

- Threshold: `RFC 9700 / BCP 240 2.4 -- MUST NOT (normative prohibition), published January 2025`
- Source: IETF -- RFC 9700, Best Current Practice for OAuth 2.0 Security -- https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/auth
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This kills the most common E2E auth shortcut. The usual trick is to have the setup project POST grant_type=password to /connect/token and skip the browser login entirely. Doing that requires leaving the password flow enabled on your OpenIddict server, which becomes an unthrottled internet-facing credential-stuffing endpoint the day you leave the office LAN. OpenIddict enables authorization code, hybrid, implicit, password, client credentials, refresh token, device and token exchange flows only when explicitly opted in -- confirm the password flow is off in your production configuration, and drive login through the real authorization-code+PKCE flow once in the setup project instead.

**A3.11** `[yes]` Playwright cannot override the HTTP Host header from a browser context. The Route API documentation warns: "Some request headers are forbidden and cannot be overridden (for example, Cookie, Host, Content-Length and others...). If an override is provided for a forbidden header, it will be ignored and the original request header will be used." `testOptions.extraHTTPHeaders` (added v1.10) sets additional headers on every request but is subject to the same browser restriction.

- Threshold: `Host is a forbidden request header -- overrides silently ignored, no error raised`
- Source: Playwright -- API: class Route (route.continue / route.fallback) -- https://playwright.dev/docs/api/class-route
- Cross-reference: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Glossary/forbidden_header_name
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the most consequential finding in this area for your app. Tenancy resolves from the Host header only, so a naive cross-tenant isolation test that tries to spoof Host via extraHTTPHeaders will be silently ignored, hit the default tenant, and PASS -- a false green on your highest-consequence failure mode. The only correct approach is real distinct hostnames: add one /etc/hosts line per test tenant in the workflow (`echo '127.0.0.1 tenant-a.test tenant-b.test' | sudo tee -a /etc/hosts`) and define one Playwright project per tenant with its own `use.baseURL`. Cross-referenced against MDN's forbidden-header-name glossary, which Playwright's warning links to.

Cypress commands will timeout after the navigation and will eventually error." The GitHub Actions doc states: "Using parallelization with the Cypress GitHub Action requires setting up recording to Cypress Cloud." Playwright's `--shard=x/y` is built in and free.

Cypress additionally documents that "Navigating from HTTPS to HTTP will error" and "Cypress requires that the URLs navigated to have the same port", both of which bite in a local multi-host setup. None of this makes Cypress unusable; it makes Playwright the lower-friction choice for exactly your shape.

**A3.13** `[partially]` No credible source gives an absolute E2E test count; three primary sources converge on 'as few as possible.' Microsoft Learn goes furthest: "Discourage the use of UI tests, because they tend to be unreliable."

The 70/20/10 ratio is a proportion, not a count -- it only yields a number once you know your unit-test total, which for a greenfield suite you don't. Use the time budget in the next entry as the real cap.

Fowler is at https://martinfowler.com/articles/practical-test-pyramid.html ("Due to their high maintenance cost you should aim to reduce the number of end-to-end tests to a bare minimum" / "Maybe you'll find one or two more crucial user journeys that you can translate into end-to-end tests") and Microsoft at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/devops/develop/shift-left-make-testing-fast-reliable ("Discourage the use of UI tests, because they tend to be unreliable."). Resolve by gating a smoke subset (L3-lite) and pushing the rest nightly. Microsoft Learn also gives the specific L2 isolation rule that governs your auth setup: legacy tests calling external auth providers violate isolation and break on provider flakiness -- "Consider preventing these issues by investing in identity support within the test framework", which is precisely Playwright's setup-project pattern.

**A3.15** `[yes]` Flakiness is a permanent operating cost, not a defect to eliminate once. Google reports "a continual rate of about 1.5% of all test runs reporting a 'flaky' result", "Almost 16% of our tests have some level of flakiness associated with them", and "about 84% of the transitions we observe from pass to fail involve a flaky test." Google's documented mitigations are re-running only failing tests, marking a test flaky so it reports failure only after 3 consecutive failures, and automated quarantine: "if the flakiness is too high, it automatically quarantines the test... removes the test from the critical path and files a bug."

- Threshold: `1.5% of test runs flaky; ~16% of tests have some flakiness; 84% of pass->fail transitions are flakes`
- Source: Google Testing Blog -- Flaky Tests at Google and How We Mitigate Them -- https://testing.googleblog.com/2016/05/flaky-tests-at-google-and-how-we.html

Calibration point for two SDE 1s: these rates come from Google's engineers and infrastructure, so 'we'll just write non-flaky tests' is not a plan. The cheap version of quarantine for a two-person team is `test.fixme()` plus a line in a markdown file, reviewed once a month. Google's own warning against the 3-strikes rule applies: it "encourages developers to ignore flakiness in their own tests."

The documented WCAG scoping call is `new AxeBuilder({ page }).withTags(['wcag2a','wcag2aa','wcag21a','wcag21aa']).analyze()` asserted with `expect(results.violations).toEqual([])`, with `.include()`, `.exclude()` and `.disableRules()` for known issues. axe-core also defines a `wcag22aa` tag for WCAG 2.2 Level AA.

First, the official Playwright snippet omits `wcag22aa`, so copy-pasting it silently skips every WCAG 2.2 rule even though WCAG 2.2 has been a W3C Recommendation since 12 December 2024 -- add it. Second, Deque's own study across 2,000+ audits, 13,000+ pages and ~300,000 issues found automated testing "completely covered" 57 percent of accessibility issues, so a green axe run is not a WCAG 2.2 AA conformance claim; Playwright's doc says the same ("many accessibility problems can only be discovered through manual testing"). **A3.17** `[partially]` GitHub Actions economics constrain E2E frequency for a private repo. Included minutes per month: GitHub Free 2,000, Pro 3,000, Free for organizations 2,000, Team 3,000, Enterprise Cloud 50,000. Linux 2-core (x64) bills at $0.006/minute beyond quota. Artifact storage is 500 MB (Free) / 2 GB (Team) and is shared with GitHub Packages. Standard runners are free only for public repositories. GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest is 2 CPU / 8 GB / 14 GB SSD for private repositories versus 4 CPU / 16 GB for public, both on ubuntu-24.04.

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Do the arithmetic before you design the gate. A run that boots SQL Server, migrates a tenant DB, builds Angular 20 and drives ~12 tests is realistically 12-18 minutes on that runner. At 120 runs/month that is ~1,800 minutes -- most of a Team plan, before the build, unit-test and static-analysis jobs. Mitigations that cost nothing: trigger on `pull_request` only (not `push`), and add `concurrency: { group: ${{ github.ref }}, cancel-in-progress: true }`, which GitHub documents as cancelling in-progress runs on the same ref. Also note Playwright reports and traces land in that 500 MB / 2 GB artifact allowance, so keep `retention-days` low and upload only on failure.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Billing figures all exact. The runner hardware sentence is not on the billing page -- it is at https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/runners/github-hosted-runners, which confirms private = 2 CPU / 8 GB / 14 GB SSD, public = 4 CPU / 16 GB / 14 GB SSD, ubuntu-latest = ubuntu-24.04. Cite that second URL. (Minor: the claim's storage list omits Pro = 1 GB and Enterprise Cloud = 50 GB, which the page also gives.)

**A3.18** `[partially]` Playwright explicitly advises against caching browser binaries in CI: "Caching browser binaries is not recommended, since the amount of time it takes to restore the cache is comparable to the time it takes to download the binaries. The supported alternative if you want to skip the download is the pinned container image mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.62.0-noble. Install only the browser you gate on -- `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` -- rather than all three.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The caching quote and the stated threshold (including the hash-on-Playwright-version fallback) are verbatim on the cited page. The MB figures are NOT -- they are on https://playwright.dev/docs/browsers ("These browsers will take a few hundred megabytes of disk space when installed: 281M chromium-XXXXXX / 187M firefox-XXXX / 180M webkit-XXXX"). Cite that URL for the sizes, and note they are a macOS `du -hs` sample with placeholder build numbers, not a guaranteed per-platform figure.
