Especially under Linux, operating system dependencies need to be installed, which are not cacheable." ci.md:1001 "If you still want to cache the browser binaries between CI runs, cache these directories in your CI configuration, against a hash of the Playwright version.""

**A3.19** `[yes]` Playwright ships first-party AI test-authoring agents: planner, generator and healer, installed with `npx playwright init-agents --loop=claude` (also vscode, codex, opencode). The planner "explores the app and produces a Markdown test plan", the generator "transforms the Markdown plan into the Playwright Test files", and the healer "executes the test suite and automatically repairs failing tests." The docs state agent definitions "should be regenerated whenever Playwright is updated."

- Threshold: `npx playwright init-agents --loop=<vscode|claude|codex|opencode>; three agents (planner, generator, healer)`
- Source: Playwright -- Playwright Test Agents -- https://playwright.dev/docs/test-agents
- Cross-reference: https://playwright.dev/docs/release-notes
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Relevant precisely because you have zero E2E tests and a nine-step wizard that exists only in someone's head. The planner takes an optional seed test that sets up fixtures and global setup, so you write the auth setup project by hand once (which you must, given the OIDC and Host-header specifics above) and let the planner explore the wizard from an authenticated state. Treat generated tests as drafts to review, not output to trust -- but for a first suite this is the cheapest entry point available and it is a Microsoft-maintained feature, not a third-party add-on.

### Where sources disagree

- Playwright contradicts itself on CI workers: https://playwright.dev/docs/ci says `workers: process.env.CI ? 1 : undefined` ("We recommend setting workers to '1' in CI environments to prioritize stability and reproducibility"), while https://playwright.dev/docs/test-parallel shows `workers: process.env.CI ? 2 : undefined`. The generated default config on /docs/test-configuration uses 1. Resolved here in favour of 1 because private-repo ubuntu-latest is 2 vCPU / 8 GB and is shared with SQL Server and the .NET API -- but flagged because it means Playwright has no single house position.
- Cypress and Playwright disagree on whether to log in through the UI. Cypress's best-practices page lists it as an anti-pattern outright ("Anti-Pattern: ...using your UI to log in"; "Best Practice: ...programmatically log into your application"). Playwright's authentication doc leads with a UI-driven setup project as the recommended approach and files API-based auth under "Advanced scenarios." Both are defensible; the disagreement is real and unacknowledged by either vendor.
- No authority agrees on an E2E test count, and the three primary sources are not reconcilable into a number. Google gives a proportion (70/20/10) but no absolute count. Fowler gives neither, only "a bare minimum" and "one or two more crucial user journeys." Microsoft Learn gives no ratio at all and instead says "Discourage the use of UI tests, because they tend to be unreliable." Anyone quoting you a specific number of E2E tests is extrapolating, not citing.
- Microsoft's PR-gate rule and DORA's feedback-time rule cannot both be satisfied here. Microsoft Learn's taxonomy expects "a pull request automatically fails if the L3 test run fails" (L3 = functional tests against a deployed service, i.e. your E2E tier). DORA requires "feedback from automated tests in less than ten minutes" from CI. On a 2 vCPU private runner that also boots SQL Server and builds Angular, a full E2E suite cannot do both. Resolved by gating a smoke subset and scheduling the remainder -- but this is a genuine tension between two primary sources, not a settled practice.
- Deque's own 57% automated-coverage figure is contested inside Deque's own article. The study measured "total volume of issues detected by severity and impact" and explicitly notes that measuring "based on WCAG Success Criteria downplays the effectiveness of automated testing" -- i.e. the SC-based number is materially lower. Playwright's accessibility doc separately warns that "many accessibility problems can only be discovered through manual testing." Do not let anyone cite 57% as a coverage guarantee.
- Angular's official documentation and the actual maintenance record point in different directions for Angular integration specifically. angular.dev lists Cypress first and Cypress's Angular schematics are current (2026-08-26), while the Playwright Angular schematic is a community package that has not shipped an Angular-20-compatible release since 2025-12-11. The CI, auth, cost and cross-origin evidence all favour Playwright decisively; the Angular-schematic evidence mildly favours Cypress. The resolution is to note that `ng e2e` is only a builder shim, so the schematic is not load-bearing -- but the surface-level signal genuinely conflicts.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- You cannot fake the Host header from a browser, and the failure is silent. Playwright's Route documentation states plainly that Host is a forbidden header and "If an override is provided for a forbidden header, it will be ignored and the original request header will be used." Since tenancy resolves from Host only, the obvious cross-tenant isolation test -- set `extraHTTPHeaders: { Host: 'tenant-b.example' }` and assert you get denied -- will hit the default tenant, get denied for the wrong reason, and PASS. You would ship a green check that proves nothing about your highest-consequence failure mode. The only correct construction is real distinct hostnames resolving to the runner (one /etc/hosts line per tenant in the workflow) plus one Playwright project per tenant with its own baseURL. Almost nobody discovers this before writing the tests.
- Playwright's storageState does not include sessionStorage, and ABP's Angular OIDC client may store your token there. The auth docs say saved state covers "cookies, local storage, IndexedDB and passkey (WebAuthn) based authentication" and that "Playwright does not provide API to persist session storage." angular-oauth2-oidc -- the library ABP's Angular UI uses -- defaults its OAuthStorage to sessionStorage (abpframework/abp issue #13073 is titled exactly that). If your app is on that default, the canonical setup-project pattern hands every test a browser with the auth cookie but no access token: the app looks logged-out or silently re-runs the code flow, and you will spend a day blaming Playwright. Check your `OAuthStorage` provider before writing a line of test code.
- The E2E auth shortcut everyone reaches for is now formally prohibited. RFC 9700 (BCP 240, January 2025) 2.4: "The resource owner password credentials grant MUST NOT be used." The classic move -- have the setup project POST `grant_type=password` to /connect/token to mint a token and skip the browser login -- requires leaving the password flow enabled on OpenIddict. On an office LAN that was a testing convenience. The week you go public it is an unthrottled credential-stuffing endpoint against accounts that will hold PHI. Your E2E convenience choice quietly becomes production attack surface, and no E2E tutorial mentions this.
- Your CI runner is half the machine every tutorial assumes. GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest is 4 CPU / 16 GB for public repositories but 2 CPU / 8 GB for private ones. Every Playwright benchmark, every 'we run 200 tests in 4 minutes' post, and most of the parallel-worker tuning advice online was measured on the public-repo runner. Your numbers will be worse than everything you read, and the correct response is fewer workers, not more.
- E2E on every push will exhaust your Actions quota before it exhausts your patience. Private repos get 2,000 included minutes/month on Free and 3,000 on Pro or Team, with overage at $0.006/min for Linux 2-core. A run that boots SQL Server, migrates a tenant database, builds Angular 20 and drives ~12 tests is realistically 12-18 minutes. At 120 runs/month that is ~1,800 minutes -- most of a Team plan -- before build, unit-test and static-analysis jobs touch it. Two free mitigations: trigger on `pull_request` only, and set `concurrency: { group: ${{ github.ref }}, cancel-in-progress: true }`. Also remember Playwright reports and traces consume the 500 MB / 2 GB artifact allowance, which is shared with GitHub Packages.
- Playwright's own documentation contradicts itself on CI worker count. /docs/ci says "We recommend setting workers to '1' in CI environments" with `workers: process.env.CI ? 1 : undefined`; /docs/test-parallel shows `? 2 :`. Neither page acknowledges the other. On a 2 vCPU runner already hosting a .NET API and SQL Server, 1 is the right read -- but the more important point is that Playwright's docs are not internally consistent, so verify config advice against the specific page rather than assuming a single house position.
- Caching Playwright browsers is explicitly discouraged by Playwright, contradicting the standard CI-optimization advice. "Caching browser binaries is not recommended, since the amount of time it takes to restore the cache is comparable to the time it takes to download the binaries. Especially under Linux, operating system dependencies need to be installed, which are not cacheable." Nearly every CI-speedup blog post tells you to cache ~/.cache/ms-playwright. Obey Playwright instead -- a stale browser cache produces failures that look exactly like app bugs.
- Microsoft ships AI test-authoring agents inside Playwright itself, as of 2026. `npx playwright init-agents --loop=claude` installs a planner (explores the app, emits a Markdown test plan), a generator (turns the plan into spec files) and a healer (runs the suite and repairs failures). For a team with zero E2E tests and a nine-step wizard that is documented nowhere, seeding the planner with a hand-written authenticated seed test is the cheapest possible start -- and it is a first-party Microsoft feature, not a third-party add-on. It appears in almost no 2026 Playwright-vs-Cypress comparison.
- `retryStrategy: 'isolated'`, added in Playwright 1.62 (July 2026), will fix more of your flakiness than any amount of 'add explicit waits' advice. It runs all retries at the end, one at a time in a single worker. On a resource-starved runner most flakes are contention with SQL Server and the Angular build, not app bugs, so retrying in a quiet worker converts a large share of them into passes with zero test changes. It is two months old and therefore absent from every guide.
- Cypress's parallelization is paywalled and its own best-practices page contradicts Playwright's. Cypress's GitHub Actions doc: "Using parallelization with the Cypress GitHub Action requires setting up recording to Cypress Cloud." Playwright's `--shard=x/y` is free and built in. Separately, Cypress lists "using your UI to log in" as an outright anti-pattern, while Playwright's auth docs present UI login as the primary recommended pattern with API login relegated to "Advanced scenarios." There is no industry consensus here -- pick per-app, and for you UI login once in the setup project is right because it is the only thing that proves the OIDC redirect chain across two hosts actually works.
- A green axe run is not a WCAG claim, and the official Playwright snippet is incomplete. Deque's own study across 13,000+ pages found automated testing covered 57% of issues -- and Deque notes that measuring by WCAG Success Criteria instead of issue volume gives a materially lower number, so even the vendor's headline figure is contested within its own source. Worse, Playwright's documented example uses `withTags(['wcag2a','wcag2aa','wcag21a','wcag21aa'])` and omits `wcag22aa`, so copy-pasting it silently skips every WCAG 2.2 rule despite WCAG 2.2 having been a W3C Recommendation since 12 December 2024.
- The Angular ecosystem's E2E integration story mildly favours Cypress, which is the opposite of the overall verdict. `@cypress/schematic` 6.0.0 and `@cypress/angular` 5.0.0 both shipped 2026-08-26; `playwright-ng-schematics` is a community package whose current 22.1.0 pins Angular 22 devkit and whose last Angular-20-line release was 20.0.2 on 2025-12-11. This does not change the recommendation -- `ng e2e` is, per Angular's docs, "really just running the e2e builder under the hood" -- but it means you should install Playwright with `yarn create playwright` rather than `ng add`, and you should not read Angular's alphabetically-first listing of Cypress as an endorsement.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Cross-browser matrix on every PR (chromium + firefox + webkit). Playwright makes it a three-line config change, which is exactly why teams do it reflexively. It triples your gate's wall clock and CI minutes on a 2 vCPU private runner for a defect class that surfaces on a weekly cadence, not a per-PR one. Chromium on the gate, WebKit nightly. Revisit only if a real Safari bug reaches a user.
- Sharding across multiple runners (`--shard=x/y` + blob reporter + `merge-reports`). This is the standard scaling answer and it is actively counterproductive below roughly a 15-minute suite: each shard re-boots SQL Server, re-runs migrations, re-builds Angular and re-downloads browsers, so fixed startup cost gets multiplied by N while only the test-execution slice gets divided. At 12 tests your run is mostly startup. Playwright's sharding doc also notes balanced shards require `fullyParallel: true`, which conflicts with the `workers: 1` you want on a 2 vCPU box.
- Visual regression / screenshot snapshot testing (`toHaveScreenshot`, including 1.62's new WebP snapshots). Golden-image tests need a pinned rendering environment or they fail on font-hinting and antialiasing differences between a developer's machine and ubuntu-24.04. That means running everything in the pinned container and re-baselining on every intentional UI change. Two people shipping features will disable it within a month, and a disabled check is worse than an absent one because it teaches you that red is negotiable.
- Cypress Cloud, Microsoft Playwright Testing / Playwright Service, or a third-party analytics layer (Currents, TestDino). These sell test-run history, flake analytics and parallel orchestration. At 12-25 tests, Playwright's free HTML reporter already gives you the passed/flaky/failed split that is 90% of the value, and the weekly `--repeat-each=3` job gives you the rest. Revisit at 100+ tests or when a third engineer joins.
- A formal Page Object Model layer. Playwright documents POM and it is genuinely standard at scale, but at 12 tests the page objects are more code than the tests, and they add an indirection layer between a failing check and the line that broke. Cypress's own best-practices page even lists 'sharing page objects' as an anti-pattern. Use Playwright fixtures for shared setup and `getByRole` locators inline; extract a helper only on the third repetition.
- GitHub merge queue. GitHub documents it as providing 'the same benefits as the Require branches to be up to date before merging branch protection, but does not require a pull request author to update their pull request branch.' That benefit only materialises when concurrent merges contend for the same base -- which needs more than two developers. Just enable required status checks with 'Require branches to be up to date' off, and skip the queue.
- Automated flaky-test quarantine tooling. Google's automatic quarantine service is the right answer at Google's scale; building or buying one for a 12-test suite is pure overhead. `test.fixme()` plus a one-page markdown list reviewed monthly does the same job. Also skip Google's 3-consecutive-failures rule -- Google itself warns it 'encourages developers to ignore flakiness in their own tests.'
- A BDD/Cucumber layer (playwright-bdd, cucumber-js) over the suite. Gherkin earns its keep when a non-engineer writes or reads the specs. You have two SDE 1s and no product owner writing scenarios, so it is an extra translation layer that will drift out of sync with the tests.
- Playwright's new 1.62 component testing (the stories/galleries model). It is two months old, it replaces the previous component-testing model, and its API will move again. Angular component tests belong in TestBed/Angular Testing Library within the unit-test tier anyway -- do not let a brand-new E2E-adjacent feature pull component testing into this area's budget.
- Contract testing between the Angular client and the .NET API (Pact or similar). Justified when client and server ship on independent cadences owned by different teams. Yours are in one repo, deployed together, by the same two people. ABP's generated API proxies plus TypeScript already catch the shape mismatches that contract tests would.
- One E2E test per wizard step (nine tests just for the wizard). Tempting because the wizard has nine steps, but eight of those tests would be asserting per-step field validation, which is an Angular reactive-forms unit test costing seconds instead of minutes. One happy path through all nine, plus back-navigation and mid-wizard resume -- the two things only a browser can prove -- and push the rest down the pyramid.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`e2e / smoke (chromium)`** -- tool: @playwright/test 1.62.1 (Node 22/24/26), installed via `yarn create playwright` -- not the Angular schematic

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 24-32 h initial (harness, docker-compose for SQL Server, auth setup project, /etc/hosts wiring, CI workflow, first 3 specs); +2-3 h per additional spec; ~2 h/month upkeep | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs 8-12 Playwright specs against a locally composed stack (SQL Server + .NET API + built Angular app started via Playwright's `webServer` option) on every pull request to main. Chromium only, `workers: 1`, `retries: 2`, `retryStrategy: 'isolated'`, `trace: 'on-first-retry'`, `forbidOnly: true`. Two Playwright projects -- tenant-a and tenant-b -- each with its own `use.baseURL` pointing at a real hostname added to /etc/hosts in the workflow, plus a `setup` project that performs the real authorization-code+PKCE login once and saves storageState. Uploads playwright-report and traces on failure only.
- Why at this team size: This is the only new required gate worth adding in this area, and it is the one that buys back the manual regression pass you currently run by hand before every release. Keep it to 8-12 specs so it lands inside DORA's ten-minute feedback rule on a 2 vCPU private runner. Concretely: (1) tenant-A user completes the OIDC login and lands on the dashboard; (2) tenant-A storageState against tenant-B's host is refused -- asserted both in the UI and via APIRequestContext; (3) tenant-B login page rejects tenant-A credentials; (4) anonymous self-registration for the highest-volume external role; (5) password reset request -> token -> new password -> login; (6) the nine-step wizard, one happy path, asserting a persisted appointment; (7) back-navigation from a late step to an early step preserves entered data; (8) mid-wizard reload resumes state; (9) anonymous document upload rejects a disallowed content type; (10) authenticated upload succeeds and the file is invisible from the other tenant. Tests 2, 3 and 10 are the highest-value tests in the entire suite -- they are the only automated proof you will have that Host-header tenancy holds, and they must use real distinct hostnames because the Host header cannot be spoofed. Trigger on `pull_request` only, never `push`, with `concurrency: cancel-in-progress: true`.
- Tool maintenance evidence: @playwright/test 1.62.1 published 2026-07-30; 1.62.0 2026-07-24; 1.61.0 2026-06-15; nightly 1.63.0-alpha-2026-08-28 still shipping. https://registry.npmjs.org/@playwright/test and https://playwright.dev/docs/release-notes

**`e2e-nightly / full-suite`** -- tool: @playwright/test 1.62.1 with `schedule:` cron trigger and `workflow_dispatch`

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 4-6 h once the smoke job exists (reuse the workflow, change the trigger and the grep tags) | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Scheduled run (weeknights) of the entire E2E suite -- the smoke specs plus the remaining three external-role registrations, per-step wizard validation branches, the role/permission matrix, and a second Playwright project running the smoke subset on WebKit. Same workflow file as the gate, selected by tag with `--grep` or a separate project list. Posts failures to whatever channel the two of you actually read.
- Why at this team size: This is where the count grows without the gate slowing down. Ceiling of roughly 25 E2E tests total -- past that you are contradicting Fowler's 'bare minimum' and Microsoft's 'discourage the use of UI tests', and you will start bypassing the gate. A nightly failure that neither of you looks at is worse than no nightly, so wire the notification before you wire the schedule. WebKit belongs here rather than in the gate: it is the only browser that catches Safari-specific layout and date-input bugs, which a workers' comp portal on the public internet will hit, but it triples PR cost for a defect class that shows up weekly, not hourly.

**`e2e / a11y-axe`** -- tool: @axe-core/playwright 4.13.0 (MPL-2.0, depends on axe-core ~4.13.0, peer playwright-core >= 1.0.0)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-6 h (install, one shared AxeBuilder fixture, capture the baseline, wire the assertion) | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Runs `new AxeBuilder({ page }).withTags(['wcag2a','wcag2aa','wcag21a','wcag21aa','wcag22aa']).analyze()` at 6-8 key states reached inside the existing smoke specs -- the anonymous login page, the registration form, the dashboard, and three or four of the nine wizard steps -- asserting `violations` equals a checked-in baseline rather than an empty array on day one.
- Why at this team size: Nearly free because it rides inside tests you are already writing and already have an authenticated page for -- no separate job, no extra app boot, no extra CI minutes. Start advisory against a frozen baseline so the existing violation backlog does not block every PR on day one, then flip to required-gate on the no-new-violations delta once the baseline is green-ish (realistically 60-90 days post-launch). Add `wcag22aa` explicitly: Playwright's own documented example omits it, so the copy-paste version silently skips every WCAG 2.2 rule. Be honest internally about what green means -- Deque's own study puts automated coverage at 57% of issues by volume, so this is a floor, not a conformance claim, and a California-facing public portal will eventually need a manual assessment too.

**`e2e-weekly / flake-check`** -- tool: @playwright/test 1.62.1 (`--repeat-each`, HTML reporter's passed/flaky/failed categorisation)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 2-3 h to stand up; ~1 h/month triage | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Weekly scheduled job running the gated smoke suite with `--repeat-each=3 --retries=0` and publishing the HTML report. Any spec that does not pass 3/3 is triaged: fixed, or marked `test.fixme()` with a line in a one-page markdown quarantine list reviewed monthly.
- Why at this team size: Google measures 1.5% of test runs flaky and ~16% of its tests carrying some flakiness, with 84% of pass-to-fail transitions being flakes -- with far better engineers and infrastructure than you have. Without an explicit flake signal, a two-person team's failure mode is predictable: the gate goes red, someone re-runs it, it goes green, and within two months nobody believes the check. This is the cheapest possible version of Google's automated quarantine -- a cron job and a markdown file, not a service. Run it against the merged main so the results are attributable to the tests rather than to whatever is on someone's branch.

**`e2e / post-deploy-smoke`** -- tool: @playwright/test 1.62.1 with the `deployment_status` trigger

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 3-4 h once a deployed environment with seeded synthetic tenants exists | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Runs a 3-4 spec subset (login, tenant isolation, one wizard happy path) against the actually deployed environment, triggered by `on: deployment_status` with `if: github.event.deployment_status.state == 'success'` and `PLAYWRIGHT_TEST_BASE_URL: ${{ github.event.deployment_status.target_url }}` -- the pattern Playwright documents for exactly this.
- Why at this team size: The composed CI stack does not exercise the thing you are actually about to change: real hostnames, real TLS, real per-tenant database routing. This subset is the cheapest check that the Host-header tenancy you assert in CI still holds against the deployment. Keep it to 3-4 read-mostly specs and run it against synthetic tenants only -- it must never write test data into a tenant that will later hold PHI. One infrastructure dependency, stated once and not designed here: this requires a non-production deployed environment with seeded synthetic tenant hostnames, which the separate infrastructure exercise must provide.



## 4. Code coverage

**Verification verdict for this area: `material-errors`** (19 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- CLAIM 12 IS A MATERIAL ERROR. The claim says coverlet 'can emit every format this project needs from a single test run AND can fail the build on a threshold', citing VSTestIntegration.md for both. That page explicitly contradicts the threshold half twice: 'At the moment VSTest integration **doesn't support all features** of msbuild and .NET tool, for instance show result on console, report merging and threshold validation' and 'Feature parity with other modes is partial (for example, threshold validation and built-in merge are not provided by this integration mode).' The /p:CollectCoverage=true /p:Threshold=80 quote is from a DIFFERENT document (MSBuildIntegration.md). The two capabilities belong to two mutually exclusive coverlet integration modes and cannot be obtained from one `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage"` run. Any CI design built on this claim will silently not enforce its threshold.
- CLAIM 9 LOC METHODOLOGY IS WRONG AND THE CONCLUSION MAY REVERSE. The claim compares a raw ~80k line count against Sonar's 50k cap. The cited page states Sonar's LOC calculation EXCLUDES 'Test code. Files excluded from analysis. Code in unsupported languages. Comments or blank lines.' A raw wc-style count typically overstates Sonar LOC by 40-60%, so ~80k raw lines may well fall UNDER the 50k cap. The cap also applies only to PRIVATE projects ('Analysis of public projects: unlimited number of projects'). The claim's headline conclusion 'it exceeds the free private-project cap' is not established.
- CLAIM 12/9/10/15/16/17 CITATION HYGIENE: six claims attach quotes to URLs that do not contain them. Most consequential: claim 17 cites an eCFR HIPAA API endpoint as the source for assertions about OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 and NIST SP 800-218 (it can support neither); claim 9 cites a SonarQube CLOUD subscription page for a quote about self-hosted SonarQube SERVER Community Build vs Developer Edition, and that verbatim sentence does not appear on the current editions page either.
- CLAIM 8 CITES A DEPRECATED PARAMETER AS A CURRENT DEFAULT. The claim lists `base: auto` among Codecov's patch/project defaults. The cited page annotates that parameter '[Deprecated as of July 2020 -- Codecov's product roadmap includes a novel way to manually pick base of pull requests]'. It should not appear in a 2026 standard.

### Standard, in summary

The honest answer is that there is no universal number, and the most authoritative sources say so explicitly. Google's testing organisation states "There is no 'ideal code coverage number' that universally applies to all products" and offers 60/75/90% as acceptable/commendable/exemplary while refusing to mandate any of them; the largest empirical study (Inozemtseva & Holmes, ICSE 2014, 31,000 suites over 724k LoC) concludes coverage "should not be used as a quality target because it is not a good indicator of test suite effectiveness." What HAS converged by 2026 is the shape of enforcement rather than the number: gate on the diff (new/changed lines), trend the absolute number without gating it. Sonar's default "Sonar way" gate is coverage on new code >= 80.0% and duplication on new code <= 3.0%, and Sonar's rationale is that this "focuses on keeping high quality standards for new code, rather than spending a lot of effort remediating old code"; Codecov's `patch` status "only measures lines adjusted in the pull request" with default `target: auto, threshold: 0%`; Google says "While project wide goals above 90% are most likely not worth it, per-commit coverage goals of 99% are reasonable, and 90% is a good lower threshold." No healthcare regulation names a number: the HIPAA Security Rule text (45 CFR 164 Subpart C) contains the word "coverage" zero times, OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 has no coverage requirement, NIST SSDF PW.8 has none, and FDA's guidance -- which does not apply to a non-device scheduling portal anyway -- is explicitly risk-based ("Validation coverage should be based on the software's complexity and safety risk -- not on firm size or resource constraints"). For this stack the mechanics are settled: coverlet 10.0.1 emits Cobertura/LCOV/OpenCover in one `dotnet test` pass, Angular v20 emits LCOV via `ng test --code-coverage`, and diff-cover 10.5.1 (Apache-2.0, released 2026-08-16) reads both formats and enforces `--fail-under` against `--compare-branch=origin/main` with no paid service and no data leaving GitHub.

### Claims and sources

**A4.1** `[yes]` Google's official testing guidance states there is no universal coverage number and refuses to mandate one, offering 60% as 'acceptable', 75% as 'commendable' and 90% as 'exemplary' as guidelines only. Verbatim: "Although there is no 'ideal code coverage number,' at Google we offer the general guidelines of 60% as 'acceptable', 75% as 'commendable' and 90% as 'exemplary.' However we like to stay away from broad top-down mandates and encourage every team to select the value that makes sense for their business needs." It also warns "Be mindful that engineers may start treating your target like a checkbox and avoid increasing coverage beyond the target, even if doing so would be prudent."

- Threshold: `60% acceptable / 75% commendable / 90% exemplary (whole-project, non-binding)`
- Source: Google Testing Blog -- 'Code Coverage Best Practices' (Bender, Arguelles, Ivankovic, 7 Aug 2020) -- https://testing.googleblog.com/2020/08/code-coverage-best-practices.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.googblogs.com/code-coverage-best-practices/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: WebFetch's markdown conversion dropped the post body, so I curled the page and extracted the post-body div directly; quotes above are byte-exact from that HTML. Cross-checked against the googblogs.com mirror, which reproduces the same sentences. This is the single most useful citation for the 'we are not picking 80% because someone said so' conversation.

**A4.2** `[yes]` Google explicitly separates project-wide goals from per-change goals and says the per-change bar should be much higher than the project bar: "While project wide goals above 90% are most likely not worth it, per-commit coverage goals of 99% are reasonable, and 90% is a good lower threshold." It also names mutation testing, not more coverage, as the way to tell whether covered lines are actually tested: "A better technique to assess whether you're adequately exercising the lines your tests cover, and adequately asserting on failures, is mutation testing." And on the asymmetry of the metric: "a low code coverage number does guarantee that large areas of the product are going completely untested by automation on every single deployment."

- Threshold: `per-commit/diff: 90% lower threshold, 99% reasonable; project-wide: >90% not worth it`
- Source: Google Testing Blog -- 'Code Coverage Best Practices' -- https://testing.googleblog.com/2020/08/code-coverage-best-practices.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.googblogs.com/code-coverage-best-practices/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the clearest primary-source statement of the diff-coverage-over-absolute-coverage model, predating Sonar's marketing of it. Note the direction of the asymmetry: coverage is a valid lower bound (low number proves untested code exists) but not an upper bound (high number proves nothing).

**A4.3** `[yes]` The largest empirical study of the coverage/effectiveness relationship concludes coverage should not be used as a quality target. Abstract, verbatim: "We found that there is a low to moderate correlation between coverage and effectiveness when the number of test cases in the suite is controlled for. In addition, we found that stronger forms of coverage do not provide greater insight into the effectiveness of the suite. Our results suggest that coverage, while useful for identifying under-tested parts of a program, should not be used as a quality target because it is not a good indicator of test suite effectiveness." Study scale: 31,000 generated test suites across five Java systems up to 724,000 lines of source, evaluated by mutation testing.

- Threshold: `n/a -- the finding is that no threshold is defensible as a quality target`
- Source: Inozemtseva & Holmes, 'Coverage Is Not Strongly Correlated with Test Suite Effectiveness', ICSE 2014 (ACM Distinguished Paper) -- https://neverworkintheory.org/2021/09/24/coverage-is-not-strongly-correlated-with-test-suite-effectiveness.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~rtholmes/papers/icse_2014_inozemtseva.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: I downloaded the UBC-hosted author PDF (2.3 MB) but could not extract its text in this environment (no pdftotext, pypdf broken by a native-binding failure). The abstract quoted above is reproduced verbatim on neverworkintheory.org, a research-summary site run by Greg Wilson; ACM DL returned 403. Confidence 'verified' applies to the abstract text and the study's scale, which are consistent across both the summary and the search-result snippets from ACM and Semantic Scholar. Cite the DOI 10.1145/2568225.2568271 in any internal document.

**A4.4** `[yes]` Martin Fowler's position, still the most-cited practitioner statement: coverage is a tool for finding untested code, not a management metric. Specific bands he gives: below 50% is 'a sign of trouble'; 'upper 80s or 90s' is what thoughtful testing tends to produce as a by-product; 100% is 'suspicious', suggesting tests written to satisfy the metric. He explicitly warns against mandates of the form 'you can't go into production with less than 87% coverage', quoting Brian Marick: "I expect a high level of coverage. Sometimes managers require one. There's a subtle difference."

- Threshold: `<50% = trouble; upper 80s-90s = expected by-product; 100% = suspicious`
- Source: martinfowler.com -- TestCoverage (bliki, 17 April 2012) -- https://martinfowler.com/bliki/TestCoverage.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Fourteen years old and still the reference point. It is a practitioner opinion, not a standard -- but it is the piece most reviewers will have read, and it is where the 'high coverage is an effect of good testing, not a cause' framing comes from. Fowler and Google agree on the shape and disagree slightly on the top of the band (see disagreements).

**A4.5** `[yes]` SonarQube's default, read-only, built-in quality gate ('Sonar way') applies four conditions to NEW code only: no new issues are introduced; all new Security Hotspots are reviewed; new code test coverage is greater than or equal to 80.0%; and duplication in the new code is less than or equal to 3.0%.

- Threshold: `Coverage on New Code >= 80.0%; Duplicated Lines on New Code <= 3.0%`
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Understanding quality gates (SonarQube Server) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates
- Cross-reference: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-cloud/standards/ai-code-assurance/quality-gate-for-agentic-ai
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the origin of the '80% is the industry standard' folklore -- but note the denominator: it is 80% of NEW code, not 80% of the repository. Quoting it as an absolute-coverage target misrepresents the source. The doc page is unversioned ('latest').

**A4.6** `[partially]` Sonar's published rationale for the new-code model ('Clean as You Code') is cost avoidance on legacy, not a claim that 80% is intrinsically correct: the gate "focuses on keeping high quality standards for new code, rather than spending a lot of effort remediating old code," and "Analyzing and cleaning new code allows you to fix issues in your old code and gradually improve the overall quality of your codebase." The mechanism is that files you touch become 'new code', so the legacy codebase is remediated incrementally by ordinary feature work rather than by a coverage sprint.

- Threshold: `n/a -- model, not number`
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Quality standards and new code / Understanding quality gates -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-cloud/standards/about-new-code.md
- Cross-reference: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: For a two-person team inheriting an ABP Commercial codebase with low existing coverage, this is the whole argument: a new-code gate costs you nothing on day one and cannot generate a backlog you can't clear. An absolute gate at any meaningful number is unachievable on day one and therefore gets bypassed, which is precisely the failure mode the brief warns about.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Only the SECOND quote is on this page (and it begins 'As a consequence, analyzing and cleaning...'). The first quote -- 'focuses on keeping high quality standards for new code, rather than spending a lot of effort remediating old code' -- does NOT appear anywhere on this page. The phrase 'Clean as You Code' also does not appear on this page; the page is titled 'Quality standards and new code'. Drop the first quote or find its real source. Also correct the stated mechanism: the page defines new code as 'code that you've recently added or modified' and the remediation effect comes from touching old code incidentally while adding new -- not from whole files flipping to 'new code' status.
- Verifier read from the page: "When you add new code to your projects, you usually touch a portion of the old code in the process. As a consequence, analyzing and cleaning new code allows you to fix issues in your old code and gradually improve the overall quality of your codebase."

**A4.7** `[partially]` Sonar shipped a distinct built-in gate for AI-assisted development in 2026, 'Sonar way for agentic AI', with six conditions on new code -- no new bugs at severity >= Minor, no new vulnerabilities at severity >= Minor, no new code smells at severity >= Major, no dependency risks at severity >= Low, "Coverage on new code is greater or equal to 80%", and "Duplication on new code is less or equal to 3%". It replaces the legacy 'Sonar way for AI code' gate. The coverage bar is unchanged at 80% of new code.

- Threshold: `Coverage on new code >= 80%; duplication on new code <= 3%`

Note the vendor did NOT raise the coverage bar for AI-written code -- it added issue-severity and dependency conditions instead. The Dependency Risks condition requires an Advanced Security subscription on the Enterprise plan, so the gate is not fully usable below that tier.

Also note the Dependency Risks condition requires an Enterprise plan and is skipped (grayed out) otherwise, so on non-Enterprise plans the gate effectively enforces five conditions.

**A4.8** `[partially]` Codecov distinguishes two status checks with different defaults. `patch` "only measures lines adjusted in the pull request or single commit, if the commit is not in a pull request" and defaults to `target: auto`, `threshold: 0%`, `base: auto`, `if_ci_failed: error`. `project` measures whole-repository coverage against the base commit with an example `threshold: 5`. Either can be made non-blocking with `informational: true`, which makes "the resulting status will pass no matter what the coverage is or what other settings are specified."

- Threshold: `patch: target auto, threshold 0%; project: target auto, threshold 5`
- Source: Codecov Documentation -- Commit status (project vs patch) -- https://docs.codecov.com/docs/commit-status
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: `informational: true` is the underrated setting: it lets you run the full gate configuration in shadow mode for a few sprints and collect real data on what your PRs actually achieve before you make anything blocking. That is the correct way for a two-person team to pick its number rather than copying 80% off a blog.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The patch/project descriptions and the informational quote are verbatim correct. But: (1) `base` is documented on this very page as '[Deprecated as of July 2020]' -- remove it from a 2026 standard; (2) the values `threshold: 0%` (patch) and `threshold: 5` (project) appear only inside illustrative YAML blocks, not as documented defaults. Only `target: auto` ('This is the default') and `if_ci_failed` ('the default is error') are stated as defaults on the page. Restate as: 'example configuration shows patch threshold 0% and project threshold 5; documented defaults are target: auto and if_ci_failed: error.'
- Verifier read from the page: "[Deprecated as of July 2020 -- Codecov's product roadmap includes a novel way to manually pick base of pull requests]"

(2) The ~80k figure is not comparable to the 50k cap -- Sonar excludes test code, comments and blank lines, so a raw line count materially overstates Sonar LOC; recompute with those exclusions before concluding the cap is exceeded. **A4.10** `[partially]` Sonar requires different coverage report formats for the two halves of this stack, and will silently report 0% if you feed it the wrong one. For C#: `sonar.cs.opencover.reportsPaths` (OpenCover, incl. coverlet OpenCover output), `sonar.cs.cobertura.reportsPaths` (coverlet or dotnet-coverage Cobertura), `sonar.cs.vscoveragexml.reportsPaths` (Visual Studio / dotnet-coverage XML), `sonar.cs.dotcover.reportsPaths`; `sonar.cs.ncover3.reportsPaths` is deprecated. For TypeScript: LCOV only -- "SonarQube Cloud directly supports all coverage tools that produce reports in the LCOV format" via `sonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths`, and "The parameter `sonar.typescript.lcov.reportPaths` was formerly used for TypeScript coverage. This parameter has been deprecated." Generated code is removed from the denominator with `sonar.coverage.exclusions`, which "excludes specific files from code coverage calculations" while still analysing them for quality issues.

The monorepo warning on the JS page also applies: "file paths inside each report must be unique across the whole repository."

**A4.11** `[yes]` Microsoft Learn documents coverlet plus ReportGenerator as the supported cross-platform coverage path for .NET. The canonical command is `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage"`, which emits `coverage.cobertura.xml` into the `TestResults` directory: "This is a cross-platform option that relies on the .NET CLI, and it is great for build systems where MSBuild isn't available." Microsoft also documents its own built-in collector (`--collect:"Code Coverage"`), which "generates a binary .coverage file" that "isn't human-readable, and it must be converted to a human-readable format before it can be used to generate reports outside of Visual Studio", with `dotnet-coverage` as the converter. Report generation is documented via `dotnet tool install -g dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool`.

It is the citation to use if anyone asks 'is coverlet a real Microsoft-sanctioned choice' -- Microsoft's own docs teach it in preference to their binary collector for CI.

**A4.12** `[no]` coverlet can emit every format this project needs from a single test run and can fail the build on a threshold without any external service. Formats: "The supported formats are lcov, opencover, cobertura, teamcity, json (default coverlet proprietary format)", combinable -- `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage;Format=json,lcov,cobertura"`. Threshold enforcement: `dotnet test /p:CollectCoverage=true /p:Threshold=80` "will automatically fail the build if the line, branch or method coverage of any of the instrumented modules falls below 80%", narrowable with `/p:ThresholdType=line`. Exclusions: `ExcludeFromCoverageAttribute` and `ExcludeFromCodeCoverageAttribute` "are always excluded" by default; additional attributes via `ExcludeByAttribute` (e.g. `GeneratedCodeAttribute,CompilerGeneratedAttribute`), files via `ExcludeByFile` globs, and `SkipAutoProps` to drop auto-implemented properties.

- Threshold: `/p:Threshold=80 with /p:ThresholdType=line|branch|method; Format=lcov,opencover,cobertura,teamcity,json`
- Source: coverlet -- VSTestIntegration.md and MSBuildIntegration.md (master) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/coverlet-coverage/coverlet/master/Documentation/VSTestIntegration.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/coverlet-coverage/coverlet/master/Documentation/MSBuildIntegration.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: `SkipAutoProps=true` matters disproportionately on an ABP codebase: DTOs and entities are mostly auto-properties, and leaving them in the denominator makes the absolute number a measure of how many DTOs you have rather than how well you test. Note the Threshold check is per-module, not per-solution, which makes it awkward as a repo-wide gate but excellent as a floor on one or two critical assemblies.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** MATERIAL ERROR -- split the claim in two. VSTestIntegration.md (cited URL) supports ONLY the format half: 'The supported formats are lcov, opencover, cobertura, teamcity, json (default coverlet proprietary format)', `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage;Format=json,lcov,cobertura"`, and the exclusion options (ExcludeByAttribute -- 'Default is no additional attributes, but ExcludeFromCoverageAttribute and ExcludeFromCodeCoverageAttribute are always excluded' -- ExcludeByFile globs, SkipAutoProps). The SAME page states twice that this mode does NOT do threshold validation. The threshold text ('will automatically fail the build if the line, branch or method coverage of any of the instrumented modules falls below 80%', /p:ThresholdType=line|branch|method, /p:ThresholdStat=Minimum|Total|Average) lives in a different document: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/coverlet-coverage/coverlet/master/Documentation/MSBuildIntegration.md. Corrected claim: coverlet's VSTest/collector mode emits multiple formats but enforces no threshold; coverlet's MSBuild mode (coverlet.msbuild, /p:CollectCoverage=true) enforces thresholds. You cannot get both from one --collect run -- enforce the threshold in a separate step (e.g. ReportGenerator or a CI gate) if you stay on the collector.
- Verifier read from the page: ":warning:At the moment VSTest integration **doesn't support all features** of msbuild and .NET tool, for instance show result on console, report merging and threshold validation. ... Feature parity with other modes is partial (for example, threshold validation and built-in merge are not provided by this integration mode)."

**A4.13** `[partially]` On .NET 10 / Microsoft Testing Platform the coverage story changes and one common setup breaks. Microsoft's own MTP coverage extension (`Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage`) supports only `coverage`, `xml`, and `cobertura` for `--coverage-output-format` -- it cannot emit LCOV. Version compatibility is pinned: CodeCoverage 18.1.x  MTP 2.0.x, 18.0.x  MTP 1.8.x, 17.14.x  MTP 1.6.2. Its `IncludeTestAssembly` default flipped to `false` "while it used to be `true` in VSTest". To get LCOV under MTP you need `coverlet.MTP`, a native MTP extension run as `dotnet test --coverlet` with `--coverlet-output-format` supporting `json`, `lcov`, `opencover`, `cobertura`, `teamcity`. `coverlet.msbuild` relies on VSTest MSBuild targets that MTP does not invoke, so it collects nothing under MTP.

- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Microsoft.Testing.Platform (MTP) code coverage, ms.date 2026-02-25, updated 2026-04-27 -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/testing/microsoft-testing-platform-code-coverage
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/coverlet-coverage/coverlet/master/Documentation/VSTestIntegration.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

The coverlet.msbuild incompatibility is stated in the coverlet/MS docs and in the MTP migration guidance; I did not reproduce it, so treat that specific sub-claim as documented rather than tested.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Everything verified EXCEPT the final sentence. The page says nothing whatsoever about coverlet.msbuild under MTP, VSTest MSBuild targets, or MTP failing to invoke them. Drop 'coverlet.msbuild relies on VSTest MSBuild targets that MTP does not invoke, so it collects nothing under MTP' or cite a source that actually states it. Add the page's own note that the coverage extensions are auto-registered via Microsoft.Testing.Platform.MSBuild, and that coverlet.MTP writes coverage.json to the current directory by default.

**A4.14** `[yes]` `dotnet-coverage` is the documented cross-platform way to merge many per-project coverage files into one report, which a multi-project ABP solution needs. Install: `dotnet tool install --global dotnet-coverage`. Merge with globbing: `dotnet-coverage merge -o merged.cobertura.xml -f cobertura **\*.coverage`. Supported merge/collect output formats are `coverage`, `xml`, `cobertura` (default `coverage`, the Visual Studio binary format). Applies to ".NET 8 SDK and later versions". Note: "Starting with version 18.10.0, the `dotnet-coverage` tool collects telemetry data" recording which commands and options you run, with an opt-out.

- Threshold: `dotnet-coverage merge -o merged.cobertura.xml -f cobertura; formats coverage|xml|cobertura; telemetry from 18.10.0`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- dotnet-coverage code coverage tool, ms.date 2026-08-05, updated 2026-08-13 -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/additional-tools/dotnet-coverage
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/coverlet-coverage/coverlet/master/Documentation/VSTestIntegration.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: coverlet's own docs recommend the same merge command. The telemetry note is three weeks old and worth a five-minute read for a team that will eventually have to answer 'what does your build tooling send outbound' in a security questionnaire -- it records commands and options, not source, and can be disabled.
