**A4.18** `[yes]` FDA is the one US regulator with substantive published guidance on structural coverage, and it prescribes a risk-based approach with no percentage. Verbatim from 4.8: "Validation coverage should be based on the software's complexity and safety risk - not on firm size or resource constraints." On structural testing: "The amount of structural coverage should be commensurate with the level of risk posed by the software." It also defines the vocabulary in a way that differs from industry usage: "Use of the term 'coverage' usually means 100% coverage. For example, if a testing program has achieved 'statement coverage,' it means that 100% of the statements in the software have been executed at least once." And it ranks the criteria: decision/branch coverage "is considered to be a minimum level of coverage for most software products, but decision coverage alone is insufficient for high-integrity applications." This guidance governs medical device software; a workers' compensation examination-scheduling portal is not a device software function, so it does not apply here.

- Threshold: `no percentage -- risk-commensurate; branch/decision coverage named as the minimum criterion for most products`
- Source: FDA -- General Principles of Software Validation; Final Guidance for Industry and FDA Staff (January 2002, v2.0), 4.8 and 5.2.5 -- https://www.fda.gov/media/73141/download
- Cross-reference: https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/general-principles-software-validation
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Quotes extracted by downloading the FDA PDF and decompressing its content streams (154,711 chars recovered; 'software validation' appears 116 times, confirming a good extraction). I include this because teams in healthcare reflexively reach for FDA guidance and then either over-apply it or mis-cite it. The correct use here is rhetorical: the strictest healthcare software regulator in the country declines to name a number and ties the bar to risk -- which is exactly the policy you should write.

**A4.19** `[yes]` HITRUST CSF is the one framework commonly cited in US healthcare vendor questionnaires whose control text I could not inspect, so I cannot confirm or deny that it names a coverage figure.

- Threshold: `unknown`
- Source: HITRUST Alliance -- HITRUST CSF (control text not publicly available) -- https://hitrustalliance.net/hitrust-framework
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: unverified
- Notes: What I tried: targeted search for HITRUST CSF control text naming a code-coverage percentage; the public materials I could reach (framework overview page, the publicly posted CSF v11.2.0 Introduction PDF, and third-party control summaries) discuss SA-11 'Developer Testing and Evaluation' and system acceptance testing but expose no coverage metric. The full CSF requirement statements are licensed and paywalled. Practical read: no third-party summary I found alleges a coverage percentage, and HITRUST derives heavily from NIST/ISO sources that contain none -- but treat this as unverified. If a customer contract references HITRUST, get the control text from them rather than guessing.

### Where sources disagree

- Fowler vs Google on the top of the range. Fowler expects "upper 80s or 90s" as the natural by-product of thoughtful testing and treats 100% as suspicious. Google says project-wide goals "above 90% are most likely not worth it" but that "per-commit coverage goals of 99% are reasonable". These are reconcilable once you notice they are talking about different denominators (whole-project vs per-change), but the headline numbers look contradictory and are frequently quoted against each other. If someone cites 'Fowler says 90%' at you, ask which denominator.
- Academic finding vs vendor default, and it is a real conflict. Inozemtseva & Holmes conclude coverage "should not be used as a quality target because it is not a good indicator of test suite effectiveness." Sonar ships an 80% coverage target as the default condition of its default quality gate, and has done so for years. Sonar's implicit defence is that the target applies to new code only and is paired with issue, hotspot and duplication conditions -- a scope the 2014 study did not examine. I do not think either side is wrong, but nobody should present '80% on new code' as evidence-backed; it is a vendor default that has become a convention, and the strongest empirical evidence available argues against coverage targets in general. Say that out loud in your policy doc rather than pretending the research endorses the number.
- Google's 60% 'acceptable' vs the near-universal 80% convention. This is a 20-point spread between the largest published corporate guidance and the de facto default, and it is almost entirely a denominator artefact -- Google's 60/75/90 is whole-project, the 80% convention is Sonar's new-code condition. But note that even on new code Google would put the bar HIGHER than Sonar (90% lower threshold, 99% reasonable). So depending on which source you cite and which denominator you mean, 'the standard' is anywhere from 60% to 99%. There is no reconciling this into a single number, which is itself the answer to the question the brief asked.
- Whether Cobertura is a valid Sonar input for C#. Current Sonar documentation lists `sonar.cs.cobertura.reportsPaths` as supported and shows a coverlet `-f=cobertura` example. A large body of community advice and older documentation states that OpenCover is required and that Cobertura will not be read. Both were true at different times. The failure mode is silent (0% coverage, green build), so verify empirically on your first run by checking that Sonar reports a non-zero figure for a file you know is tested -- do not trust either source blind.
- Whether coverage thresholds belong in the test runner or in CI. coverlet (`/p:Threshold`) and karma-coverage (`check.global`) both enforce absolute thresholds by failing the test command itself; Sonar, Codecov and diff-cover enforce at the CI/PR layer against a diff. Both approaches are documented as normal by their respective first-party sources. They pull in opposite directions: runner-level thresholds are absolute and fail locally (good feedback, wrong metric), CI-level gates are diff-based and only fail in the pipeline (right metric, later feedback). My recommendation splits them deliberately -- runner-level thresholds ONLY as a high floor on the two or three tenant-isolation and authorization assemblies, diff-based gating for everything else -- but be aware that is a judgement call reconciling two legitimate conventions, not a settled standard.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The obvious plan -- 'enable SonarCloud free, turn on Clean as You Code' -- fails on day one at your size, and nothing in the documentation says so up front. SonarQube Cloud's Free plan caps PRIVATE projects at 50k LoC with a 5-member limit and only main-branch analysis; you are at roughly 80k LoC across C# and TypeScript. Self-hosting does not rescue it, because SonarQube Server Community Build has no branch or pull request analysis at all -- that starts at Developer Edition, per Sonar's own edition comparison. Every '80% coverage on new code is the 2026 standard' article assumes a tier this project does not qualify for.
- The '80% industry standard' is two different numbers wearing the same label. Sonar's 80.0% is coverage of NEW CODE ONLY. Google's 60/75/90 is WHOLE-PROJECT. Angular's 80 is a whole-project karma-coverage `check.global` example. Anyone who quotes '80%' without naming the denominator is silently conflating a demanding per-PR bar with a moderate repo-wide bar. When you write your policy, state the denominator in the first sentence -- that one clause is the difference between a rule you can keep and one you will bypass.
- Sonar cannot read LCOV for C# and cannot read Cobertura for TypeScript. C# needs `sonar.cs.opencover.reportsPaths`, `sonar.cs.cobertura.reportsPaths` or `sonar.cs.vscoveragexml.reportsPaths`; TypeScript accepts LCOV only, via `sonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths` (the old `sonar.typescript.lcov.reportPaths` is deprecated). Worse, plenty of 2020-2022 advice still insists OpenCover is the ONLY valid C# input; Cobertura is now documented as supported. Configure the wrong parameter and you do not get an error -- you get 0% coverage and a green build, which is the most dangerous possible outcome for a gate. Emit both formats in one pass with `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage;Format=cobertura,opencover"`.
- Microsoft's own next-generation coverage collector cannot emit LCOV. Under Microsoft Testing Platform, `Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage` supports only `coverage`, `xml` and `cobertura`. And `coverlet.msbuild` does not work under MTP at all, because MTP does not invoke the VSTest MSBuild targets it depends on -- it collects nothing, quietly. There is also a hard version-pinning table (CodeCoverage 18.1.x  MTP 2.0.x, 18.0.x  MTP 1.8.x) and `IncludeTestAssembly` flipped its default from true to false versus VSTest, which will move your number when you migrate. ABP 10 is on VSTest today so nothing is broken now, but this is precisely how a coverage gate silently zeroes itself during an SDK bump.
- The most widely recommended GitHub Action for .NET coverage PR comments, irongut/CodeCoverageSummary, has had no commit since 20 October 2022 -- nearly four years. For a private repo about to hold PHI, an unmaintained third-party action holding a token with write access to your pull requests is a supply-chain finding, not a staleness annoyance. The maintained alternative is better anyway and needs no marketplace action at all: ReportGenerator's `MarkdownSummaryGithub` report type plus `gh pr comment --edit-last --create-if-none --body-file`, which is the pattern in the ReportGenerator author's own action README. (For contrast: danielpalme/ReportGenerator-GitHub-Action last committed 2026-07-27, davelosert/vitest-coverage-report-action 2026-08-25, codecov/codecov-action 2026-06-06.)
- Your Angular test runner is formally dead and the coverage flags are being renamed under you. Karma's own README says "Karma is deprecated and is not accepting new features or general bug fixes"; karma-coverage's last release was 2023-06-23. Angular 20 still defaults to Karma, but Angular's current docs have moved to Vitest and renamed everything: `--code-coverage` becomes `--coverage`, `codeCoverageExclude` becomes `--coverage-exclude`, `--coverage-reporters` appears with lcov/cobertura/json-summary values, and karma-coverage's `check.global` block becomes `coverageThresholds`. Any coverage scripting you write against Karma today gets rewritten at the v21/v22 upgrade -- so make the output file path and format the CI contract, and keep the `ng test` invocation to one substitutable line.
- ABP will make your absolute number meaningless in both directions if you do not tune the denominator. Generated Angular service proxies land in `src/app/proxy/**`; EF Core migrations, ABP-generated DTOs and the entity layer are almost entirely auto-properties. coverlet excludes `[ExcludeFromCodeCoverage]` and `ExcludeFromCoverage` by default and NOTHING else, so all of that generated bulk sits in your denominator. `SkipAutoProps=true`, `ExcludeByAttribute=GeneratedCodeAttribute,CompilerGeneratedAttribute`, `ExcludeByFile` globs for migrations, and `sonar.coverage.exclusions` for the proxies are prerequisites for the number meaning anything -- configure exclusions BEFORE you pick a threshold, or you will pick a number calibrated to noise.
- FDA's definition of 'coverage' is not the industry's. Verbatim: "Use of the term 'coverage' usually means 100% coverage. For example, if a testing program has achieved 'statement coverage,' it means that 100% of the statements in the software have been executed at least once." So in a regulated-language document, 'we have statement coverage' asserts something far stronger than 'our statement coverage is 78%'. If you ever produce a validation or assurance document for a customer, that wording distinction is a real liability -- say 'statement coverage of 78%', never 'we have statement coverage'.
- The number you write down becomes the number you are audited against, and no regulator supplies one. The HIPAA Security Rule text contains the word 'coverage' zero times; ASVS 5.0.0 has no coverage requirement; SSDF PW.8 has none. So the auditable artifact is not compliance with an external threshold -- it is your own written policy plus evidence you actually follow it. That inverts the usual instinct: picking a high number to look rigorous creates an obligation you will visibly fail, while picking a modest number you genuinely hold every single PR is the stronger evidentiary position. Write down the number you can keep.
- Diff coverage has a blind spot nobody mentions: it says nothing about code you did not touch. A PR at 100% patch coverage can still ship on top of a completely untested Host-header tenant-resolution path, because that path is not in the diff. This is the structural reason the tenant-isolation suite has to be its own separate, deliberately-written check rather than something you expect a percentage gate to produce as a side effect. Relatedly, a PR that only deletes code or only touches non-instrumented files has no new lines to measure and passes trivially -- correct behaviour, but do not mistake a green diff-coverage check for evidence that anything was tested.
- `dotnet-coverage` started collecting telemetry in version 18.10.0 (published 2026-08-12), recording which commands you run and which options you set, with an opt-out documented by Microsoft. Harmless in itself -- it does not transmit source -- but it is the kind of question that lands in a customer security questionnaire ('what build tooling transmits data outbound?'), and knowing the answer before you are asked is worth the five minutes. `coverlet` has no equivalent.
- There is a genuinely free tool that does the whole 2026 diff-coverage standard across both your stacks, and almost nobody recommends it because it looks like a Python-ecosystem tool. `diff-cover` (Apache-2.0) reads Cobertura, Clover, JaCoCo XML AND LCOV, takes `--compare-branch=origin/main` and `--fail-under=80`, and emits `--format markdown:report.md` for a PR comment. Version 10.5.1 shipped 2026-08-16, with five releases in the preceding five weeks. One tool, both languages, no account, no per-seat cost, and no source code or coverage data leaving GitHub -- which for a PHI-adjacent private repo is a security property, not just a convenience.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- A repository-wide absolute coverage gate at 80% (or any meaningful number). At a larger company this is a standard merge requirement backed by a platform team who spent a quarter getting the codebase there first. Here it is unachievable on day one on an inherited ABP Commercial codebase, so it would be set to whatever you happen to have today (say 34%), which gates nothing, or set aspirationally, which blocks every PR until someone adds `continue-on-error` and everyone stops looking. Inozemtseva & Holmes give you the citation to decline it; Sonar's own Clean-as-You-Code rationale gives you the alternative.
- Buying SonarQube Cloud Team or SonarQube Server Developer Edition purely for the coverage gate. The Clean-as-You-Code new-code model is genuinely the right model and Sonar's tooling for it is excellent, but this codebase (~80k LoC) exceeds the free plan's 50k-LoC private-project cap and Community Build has no PR analysis at all, so adopting it means a paid tier. `diff-cover` implements the same gating idea for free, on both stacks, with no data leaving GitHub. If you later buy Sonar it should be for the taint analysis and SAST on a public-internet PHI app, not for the coverage number -- evaluate that as a security decision in the SAST workstream, not this one.
- Codecov's `project` status as a required gate (as opposed to `patch`). Larger orgs run both and treat a project-coverage drop as a blocking regression. With two devs and a codebase whose baseline is about to move a lot, project status with `target: auto` will fail PRs for reasons unrelated to the PR -- a refactor that deletes well-covered code lowers the ratio and blocks the merge. If you adopt Codecov at all, run `patch` only and set `project` to `informational: true`.
- Mutation testing as a pull-request gate. Standard at companies with dedicated test-infrastructure engineers and a build farm. Here the runtime alone (a full suite re-run per mutant) makes it a non-starter in the PR path, and the output needs human triage rather than a pass/fail. Scheduled, scoped to two or three assemblies, is the version that survives contact with a two-person team.
- 100% branch coverage, MC/DC, or path coverage. These are real standards -- MC/DC is mandated by DO-178C for Level A avionics, and FDA's guidance catalogues condition, multi-condition, loop and path coverage as criteria. None of them apply to a scheduling portal, and FDA itself notes that "Because of the very large number of possible paths through a software program, path coverage is generally not achievable." Do not let a HIPAA conversation drift into aerospace-grade structural coverage; the regulator that comes closest to caring says the amount should be commensurate with risk.
- Per-file or per-directory coverage policies, CODEOWNERS-scoped coverage rules, and coverage-based review routing. These solve a coordination problem that appears at maybe fifteen engineers. With two people who both touch everything, the config is pure overhead and will drift out of date within a month.
- Merging unit, integration and E2E coverage into one aggregate figure. Google says the aggregate view across the pipeline "is paramount" and they are right in principle -- but instrumenting a running ASP.NET Core host for E2E coverage collection, merging it with unit coverage across two languages, and keeping the source-path mapping correct is multi-week work that breaks on every upgrade. Revisit when you have a third engineer.
- Standing up your own SonarQube Server instance to dodge the licensing. Community Build cannot do PR analysis, so you would get an absolute-coverage dashboard you already decided not to gate on, plus a server to patch and back up. That is infrastructure work, which is out of scope here anyway, in exchange for less capability than the free `diff-cover` path.
- A coverage badge in the README. Zero engineering value, non-trivial plumbing on a private repo, and it invites exactly the 'what's our number' conversation you want to replace with 'what's our patch coverage and what isn't tested'.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`coverage / backend-patch`** -- tool: coverlet.collector 10.0.1 + dotnet-coverage 18.10.0 + diff-cover 10.5.1

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-6 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Runs the .NET test suite with coverage, merges the per-project Cobertura files, then computes coverage of ONLY the lines this PR added or changed and fails if that number falls below a threshold. Concretely: `dotnet test --collect:"XPlat Code Coverage;Format=cobertura,opencover"` -> `dotnet-coverage merge -o merged.cobertura.xml -f cobertura **/coverage.cobertura.xml` -> `diff-cover merged.cobertura.xml --compare-branch=origin/main --fail-under=<N> --format markdown:patch-backend.md`. Requires `fetch-depth: 0` on actions/checkout so the merge-base exists.
- Why at this team size: This is the whole 2026 standard in one check, and it costs nothing per month. Start it ADVISORY and set no threshold for the first four to six weeks -- just print the number on every PR. You will then have real data on what your PRs actually achieve, and you can set the threshold at roughly your observed median rather than importing Sonar's 80% or Google's 90%. Flip it to required-gate only after two consecutive weeks where it would have passed unmodified. A gate you cannot pass on the day you enable it is a gate you will start bypassing, which is the exact failure the brief warns about. Before you set any number, add coverage exclusions for generated code: ABP's Angular proxies live under `src/app/proxy/**`, and on the C# side EF Core migrations and ABP-generated DTOs should be excluded via `ExcludeByFile` globs plus `SkipAutoProps=true` -- otherwise you are measuring how many DTOs you have.
- Tool maintenance evidence: coverlet.collector 10.0.1 published 2026-05-18 (NuGet flat-container + registration API: https://api.nuget.org/v3-flatcontainer/coverlet.collector/index.json); dotnet-coverage 18.10.0 published 2026-08-12 (https://api.nuget.org/v3-flatcontainer/dotnet-coverage/index.json), documented on Microsoft Learn with ms.date 2026-08-05; diff-cover 10.5.1 published 2026-08-16, Apache-2.0, requires Python >=3.10 (https://pypi.org/pypi/diff-cover/json) with five releases in the last five weeks.

**`coverage / frontend-patch`** -- tool: Angular CLI 20.3.24 karma-coverage + diff-cover 10.5.1

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 3-4 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Same mechanism for Angular: `ng test --no-watch --code-coverage` produces `coverage/lcov.info`, then `diff-cover coverage/lcov.info --compare-branch=origin/main --fail-under=<N> --format markdown:patch-frontend.md`. diff-cover reads LCOV natively, so one tool covers both stacks.
- Why at this team size: Deliberately identical in shape to the backend check so there is one mental model, one tool, one threshold conversation -- not two. Write the workflow step so the coverage FILE PATH and FORMAT are the contract and the `ng test` invocation is a single variable. When you move to the `@angular/build:unit-test` Vitest builder (experimental in v20, default in current Angular), the flags rename from `--code-coverage` to `--coverage` and you add `--coverage-reporters lcov` plus `@vitest/coverage-v8`; if the path/format is the contract, that upgrade is a one-line change instead of a pipeline rewrite. Expect the frontend number to be lower than the backend number and do not force them to match -- Angular component specs and C# application-service tests have genuinely different cost curves.
- Tool maintenance evidence: diff-cover 10.5.1 published 2026-08-16 (https://pypi.org/pypi/diff-cover/json). Caveat, stated plainly: karma-coverage 2.2.1 last published 2023-06-23 and karma 6.4.4 last published 2024-07-29, and karma's own README says "Karma is deprecated and is not accepting new features or general bug fixes" (https://registry.npmjs.org/karma). The LCOV file it emits is fine; the runner underneath it is on a dead path.

**`coverage / pr-report`** -- tool: ReportGenerator 5.5.11 (dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool) + gh CLI

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Renders the merged coverage into a GitHub-flavoured markdown summary and posts/updates a single sticky comment on the PR, plus uploads the full HTML report as a workflow artifact. `reportgenerator -reports:merged.cobertura.xml -targetdir:coveragereport -reporttypes:Html;MarkdownSummaryGithub;MarkdownDeltaSummary` then `gh pr comment $PR_NUMBER --edit-last --create-if-none --body-file coveragereport/SummaryGithub.md`.
- Why at this team size: Do NOT use a third-party PR-comment action for this. The `gh pr comment --edit-last --create-if-none` pattern is straight out of the ReportGenerator author's own action README, needs no marketplace action, no external service, and no additional token scope beyond the default GITHUB_TOKEN. That matters more than convenience here: the most widely recommended action for exactly this job, irongut/CodeCoverageSummary, has had no commit since 2022-10-20 (verified by shallow clone), and an unmaintained third-party action with write access to a PHI-adjacent private repo is a supply-chain exposure, not just a staleness annoyance. Visibility is the point of this check -- with two devs, a number on the PR does most of the behavioural work that a blocking gate would do, without the bypass pressure.
- Tool maintenance evidence: dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool 5.5.11 published 2026-07-27 (NuGet registration API); the author's own danielpalme/ReportGenerator-GitHub-Action last commit 2026-07-27 tagged 5.5.11 (verified by shallow clone). Report types confirmed from the upstream README (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/danielpalme/ReportGenerator/main/README.md): Html, Cobertura, MarkdownSummaryGithub, MarkdownDeltaSummary, TextSummary and others. Microsoft Learn documents ReportGenerator as the report step for the coverlet path.

**`tests / tenant-isolation`** -- tool: xUnit (ABP test host) + coverlet.msbuild 10.0.1 threshold, scoped to tenant-resolution and authorization projects only

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 12-20 (one-off) + ~1/month | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A small, explicitly named test suite (10-25 tests) asserting the failure mode that actually ends this company: that a request resolving to tenant A can never read, write or enumerate tenant B's data. Covers Host-header tenant resolution including missing/unknown/spoofed hosts, connection-string selection per tenant, repository queries executed under an impersonated tenant, and the anonymous-reachable endpoints (registration, login, password reset, document upload). Gated on the suite existing and passing -- plus a coverlet floor scoped to just those assemblies (`/p:Threshold=<high> /p:ThresholdType=line`), since coverlet's threshold is evaluated per module.
- Why at this team size: This is the only coverage-adjacent item I would block launch on, and note carefully what is being gated: the existence and passing of a named suite over your highest-consequence code path, with a high coverage floor on two or three assemblies -- NOT a repository-wide percentage. The brief states cross-tenant leakage is the highest-consequence failure mode and that tenancy resolves from the Host header alone; a global 80% number gives you no assurance whatsoever about that specific path, because the metric averages your riskiest code together with your CRUD. This is also the one place where the 'no data yet, destructive testing is safe' window is a genuine asset -- write the tests that deliberately try to cross the boundary while there is no real PHI to leak. If you only do one thing from this entire area before going public, do this one and skip the percentages.
- Tool maintenance evidence: coverlet.msbuild 10.0.1 published 2026-05-18 (https://api.nuget.org/v3-flatcontainer/coverlet.msbuild/index.json). Threshold semantics confirmed in coverlet's MSBuildIntegration.md: `/p:Threshold=80` "will automatically fail the build if the line, branch or method coverage of any of the instrumented modules falls below 80%", narrowable with `/p:ThresholdType=line`. Forward-compatibility note: coverlet.msbuild depends on VSTest MSBuild targets and does not function under Microsoft Testing Platform (per Microsoft Learn's MTP coverage page); if you ever migrate test projects to MTP, switch to coverlet.MTP 10.0.1 or use `Microsoft.Testing.Extensions.CodeCoverage`.

**`coverage / trend (scheduled)`** -- tool: ReportGenerator 5.5.11 with -historydir, on a GitHub Actions schedule trigger

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 2 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A nightly or weekly scheduled workflow on main that produces the absolute coverage number for both stacks, publishes the HTML report as an artifact and writes a one-line summary to the job summary. Never blocks anything. Optionally uses ReportGenerator's `MarkdownDeltaSummary` with a `-historydir` so you can see direction over time.
- Why at this team size: Absolute coverage is worth KNOWING and not worth GATING -- this is precisely Google's position ("a low code coverage number does guarantee that large areas of the product are going completely untested") combined with Inozemtseva & Holmes' finding that it is not a valid quality target. Running it on a schedule rather than per-PR also keeps it off your critical path: it never makes a PR slower and it never fails a merge, so there is nothing to bypass. The trend line is what you show a customer's security reviewer or a future auditor; the number itself is not a promise you have to keep.
- Tool maintenance evidence: dotnet-reportgenerator-globaltool 5.5.11 published 2026-07-27; history/delta report types documented in the upstream README. Runs on .NET 8/9/10.

**`mutation / critical-paths (scheduled)`** -- tool: dotnet-stryker 4.16.0 (Stryker.NET)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 6-8 (one-off) + ~2/quarter | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Weekly Stryker.NET run scoped to the tenant-resolution, authorization and file-upload projects only. Mutation testing modifies the code (flipping comparisons, removing statements) and reports which mutants your tests failed to kill -- i.e. which covered lines are covered but not actually asserted on. Advisory report only; triage findings into normal backlog.
- Why at this team size: Google names this directly as the answer to the question coverage cannot answer: "A better technique to assess whether you're adequately exercising the lines your tests cover, and adequately asserting on failures, is mutation testing." It is also the single strongest defence against the gaming your patch-coverage gate will otherwise incentivise -- a test that executes a line without asserting anything scores 100% patch coverage and kills zero mutants. The reason this is scoped and scheduled rather than a gate: mutation runs are slow (they re-run the suite once per mutant) and the report needs human triage. Point it at two or three assemblies, run it Sunday night, spend thirty minutes on Monday. Do not point it at the whole solution and do not put it in the PR path. If you are short on time this is the first check to defer -- it is the highest-value optional item, not a required one.
- Tool maintenance evidence: dotnet-stryker 4.16.0 published 2026-07-03, with 4.15.0 on 2026-06-23 and 4.14.2 on 2026-05-18 -- three releases in the last three months (https://api.nuget.org/v3-flatcontainer/dotnet-stryker/index.json). Sibling project @stryker-mutator/core 10.0.0 published 2026-08-14 (npm registry) confirms the wider project is active.



## 5. Static application security testing

**Verification verdict for this area: `material-errors`** (24 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- [7] C# CodeQL query counts are wrong. The cited page lists 55 built-in C# queries, 44 in `default`, 11 security-extended-only -- not 64/54/10. The claim's own list of extended-only queries also omits 'Value shadowing: server variable' (CWE-348), which is the 11th.
- [5] security-extended is NOT 'the default set plus precision medium'. In misc/suite-helpers/security-extended-selectors.yml the high/very-high-precision include block has NO `problem.severity` filter at all (so it also admits `recommendation`-severity security queries), and it omits kinds `alert`/`path-alert` that the default selectors include. The stated threshold 'both require problem.severity error|warning' is false for security-extended. The cited URL contains only the default selectors and documents nothing about security-extended.
- [4] Two of the three quoted strings do not appear on the cited page. Grepping the fetched page: 'block the merge' = 0 hits, 'pull request' = 0 hits, 'CI pipeline' = 0 hits. The Sonar way sentence is also misquoted -- the page says the gate 'focuses on keeping new code clean, rather than spending a lot of effort remediating old code', not 'keeping high quality standards for new code'. The page is additionally an archived 10.8 version banner-marked 'no longer maintained'.
- [20] CodeQL 2.24.0 shipped 2026-01-26, not 29 January 2026 (changelog title: 'CodeQL 2.24.0 (2026-01-26)'). It also states only 'Basic extractor support for .NET 10' and a narrow C# 14 item (implicit span conversions), not blanket 'support for .NET 10 and C# 14'. None of this appears on the cited supported-languages page.
- [15] Microsoft documents THREE suppression mechanisms on the cited page (Disable the rule via EditorConfig severity=none; #pragma warning / #Disable; SuppressMessageAttribute), not four.
- [24] The cited SonarSource blog never mentions rule S6268 -- no rule ID appears anywhere in it. The rule ID and its Security Hotspot classification are unsupported by this citation, and rules.sonarsource.com was unreachable for independent confirmation.

### Standard, in summary

The 2026 standard is three-layered, not one-tool. Layer one is in-compiler analysis that is already paid for: .NET SDK Roslyn analyzers are on by default for .NET 5+ targets, but in `Default` analysis mode "only a small number of rules are enabled as build warnings" and the taint rules that matter here (CA3001 SQL injection, CA3002 XSS) are explicitly "Enabled by default in .NET 10: No". Layer two is a semantic whole-program SAST -- CodeQL -- where the meaningful configuration decision is `default` vs `security-extended` query suite; those suites are defined by a precision filter (`default` = precision high/very-high, security-extended adds precision medium, both filtered to `problem.severity` error/warning and `tags contain security`), and for C# that means 54 queries vs 64. Layer three is SonarQube Cloud, whose built-in read-only "Sonar way" gate applies four conditions to *new code only* -- no new issues, 100% of new Security Hotspots reviewed, new-code coverage >= 80.0%, new-code duplication <= 3.0% -- with a documented "fudge factor" waiving coverage and duplication until 20 new lines exist. On gating, no authority requires a hard block: NIST SSDF PW.7.2 requires that analysis be performed and that all discovered issues be "record[ed] and triage[d] ... in the development team's workflow or issue tracking system," and OWASP's DevSecOps Verification Standard DSOVS-CODE-004 Level 2 accepts "fail *or flag*" a build. SonarSource states the gate "can be used to block the merge of the pull request," and GitHub's code scanning check fails only on severity `error`, `critical` or `high` by default. Suppressions are the weak point: Sonar "ignores accepted issues in the quality reports and ratings," so a green gate is silent about suppressed risk, and Sonar's own docs say issue-ignore multicriteria should be configured in the UI and that the CI/CD-properties route "is not documented in this guide."

### Claims and sources

**A5.1** `[yes]` The built-in "Sonar way" quality gate contains exactly four conditions, all scoped to new code: no new issues are introduced; all new Security Hotspots are reviewed; new code test coverage >= 80.0%; duplication in new code <= 3.0%. It is provided by Sonar, activated by default, and read-only (not editable).

- Threshold: `4 conditions; new-code coverage >= 80.0%; new-code duplicated lines <= 3.0%; new Security Hotspots reviewed = 100%`
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Understanding quality gates (SonarQube Server, current) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates
- Cross-reference: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-cloud/standards/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The two Sonar pages disagree on wording -- see disagreements. The Cloud page still describes the older rating-based framing (Reliability A / Security A / Maintainability A) alongside the coverage and duplication numbers; the current Server page states the consolidated "No new issues are introduced". The numeric thresholds (80.0% / 3.0%) are identical on both.

**A5.2** `[yes]` The Sonar way gate applies a "fudge factor": duplication conditions are waived until at least 20 new lines are added, and coverage conditions are waived until at least 20 new lines to cover exist. Small pull requests therefore pass the coverage and duplication conditions without meeting them.

- Threshold: `20 new lines (duplication); 20 new lines to cover (coverage)`
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Understanding quality gates (SonarQube Server, current) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: Confirmed on the current Server docs page. I did not find the same paragraph restated on the SonarQube Cloud page, so I have not cross-verified that Cloud applies the identical 20-line threshold.

**A5.3** `[partially]` SonarQube Cloud supports four new code definitions: Previous version, Number of days, Specific version, Specific date (plus reference-branch comparison for branches). "Previous version" means the start date is the date of the first analysis performed for the current project version, as determined by the `sonar.projectVersion` property supplied at analysis time. Only Previous version and Number of days can be set at organization level; Specific version and Specific date are project-level or Web API only.

- Threshold: `Number of days default 30, maximum 90; Previous version keys off `sonar.projectVersion``
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Quality standards and new code (SonarQube Cloud) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-cloud/standards/about-new-code
- Cross-reference: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/user-guide/about-new-code
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Practical consequence for this team: `previous_version` is a trap unless CI actually increments `sonar.projectVersion` on every release. If the version string never changes, the new-code window silently grows until it spans the whole project history and the gate stops meaning anything. For a team that does not cut versioned releases on a fixed cadence, reference-branch (compare to `main`) is the safer definition.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Every stated threshold is exact. The parenthetical '(plus reference-branch comparison for branches)' is NOT supported -- the word 'reference' does not appear on the page; it lists exactly four options. Drop that parenthetical or cite a different page for it.
- Verifier read from the page: "SonarQube Cloud supports the following options for new code definition: Previous version, Number of days, Specific version, and Specific date. ... the new code period's start date is the date of the first analysis performed for the current project version. The current project version is defined at analysis time through the `sonar.projectVersion` property. ... The default value is 30 days ... The maximum possible value is 90 days. ... Only the Previous version and Number of days options can be se"

SonarSource states the gate result "can be used to block the merge of the pull request if the quality gate fails" and "can be used to fail your CI pipeline" -- it is stated as a capability, not as a mandate.

**A5.5** `[no]` CodeQL's `default` and `security-extended` query suites are defined by metadata filters, not curated lists. `default` selects queries with `precision` in {high, very-high}, `problem.severity` in {error, warning}, and `tags contain security`. `security-extended` selects that same set PLUS queries with `precision` medium and `problem.severity` in {error, warning} tagged security. Both exclude deprecated and `experimental/` queries.

- Threshold: `default = precision high|very-high; security-extended adds precision medium; both require problem.severity error|warning and tag security`
- Source: github/codeql repository -- misc/suite-helpers/code-scanning-selectors.yml and security-extended-selectors.yml -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/github/codeql/main/misc/suite-helpers/code-scanning-selectors.yml
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/github/codeql/main/misc/suite-helpers/security-extended-selectors.yml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the actual mechanism, read from the source of truth in the CodeQL repo, and it makes the false-positive tradeoff quantifiable rather than vague: security-extended adds exactly the medium-precision tier. The language-level suite files (e.g. csharp/ql/src/codeql-suites/csharp-security-extended.qls) are two-line files that `apply` these shared selectors.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The `default` half is correct. Corrected security-extended description: it is a superset defined by TWO include blocks -- (a) kind problem|path-problem, precision high|very-high, tag security, with NO problem.severity constraint (so `recommendation`-severity security queries are admitted), and (b) kind problem|path-problem, precision medium, problem.severity error|warning, tag security. It also omits the `alert`/`path-alert` kinds that default includes. The threshold line 'both require problem.severity error|warning' is false. Additionally the cited URL is the default-suite file only; cite misc/suite-helpers/security-extended-selectors.yml for the security-extended half. (Both files do share the deprecated / ^experimental\/ / modeleditor / modelgenerator excludes -- that part holds.)
- Verifier read from the page: "code-scanning-selectors.yml: include: kind: [problem, path-problem, alert, path-alert]; precision: [high, very-high]; problem.severity: [error, warning]; tags contain: [security]. security-extended-selectors.yml first block: include: kind: [problem, path-problem]; precision: [high, very-high]; tags contain: [security]  (no problem.severity filter)"

**A5.7** `[no]` For C#, CodeQL ships 64 built-in queries across both suites: 54 in `default`, with 10 additional queries available only in `security-extended`. The security-extended-only queries include Missing function level access control (CWE-285/284/862), Insecure Direct Object Reference (CWE-639), Use of file upload (CWE-434), Missing XML validation (CWE-112), Insecure SQL connection (CWE-327), Empty password in configuration file (CWE-258/862), Serialization check bypass (CWE-020), Value shadowing (CWE-348), and two thread-unsafe ICryptoTransform queries (CWE-362).

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

The three queries that map directly to the new risk surface -- missing authorization on an endpoint, IDOR, and file upload -- are ALL excluded from the default suite. Default setup uses `default`. Enabling CodeQL and leaving it on the default suite would miss exactly the class of bug that going public with anonymous registration, self-service roles and document upload creates.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Corrected: 55 built-in C# queries total, 44 in `default`, all 55 in `security-extended`, therefore 11 security-extended-only. Note the page says it 'lists the queries available with the latest release', so any count is version-dependent and should be dated.

**A5.10** `[partially]` GitHub code scanning merge protection via rulesets is explicitly NOT the same mechanism as a required status check: "Merge protection with rulesets is not related to status checks." A ruleset blocks a merge when a required tool finds an alert at or above a configured severity, when a required tool's analysis is still in progress, or when a required tool is not configured for the repository. Severity options are Alerts: None / Errors / Errors and Warnings / All; Security alerts: None / Critical / High or higher / Medium or higher / All. It does not apply to merge queue groups or to Dependabot pull requests analyzed by default setup.

**A5.11** `[yes]` NIST SP 800-218 (SSDF v1.1, February 2022) locates static analysis in practice PW.7, "Review and/or Analyze Human-Readable Code to Identify Vulnerabilities and Verify Compliance with Security Requirements". Task PW.7.2 requires: "Perform the code review and/or code analysis based on the organization's secure coding standards, and record and triage all discovered issues and recommended remediations in the development team's workflow or issue tracking system." Notional Implementation Example 4 is "Use a static analysis tool to automatically check code for vulnerabilities and compliance with the organization's secure coding standards with a human reviewing the issues reported by the tool and remediating them as necessary"; Example 6 is "Use automated tools to identify and remediate documented and verified unsafe software practices on a continuous basis as human-readable code is checked into the code repository."

- Threshold: `Practice PW.7; tasks PW.7.1 and PW.7.2; PW.7.2 examples 4 and 6. Document: SP 800-218 SSDF v1.1, Feb 2022`
- Source: NIST SP 800-218, Secure Software Development Framework (SSDF) Version 1.1 -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/218/final
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Quotes extracted from the actual PDF text, not from memory. SSDF v1.1 (Feb 2022) is still the current final version; no Rev 1 / v1.2 is listed on the CSRC page, only the related SP 800-218A. Note what PW.7.2 does NOT say: it never requires that analysis block a merge. It requires that findings be recorded and triaged. A team that files every finding as an issue and triages it is SSDF-conformant even with an advisory-only scan.

**A5.12** `[yes]` NIST SSDF PW.6.2 Example 2 defines the "clean build" concept: "Implement the 'clean build' concept, where all compiler warnings are treated as errors and eliminated except those determined to be false positives or irrelevant." This is the SSDF basis both for treating analyzer warnings as build errors and for permitting suppressions -- but only after a per-item determination.

- Threshold: `PW.6.2, Notional Implementation Example 2`
- Source: NIST SP 800-218, SSDF v1.1, practice PW.6 (Configure the Compilation, Interpreter, and Build Processes to Improve Executable Security) -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The clause "except those determined to be false positives or irrelevant" is the governing standard for a suppression list. It permits suppressions and simultaneously requires that each one carry a determination. A 19-entry `sonar.issue.ignore.multicriteria` block with no recorded rationale does not meet this bar; the same 19 entries each with a one-line justification and a date would.

**A5.13** `[yes]` OWASP's DevSecOps Verification Standard defines requirement DSOVS-CODE-004 (Static Application Security Testing) with four maturity levels. Level 2 is "SAST scanning is implemented into the software build pipeline" with automated scans on each build, where "the pipeline can be configured to fail or flag a build when issues of a given severity are detected." Level 3 adds that all identified vulnerabilities are recorded in a centralised issue tracking system and "periodically reviewed to evaluate the effectiveness of the SAST tool," enabling teams to suppress false positives and confirm the analyser is keeping pace with the languages and frameworks in use.

- Threshold: `DSOVS-CODE-004; Level 2 = SAST in build pipeline (fail OR flag); Level 3 = centralised tracking + periodic review`
- Source: OWASP DevSecOps Verification Standard -- CODE-004 Static Application Security Testing (SAST) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/www-project-devsecops-verification-standard/main/document/CODE-004-Static-Application-Security-Testing-SAST.md
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/www-project-devsecops-guideline/latest/02a-Static-Application-Security-Testing
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the closest thing to a citable OWASP position on SAST-in-CI. Two things matter for a two-person team: (a) OWASP treats "flag" as equally valid as "fail" at Level 2, so an advisory-first rollout is standard-conformant, not a shortcut; (b) Level 3's "periodically reviewed" is the OWASP hook for a suppression-review cadence. The OWASP DevSecOps Guideline page itself is thinner than its reputation -- it names SAST and lists tools (SonarQube, Semgrep, Checkmarx, Fortify) but gives no gating or false-positive guidance.

**A5.14** `[partially]` .NET Roslyn code quality analysis is enabled by default for projects targeting .NET 5 or later, but in the default analysis mode "only a small number of rules are enabled as build warnings." The security taint rules are not among them: CA3001 (Review code for SQL injection vulnerabilities) is documented as "Enabled by default in .NET 10: No". Analysis mode is raised with `<AnalysisMode>` (None | Default | Minimum | Recommended | All) or the compound `<AnalysisLevel>` (e.g. `latest-Recommended`). Under `-warnaserror`, CA warnings become errors unless `CodeAnalysisTreatWarningsAsErrors` is set to false.

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The .NET 10 default-enabled table on the overview page contains only Interoperability, Performance, Reliability and Usage rules -- zero Security-category rules. CA3001 also carries a hard architectural caveat: "This rule can't track data across assemblies. For example, if one assembly reads the HTTP request input and then passes it to another assembly that executes the SQL command, this rule won't produce a warning." In a layered ABP solution (HttpApi / Application / Domain / EntityFrameworkCore projects) that is precisely where the flow goes, so CA3xxx is worth enabling but must not be treated as sufficient taint coverage.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Everything except the CA3001 fact is verbatim on this page. CA3001 is not mentioned here -- cite https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/fundamentals/code-analysis/quality-rules/ca3001 , which I verified has the property row 'Enabled by default in .NET 10 | No'. The claim's substance is correct; only that one citation is misplaced. (Corroborating: CA3001 is absent from this page's .NET 10 enabled-by-default table.)

In the default analysis mode (`Default`), only a small number of rules are enabled as build warnings. ... The allowable values are: None | Default | Minimum | Recommended | All ... The default value for the `AnalysisLevel` property is `latest` ... If you use the `-warnaserror` flag ... you can set the `CodeAnalysisTreatWarningsAsErrors` MSBuild property to `false`."

**A5.15** `[partially]` Microsoft documents four suppression mechanisms for .NET analyzer warnings, of which only one carries a machine-readable rationale: `SuppressMessageAttribute` takes a `Justification` property and can be scoped in-source or in a per-project GlobalSuppressions.cs with `Scope` and `Target`. The alternatives -- `dotnet_diagnostic.<rule-ID>.severity = none` in EditorConfig, and `#pragma warning disable` -- carry no justification field and apply file- or project-wide.

- Threshold: `SuppressMessageAttribute(category, ruleId, Justification=..., Scope=..., Target=...); EditorConfig severity = none; #pragma warning disable/restore`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Suppress code analysis warnings -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/fundamentals/code-analysis/suppress-warnings
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/fundamentals/code-analysis/quality-rules/ca3001
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the practical answer to "how should suppressions be reviewed": prefer the mechanism that forces a written reason next to the code, so the suppression shows up in the PR diff of the file it protects rather than in a central properties file nobody reads. CA3001's own page adds the correct standard for a security suppression: "It's safe to suppress a warning from this rule if you know that the input is always validated against a known safe set of characters" -- i.e. suppression requires an affirmative safety argument, not just noise-fatigue.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Three mechanisms, not four: (1) EditorConfig/AnalyzerConfig severity = none, (2) #pragma warning disable/restore (C#) or #Disable Warning (VB), (3) SuppressMessageAttribute -- applied either in-source or in a project-level GlobalSuppressions.cs, which is one mechanism with two placements, not two mechanisms. The Justification/Scope/Target syntax and the 'only SuppressMessageAttribute carries a rationale' point are correct. Note also that #pragma is documented as line/region-scoped, not 'file- or project-wide' as the claim asserts -- only the EditorConfig route is file/project-wide.
- Verifier read from the page: "Page sections: 'Disable the rule' (dotnet_diagnostic.<rule-ID>.severity = none), 'Use a preprocessor directive' (#pragma warning disable/restore CA2200), 'Use the SuppressMessageAttribute' ([SuppressMessage("Usage", "CA2200:...", Justification = "Not production code.", Scope = "member", Target = "~M:...")])."

**A5.16** `[partially]` Sonar's own documentation discourages the configuration pattern this repository uses. On advanced exclusions: "It is strongly advised to configure these features in SonarQube UI. The configuration on the CI/CD host which is very tedious and implies using a set of properties is not documented in this guide." On scope narrowing generally: "Inclusions and exclusions should not be part of the initial analysis configuration" -- they are intended to resolve specific problems found during analysis. The `sonar.issue.ignore.multicriteria` property pairs a rule key pattern (`<repository>:<ruleKey>`, e.g. `java:S1195`, or a globbing pattern such as `java:*Naming*`) with a file path pattern.

- Threshold: `sonar.issue.ignore.multicriteria=<ids>; .<id>.ruleKey=<repo>:<S-number>; .<id>.resourceKey=<glob>`
- Source: Sonar Documentation -- Applying advanced exclusions; Analysis scope (SonarQube Server 10.7) -- https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/project-administration/adjusting-analysis/setting-analysis-scope/advanced-exclusions
- Cross-reference: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/10.7/project-administration/analysis-scope
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Worth being precise about the tradeoff rather than repeating Sonar's advice uncritically. Properties-file suppressions ARE version-controlled, diffable and reviewable in a PR, which UI suppressions are not -- that is a genuine governance advantage Sonar's docs ignore. The real risk is not the file; it is that `ruleKey` accepts globs and `resourceKey` accepts `**`, so a single entry can silently disable a rule across the entire codebase, and nothing in the gate reports how much is being suppressed.

The page explicitly says the property-based CI/CD configuration 'is not documented in this guide', so the multicriteria syntax cannot be sourced here. Issues filtered by `sonar.issue.ignore.multicriteria` are never raised at all, so they do not even appear as suppressed.

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

Sonar does expose accepted-issue and false-positive counts as metrics, so the count can be tracked, but a filter-based multicriteria suppression is not even counted -- the issue is suppressed before it is created. The only durable control is reviewing the properties file itself.

**A5.21** `[yes]` For C#, CodeQL default setup automatically selects `build-mode: none` (buildless analysis), which has documented accuracy caveats: when multiple versions of the same NuGet dependency exist CodeQL can use only one (typically the newer); when multiple .NET target frameworks are referenced only a single version is used; and colliding class names may cause "missing method call targets, impacting dataflow analysis."

- Threshold: `C# build modes: none | autobuild | manual. Default setup sets build-mode: none for C# automatically.`
- Source: GitHub Docs -- CodeQL build options and steps for compiled languages -- https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/reference/code-scanning/codeql/build-options-for-compiled-languages
- Cross-reference: https://github.blog/changelog/2024-06-20-codeql-can-scan-c-projects-without-requiring-working-builds-public-beta/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Directly relevant to a multi-project ABP solution with a private NuGet feed for ABP Commercial packages. Degraded dataflow is silent -- there is no banner on the alerts page saying "taint analysis was incomplete." If the ABP Commercial feed is not reachable from the CodeQL job, dependency restore partially fails and the taint queries quietly under-report. Building explicitly (`build-mode: manual`) removes this class of doubt at the cost of a slower job.

**A5.22** `[yes]` NIST's own multi-tool study found low overlap between static analysis tools: "As Figure 4 shows, tools mostly find different weaknesses. This is partly due to the fact that tools often look for different weakness types." In the Dovecot test case, of 23 weaknesses reported by multiple tools, 21 were reported by exactly 2 tools and only 2 by 3 tools, with 7 tools analysed; for the Pebble test case one tool's warnings "did not overlap with the warnings from other tools at all."

- Threshold: `Dovecot: 21 of 23 multi-tool weaknesses found by exactly 2 of 7 tools; 2 found by 3 tools; none by 4+`
- Source: NIST SP 500-283, Report on the Third Static Analysis Tool Exposition (SATE 2010) -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication500-283.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://www.nist.gov/itl/csd/secure-systems-and-applications/samate/static-analysis-tool-exposition-sate
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: This is the best primary evidence I could find for the "is running two SAST tools redundant?" question, and it says no -- tools mostly do not overlap. But it is dated: SP 500-283 was published in 2011 on SATE 2010 data, long before CodeQL existed and before Sonar's current taint engine. I am marking it partial because the finding is directionally sound and primary, but the specific tools studied are not the tools being recommended. I found no modern equivalent study from NIST or OWASP.
