- Notes: Opened. Relevant because the expensive step is not the scan (Trivy on a .NET image is roughly 30-90 seconds after DB download) but the docker build that has to happen first. If they are not already building images in CI, that build is the real cost of adopting image scanning -- both in minutes and in setup effort.

### Where sources disagree

- Minimal base image: Docker's own best-practices page recommends Alpine ('tightly controlled and small in size (under 6 MB), while still being a full Linux distribution'), while Microsoft ships Alpine .NET images but positions Ubuntu Chiseled as the distroless option and has made Ubuntu 24.04 the .NET 10 default. Following Docker's generic advice would push a .NET shop onto musl libc, which is a materially larger compatibility risk for EF Core, ICU and native dependencies than chiseled Ubuntu. For this stack, follow Microsoft.
- Digest pinning: Docker Docs say pin to a digest to 'fully secure your supply chain integrity'; Microsoft's MCR guidance says floating minor-version tags are 'the form of tag for most cases' because they 'balance the competing concerns of ease of use and the risk of updates', and elsewhere that digest pinning is required only to request a specific OS patch level. These are genuinely opposed defaults. The resolution is not to pick a side but to note that pinning is only safe with an automated bump attached; without Dependabot, Microsoft is right, and with it, Docker is.
- Scanner accuracy: Trivy documents that it deliberately consumes only OS-vendor advisories for OS packages and takes vendor severity over NVD, explicitly to reduce false positives (its example: CVE-2023-0464 is NVD HIGH but Red Hat Low, and Trivy reports Low). Independent testing points the other way on false NEGATIVES -- an August 2025 technical analysis (augmentedmind.de, SECONDARY source, single author) of eight popular Docker Hub images found Trivy's open-source version 'rarely detects the tested CVEs' in an image's primary component compared with Grype. Both claims can be simultaneously true, and together they mean neither tool's HIGH count is an absolute measure of risk. I did not find a peer-reviewed or vendor-neutral 2026 benchmark; treat comparative accuracy claims as unsettled.
- Severity threshold for a gate: there is no standards-body number. What exists is convergent vendor default -- Aqua's own trivy-action example (CRITICAL,HIGH + ignore-unfixed) and Docker Scout's built-in policy (critical and high 'where a fix version is available', plus a grace period) -- against OWASP ASVS 5.0, which declines to name a severity at all and instead requires at Level 1 that you DEFINE risk-based remediation timeframes (15.1.1) and hold components inside them (15.2.1). These are not in conflict so much as answering different questions, but a team looking for 'the standard threshold' should know that the severity number is a vendor convention and the SLA is the actual requirement.
- Handling unfixable CVEs: sources split between suppression and assertion. Trivy and Grype both ship blunt suppression flags (--ignore-unfixed, --only-fixed) and Docker Scout's default policy simply excludes them. The VEX ecosystem (OpenVEX v0.2.0, and CISA's minimum-requirements guidance) argues an exception should instead be a published, justified, machine-readable statement using one of five not_affected justifications. The VEX position is clearly better practice and clearly heavier; for two developers the honest middle is .trivyignore.yaml entries with a mandatory expired_at date, which preserves the re-review property that makes VEX valuable without the authoring burden.
- CIS Docker Benchmark item numbering: CIS's own site confirms v1.8.0 is current (and the August 2025 CIS Benchmarks update blog names it, with support for Docker Server v28), but the specific Section 4 item numbers I could find documented (4.1 user, 4.6 HEALTHCHECK, 4.7 update instructions, 4.9 COPY over ADD, 4.10 secrets) are third-party writeups against v1.6.0. The v1.8.0 PDF sits behind a download form I could not open, and the widely-cited dev-sec/cis-docker-benchmark InSpec profile still targets CIS Docker 1.13.0, so it is not a valid cross-reference. Confidence in the item CONTENT is high; confidence in the exact numbering under v1.8.0 is partial.
- Docker Scout free-tier repo count: aggregator and pricing-comparison sites report 1 or 3 Scout repos on the free tier with a 3-to-1 cut in December 2024. Docker's own Plans FAQ, which I opened, says Personal 1, Pro 2, Team and Business unlimited. I am citing Docker. Similarly a search snippet claimed Snyk Free allows 400 Open Source tests where the live snyk.io/plans page I opened says 200 (Container is 100 in both) -- another reason not to take pricing numbers from secondary sources.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- Your scanner is an attack path. Trivy was supply-chain compromised TWICE in March 2026: a malicious v0.69.4 binary published to GHCR, ECR Public, Docker Hub, deb, rpm and get.trivy.dev, and separately every aquasecurity/trivy-action tag prior to v0.35.0 plus every setup-trivy release were poisoned for windows of 4-12 hours. The maintainers' remediation guidance is explicit commit SHAs (trivy-action@57a97c7, setup-trivy@3fb12ec) and rotating pipeline secrets. Adopting container scanning without SHA-pinning the action is a net negative for a HIPAA system. This is the single finding here that most teams would not anticipate.
- Almost every container-scanning tutorial ends with github/codeql-action/upload-sarif, and that step returns 403 on a private repo without paid GitHub Code Security / Advanced Security. Two developers following a blog post will spend an afternoon debugging permissions on a step that cannot work. Design the pipeline around exit codes and artifacts from the start.
- Dependabot's docker ecosystem does NOT do security updates -- only scheduled version updates. This is documented in GitHub's own support matrix and is easy to miss because npm and NuGet DO get security updates. Concretely: a critical CVE published tomorrow against your .NET 10 base image will never open a Dependabot PR. Nothing in a default GitHub setup tells you about post-merge base-image CVEs; that is the entire reason the weekly advisory scan exists.
- Digest pinning has a hidden expiry. Docker's best-practices page tells you to pin to a digest for supply-chain integrity; Microsoft's MCR tagging documentation says fixed .NET version tags are serviced only for 'the supported life of the image (typically one month)'. So a pinned digest is not a stable choice -- it is a decaying one, and after a couple of months your 'hardened' pin is the most vulnerable thing in the build. Pin plus Dependabot, or do not pin.
- Chiseled .NET images omit ICU and tzdata. The chiseled image only works for apps in globalization-invariant mode unless you use the -extra tag suffix. An ABP application scheduling California workers'-comp medical exams -- dates, timezones, culture-sensitive formatting -- will break in subtle, data-dependent ways rather than failing loudly at startup. If you go chiseled, go 10.0-noble-chiseled-extra.
- .NET 10 removed Debian images entirely. Default tags now reference Ubuntu 24.04 'Noble Numbat' and Microsoft states plainly that 'Debian-based images are no longer provided'. Any Dockerfile carrying a -bookworm or -bullseye suffix inherited from a .NET 8 or 9 template simply has no .NET 10 equivalent to resolve to.
- The non-chiseled aspnet:10.0 image still runs as ROOT. The 'app' user (UID 1654, home /home/app) has shipped in every Linux .NET image since .NET 8 -- but it is not the default; you must add USER $APP_UID. Teams frequently assume that because the user exists, it is in use. Microsoft's own phrasing gives it away: 'Configure your container as non-root with a one-line USER instruction.'
- Turning on Trivy image scanning turns on secret scanning whether you meant to or not -- the trivy image --scanners default is [vuln,secret]. Expect your first run to fail on something embedded in the image rather than on a CVE. For a database-per-tenant system where tenancy resolves from the Host header, a connection string baked into an image layer is a cross-tenant exposure, so this default is a feature; just do not be surprised when the first red build is not about CVEs.
- CVSS severity is a weakening gate signal in 2026. NIST announced on 15 April 2026 that all backlogged CVEs published before 1 March 2026 move to 'Lowest Priority - not scheduled for immediate enrichment', with enrichment reserved for CISA KEV entries, federal software and EO 14028 critical software -- driven by a 263% rise in CVE submissions between 2020 and 2025. The cutoff has effectively walked forward to the present. This is why Grype now sorts by an EPSS/KEV risk score by default and why Trivy prefers vendor severity over NVD, and it is a real argument against building policy on NVD base scores.
- Docker Scout's GitHub App integration was retired on 1 July 2026. Anyone following a 2024 or 2025 walkthrough will try to install a dead integration. Docker's own replacement advice is Dependabot with package-ecosystem 'docker' plus building with --provenance=mode=max -- which is exactly the free path recommended above, from the vendor whose paid product it replaced.
- hadolint cannot see the image you actually shipped. DL3002 checks the last USER instruction in the Dockerfile text; it cannot detect a final stage that inherited root from a base image, or a base image that reset the user. That is why the image-hardening-assert check inspects the built artifact's config rather than trusting the linter -- three lines of shell that close a gap a linter structurally cannot.
- BuildKit is already generating SLSA provenance attestations for you at mode=min by default. You are not starting from zero on supply-chain metadata; you are one flag (--provenance=mode=max) from commit-to-image traceability. The catch is that attestations require pushing to a registry or using the containerd image store -- the classic image store cannot hold an image with attestations, so a --load-based local build silently drops them.
- The expensive part of adopting image scanning is probably not the scanner. Trivy on a .NET image runs in well under a minute with a warm DB cache. If you do not currently build images in CI, the docker build is the new cost -- in setup effort and against the 2,000 free Actions minutes per month on a private GitHub Free repo (3,000 on Team). Budget accordingly and cache aggressively.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Image signing and verification (cosign / Sigstore) with an enforcing admission policy. Signing an image is easy; the value comes only from something REFUSING to run an unsigned image, and that enforcement point is infrastructure and out of scope here. It also introduces key management, which is a genuine operational burden for two SDE 1s with no security specialist. BuildKit's default provenance attestation (mode=min, already on) plus --provenance=mode=max gives you the commit-to-image linkage that actually helps during an incident, for one flag.
- Running two scanners as merge gates (Trivy AND Grype). Their severity ratings are architecturally different -- Trivy deliberately prefers OS vendor severity over NVD, Grype ranks by an EPSS/KEV risk score -- so the same image gets two different HIGH counts and every disagreement becomes a triage conversation. Doubling triage load is precisely how a two-person team ends up bypassing both. Use one gate, keep the second scanner as an occasional manual second opinion in the weekly advisory run.
- Uploading SARIF to GitHub code scanning. On a private repo this requires paid GitHub Code Security / Advanced Security and returns 403 without it. It is a reporting convenience, not a control: the scanner exit code is what blocks the merge. Skip it, and treat the price tag as a later question tied to whether you want CodeQL as well.
- Authoring OpenVEX documents per image as your exception mechanism. VEX is the technically correct answer to unfixable CVEs and is what a larger company with a product-security function does -- signed, publishable, machine-readable non-exploitability assertions. For two developers it is a job, not a config file. The 90% version is a .trivyignore.yaml where every entry carries a mandatory expired_at date and a one-line justification borrowed from the OpenVEX vocabulary; the expiry is the part that matters, because it forces re-review instead of letting suppressions accumulate silently.
- A full CIS Docker Benchmark audit via docker-bench-security. The large majority of the benchmark is daemon and runtime configuration -- host hardening, daemon flags, container runtime options -- which is both out of scope for this exercise and unactionable from a Dockerfile. Section 4 is the code-level slice and hadolint plus the image-hardening assert covers its substance. Do not buy CIS SecureSuite for this.
- Migrating to chiseled/distroless images before launch. It is the right destination and Microsoft does publish mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0-noble-chiseled, but the default chiseled image has no ICU and no tzdata, which for a California workers'-comp scheduling system doing date and timezone arithmetic is a live functional risk (you need the -extra variant). It also removes the shell, which removes your ability to exec in and debug during your first weeks on the public internet. Do it as a deliberate post-launch change with a test pass, not as a hardening checkbox.
- Paying for Docker Scout Team or Snyk Team. Scout free is 1 Scout-enabled repo; Snyk free is 100 container tests/month against private repos and $25/dev/month beyond. Neither buys you anything Trivy does not do free for two images, and both add a vendor account as a CI dependency. Docker Scout is genuinely nice as a local desk tool (docker scout quickview needs only docker login, not repo enrollment) -- use it that way for free and do not put it in the pipeline.
- Gating on MEDIUM or LOW severity, or on unfixed vulnerabilities. Every vendor default stops at fixable HIGH/CRITICAL for the same reason: below that line the volume exceeds what any small team can triage, and the gate stops being a signal. Collect them in the weekly advisory run instead.
- Chasing a zero-CVE image or buying 'hardened base image' subscriptions. A .NET runtime image contains hundreds of components and there is always residual unfixable surface; ASVS 5.0 asks at Level 1 for documented remediation timeframes that are honoured (15.1.1, 15.2.1), not a zero count. Chiseled images plus the weekly advisory run get you most of the reduction for free.
- Building and maintaining your own hardened base images. This is a standing maintenance commitment measured in developer-days per month and it makes you responsible for OS patch currency that Microsoft currently handles for you on a roughly monthly rebuild cadence.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`dockerfile-lint`** -- tool: hadolint via hadolint/hadolint-action, SHA-pinned

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs hadolint against every Dockerfile in the repo on every PR, failing only on rules at severity 'error'. Enforces the code-level CIS Section 4 / OWASP RULE #2 items directly: DL3002 last user must not be root, DL3006/DL3007 base image must be explicitly tagged and not 'latest', DL3020 COPY instead of ADD, DL3064 no sensitive data in ARG/ENV. Configured via a checked-in .hadolint.yaml so the rule set is reviewable and exceptions are visible in the diff.
- Why at this team size: This is the cheapest possible gate: it runs in seconds, needs no image build, no registry, no account, and no network beyond pulling the action. Crucially it fails only on new bad input -- once the existing Dockerfiles are clean it is silent, which is what keeps a two-person team from bypassing it. Start at --failure-threshold error (NOT the default 'info', which fires on style rules and will get the gate disabled in a fortnight). Promote individual warning-level rules to errors later if you want, one at a time.
- Tool maintenance evidence: hadolint v2.15.1 released 31 Jul 2026 (https://github.com/hadolint/hadolint/releases); hadolint-action v3.5.0 released 24 Aug 2026, whose release note is 'Bump Hadolint to v2.15.1' (https://github.com/hadolint/hadolint-action/releases). License GPL-3.0, invoked as a separate binary so no licensing concern for a proprietary product.

**`image-scan`** -- tool: Trivy via aquasecurity/trivy-action, pinned to a full commit SHA, with severity 'CRITICAL,HIGH', ignore-unfixed true, exit-code 1

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 4-6 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Builds the two images the team actually authors (the .NET 10 API and the Angular/nginx frontend) and runs Trivy against each, failing the PR only on CRITICAL and HIGH vulnerabilities that have a fix available. Because Trivy's default --scanners is [vuln,secret], the same run also fails on secrets baked into image layers, which enforces CIS 4.10 and -- given database-per-tenant -- catches a baked-in connection string before it ships. Results are written to a table-format file and uploaded as a workflow artifact plus posted as a PR comment; do NOT attempt SARIF upload.
- Why at this team size: This is the one check that would actually have caught something before going public, and the fixable-only threshold is what makes it survivable rather than aspirational -- it is Aqua's own documented example and matches Docker Scout's default policy, so you are not inventing a lax standard. The real cost here is not Trivy, it is that you may not build images in CI yet; budget most of the hours for the docker build step. Pin trivy-action to a commit SHA, not a tag: all trivy-action tags before v0.35.0 were compromised in March 2026. Add a persistent Trivy DB cache to keep the job under a minute so it does not eat your 2,000 free private-repo minutes.
- Tool maintenance evidence: trivy v0.74.0 released 14 Aug 2026, with v0.73.0 on 3 Aug 2026 and v0.72.0 on 30 Jun 2026 (https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/releases); Apache-2.0, 37.7k stars (https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy). trivy-action latest v0.36.0; v0.35.0 was the post-incident clean release (https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy-action/releases).

**`image-hardening-assert`** -- tool: A ~20-line shell script in the workflow using docker inspect; no third-party tool or account

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 1-2 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: After the build, inspects the resulting image config (docker inspect / docker image inspect --format) and fails if: the configured User is empty, 'root' or '0'; the exposed port is a privileged port below 1024; or the image carries a 'latest' base reference. This asserts on the BUILT ARTIFACT, which is what actually runs, rather than on the Dockerfile text that hadolint reads. For the .NET side it verifies USER $APP_UID (the app user, UID 1654) and port 8080; for nginx it verifies the unprivileged configuration.
- Why at this team size: hadolint's DL3002 only reads the last USER in the Dockerfile text; it cannot see that a multi-stage build's final stage inherited root, or that a base image reset the user. A three-line inspect closes that gap for effectively zero maintenance and zero false positives -- it is a deterministic assertion, not a heuristic. This is also the check that survives a base-image swap: if someone later moves to a chiseled image or back, the assertion still holds the invariant. mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0 runs as root unless you explicitly add USER, so assume you currently fail this.
- Tool maintenance evidence: No external dependency -- uses the Docker CLI already present on GitHub-hosted runners. The values asserted come from Microsoft's own guidance (app user UID 1654, port 8080) at https://devblogs.microsoft.com/dotnet/securing-containers-with-rootless/ and https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/compatibility/containers/8.0/aspnet-port

**`workflow-audit`** -- tool: zizmor (zizmorcore/zizmor), MIT licensed

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 1-2 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs zizmor over .github/workflows on every PR touching workflow files. Its unpinned-uses audit flags any 'uses:' clause not pinned to an immutable full commit SHA; it also flags template injection via ${{ }} expansion of attacker-controllable context, excessive GITHUB_TOKEN permissions, credential persistence from actions/checkout (artipacked), and known-vulnerable actions.
- Why at this team size: Ordinarily I would call a workflow linter a nice-to-have. It is not, this year, for this team. Trivy -- the scanner you are about to adopt -- was itself compromised twice in March 2026, with every trivy-action tag before v0.35.0 malicious for about twelve hours. If you adopt scanners without SHA-pinning them, you have added an attack path to a HIPAA system in exchange for a vulnerability report. Run zizmor once now, SHA-pin everything it flags, then leave it as a gate: like hadolint it is silent until someone adds an unpinned action. Pair it with Dependabot's github-actions ecosystem so the pins do not rot.
- Tool maintenance evidence: zizmor v1.29.0 released 1 Aug 2026, with v1.30.0-rc1 on 13 Aug 2026 and a steady monthly cadence back through v1.24.1 in April 2026 (https://github.com/zizmorcore/zizmor/releases). 6.4k stars, sponsored by Grafana Labs and Trail of Bits (https://github.com/zizmorcore/zizmor). Audit list at https://docs.zizmor.sh/audits/

**`image-scan-full (scheduled)`** -- tool: Trivy (same SHA-pinned action, different inputs) on a cron schedule; optionally a second Grype pass for a differing opinion

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A weekly scheduled workflow that scans a broader surface than the PR gate: all severities including MEDIUM and LOW, unfixed vulnerabilities included, and the third-party images the stack runs but does not build (SQL Server, Redis, MinIO). It never blocks anything; it opens or updates a single GitHub issue with the delta since last week. This is where unfixable base-image CVEs and newly disclosed CVEs against already-merged images become visible.
- Why at this team size: This is the half of the story the PR gate deliberately drops, and it is the reason the gate can afford to be lenient. It also covers the structural blind spot that Dependabot's docker ecosystem supports version updates but NOT security updates -- nothing else in your pipeline will ever tell you that a CVE was disclosed against an image you merged last month. Keep it advisory and keep it to ONE issue: a weekly workflow that fails, or that opens five issues, is a weekly workflow that gets disabled. If you add Grype here, add it as a second opinion for a human to read, never as a second gate -- it will disagree with Trivy on severity by design, since Trivy prefers vendor severity and Grype ranks by EPSS/KEV.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Trivy as above. Grype v0.118.0 released 27 Aug 2026, Apache-2.0, sorts by an EPSS/KEV-derived risk score by default (https://github.com/anchore/grype/releases and https://oss.anchore.com/docs/guides/vulnerability/interpreting-results/)

**`dependabot (docker + github-actions)`** -- tool: GitHub Dependabot (native, free on private repos)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 1 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A .github/dependabot.yml declaring package-ecosystem 'docker' (weekly, for every Dockerfile directory) and 'github-actions' (weekly). The docker updater bumps base image tags and, where a digest is already present in the FROM line, updates the digest alongside it. The github-actions updater keeps your SHA pins current so pinning does not turn into permanent staleness.
- Why at this team size: One YAML file, and it is the only thing that makes digest pinning safe. Microsoft services a fixed .NET tag for typically one month, so a naked digest pin quietly turns into a frozen, unpatched base image -- pin plus Dependabot, or do not pin at all. Set open-pull-requests-limit low (2-3) so it cannot bury two developers, and review the PRs in the same slot as the weekly scan issue. Deliberately excluded: any expectation that this reacts to CVE disclosure. It does not, for docker. That is what image-scan-full is for.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Support matrix current at https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/reference/supply-chain-security/supported-ecosystems-and-repositories (docker: version updates supported, security updates NOT supported; github-actions: both supported). Docker's own docs now name this as the replacement for the retired Scout GitHub integration, at https://docs.docker.com/scout/integrations/source-code-management/github/



## 8. Dynamic application security testing

**Verification verdict for this area: `material-errors`** (19 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- Claim 1: the cited ZAP Ownership page says ZAP was taken out of OWASP "in September 2023", contradicting the claim's stated threshold of 2023-08-01. The page also says nothing about Apache License 2.0.
- Claim 2: weekly pre-releases are NOT on a Monday cadence. The last five weeklies are w2026-08-26 (Wednesday), w2026-08-19 (Wednesday), w2026-08-11 (Tuesday), w2026-08-06 (Thursday), w2026-07-28 (Tuesday). Only w2026-07-13 and w2026-07-06 fell on a Monday.
- Claim 3: there are not four first-party ZAP scan actions. The cited org listing shows zaproxy/action-mcp-scan (updated 20 Aug 2026) as a fifth first-party action, plus zaproxy/actions-common. The four version numbers and repo dates are all correct.
- Claim 6: -f has no documented default. The api-scan usage synopsis is 'zap-api-scan.py -t <target> -f <format> [options]', i.e. -f is required, and the docs list 'openapi, soap, or graphql' with no default marked. The threshold's 'openapi (default)' is unsupported.
- Claim 11: the sub-alert names are wrong. 40040-1 is 'CORS Header'; only 40040-2 and 40040-3 are 'CORS Misconfiguration'.

### Standard, in summary

The 2026 baseline is unchanged in shape but changed in branding and in what "the tool" means: ZAP is still the only credible free DAST engine, it is actively developed (3 full-time engineers funded by Checkmarx, stable 2.17.0 released 2025-12-15, weekly builds through w2026-08-26), but it is no longer an OWASP project -- it left OWASP in August 2023 and has been "ZAP by Checkmarx" since 2024-09-24, still Apache 2.0 and still controlled by the ZAP Core Team. NIST IR 8397 2.10 states the minimum plainly: if the software provides a web service, use a DAST tool; SSDF SP 800-218 PW.8.2 Example 2 says to integrate dynamic vulnerability testing into the automated test suite, and hedges penetration testing with "if resources are available". For a two-person team the sustainable shape is: one passive ZAP baseline scan on merges to main and nightly, one OpenAPI-driven ZAP API scan with a bearer token injected via ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_VALUE, a weekly full active scan out of the PR path, and -- critically -- deterministic integration tests rather than scanner output for the things you actually gate on (response headers, cookie flags, tenant isolation). The scan target is an ephemeral docker-compose stack brought up inside the GitHub Actions job; where any persistent staging environment lives is an infrastructure decision covered elsewhere. DAST earns its place here because it observes what SAST structurally cannot: the actual bytes on the wire -- Set-Cookie attributes, HSTS/CSP/nosniff/frame-ancestors headers, CORS reflection, TLS behaviour, and which HTTP verbs the deployed pipeline actually accepts -- all of which are properties of the assembled runtime (Kestrel + ABP middleware + reverse proxy + Angular build), not of any single source file.

### Claims and sources

**A8.1** `[partially]` ZAP is no longer an OWASP project. The ZAP Core Team left OWASP for the Software Security Project in August 2023 and the project has been branded "ZAP by Checkmarx" since 24 September 2024. It remains an independent open-source project controlled by the ZAP Core Team under Apache License 2.0, with three Core Team members employed by Checkmarx.

- Threshold: `Left OWASP 2023-08-01; "ZAP by Checkmarx" from 2024-09-24; Apache License v2`
- Source: ZAP official documentation - ZAP Ownership -- https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/zap-ownership/
- Cross-reference: https://www.zaproxy.org/blog/2024-09-24-zap-has-joined-forces-with-checkmarx/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

The 2024 blog post states: "ZAP will stay under the control of the ZAP Core Team, remain open source, and stay licensed under Apache v2" and "ZAP will now be known as 'ZAP by Checkmarx'". Practical consequence: policy documents, HIPAA vendor questionnaires and security narratives that say "we run OWASP ZAP" are factually wrong in 2026, and the legacy owasp/zap2docker-* Docker image names in older tutorials are dead.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** ZAP left OWASP in September 2023 (not August 2023 / 2023-08-01) per the cited page; the 2023-08-01 date belongs to the separate 'joining the Software Security Project' blog post, not the departure. The page does not state any licence, so 'Apache License 2.0' is not supported by this URL. Confirmed by the page: independent open-source project controlled by the ZAP Core Team, supported by Checkmarx, 3 Core Team members employed by Checkmarx, 'ZAP by Checkmarx' branding, and the linked blog post 'ZAP Has Joined Forces With Checkmarx' at /blog/2024-09-24-... (HTTP 200).

**A8.4** `[yes]` The ZAP baseline scan is passive-only -- it spiders the target and waits for passive scanning, performing "no actual 'attacks'" -- with a default spider duration of 1 minute (-m). It exits 0 on success, 1 if at least one FAIL, 2 if at least one WARN and no FAILs, 3 on other failure. -I suppresses failure on warnings.

- Threshold: `Default spider -m = 1 minute; exit codes 0/1/2/3; passive rules only`
- Source: ZAP Docker documentation - Baseline Scan -- https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/docker/baseline-scan/
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/zaproxy/action-baseline/blob/master/README.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the check that fits a PR/merge budget: minutes, not hours, and structurally incapable of writing data. Because WARN-level rules cause a non-zero exit by default, the action's fail_action input and the -c rules config file are the two knobs that decide whether the team keeps the check or bypasses it.

**A8.5** `[partially]` The ZAP full scan runs the spider (by default with no time limit), then an optional modern spider, then a full active scan. **A8.8** `[partially]` ZAP supports bypassing its own authentication machinery by injecting a pre-obtained token: ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_VALUE (the token), ZAP_AUTH_HEADER (header name, defaults to Authorization) and ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_SITE (restrict to matching sites). These must be set at system level before ZAP starts and apply however ZAP is run, including via the Automation Framework.

- Threshold: `ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_VALUE / ZAP_AUTH_HEADER / ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_SITE; set before ZAP starts`
- Source: ZAP documentation - Handling Authentication Yourself (in Automation) -- https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/getting-further/authentication/handling-auth-yourself/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The page's caveat is explicit: this works only "if you can generate an authentication token ... and you know that your app will not invalidate it while you are using ZAP" -- token lifetime becomes your problem. For a scan bounded to ~20 minutes against a CI-only tenant this is a non-issue. The Replacer add-on offers the same via -config replacer.full_list(0).matchtype=REQ_HEADER when you need per-site control.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** All three variable names, the Authorization default, the site restriction and the 'must be set before ZAP starts' constraint are verbatim supported. The trailing clause 'apply however ZAP is run, including via the Automation Framework' is not stated on the page -- it says only that they are standard OS env vars. Drop or soften that clause.
- Verifier read from the page: "The env vars are standard operating system env vars so how you will need to set them will depend on your OS and possibly your shell. They need to be set before you start ZAP, they cannot be set once ZAP is already running."

**A8.9** `[yes]` As of July 2026 ZAP recommends the Client Spider (introduced in ZAP 2.16.0) over the AJAX Spider for crawling modern web applications; in Automation Framework plans the spiderAjax job is replaced by spiderClient, and the packaged Baseline/Full scan scripts expose it via -j (default modern spider) and --client-spider (always Client Spider).

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

The AJAX Spider is built on Crawljax, which ZAP describes as "maintained intermittently"; the Client Spider reads the DOM via ZAP's browser extension and finds content the AJAX Spider cannot. Nightly packaged scans already default to Client Spider; on the :stable tag you should pass --client-spider explicitly rather than assume.

**A8.10** `[yes]` ZAP's release-status passive scan rules cover exactly the response-header and cookie controls that DAST exists to catch: 10010 Cookie HttpOnly, 10011 Cookie Secure Flag, 10054 Cookie Without SameSite Attribute, 10020 Anti-clickjacking Header, 10038 CSP Header Not Set, 10021 X-Content-Type-Options Header Missing, 10035 Strict-Transport-Security Header, 10023 Information Disclosure - Debug Errors, 10036 HTTP Server Response Header (version leakage).

- Threshold: `Rule IDs 10010, 10011, 10020, 10021, 10023, 10035, 10036, 10038, 10054 -- all release status`
- Source: ZAP documentation - Passive Scan Rules (release add-on) -- https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/desktop/addons/passive-scan-rules/

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: All nine are in the default-installed release add-on, so a plain baseline scan raises them with zero configuration. This is the concrete answer to "what does a baseline scan actually buy us": deterministic detection of the ASVS V3.3/V3.4 control set on every response the spider touches.

**A8.12** `[partially]` OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 (released 30 May 2025) locates the DAST-observable controls as: 3.3.1 Secure attribute / __Secure- prefix (L1), 3.3.2 SameSite (L2), 3.3.3 __Host- prefix (L2), 3.3.4 HttpOnly (L2), 3.4.1 Strict-Transport-Security on all responses (L1), 3.4.2 CORS Access-Control-Allow-Origin is a fixed value set by the application (L1), 3.4.3 Content-Security-Policy (L2), 3.4.4 X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff (L2), 3.4.6 CSP frame-ancestors on every response (L2), and 4.1.4 only explicitly supported HTTP methods usable, unused methods blocked (L3).

- Threshold: `ASVS v5.0.0: 3.3.1 L1, 3.3.2 L2, 3.3.3 L2, 3.3.4 L2, 3.4.1 L1, 3.4.2 L1, 3.4.3 L2, 3.4.4 L2, 3.4.6 L2, 4.1.4 L3`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0 - V3 Web Frontend Security / V4 API and Web Service (source markdown) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x12-V3-Web-Frontend-Security.md
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/www-project-application-security-verification-standard/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Chapter numbering changed substantially from 4.0.3 -- cookies moved from V3.4 (Session Management) into V3.3 (Web Frontend Security), and headers from V14.4 into V3.4. Blog posts and tool mappings still citing 4.0.3 IDs will not line up. V4.2.1-4.2.3 additionally cover request smuggling, and V10.4.1 (L1) requires redirect_uri validation "using exact string comparison" with V10.4.6 (L2) requiring PKCE -- directly relevant to the OpenIddict surface being exposed publicly.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** All nine 3.3.x/3.4.x IDs and levels are exactly right at this URL. 4.1.4 is NOT in this file -- it lives in 0x13-V4-API-and-Web-Service.md, where it is confirmed at L3: 'Verify that only HTTP methods that are explicitly supported by the application or its API (including OPTIONS during preflight requests) can be used and that unused methods are blocked.' Add that second URL. The 30 May 2025 release date is not on this page either. Also note this is the master branch, not a pinned v5.0.0 tag -- pin the tag if the standard depends on stable IDs.
- Verifier read from the page: "| **3.3.1** ... 'Secure' attribute ... | 1 | ... | **3.3.2** ... 'SameSite' ... | 2 | ... | **3.4.1** ... Strict-Transport-Security ... | 1 | ... | **3.4.2** ... Access-Control-Allow-Origin ... | 1 | ... | **3.4.6** ... frame-ancestors ... | 2 |"

**A8.13** `[partially]` OWASP WSTG defines HTTP verb testing as WSTG-CONF-06 (Test HTTP Methods), with objectives to enumerate supported methods, test for access-control bypass, test XST, and test HTTP method overriding via headers such as X-HTTP-Method-Override. ZAP's corresponding rule, "Insecure HTTP Method" (90028), is a BETA active rule and covers DELETE, PUT, TRACE, CONNECT and PROPFIND.

- Threshold: `WSTG-CONF-06; ZAP rule 90028 (beta, ascanrulesBeta)`
- Source: OWASP WSTG - Test HTTP Methods -- https://owasp.org/www-project-web-security-testing-guide/v42/4-Web_Application_Security_Testing/02-Configuration_and_Deployment_Management_Testing/06-Test_HTTP_Methods
- Cross-reference: https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/alerts/90028/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verb tampering sits at ASVS L3 (4.1.4) and in ZAP's beta rule set, which means a team claiming "ASVS L2, default ZAP rules" has zero verb-tampering coverage. WSTG's X-HTTP-Method-Override note matters for ASP.NET Core, where middleware ordering can make an override header meaningful.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The WSTG half is fully confirmed at this URL. The ZAP half is not on this page -- verified separately at https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/alerts/90028/: ID 90028, Alert Type Active, Status beta, org/zaproxy/zap/extension/ascanrulesBeta/InsecureHttpMethodScanRule.java. Note there are six sub-alerts, not five: 90028-1 DELETE, -2 PUT, -3 TRACE, -4 CONNECT, -5 PROPFIND, -6 PUT (a second PUT variant). Add the 90028 URL.
- Verifier read from the page: "WSTG-CONF-06 ... Enumerate supported HTTP methods. Test for access control bypass. Test XST vulnerabilities. Test HTTP method overriding techniques. ... X-HTTP-Method-Override"

**A8.14** `[partially]` NIST sets DAST as a minimum verification standard: NIST IR 8397 2.10 states "If the software provides a web service, use a dynamic application security testing (DAST) tool, e.g., web application scanner ... or Interactive Application Security Testing (IAST) tool to detect vulnerabilities." NIST SP 800-218 (SSDF v1.1, Feb 2022) task PW.8.2 Example 2 is "Integrate dynamic vulnerability testing into the project's automated test suite", with Example 6 qualifying penetration testing as "If resources are available".

- Threshold: `IR 8397 2.10; SP 800-218 PW.8.2 Examples 2 and 6`
- Source: NIST IR 8397, Guidelines on Minimum Standards for Developer Verification of Software -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ir/2021/NIST.IR.8397.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Text extracted directly from both PDFs. IR 8397 3.5 lists ZAP among example DAST tools and separately defines penetration testing as "humans using tools, technologies, and their knowledge and expertise" -- i.e. NIST does not treat a scanner as a pen test. Neither document specifies a frequency, which is why the HIPAA NPRM cadence below is the only concrete number available.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The IR 8397 2.10 quote is verbatim correct (section title: 'Web Application Scanning'). The SP 800-218 half is not in this PDF -- verified separately at https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-218.pdf, PW.8.2: 'Example 2: Integrate dynamic vulnerability testing into the project's automated test suite.' and 'Example 6: If resources are available, use penetration testing to simulate how an attacker might attempt to compromise the software in high-risk scenarios.' Add that second URL. Framing nit: IR 8397 is guidance on minimum standards for developer verification, and 2.10 is conditional ('If the software provides a web service'), so 'NIST sets DAST as a minimum verification standard' is a fair but slightly strong paraphrase.
- Verifier read from the page: "2.10 Web Application Scanning -- If the software provides a web service, use a dynamic application security testing (DAST) tool, e.g., web application scanner, see Sec. 3.5, or Interactive Application Security Testing (IAST) tool to detect vulnerabilities."

**A8.15** `[yes]` The HHS OCR HIPAA Security Rule NPRM published 27 December 2024 proposes to "Require vulnerability scanning at least every six months and penetration testing at least once every 12 months", alongside mandatory MFA, encryption of ePHI at rest and in transit, and an annually revised technology asset inventory and network map.

- Threshold: `Vulnerability scanning >= every 6 months; penetration testing >= every 12 months (proposed, not final)`
- Source: HHS.gov - HIPAA Security Rule NPRM Fact Sheet -- https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/security/hipaa-security-rule-nprm/factsheet/index.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: The proposed thresholds are verified verbatim from the HHS fact sheet. What is NOT verified from a primary source is the 2026 rulemaking status; secondary sources (Medcurity, Blaze Infosec -- both labelled secondary) state that as of mid-2026 the rule remains proposed and OCR is still reviewing comments, with the pre-2025 Security Rule still in effect. Treat the six-month cadence as a planning target that a nightly CI job satisfies for free, not as a current legal obligation.

**A8.18** `[partially]` OWASP WSTG-ATHZ-02 (Testing for Bypassing Authorization Schema) requires two concurrently active authenticated sessions with identical privileges, swapping the session identifier on every request and comparing responses -- a methodology no unattended scanner performs. OWASP Top 10 A05:2021 Security Misconfiguration (20 CWEs mapped, 4.51% average incidence rate) explicitly includes "The server does not send security headers or directives, or they are not set to secure values" and prescribes "An automated process to verify the effectiveness of the configurations and settings in all environments."

- Threshold: `WSTG-ATHZ-02: two sessions, identical privileges, per-request token swap. A05:2021: 20 CWEs, 4.51% avg incidence`
- Source: OWASP WSTG - Testing for Bypassing Authorization Schema -- https://owasp.org/www-project-web-security-testing-guide/latest/4-Web_Application_Security_Testing/05-Authorization_Testing/02-Testing_for_Bypassing_Authorization_Schema
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/Top10/2021/A05_2021-Security_Misconfiguration/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: This is the clean division of labour for this app. A05 (headers/misconfiguration) is exactly what ZAP automates and what you should let it own. Horizontal privilege escalation across tenants -- the stated highest-consequence failure mode -- is WSTG-ATHZ-02 and must be a hand-written integration test; buying a bigger scanner will not close it.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The WSTG-ATHZ-02 half is fully supported here. The A05:2021 half is not on this URL -- verified at https://owasp.org/Top10/2021/A05_2021-Security_Misconfiguration/ (note the cited-style /Top10/A05_2021-.../ path only serves a redirect stub): CWEs Mapped 20, Avg Incidence Rate 4.51%, 'The server does not send security headers or directives, or they are not set to secure values.' and 'An automated process to verify the effectiveness of the configurations and settings in all environments.' Add that URL. The editorial aside 'a methodology no unattended scanner performs' is the researcher's own judgement, not something WSTG states.
- Verifier read from the page: "Register or generate two users with identical privileges. Establish and keep two different sessions active (one for each user). For every request, change the relevant parameters and the session identifier from token one to token two and diagnose the responses for each token."

**A8.19** `[yes]` ABP's default tenant resolver chain is CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor, then QueryStringTenantResolveContributor, RouteTenantResolveContributor, HeaderTenantResolveContributor and CookieTenantResolveContributor, all keyed on the name __tenant. DomainTenantResolveContributor is not in the default set and must be added explicitly via options.AddDomainTenantResolver(...). The documentation states the current-user contributor "should always be the first contributor for the security".

- Threshold: `Default order: CurrentUser, QueryString, Route, Header, Cookie; parameter name __tenant; domain resolver not default`
- Source: ABP Documentation - Multi-Tenancy -- https://abp.io/docs/latest/framework/architecture/multi-tenancy
- Cross-reference: https://abp.io/docs/latest/guides/ms-multi-tenant-domain-resolving
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Directly contradicts the stated design assumption that "tenancy resolves from the HTTP Host header ONLY" unless the team has explicitly cleared AbpTenantResolveOptions.TenantResolvers. Neither ABP page documents the insertion index used by AddDomainTenantResolver or whether it removes the other contributors, so this must be verified empirically against the codebase -- which is a five-line test, and is the single most valuable dynamic check available here.

### Where sources disagree

- Spider selection: ZAP's July 2026 blog post says 'We now recommend everyone use the Client Spider when crawling modern web applications' and that packaged scans in NIGHTLY releases already default to it, while the Baseline/Full scan documentation still describes -j as enabling the modern spider generically and --client-spider as forcing it. So the behaviour of -j depends on which image tag you pin. Resolution: pass --client-spider explicitly on the :stable tag rather than relying on -j.
- Whether the ZAP :stable Docker image includes the beta add-ons is not documented. The Docker image page describes stable/bare/weekly/nightly but never states which add-ons each contains, and the packaged-scan docs describe -a as adding ALPHA rules (implying beta is present). Since CORS (40040) and Insecure HTTP Method (90028) are both beta active rules, this determines whether two of your five named DAST targets are covered at all. Resolution: do not assume - read the add-on list in the first scan report and, if needed, install ascanrulesBeta explicitly in an Automation Framework plan.
- Frequency of dynamic testing: NIST IR 8397 2.10 states DAST as a minimum standard with no cadence; SSDF SP 800-218 PW.8.2 treats dynamic testing as an automated-suite item but qualifies penetration testing with 'If resources are available'; the HHS HIPAA NPRM proposes a hard six-month scanning and twelve-month pen-test cadence. These are not reconcilable into a single number, and the only one with a specific figure is not yet law.
- Status of the HIPAA Security Rule NPRM in 2026: the HHS fact sheet itself is dated to the 27 December 2024 proposal and does not report a current status, while secondary sources (Medcurity, Blaze Infosec - both labelled secondary and not authoritative) state that as of mid-2026 the rule remains proposed with comments still under review. I could not find a primary HHS source confirming or denying a final rule, so the 'still proposed' status is marked partial confidence rather than verified.
- ZAP alert severity versus ASVS level: ZAP assigns risk ratings per rule (e.g. Cookie Without SameSite at Low/Medium) with no reference to ASVS levels, while ASVS 5.0.0 assigns those same controls to L1/L2/L3. Neither source claims to map to the other, and the two orderings genuinely disagree - a ZAP 'Low' can be an ASVS L1 requirement and a ZAP 'Medium' can be L2. Any policy that says 'fail the build on ZAP Medium and above' is therefore not the same policy as 'meet ASVS L2', and should not be described as such.
- Nuclei's positioning: ProjectDiscovery's own overview describes it as pinpointing 'exploitable vulnerabilities' with real-world relevance and the OWASP DevSecOps Guideline lists it under DAST tools, while the same overview makes clear it is template/signature-based and the fuzzing-templates repository exists specifically because 'unlike the nuclei-templates project, which focuses on known vulnerabilities, fuzzing templates are designed to discover previously unknown vulnerabilities'. Treating Nuclei as a general web-application scanner for a bespoke app overstates what the default template set does.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- ZAP has not been an OWASP project since August 2023, and the images most tutorials tell you to pull no longer exist. The Core Team left OWASP for the Software Security Project, and since 2024-09-24 the project is branded 'ZAP by Checkmarx'. Practically: any compliance narrative or vendor questionnaire answer saying 'we run OWASP ZAP' is now inaccurate, and copy-pasted workflows referencing owasp/zap2docker-stable will fail - the current images are ghcr.io/zaproxy/zaproxy:stable and :bare.
- A ZAP baseline scan of this application will find almost nothing about the backend, and it will look like it succeeded. The spider crawls a compiled Angular 20 bundle, which contains no <a href> pointing at /api/... - so the entire ~53k-line C# surface is invisible. The check that actually reaches the API is the ZAP API scan driven by ABP's Swagger JSON, using -O to rewrite the host in the spec. Teams routinely ship a green baseline scan and believe their API is covered.
- The Host header is both your tenancy key and the thing ZAP will silently get wrong. In CI the target is something like http://localhost:8080, so every request carries Host: localhost:8080, which resolves to no tenant at all. The scan then exercises only the host-side, tenant-less surface and reports clean. You must give the compose stack a hostname that maps to a seeded tenant and point ZAP at that name, or you are scanning a different application from the one you ship.
- ABP's default tenant resolver chain probably contradicts your stated design. The documented default order is CurrentUser, QueryString, Route, Header, Cookie - all keyed on __tenant - and DomainTenantResolveContributor is NOT in that default set; it has to be added explicitly. If nobody cleared AbpTenantResolveOptions.TenantResolvers, then ?__tenant=<other-guid> may override the Host-derived tenant. ABP's own docs warn that the current-user resolver 'should always be the first contributor for the security'. This is a five-line test with the highest expected value of anything in this document.
- CORS misconfiguration is on everyone's list of 'what DAST catches', but ZAP will not catch it in the configuration you are most likely to run. Rule 40040 'CORS Header' is an ACTIVE rule in the ascanrulesBeta add-on, and it is absent from the release Active Scan Rules add-on. A passive baseline scan cannot raise it at all, and a full scan will only raise it if the beta active rules are installed and enabled in the policy. The same applies to verb tampering: 'Insecure HTTP Method' (90028) is also beta-only, and ASVS puts HTTP-method restriction at L3 (4.1.4) - so 'we are ASVS L2 with default ZAP rules' means zero coverage of two of the five things you named.
- ZAP's own documentation tells you not to fight your OIDC login. It says verbatim that 'Single Sign On systems can be especially hard to work with', that 2FA 'can be even harder', and that 'If you can use a simpler authentication mechanism like HTTP auth or a simple POST form then do that'. Only Keycloak and LemonLDAP::NG have documented ZAP automation solutions - OpenIddict does not. Since ABP's OpenIddict module enables the password grant by default, the cheap path is to mint a token via ROPC against a CI-only tenant and inject it with ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_VALUE: roughly twenty lines of workflow instead of days of browser-auth debugging. The corollary is a finding in its own right - if the password grant is still enabled on the internet-facing server at launch, close that before it becomes someone else's shortcut.
- OpenIddict will refuse to serve your CI stack over plain HTTP. It 'will refuse to serve non-HTTPS requests for security reasons' by default, so the token-minting step of an authenticated scan fails unless DisableTransportSecurityRequirement() is set - and the docs say that requirement 'SHOULD NEVER be disabled in production, even when using a reverse proxy doing TLS termination'. That switch is exactly the kind of thing that gets added to a shared appsettings.json 'temporarily' and reaches production. Scope it to a CI-only configuration source and add an assertion that it is off in the Production environment.
- The window for destructive scanning closes at go-live, and it is open right now. The full active scan submits forms: self-registration, password reset, document upload. On a workers' comp scheduling portal with four self-registering external roles, that means real emails to claimants and providers and junk rows in per-tenant databases. Everything is synthetic today, so the correct sequencing is to run the full scan aggressively in the next few weeks, fix what it finds, and only then demote it to a scheduled ephemeral-stack job. Deferring it until 'after launch' means never running it properly.
- Rendering ZAP results in GitHub's code-scanning UI is paywalled for you. Code scanning on a private repository requires a GitHub Code Security licence. ZAP does emit SARIF 2.1.0 (report template sarif-json) and the Nuclei action has sarif-export, so every tutorial you will find routes through github/codeql-action/upload-sarif - and it will fail on your repo. The free path that works today is the ZAP actions' built-in tracked GitHub issue plus the uploaded report artifact. Decide this before writing the workflow.
- There is no authoritative answer to 'how often'. NIST IR 8397 2.10 mandates DAST for anything providing a web service but states no frequency; SSDF PW.8.2 hedges penetration testing with 'if resources are available'; the only concrete cadence anywhere is the HIPAA NPRM's proposed six-monthly vulnerability scanning and annual penetration testing - and that rule is still proposed, not final. The useful reframe: a nightly GitHub Actions job produces the artifact trail that satisfies a six-month cadence at essentially zero marginal cost, so build it now as cheap insurance rather than waiting to be told to.
- ASVS chapter numbers moved under you between 4.0.3 and 5.0.0. Cookie requirements migrated from V3.4 (Session Management) into V3.3 (Web Frontend Security), and security headers from V14.4 into V3.4. Every tool mapping, checklist and blog post citing 'ASVS V14.4.3' or 'V3.4.1' means something different depending on which version it was written against. If you are going to cite ASVS in a compliance document, pin the version string (v5.0.0) next to every requirement ID.
- ZAP's severity does not map to ASVS level, so gating on ZAP risk ratings gates you on the wrong things. ZAP will flag missing SameSite and missing CSP (both ASVS L2) at Medium or Low, while the L1 requirement 3.4.2 - that Access-Control-Allow-Origin be a fixed value rather than reflected - can pass silently because the rule that tests it does not run in a passive scan. Choose what to gate on from ASVS levels, and use ZAP to tell you whether the gate is actually holding.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Commercial DAST (Burp Suite Enterprise, Invicti, Veracode Dynamic, StackHawk). At a larger company these are standard because someone owns tuning and triage full-time. Here the licence cost is the small part; the real cost is that a higher-recall scanner produces proportionally more findings, and with roughly one developer-week per month there is no capacity to triage them. ZAP's release passive rules already cover the entire ASVS V3.3/V3.4 control set that constitutes the realistic yield for this app.
- Authenticated full active scan on every pull request. The ZAP full scan has no default spider time limit and performs real attacks; a PR check that takes 30-90 minutes and occasionally fails on a timing-dependent alert will be marked non-required within two sprints. Merge gates must be measured in seconds. Keep active scanning on a schedule.
- Gating merges on raw ZAP alert output at all. ZAP 2.17.0's headline features are alert de-duplication and 'Systemic' alert flagging, which is the project acknowledging that alert volume was the main obstacle to CI use. Gate on the deterministic header-contract and tenant-isolation tests instead, and let ZAP be the advisory net that tells you which new assertions to write.
- Running DAST separately against all 11 (eventually 33) tenant databases. The database-per-tenant topology tempts you into an 11-way scan matrix. The isolation property is the same code path for every tenant; one scanned tenant plus the targeted tenant-isolation probe covers it, at 1/11th the CI minutes and 1/11th the triage.
- IAST or RASP agents (Contrast, Datadog ASM and similar). These require someone to own runtime instrumentation, agent upgrades and the performance envelope on every deployment. With no DevOps and no security specialist, an agent that silently stops reporting is worse than no agent.
- Authoring Nuclei fuzzing/DAST templates for this application. The -dast mode and the fuzzing-templates repository are real and maintained, but writing custom fuzzing templates for a bespoke ABP API is security-researcher work measured in weeks. Use Nuclei's off-the-shelf exposure templates and stop there.
- Buying a GitHub Code Security licence solely to render ZAP SARIF in the code-scanning UI. Code scanning on a private repo requires that licence, but the ZAP actions already write a tracked GitHub issue and upload the HTML/JSON report as an artifact for free. Revisit only if you are buying the licence for other reasons.
- A formal DAST programme wrapper: findings-SLA policy documents, a security-champion rotation, a triage board, severity-negotiation ceremonies. For two people, the triage process is 'read the weekly issue on Monday'. Process artefacts here consume the budget the actual checks need.
- Continuous DAST against production. Standard at scale with a dedicated read-only scanning identity and rate limits; here it would submit forms on a live workers' comp portal, generating real emails to claimants and providers and junk rows in tenant databases. Production gets a passive, non-spidering header/TLS check only.
- A recurring quarterly external penetration-test programme. The HIPAA NPRM's 12-month pen-test cadence is proposed, not final, and NIST IR 8397 is explicit that a pen test is humans, not a scanner - so it is neither a CI activity nor a current legal requirement. Whether to commission a single pre-launch review is a separate, non-CI decision; a recurring programme is not affordable yet.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`security / response-header-contract`** -- tool: xUnit + Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing (WebApplicationFactory), .NET 10

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 4-6 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A small set of xUnit integration tests using WebApplicationFactory that assert the exact response headers and Set-Cookie attributes on (a) an anonymous GET, (b) an authenticated API GET, (c) a 401 and (d) a 500: Strict-Transport-Security present with a max-age, X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, Content-Security-Policy present including frame-ancestors, Referrer-Policy present, no Server/X-Powered-By version leak, and every auth/antiforgery cookie carrying Secure + HttpOnly + SameSite. Plus an assertion that Access-Control-Allow-Origin is never reflected from an arbitrary Origin header.
- Why at this team size: This is the move that makes the rest sustainable. It encodes ASVS 3.3.1-3.3.4 and 3.4.1-3.4.6 as deterministic, zero-false-positive, sub-second tests you can gate merges on, so ZAP never has to be a required check. A scanner that blocks merges gets bypassed within a month; a unit test that blocks merges does not. It also runs on every PR without standing up any environment.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party Microsoft test host shipped with the ASP.NET Core version already in use (.NET 10); no third-party dependency to age out.

**`security / tenant-isolation-probe`** -- tool: xUnit integration test (or a Playwright/HttpClient script) run against the ephemeral docker-compose stack

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 6-10 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A scripted API test that takes a valid token for tenant A and replays the same authenticated request while varying, one at a time: ?__tenant=<tenant-B-guid>, the __tenant request header, the __tenant cookie, a route segment, and a spoofed Host header for tenant B. Asserts that every variant either errors or returns tenant A's data unchanged - never tenant B's. Also asserts an unauthenticated request with a tenant-B Host cannot enumerate tenant A.
- Why at this team size: Cross-tenant leakage is the stated highest-consequence failure, and it is precisely the thing DAST cannot find - WSTG-ATHZ-02 requires two concurrent identical-privilege sessions with per-request token swapping. ABP's default resolver chain includes QueryString/Route/Header/Cookie __tenant contributors that must be explicitly removed for the 'Host header only' claim to hold. This is a day of work that directly tests the assumption the whole architecture rests on, and it costs nothing per run thereafter.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Built on the same first-party .NET test host as above; no external tool to track.

**`dast / zap-baseline`** -- tool: zaproxy/action-baseline (ghcr.io/zaproxy/zaproxy:stable, ZAP 2.17.0)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 6-10 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Brings up the app via docker-compose in the job, waits for health, then runs the passive ZAP baseline scan (spider + passive rules, no attacks) against the Angular origin and the API origin with --client-spider. Publishes the HTML/JSON report as an artifact and maintains a single GitHub issue with the findings.
- Why at this team size: Minutes per run, structurally incapable of writing data, and it catches the whole A05 header/cookie/information-disclosure class across every page the spider reaches - including pages your header-contract tests forgot. Keep it advisory with fail_action:false and a checked-in -c rules file: the WARN-by-default exit code is the single most common reason small teams disable ZAP. Run it on merges to main and nightly, not on every PR.
- Tool maintenance evidence: action-baseline latest release v0.15.0; repository last committed 2026-08-12 - https://github.com/zaproxy/action-baseline/commits/master . ZAP stable 2.17.0 released 2025-12-15 - https://www.zaproxy.org/blog/2025-12-15-zap-2-17-0/

**`dast / zap-api-scan-authenticated`** -- tool: zaproxy/action-api-scan (or a plain docker run of zap-api-scan.py for finer env-var control)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 12-20 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Mints an access token against the CI stack's OpenIddict server using a seeded test user in a CI-only tenant, exports it as ZAP_AUTH_HEADER_VALUE, then runs the ZAP API scan against the ABP Swagger/OpenAPI JSON (-f openapi, -O to rewrite the spec host to the compose service). This active-scans the real backend endpoints with a valid bearer token.
- Why at this team size: This is the only check that actually reaches the ~53k lines of C# with authentication attached, and it sidesteps the hard problem entirely: no browser-based auth, no OIDC redirect choreography, no session management config. ZAP's own docs recommend exactly this shortcut over fighting SSO. Budget the 12-20 hours mostly for the compose stack and the token-minting step, not for ZAP. Run weekly plus on demand; do not gate merges on it.
- Tool maintenance evidence: action-api-scan latest release v0.10.0; repository last updated 2026-08-12 per https://github.com/orgs/zaproxy/repositories?q=action . Auth env vars documented at https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/getting-further/authentication/handling-auth-yourself/

**`dast / zap-full-scan-weekly`** -- tool: zaproxy/action-full-scan

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 4-6 (once the compose stack from the baseline check exists) | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Weekly scheduled full active scan (spider + client spider + full active scan rules, plus the beta active rules so CORS rule 40040 and Insecure HTTP Method 90028 actually run) against a freshly-built ephemeral docker-compose stack with synthetic data only. fail_action:false; report published as an artifact.
- Why at this team size: Almost free once the ephemeral stack exists, and it is the only ZAP mode that catches CORS reflection, verb tampering and injection classes. It must never touch anything with real data - the full scan submits forms, so on this app that means self-registrations, password-reset emails to real addresses and junk rows in tenant databases. Run it hard in the next few weeks while everything is synthetic, then leave it on a weekly schedule against the ephemeral stack forever.
- Tool maintenance evidence: action-full-scan latest release v0.13.0; repository last updated 2026-08-12 per https://github.com/orgs/zaproxy/repositories?q=action

**`dast / nuclei-exposures-weekly`** -- tool: projectdiscovery/nuclei-action (nuclei v3.11.1, MIT)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 3-5 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Weekly Nuclei run against the ephemeral stack restricted to the exposure and misconfiguration template sets (-t http/exposures -t http/misconfiguration -t ssl, plus -severity medium,high,critical) to catch exposed /swagger, /hangfire, .git, .env, backup files, stack-trace pages and default-credential panels.
- Why at this team size: Cheap to add and near-silent, which is the point: Nuclei is signature-based and a bespoke ABP app has no CVEs of its own, so expect few findings. Its actual value here is the exposure set, which is where a first internet exposure typically goes wrong. Do not scope it to the full 12,000-template default set and do not gate on it - a broad Nuclei run is a triage tax you cannot afford.
- Tool maintenance evidence: nuclei dev branch last committed 2026-08-26 - https://github.com/projectdiscovery/nuclei/commits/dev ; latest release v3.11.1; nuclei-templates MIT with ~12,000 files - https://github.com/projectdiscovery/nuclei-templates

**`security / external-tls-and-headers-monthly`** -- tool: testssl.sh 3.2 branch + zaproxy/action-baseline in passive mode

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: After go-live, a monthly scheduled job that runs testssl.sh plus a read-only ZAP baseline (spider disabled, -m 0, passive rules only) against the real public hostname, to confirm that what the edge actually serves matches what CI proved - HSTS max-age, TLS versions and cipher suites, certificate chain, and the absence of version-leaking headers.
- Why at this team size: Two hours for the only check that observes the thing users actually connect to. What the CI stack serves over plain HTTP tells you nothing about the production TLS terminator. One line on the dependency: whatever terminates TLS in production is an infrastructure decision covered by the separate exercise; this check only verifies the result, it does not choose it. Keep it advisory and monthly - a failing external check is an ops ticket, not a merge blocker.
- Tool maintenance evidence: testssl.sh 3.2 branch last committed 2026-08-28 (CA store update 2026/08) - https://github.com/testssl/testssl.sh/commits/3.2



## 9. OWASP ASVS and Top 10

**Verification verdict for this area: `minor-corrections`** (16 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- Claim 14 states A03:2025 has 'the highest average incidence rate of any 2025 category at 5.72%'. The A03 page's own prose says 'this category has the highest average incidence rate at 5.19%' while its factors table says 5.72% Avg Incidence Rate. The claim welds the 'highest' sentence to the wrong number. Additionally, OWASP's own Introduction page says A03 has 5 CWEs while the A03 page table says 6 CWEs Mapped -- the '6 CWEs' figure is defensible but OWASP contradicts itself, so it should not be asserted as settled.
- Claim 2 says '246 excluding the 7 non-applicable WebRTC L1/L2 requirements'. V17 WebRTC contains ZERO L1 requirements: its 12 requirements are 7x L2 and 5x L3. The arithmetic (253-7=246) is right but the 'L1/L2' framing is wrong -- all 7 excluded are L2.
- Six claims (1, 2, 7, 11, 12, 14) cite a URL that does not actually contain the stated threshold. The numbers are correct but are sourced from a different page than the one cited: ASVS counts are in the CSV not the Frontispiece; the 4.0.3 healthcare wording is in 4.0/en/0x03-Using-ASVS.md not the 5.0 file; Top 10 methodology stats are in 0x00_2025-Introduction not the /Top10/2025/ index; 'AllowedHosts: *' in the default template is not on the Kestrel host-filtering page; ABP's Insert-vs-Clear behavior is not in the ABP docs; NuGet audit defaults are not on the A03 page. Each needs its citation swapped before this goes into a standard.
- Claim 11's example AllowedHosts value 'office1.example.com;office2.example.com' does not appear on the cited Kestrel page -- that page's example is 'example.com;localhost'. The format rule (semicolon-delimited hostnames without ports) IS verbatim on the page; only the example is imported from elsewhere.
- Claim 12 asserts AddDomainTenantResolver works by 'TenantResolvers.Insert(1, ...) -- Insert, not Clear'. The ABP docs page shows only options.AddDomainTenantResolver("{0}.mydomain.com") and options.TenantResolvers.Add(...) for custom resolvers; it never shows Insert or documents the internal implementation. This is a source-code fact stated as a documented one.

### Standard, in summary

The current standard is OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 (May 2025) -- a full rewrite, 345 requirements across 17 chapters, not the 4.0.3 many teams still cite. Level 2 is the level to target: ASVS 5.0 says "Most applications should be striving to achieve this level," and 4.0.3 named healthcare information explicitly at L2 (with "sensitive medical data" at L3). L1+L2 is 253 requirements; drop the 7 WebRTC ones and you have 246 in scope. That is far beyond one dev-week/month, and OWASP itself says the way through is not a scanner: "testable using automation != running an off the shelf tool." The good news for this team is that the chapter that governs their highest-consequence failure mode is tiny -- V8 Authorization has only 7 requirements at L1+L2, and exactly one (V8.4.1) is the multi-tenant cross-tenant control. The OWASP Top 10:2025 is final ("We have released the OWASP Top 10:2025 (Final)"), with two new categories (A03 Software Supply Chain Failures, A10 Mishandling of Exceptional Conditions) and SSRF folded into A01. For this app the load-bearing four are A01 Broken Access Control (multi-tenant authz, #1, "100% of the applications tested were found to have some form of broken access control"), A02 Security Misconfiguration (Host filtering, headers), A07 Authentication Failures (self-registration, password reset), and A03 (dependency/supply chain). Notably, ASVS 5.0 has almost no Host-header-specific requirement -- the closest is V4.1.3 -- so the Host-header risk is covered by WSTG-INPV-17 and by framework defaults (ASP.NET Core host filtering is off by default; ABP registers four non-Host tenant resolvers by default) rather than by an ASVS ID.

### Claims and sources

**A9.1** `[partially]` The current OWASP ASVS release is version 5.0.0, dated May 2025 -- a major rewrite, not an update to 4.0.3. It contains 345 requirements across 17 chapters (V1 Encoding/Sanitization through V17 WebRTC). Level distribution is exactly L1=70, L2=183, L3=92.

- Threshold: `5.0.0, May 2025; 345 requirements; 17 chapters; L1=70 / L2=183 / L3=92`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0 Frontispiece + official 5.0.0 requirements CSV (counts computed from the CSV) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x01-Frontispiece.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/docs_en/OWASP_Application_Security_Verification_Standard_5.0.0_en.csv
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Frontispiece states verbatim 'Version 5.0.0, May 2025'. The OWASP project page cross-confirms release on 30 May 2025 at Global AppSec EU Barcelona. I downloaded the official CSV and parsed it with Python: 345 rows, level counts as stated. The narrative doc says 'around 350 requirements ... 17 chapters', consistent. Note the 4.0.3 chapter numbering (V1-V14) does NOT map to 5.0 numbering -- V8 is Authorization in 5.0 but was Data Protection in 4.0.3, so any internal doc citing 'ASVS V8' needs to state the version.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Every number is CORRECT -- I parsed the official CSV: 345 rows, Counter({'2':183,'3':92,'1':70}), 17 chapters V1 Encoding and Sanitization ... V17 WebRTC. But the cited Frontispiece contains ONLY 'Version 5.0.0, May 2025'; it has no requirement count, chapter count, or level distribution. Cite https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/docs_en/OWASP_Application_Security_Verification_Standard_5.0.0_en.csv for the counts.
- Verifier read from the page: "Version 5.0.0, May 2025"

**A9.2** `[partially]` ASVS 5.0 does not name healthcare when defining levels; it gives generic risk-based guidance and says Level 2 is what most applications should target (~70% of all requirements once L1 is included). ASVS 4.0.3 DID name healthcare -- at Level 2 for 'healthcare information' but Level 3 for 'sensitive medical data'. For a PHI app, L2 is the defensible target and L3 is the arguable one.

- Threshold: `Target ASVS L2 = 253 requirements (L1 70 + L2 183); 246 excluding the 7 non-applicable WebRTC L1/L2 requirements. L1 alone is ~20% of ASVS; L1+L2 is ~70%.`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0 'What is the ASVS' + OWASP ASVS 4.0.3 'Using the ASVS' -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x03-What-is-the-ASVS.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/v4.0.3_release/4.0/en/0x03-Using-ASVS.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: 5.0 verbatim: L1 'contains the minimum requirements ... around 20% of the ASVS requirements'; L2 'Most applications should be striving to achieve this level of security', requiring 'roughly 70% of the requirements in the ASVS (all of the L1 and L2 requirements)'; selection guidance is 'analyze its risks and decide what level it believes it should be at, depending on the sensitivity of the application', illustrated with startup->L1 and bank->L3. 4.0.3 verbatim: L2 is 'typically appropriate for applications that handle significant business-to-business transactions, including those that process healthcare information'; L3 is for applications that 'contain sensitive medical data'. The two editions genuinely conflict on where PHI lands -- see disagreements.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** 5.0 half confirmed: L1 'contains around 20% of the ASVS requirements', L1+L2 ~70%, and no healthcare/medical/industry mention anywhere on the page. 70+183=253 is right. TWO fixes: (a) V17 WebRTC has 0 L1 and 7 L2 requirements, so say 'the 7 non-applicable WebRTC L2 requirements', not 'L1/L2'; (b) the 4.0.3 healthcare evidence is NOT on this URL -- cite https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/4.0/en/0x03-Using-ASVS.md, which says L2 is 'typically appropriate for applications that ... process healthcare information' and L3 is for apps that 'contain sensitive medical data'.
- Verifier read from the page: "Most applications should be striving to achieve this level of security. Around 50% of the requirements in the ASVS are L2 meaning that an application needs to implement around 70% of the requirements in the ASVS (all of the L1 and L2 requirements) in order to comply with L2."

**A9.3** `[yes]` OWASP explicitly warns that 'automatable' ASVS requirements still require custom, application-specific tests -- buying a scanner does not satisfy them. Automated tools cannot verify business logic or access control requirements.

- Threshold: `n/a (qualitative, but it is the governing rule for scoping CI work)`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0, Assessment and Certification -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x04-Assessment_and_Certification.md

Cross-referenced against A01:2025 prevention guidance, which says to 'Include access control testing in unit and integration tests' -- i.e. OWASP's own answer for access control is your test suite, not a scanner.

**A9.4** `[yes]` ASVS V8 Authorization -- the chapter that governs cross-tenant leakage -- is only 13 requirements, of which 7 are L1/L2. V8.4.1 is the single explicit multi-tenancy requirement in all of ASVS 5.0.

- Threshold: `V8.4.1 (L2): 'Verify that multi-tenant applications use cross-tenant controls to ensure consumer operations will never affect tenants with which they do not have permissions to interact.' V8 level split: L1=4, L2=3, L3=6.`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0, V8 Authorization -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x17-V8-Authorization.md

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: 'tenant' appears exactly once in the entire 345-requirement CSV -- at V8.4.1. Grepped the official CSV to confirm. This is the single most important scoping fact in this area: the whole multi-tenancy standard is one requirement, and it is testable.

**A9.5** `[yes]` The specific ASVS 5.0 authorization requirements that are directly automatable in CI for this app are V8.2.1 (function-level, L1), V8.2.2 (data-specific / IDOR / BOLA, L1), V8.2.3 (field-level / BOPLA, L2), V8.3.1 (enforce at a trusted service layer, L1) and V8.4.1 (cross-tenant, L2). V8.1.1/V8.1.2 are documentation requirements and are prerequisites -- you cannot test 8.2.x without them.

- Threshold: `V8.2.1, V8.2.2, V8.2.3, V8.3.1, V8.4.1 (5 testable) + V8.1.1 (L1) and V8.1.2 (L2) as documentation prerequisites`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0, V8 Authorization -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x17-V8-Authorization.md

The Authorization Testing Automation Cheat Sheet gives the mechanical pattern: an authorization matrix of roles x services with an expected HTTP status per (role, service) pair, one test case 'by Point Of View (POV)', executed as integration tests. Its worked example is Java/JUnit + a reference repo (github.com/righettod/poc-authz-testing) -- the pattern ports to xUnit trivially; the tooling does not.

**A9.6** `[yes]` OWASP API Security explicitly directs teams to gate deployment on authorization tests: 'Write tests to evaluate the vulnerability of the authorization mechanism. - Threshold: `API1:2023 Broken Object Level Authorization`
