Also prescribes 'Prefer the use of random and unpredictable values as GUIDs for records' IDs' -- relevant since ABP entities are already GUID-keyed, so that one is likely already satisfied. I could not find a post-2023 API Top 10 edition; the OWASP API Security project page does not advertise one.

SSRF is no longer a standalone category -- it is absorbed into A01.

Note that same Introduction later says 'we only pick eight of the ten categories from the data ... The other two categories are from the Top 10 community survey' -- OWASP's own text is loose here, so quote the Methodology paragraph specifically. Also use OWASP's exact A09 name: 'Security Logging & Alerting Failures'.

**A9.8** `[yes]` A01:2025 Broken Access Control is #1 and is the category that owns multi-tenant authorization, IDOR and (now) SSRF. OWASP's own prevention list tells you to put access control in your test suite.

- Threshold: `40 CWEs mapped; average incidence rate 3.74%; 1,839,701 occurrences; 32,654 CVEs; '100% of the applications tested were found to have some form of broken access control'; CWE-918 (SSRF) and CWE-352 (CSRF) now sit under A01`
- Source: OWASP Top 10:2025, A01:2025 Broken Access Control -- https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/

The IDOR description -- 'Permitting viewing or editing someone else's account by providing its unique identifier' -- is exactly the cross-tenant scenario when the identifier belongs to another office's database.

**A9.9** `[yes]` A07:2025 Authentication Failures directly governs self-registration and password reset. OWASP's prevention list names identical error messages during registration as the anti-enumeration control.

- Threshold: `36 CWEs mapped; max incidence 15.80%; average incidence 2.92%; 1,120,673 occurrences; 7,147 CVEs; rank unchanged from #7 in 2021. Named CWEs include CWE-287, CWE-384 (session fixation), CWE-798/CWE-259 (hardcoded credentials).`
- Source: OWASP Top 10:2025, A07:2025 Authentication Failures -- https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Prevention items relevant to four self-registering external roles: MFA, checking passwords against known-breached lists, no default credentials, NIST 800-63b-aligned password policy rather than forced rotation, server-side session management with high-entropy IDs, and 'Identical error messages during registration to prevent account enumeration'. Corresponding ASVS IDs: V6.2.4 (L1, top 3000 passwords), V6.2.12 (L2, breached-password set), V6.2.1 (L1, min 8 chars, 15 strongly recommended), V6.2.10 (L2, no periodic rotation), V6.4.3 (L2, reset must not bypass MFA), V6.4.2 (L1, no secret questions), V7.4.3 and V7.5.1 (L2, re-auth and session termination on credential change).

**A9.10** `[yes]` ASVS 5.0 has essentially no Host-header requirement. The closest is V4.1.3 (L2): headers set by an intermediary must not be overridable by the end user. Host-header attacks are covered instead by WSTG-INPV-17, which names password reset poisoning, cache poisoning and reaching unintended virtual hosts -- and names X-Forwarded-Host as the standard bypass for naive Host validation.

- Threshold: `ASVS V4.1.3 (L2); WSTG-INPV-17 'Testing for Host Header Injection'. Related WSTG IDs for this app: WSTG-ATHZ-02 (Bypassing Authorization Schema), WSTG-ATHZ-03 (Privilege Escalation), WSTG-ATHZ-04 (Insecure Direct Object References), WSTG-BUSL-08 (Upload of Unexpected File Types), WSTG-BUSL-09 (Upload of Malicious Files).`
- Source: OWASP Web Security Testing Guide, WSTG-INPV-17, plus the WSTG checklist -- https://owasp.org/www-project-web-security-testing-guide/latest/4-Web_Application_Security_Testing/07-Input_Validation_Testing/17-Testing_for_Host_Header_Injection
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/wstg/master/checklists/checklist.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: WSTG verbatim: initial testing is 'supplying another domain (i.e. attacker.com) into the Host header field'; when that is blocked, testers 'bypass controls by supplying the value to the X-Forwarded-Host header'. Grepping the full ASVS 5.0 CSV for 'host header' returns exactly one row -- V4.1.3. I flag this as a gap in ASVS coverage rather than evidence the risk is low: for THIS app the Host header is the tenancy key, so WSTG-INPV-17 is the controlling guidance and there is no ASVS ID to hang a compliance claim on.

**A9.11** `[partially]` ASP.NET Core does not validate the Host header by default. Kestrel 'largely ignores the host name' and 'Host headers aren't validated'; host-filtering middleware is disabled by default, and the default project template ships "AllowedHosts": "*", which accepts any host.

- Threshold: `AllowedHosts must be a semicolon-delimited list of hostnames without ports (e.g. "office1.example.com;office2.example.com"), never "*", in every non-Development appsettings file`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Host filtering with ASP.NET Core Kestrel web server (aspnetcore-10.0) -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/servers/kestrel/host-filtering
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/dotnet/aspnetcore/issues/47862
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim from Microsoft Learn: 'Kestrel largely ignores the host name... Host headers aren't validated.' and 'Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default. To enable the middleware, define an AllowedHosts key'. The page also distinguishes host-filtering AllowedHosts from ForwardedHeadersOptions.AllowedHosts -- you need the right one depending on whether a reverse proxy preserves Host, which is the one infrastructure dependency in this area (see notes on infra). The dotnet/aspnetcore issue thread is a secondary source confirming the template default of '*' is contested by the ASP.NET Core team themselves.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The Kestrel behaviour and the AllowedHosts format rule are verbatim on the page. TWO unsupported bits: (a) the page never says the default project template ships "AllowedHosts": "*" -- that is true but must be cited to the ASP.NET Core configuration/app-startup docs or the template's appsettings.json, not here; (b) the page's own example is "AllowedHosts": "example.com;localhost", not "office1.example.com;office2.example.com" -- use the page's example or drop the quotation marks around yours.
- Verifier read from the page: "Kestrel largely ignores the host name ... `Host` headers aren't validated. ... Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default. To enable the middleware, define an `AllowedHosts` key in `appsettings.json`/`appsettings.{Environment}.json`. The value is a semicolon-delimited list of host names without port numbers"

**A9.12** `[partially]` ABP does NOT resolve tenancy from the Host header only by default. Out of the box ABP registers CurrentUser, QueryString (?__tenant=), Route (/{__tenant}/), Header (__tenant) and Cookie (__tenant) resolvers. DomainTenantResolveContributor is not registered by default and is added by INSERTING into the existing chain, which leaves the other resolvers in place.

- Threshold: `Default AbpTenantResolveOptions.TenantResolvers: CurrentUser, QueryString, Route, Header, Cookie (all default __tenant). AddDomainTenantResolver("{0}.mydomain.com") / TenantResolvers.Insert(1, new DomainTenantResolveContributor(...)) -- Insert, not Clear.`
- Source: ABP.IO Documentation -- Multi-Tenancy (latest) -- https://abp.io/docs/latest/framework/architecture/multi-tenancy
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/docs_en/OWASP_Application_Security_Verification_Standard_5.0.0_en.csv
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: ABP docs verbatim on CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor: 'This should always be the first contributor for the security.' The docs also warn that the IDataFilter/MultiTenant filter approach 'won't work if your tenants have separate databases' -- which is this app's model, so cross-tenant safety rests entirely on which database the connection string resolver picked, i.e. on tenant resolution. The ASVS cross-reference matters here: V14.2.1 (L1) forbids sensitive data in the query string, and ABP's default ?__tenant= puts the tenant selector there. Confidence is 'verified' for the ABP defaults as documented; whether this specific codebase has cleared the resolver list is something the team must check in their own startup configuration -- I had no access to the application repo.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Confirmed on the page: the five default resolvers in order are CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor, QueryStringTenantResolveContributor, RouteTenantResolveContributor, HeaderTenantResolveContributor, CookieTenantResolveContributor, all keyed on __tenant, and DomainTenantResolveContributor is NOT registered by default. NOT supported: the 'TenantResolvers.Insert(1, new DomainTenantResolveContributor(...)) -- Insert, not Clear' detail. The docs show only AddDomainTenantResolver(...) usage and TenantResolvers.Add(...) for custom contributors, and explicitly do not document the internal implementation. Either verify against the AbpTenantResolveOptionsExtensions source in the abp repo and cite that, or restate as 'AddDomainTenantResolver adds the domain resolver without removing the query-string/header/cookie resolvers, so those remain live attack surface -- confirm against source.'
- Verifier read from the page: "Configure<AbpTenantResolveOptions>(options => { options.AddDomainTenantResolver("{0}.mydomain.com"); });"

**A9.13** `[yes]` ASVS 5.0 V5 File Handling gives a concrete, testable contract for the anonymous document-upload surface: 4 L1 and 5 L2 requirements. The L1 core is size limit (5.2.1), extension-plus-content validation including magic bytes (5.2.2), no server-side execution of uploaded files (5.3.1), and internally generated file paths rather than user-submitted filenames (5.3.2).

- Threshold: `V5.2.1 (L1 size), V5.2.2 (L1 magic bytes / content-matches-extension; L1 may scope to security-relevant files, 'For L2 and up, this must apply to all files'), V5.2.3 (L2 archive decompression limits), V5.3.1 (L1 no execution), V5.3.2 (L1 internally generated paths), V5.4.1 (L2 Content-Disposition filename), V5.4.2 (L2 RFC 6266 filename encoding), V5.4.3 (L2 antivirus scan), V5.1.1 (L2 documented permitted types/extensions/max size)`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0, V5 File Handling -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x14-V5-File-Handling.md
- Cross-reference: https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/File_Upload_Cheat_Sheet.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The File Upload Cheat Sheet cross-confirms and adds detail: allowlist extensions ('Only allow safe and critical extensions for business functionality'); 'The Content-Type for uploaded files is provided by the user, and as such cannot be trusted, as it is trivial to spoof'; magic-byte checking 'should not be used on its own'; generate 'a random string as a filename, such as generating a UUID/GUID'; storage preference order is different host > outside webroot > inside webroot with write-only permissions. The OWASP community Unrestricted File Upload page adds 'Content-Disposition: Attachment' and 'X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff' on download, which pairs with ASVS V3.2.1 (L1).

**A9.14** `[partially]` A03:2025 Software Supply Chain Failures is a new Top 10 category and was ranked #1 by 50% of community survey respondents. For a .NET 10 + Yarn 4 stack the first-party audit tooling already covers most of it: NuGet audit is on by default and, for projects targeting net10.0 or higher, NuGetAuditMode now defaults to 'all' (transitive included) rather than 'direct'.

- Threshold: `NuGetAudit default true; NuGetAuditLevel default 'low'; NuGetAuditMode defaults to 'all' for net10.0+ (otherwise 'direct'). Warning codes NU1901 (low) / NU1902 (moderate) / NU1903 (high) / NU1904 (critical) / NU1905 (audit source missing). A03 stats: highest average incidence rate of any 2025 category at 5.72%; 6 CWEs; 215,248 occurrences.`
- Source: OWASP Top 10:2025 A03 + Microsoft Learn 'Auditing package dependencies for security vulnerabilities' -- https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A03_2025-Software_Supply_Chain_Failures/
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Critical detail a two-person team will miss: audit findings are WARNINGS and do not fail a build. Microsoft Learn gives the exact recipe for a dedicated audit pipeline -- set <WarningsAsErrors>$(WarningsAsErrors);NU1901;NU1902;NU1903;NU1904</WarningsAsErrors> only when a property like AuditPipeline=true is passed, and <WarningsNotAsErrors> otherwise, so new advisories never break unrelated PRs. It also gives an MSBuild target asserting RestoreProjectsAuditedCount == RestoreProjectCount so a silently-skipped audit fails loudly. A03's own prevention guidance names 'OWASP Dependency Track and Dependency Check' for continuous inventory, and 'Centrally generate and manage the Software Bill of Materials (SBOM)'.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Three problems. (1) The '5.72%' and 'highest average incidence rate' come from two different places: the factors table reads '6 CWEs Mapped | 9.56% Max Incidence Rate | 5.72% Avg Incidence Rate | 215,248 Total Occurrences | 11 Total CVEs', while the prose says the highest average incidence rate is 5.19%. State it as 'the highest average incidence rate of any 2025 category; the factors table reports 5.72% Avg Incidence Rate (the narrative text cites 5.19%)'. (2) The CWE count is contested inside OWASP's own docs -- the A03 page says 6, the Introduction says 'This category has 5 CWEs'. (3) The NuGet half of the threshold is not on this page at all; it is correct and should be cited to https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages, which gives NuGetAudit default true, NuGetAuditLevel default low, and the footnote 'NuGetAuditMode defaults to all when a project targets net10.0 or higher. Otherwise NuGetAuditMode defaults to direct', with NU1901 low / NU1902 moderate / NU1903 high / NU1904 critical / NU1905 audit source provides no vulnerability database.
- Verifier read from the page: "This was top-ranked in the Top 10 community survey with exactly 50% respondents ranking it #1. ... when tested and reported in the contributed data, this category has the highest average incidence rate at 5.19%."

**A9.15** `[yes]` ASVS 5.0 gives exact numeric/string thresholds for browser security headers and cookies that an integration test can assert byte-for-byte -- and A02:2025 explicitly asks for 'an automated process to verify the effectiveness of the configurations and settings in all environments'.

- Threshold: `V3.4.1 (L1) HSTS max-age >= 1 year, includeSubDomains for L2+; V3.4.3 (L2) CSP must include object-src 'none' AND base-uri 'none' plus an allowlist/nonces/hashes; V3.4.4 (L2) X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff on ALL responses; V3.4.6 (L2) CSP frame-ancestors on every response (X-Frame-Options declared obsolete); V3.3.1 (L1) cookies Secure + __Host-/__Secure- prefix; V3.3.3 (L2) __Host- prefix; V14.3.2 (L2) Cache-Control: no-store on sensitive responses; V14.2.1 (L1) no sensitive data in URL or query string; V16.5.1 (L2) generic error message, no stack traces; V13.4.2 (L2) debug modes disabled`
- Source: OWASP ASVS 5.0 requirements CSV (V3, V13, V14, V16) + OWASP Top 10:2025 A02 -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/docs_en/OWASP_Application_Security_Verification_Standard_5.0.0_en.csv
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A02_2025-Security_Misconfiguration/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: A02 moved from #5 (2021) to #2 (2025); 16 CWEs, 719k+ occurrences, average incidence 3.00%, and '100% of tested applications exhibited some misconfiguration'. Verbatim prevention: 'An automated process to verify the effectiveness of the configurations and settings in all environments'. These are the cheapest ASVS wins in the whole standard for this team -- every one is a single assertion in an ASP.NET Core WebApplicationFactory test, no external tool, no license, no flake.

**A9.16** `[yes]` ASVS 5.0 dropped the CWE and NIST SP 800-63B mappings that 4.0.3 carried, and there is no official ASVS-to-Top-10 mapping file at all. The only CWE/NIST mappings in the repo are archived exports of the pre-release v5.0.be, not of 5.0.0.

- Threshold: `5.0/mappings/ contains only mapping_v5.0.be_to_v5.0.0.yml, mapping_v4.0.3_to_v5.0.0.yml, mapping_v5.0.0_to_v4.0.3.yml, mapping_v4.0.3_to_v5.0.be.yml, mapping_v5.0.be_to_v4.0.3.yml, plus archived nist.md and v5.0.be_cwe_mapping.json`
- Source: OWASP/ASVS repository, 5.0/mappings directory -- https://github.com/OWASP/ASVS/tree/master/5.0/mappings
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/OWASP/ASVS/tree/master/5.0/en
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: Confidence 'partial' because I read the directory listing and the file descriptions rather than parsing each YAML. This matters for HIPAA paperwork: if anyone plans to say 'ASVS requirement X satisfies HIPAA safeguard Y via CWE Z', that traceability chain does not exist for 5.0.0 and would have to be hand-built or taken from the archived v5.0.be export with a caveat. The v4.0.3<->v5.0.0 mappings DO exist, so an old 4.0.3-based checklist can be migrated mechanically.

### Where sources disagree

- ASVS 4.0.3 and ASVS 5.0.0 conflict on which level a PHI application needs. 4.0.3 says Level 2 is 'typically appropriate for applications that handle significant business-to-business transactions, including those that process healthcare information' -- but also says Level 3 is for applications that 'contain sensitive medical data'. A workers' comp medical-examination portal is arguably both. ASVS 5.0.0 removed the healthcare wording entirely and replaced it with generic risk guidance (startup -> L1, bank -> L3, 'Most applications should be striving' for L2). So OWASP's current text does not answer 'which level for PHI', and OWASP's previous text answers it two different ways. My read, stated as a judgement rather than a citation: target L2, and treat the L3 authorization requirements specifically (V8.3.2 immediate effect of permission changes, V8.3.3 originating-subject permissions, V8.4.2 layered admin access) as a deliberate, dated deferral -- because for this app the authorization chapter is where the L3 arguments actually have force, and the rest of L3 does not.
- Release status of the OWASP Top 10:2025 is inconsistently presented. Search engines still index owasp.org/Top10/ with the title 'OWASP Top 10:2025 RC1', and third-party write-ups variously date the release to November 2025 (announced at Global AppSec USA, Washington DC) or January 2026 (final). I resolved this by fetching primary sources: owasp.org/Top10/ now simply redirects to the 2025 edition with no RC1 text; www-project-top-ten states 'The most current released version is the OWASP Top Ten 2025'; and the OWASP/Top10 repository README says 'OWASP Top 10 2025 - RELEASED' and 'We have released the OWASP Top 10:2025 (Final)'. Treat it as final. No OWASP primary page I opened states a calendar release date, so I have not asserted one.
- OWASP's A03:2025 prevention guidance recommends 'OWASP Dependency Track and Dependency Check' for continuous dependency inventory, while Microsoft's first-party guidance points .NET teams at NuGet audit built into restore. For a .NET 10 + Yarn 4 stack these are not equivalent in effort: Dependency-Check needs an NVD API key, a large local cache, and has weaker NuGet and Yarn-Berry support, whereas NuGet audit is already running on every restore. I have recommended the first-party path and am flagging that this diverges from OWASP's named tooling. The divergence is about tooling, not about the requirement -- both satisfy A03's intent.
- OWASP is not internally consistent about how much a scanner buys you. The Top 10:2025 programme guidance says to 'Give your developers security tools... especially static analysis, software composition analysis, secret, and Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) scanners' and to 'Create regular scanning schedules', which reads as tool-first. ASVS 5.0's assessment chapter pushes hard the other way: 'Merely running an automated tool without thorough testing is insufficient' and 'testable using automation != running an off the shelf tool'. For a two-person team these point at different budgets. I have weighted toward ASVS, because the failure mode that matters here (cross-tenant data access) is precisely the class ASVS says tools cannot verify.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The premise 'tenancy resolves from the HTTP Host header ONLY' is very likely false in the running application. ABP registers CurrentUser, QueryString (?__tenant=), Route, Header (__tenant) and Cookie (__tenant) resolvers by default, and AddDomainTenantResolver INSERTS the domain resolver into that chain rather than replacing it. Unless someone explicitly cleared AbpTenantResolveOptions.TenantResolvers, an anonymous request to a registration or upload endpoint carrying ?__tenant=<other-office-guid> may resolve to a different office's database. This is a five-minute thing to check in startup configuration and it is the highest-value five minutes in this whole analysis. Related: ASVS V14.2.1 is an L1 requirement that sensitive data must not appear in the query string -- ABP's default puts the tenant selector exactly there.
- ASP.NET Core will not validate the Host header for you. Microsoft's own words: Kestrel 'largely ignores the host name... Host headers aren't validated', and 'Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default.' The default project template ships "AllowedHosts": "*", which accepts anything. For an app whose entire tenant boundary is the Host header, going public with the template default means the boundary is enforced only by whatever the reverse proxy happens to do -- and if the proxy rewrites Host, you need ForwardedHeadersOptions.AllowedHosts instead, which is a different setting. (This is the one place in this area where the answer genuinely depends on the hosting exercise; assert AllowedHosts != "*" in CI either way.)
- ASVS 5.0 has essentially nothing to say about Host headers. Grepping all 345 requirements for 'host header' returns exactly one hit -- V4.1.3, about intermediary-set headers. And 'tenant' appears exactly once, at V8.4.1. So the two things that define this application's risk profile are covered by two requirements in the entire standard. The real guidance lives in WSTG-INPV-17, not ASVS. Anyone planning to justify the security posture by citing ASVS coverage should know there is almost no ASVS coverage to cite here.
- Password reset poisoning is the concrete way Host-header tenancy bites, and WSTG-INPV-17 names it first: if the reset link is built from the inbound Host header, an attacker sets Host to their own domain, the victim receives a genuine email from your system, clicks, and hands over the token. Worse, WSTG says the standard bypass for naive Host validation is to move the payload to X-Forwarded-Host -- so a check that only validates Host is incomplete. Password reset goes anonymous-facing on day one.
- ASVS 5.0 V16 Security Logging and Error Handling contains ZERO Level 1 requirements -- 16 at L2 and 1 at L3. V13 Configuration has exactly one L1 requirement. So a team that says 'we'll start at L1 and work up' has, by ASVS's own structure, committed to no logging requirements and almost no configuration requirements at all. For a HIPAA-regulated system that needs an audit trail of who accessed which patient record, starting at L1 is not a conservative choice, it is an empty one. Target L2 or the level label means nothing.
- CA2100 -- the .NET analyzer that reviews SQL queries for injection -- is documented as 'Enabled by default in .NET 10: No'. Teams routinely assume the SDK's built-in analyzers cover SQL injection out of the box. They do not. It costs one .editorconfig line to turn on.
- NuGetAuditMode changed its default in .NET 10: it is now 'all' (audits transitive dependencies) for projects targeting net10.0 or higher, where it was 'direct' before. Expect a sudden increase in NU1901-NU1904 warnings that has nothing to do with any code you wrote. Also: those are warnings, and warnings do not fail a build -- an audit that is 'running' in CI may be reporting into a log nobody reads.
- ZAP is not an OWASP project any more. Per zaproxy.org's own ownership page it left OWASP in September 2023 and is now 'an independent Open Source project controlled by the ZAP Core Team and supported by Checkmarx'; the site states plainly that references to 'OWASP ZAP' indicate outdated sources. Separately, OWASP Dependency-Check's long-standing repository (jeremylong/DependencyCheck) was archived on 27 Sep 2025 and moved to dependency-check/DependencyCheck. Both tools are alive and maintained, but any policy document, blog post or LLM answer that says 'run OWASP ZAP and OWASP Dependency-Check' is citing names and URLs that have moved -- and a pinned GitHub Action pointing at an archived repo rots silently.
- SSRF is no longer its own OWASP Top 10 category. It was absorbed into A01:2025 Broken Access Control (CWE-918 is now listed there). Any internal checklist derived from the 2021 list has an A10 that no longer exists, and A10:2025 is now something entirely different -- Mishandling of Exceptional Conditions. Similarly, A06:2021 Vulnerable and Outdated Components has become the much broader A03:2025 Software Supply Chain Failures. Checklists copied from 2021 will silently miss two whole categories.
- OWASP itself says the automation you are being asked to build will not be sufficient, and says why: 'testable using automation != running an off the shelf tool', and automated tools are 'unable entirely to verify many of the more complicated ASVS requirements or those that relate to business logic and access control'. The practical consequence is counter-intuitive and worth stating to whoever is funding this: money spent on scanners will not reduce cross-tenant risk, and days spent writing your own boring cross-tenant integration tests will. The one thing CI genuinely cannot substitute for, per ASVS, is source-code-led human testing before the first real patient record exists.
- ASVS 5.0.0 shipped without the CWE and NIST SP 800-63B mappings that 4.0.3 carried -- the only such files in the repo are archived exports of the pre-release v5.0.be. If anyone intends to build a HIPAA traceability story of the form 'ASVS requirement -> CWE -> safeguard', that chain does not exist for the current version and would have to be hand-built. Conversely, mapping files between 4.0.3 and 5.0.0 DO exist in both directions, so an older 4.0.3 checklist can be migrated mechanically rather than redone.
- The scope of the multi-tenancy problem is much smaller than it feels. ASVS V8 Authorization is 13 requirements total, 7 at L1+L2, and only one of those is specifically about tenancy. The reason this work feels enormous is not the number of requirements -- it is that the one requirement has to hold across every endpoint. That reframing is what makes it tractable for two people: you are not implementing a standard, you are building one table-driven test harness and then adding a row per endpoint forever.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Verifying all 253 ASVS L1+L2 requirements, or pursuing any form of ASVS certification. At one dev-week per month, a 246-requirement checklist (253 minus WebRTC) is roughly a two-year project, and the exercise of grading yourself against every requirement produces a spreadsheet rather than a defence. Do this instead: adopt L2 as the stated target, and formally verify only the ~35 requirements the four merge-gate checks above cover. Write down the other ~210 as accepted-and-untested with a date. A dated, honest gap list is a better artefact for an eventual HIPAA conversation than a half-finished self-assessment.
- The ASVS 'documentation defines...' requirements as a document set (V8.1.1, V8.1.2, V2.1.1-2.1.3, V5.1.1, V6.1.1-6.1.3, V13.1.x, V14.1.1-14.1.2, V16.1.1). A larger company staffs these as security-architecture deliverables with review cycles. Two SDE 1s should not. But do not skip them entirely, because V8.2.x is untestable without V8.1.1: the authorization matrix in check 1 IS your V8.1.1 documentation, and a permitted-types/max-size constant in the upload validator IS your V5.1.1. Let the code be the document. Budget one page total, not a doc set.
- CodeQL / GitHub code scanning. It genuinely covers C# and TypeScript well, but 'If you want to use code scanning on private repositories, you need a GitHub Code Security license', and the tuning burden on a 53k-line ABP codebase (which generates a lot of code) lands on two people who have no security background to judge the findings. The built-in .NET analyzers plus Semgrep OSS (LGPL 2.1, free on private and commercial repos; latest v1.175.0, 26 Aug 2026) cover the realistic subset at zero licence cost. Revisit when there is a third engineer.
- A ZAP full active scan, or any DAST, as a per-PR required gate. Active scanning is slow, order-of-magnitude noisier than baseline, and needs a live environment per PR. On a two-person team the first time it flakes it gets marked continue-on-error and never looked at again, which is worse than not having it. Weekly and non-blocking is the sustainable shape.
- SBOM generation plus a Dependency-Track server. A03:2025 recommends both, and at a company with a platform team they are correct. Here it means standing up and patching a Java web application and its database purely to display information that `dotnet list package --include-transitive` already prints. Revisit when a customer contract demands an SBOM -- which for California workers' comp offices is unlikely soon.
- Per-feature threat modelling, a security champions programme, and a formal secure-SDLC attestation exercise. These are how a 50-person engineering org distributes security knowledge. With two people the knowledge is already distributed. One 90-minute whiteboard session on the tenancy boundary before launch delivers most of the value; the ceremony delivers none.
- A hand-maintained cross-reference from every ASVS 5.0 requirement to CWE and to HIPAA safeguards. ASVS 5.0.0 shipped without the CWE and NIST 800-63B mappings that 4.0.3 had, so you would be building that mapping yourself. Unless a specific auditor asks for it in writing, this is pure paperwork with no effect on whether an office can read another office's data.
- Thirty required checks. The eight above are already at the outer edge of what one dev-week per month sustains, and only five are proposed as blocking gates. If something new has to become a gate, something existing should stop being one -- treat the gate count as a fixed budget, not a growing list.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`cross-tenant-authorization`** -- tool: xUnit + ABP's Volo.Abp.TestBase / WebApplicationFactory (already in the ABP Commercial solution template); pattern from the OWASP Authorization Testing Automation Cheat Sheet

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 16-24h to build the harness and matrix for the first ~15 endpoints; ~10 min per endpoint after | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: An xUnit integration-test suite that, for every API endpoint that touches tenant-scoped data, authenticates as a user in Tenant A and requests a resource ID belonging to Tenant B, asserting 404/403 and asserting the response body contains no Tenant B data. Driven by a checked-in authorization matrix (role x endpoint x expected status) so adding an endpoint means adding a row, not writing a test. Directly verifies ASVS V8.2.1, V8.2.2, V8.2.3, V8.3.1 and V8.4.1.
- Why at this team size: This is the only check on the list that addresses the stated highest-consequence failure mode, and OWASP API1:2023 says in so many words 'Write tests to evaluate the vulnerability of the authorization mechanism. Do not deploy changes that make the tests fail.' It is also the one thing no purchasable scanner will do for you -- ASVS: 'testable using automation != running an off the shelf tool.' With database-per-tenant, a leak means one office reading another office's medical exams, and ABP's row-level MultiTenant data filter explicitly does not protect you here. If you build exactly one thing from this whole area, build this. Table-driven design keeps the marginal cost near zero so it does not become the thing you bypass.
- Tool maintenance evidence: No third-party tool to keep alive -- this uses the test stack ABP Commercial 10.0.2 already ships. The pattern source (OWASP Authorization Testing Automation Cheat Sheet) is live on cheatsheetseries.owasp.org as of 2026-08-28: https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Authorization_Testing_Automation_Cheat_Sheet.html

**`tenancy-resolution-invariants`** -- tool: xUnit + WebApplicationFactory with a custom Host header, plus a ~20-line config-lint step (grep/jq over appsettings*.json) in the same workflow

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 6-10h | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A small, fast integration-test class asserting the tenancy contract: (1) an unknown/attacker Host header is rejected, not silently mapped to a default tenant; (2) ?__tenant=, the __tenant header, the __tenant cookie and a __tenant route segment do NOT change the resolved tenant when the Host says otherwise; (3) X-Forwarded-Host does not override Host; (4) a password-reset email link is built from configuration, not from the inbound Host header; (5) AllowedHosts is not "*" in any non-Development appsettings file. Covers WSTG-INPV-17 and ASVS V4.1.3.
- Why at this team size: Cheap, fast, and it defends the exact assumption the whole security model rests on. Two documented framework defaults work against you: Kestrel 'largely ignores the host name... Host headers aren't validated' and host filtering is off unless AllowedHosts is set; and ABP registers query-string, route, header and cookie __tenant resolvers by default, with AddDomainTenantResolver inserting into that chain rather than replacing it. So 'tenancy resolves from the Host header only' is a claim about your configuration, not about your framework -- and the first day on the public internet is the wrong time to find out it is false. Test 4 (password reset link) is the specific attack WSTG-INPV-17 calls out and the one most likely to be live right now.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party only: ASP.NET Core 10 host filtering documented and current on Microsoft Learn (page updated 2026-07-22, moniker aspnetcore-10.0): https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/servers/kestrel/host-filtering ; ABP resolver behaviour documented at https://abp.io/docs/latest/framework/architecture/multi-tenancy

**`upload-contract`** -- tool: xUnit + WebApplicationFactory against the MinIO-backed storage provider (Testcontainers or the existing dev MinIO); a fixture folder of ~8 crafted malicious sample files checked into the repo

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 8-12h including building the malicious-sample fixtures | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Integration tests over the anonymous document-upload endpoint asserting the ASVS V5 contract: oversized file rejected (V5.2.1); a .pdf whose bytes are a PHP/HTML payload rejected on magic-byte check (V5.2.2); stored filename is a server-generated GUID, not the client's (V5.3.2); a traversal filename (..%2f, double extension, null byte) does not escape the storage prefix; retrieving the file returns Content-Disposition: attachment and X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff (V5.4.1, V3.2.1); the storage path is not directly reachable as executable content (V5.3.1). Covers WSTG-BUSL-08 and WSTG-BUSL-09.
- Why at this team size: Anonymous upload is the single most dangerous new surface being exposed, and unlike most security work the acceptance criteria are fully mechanical -- nine ASVS requirements, each a one-line assertion. Right now is also the only moment this is free to test destructively: all data is synthetic. The one requirement here you should NOT try to satisfy in CI is V5.4.3 (antivirus scanning) -- that is a runtime service, not a build step; assert in CI that the scan hook is invoked and mocked-failure blocks the upload, and leave the real scanner to the infrastructure exercise.
- Tool maintenance evidence: No external scanner required. Guidance sources verified live 2026-08-28: OWASP ASVS 5.0 V5 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x14-V5-File-Handling.md) and the OWASP File Upload Cheat Sheet (https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/File_Upload_Cheat_Sheet.html), both current in the maintained OWASP repos.

**`security-headers-and-config`** -- tool: xUnit + WebApplicationFactory (no external tool, no license)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 4-6h | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: One test class asserting exact header values on representative anonymous and authenticated responses: HSTS max-age >= 31536000 with includeSubDomains; CSP present containing object-src 'none' and base-uri 'none' and a frame-ancestors directive; X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff on every response; auth cookies Secure with __Host- or __Secure- prefix; Cache-Control: no-store on PHI-bearing responses; a forced 500 returns a generic body with no stack trace; no PHI or tokens in any query string. Covers ASVS V3.3.1, V3.3.3, V3.4.1, V3.4.3, V3.4.4, V3.4.6, V14.2.1, V14.3.2, V16.5.1, V13.4.2.
- Why at this team size: Ten ASVS requirements for half a day, with zero ongoing maintenance and zero false positives -- the best ratio in this entire area. A02:2025 (now the #2 risk, up from #5) asks explicitly for 'an automated process to verify the effectiveness of the configurations and settings in all environments', and this is literally that. It also catches the failure mode where someone adds a CDN or an iframe embed for a demo and quietly weakens CSP six months from now.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Thresholds taken from the ASVS 5.0.0 requirements CSV downloaded 2026-08-28 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/docs_en/OWASP_Application_Security_Verification_Standard_5.0.0_en.csv), the current stable ASVS release. No tool dependency to rot.

**`secrets-scan`** -- tool: gitleaks (GitHub Action), with a checked-in .gitleaks.toml allowlist for known-safe synthetic values

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-4h including the initial full-history scan and building the allowlist | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Scans the diff on every PR (and the full history once, before going public) for committed credentials: SQL Server connection strings, OpenIddict signing/encryption certificates and keys, MinIO access keys, Redis passwords, ABP Commercial NuGet feed tokens. Supports ASVS V13.3.1 ('Secrets must not be included in application source code or included in build artifacts').
- Why at this team size: Two hours, and it is the check that stops a whole class of incident that a two-person team with no security review is unusually exposed to -- nobody is reading your diffs but you. Do the full-history scan once before the app faces the internet: if a credential is already in history, rotating it after launch is a different and much worse conversation. Keep the allowlist tight; a noisy secrets gate is the first one people learn to force-merge past.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Actively maintained: latest release v8.30.1 (21 Mar 2026), preceded by v8.30.0 (26 Nov 2025) and v8.29.1 (19 Nov 2025) -- https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases (checked 2026-08-28). Chosen over GitHub's own secret scanning because that requires a GitHub Code Security licence on private repositories: 'If you want to use code scanning on private repositories, you need a GitHub Code Security license' (https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/code-scanning/introduction-to-code-scanning/about-code-scanning).

**`dependency-audit`** -- tool: Built-in NuGet audit (.NET 10 SDK) and Yarn 4's `yarn npm audit` -- no third-party scanner

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 3-5h | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Runs `dotnet restore` with NuGet audit warnings promoted to errors under a dedicated pipeline flag (-p:AuditPipeline=true), plus `yarn npm audit --all --recursive --severity high`, plus the MSBuild assertion that RestoreProjectsAuditedCount equals RestoreProjectCount so a skipped audit fails loudly. Addresses A03:2025 Software Supply Chain Failures.
- Why at this team size: Run this nightly on main and let it open an issue, NOT on every PR. A new advisory published against a transitive dependency has nothing to do with the PR in front of you, and blocking an unrelated bugfix on it is precisely how a two-person team learns to bypass gates -- Microsoft's own docs endorse this split, and A03's guidance is to 'Create regular scanning schedules'. One .NET 10 specific warning: NuGetAuditMode now defaults to 'all' (transitive) for net10.0+ where it used to be 'direct', so expect a one-off flood of new warnings and budget a couple of hours to triage them before you make anything fail.
- Tool maintenance evidence: NuGet audit is first-party and current: Microsoft Learn page updated 2026-07-07, and NuGet 7.0 / .NET 10 SDK changed the NuGetAuditMode default (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages). `yarn npm audit` documented for Yarn 4 with -A/--all, -R/--recursive, --severity and non-zero exit on findings (https://yarnpkg.com/cli/npm/audit). I deliberately did NOT recommend OWASP Dependency-Check: its long-standing repo (jeremylong/DependencyCheck) carries the notice 'This repository was archived by the owner on Sep 27, 2025. It is now read-only.' and redirects to dependency-check/DependencyCheck -- it is alive but moved, and it adds an NVD API key, a large local cache and weak NuGet/Yarn-Berry support for no gain over the first-party tooling here.

**`dotnet-security-analyzers`** -- tool: .NET SDK built-in code analysis (Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.NetAnalyzers, ships with the .NET 10 SDK)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-6h (mostly triaging the initial findings across ~53k lines of C#) | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Enables the .NET security CA rules as build errors via .editorconfig / <AnalysisMode>, notably CA2100 (SQL injection review), covering ASVS V1.2.4 (L1, parameterised queries) and A05:2025 Injection at compile time with no separate scan step.
- Why at this team size: Nearly free to turn on and it runs inside a build you already do, so it costs no CI minutes. Start advisory (warnings) and promote the specific rules to errors once the backlog is at zero, rather than landing a red build on day one. The catch worth knowing: CA2100 is documented as NOT enabled by default in .NET 10, so the common assumption that 'the SDK analyzers already cover SQL injection' is wrong out of the box. Since almost all data access here goes through EF Core, the true positive count should be small and concentrated in any raw FromSqlRaw/ExecuteSqlRaw calls -- which is exactly where you want eyes.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party and current: the CA2100 reference page on Microsoft Learn was updated 2026-04-02 and carries an explicit 'Enabled by default in .NET 10' row -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/fundamentals/code-analysis/quality-rules/ca2100

**`zap-baseline-scan`** -- tool: ZAP baseline GitHub Action (zaproxy/action-baseline)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 4-6h to wire up, plus ~1h/month triaging | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A passive ZAP baseline scan against a deployed synthetic-data environment: crawls without attacking, and reports missing headers, cookie flags, information leakage and obvious misconfiguration. A backstop that catches what the header tests forgot to assert, on paths nobody wrote a test for.
- Why at this team size: Weekly and non-blocking, against a synthetic environment. Baseline (passive) mode only -- a full active scan takes tens of minutes, produces findings you will not have time to triage, and will not fit in a one-dev-week-per-month budget. Be honest about its limits: ZAP cannot find your cross-tenant bugs, because it does not know which resource belongs to which office. It earns its place as a cheap sweep for the misconfiguration class (A02), not as a substitute for check 1. This is also the one check with an infrastructure dependency -- it needs a deployed environment to point at, which the separate hosting exercise covers.
- Tool maintenance evidence: ZAP core is very actively maintained -- weekly releases, latest w2026-08-26 (https://github.com/zaproxy/zaproxy/releases, checked 2026-08-28). The action is at v0.15.0, released 24 Oct within the last year, notes 'Run with node24' (https://github.com/zaproxy/action-baseline/releases/tag/v0.15.0). Important naming correction: ZAP is no longer an OWASP project -- per https://www.zaproxy.org/docs/zap-ownership/ it is 'an independent Open Source project controlled by the ZAP Core Team and supported by Checkmarx', having left OWASP in September 2023. Do not write 'OWASP ZAP' in your security policy.



## 10. Performance and Core Web Vitals

**Verification verdict for this area: `minor-corrections`** (20 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- [7] The claim enumerates only three CrUX user opt-in conditions but its own THRESHOLD field says 'four'; the page lists four -- the omitted one is 'Use a supported platform.'
- [14] The cited getting-started.md contains NEITHER 'numberOfRuns' NOR any 'complete experience' recipe with 5 runs. numberOfRuns default 3 is documented in docs/configuration.md, not the cited page. The '5 runs in the complete experience recipe' threshold is unsupported by any page I could find.
- [17] The final sentence ('a bundle budget whose name matches no chunk evaluates to 0 bytes and silently passes') does not appear on the cited Angular build.md. It is true, but only verifiable from bundle-calculator.ts source.
- [18] The cited budget-stats.ts contains neither BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE = 1000 nor brotliCompress. The claim's substance is correct but needs two additional citations (utils/bundle-calculator.ts and tools/esbuild/utils.ts).
- [13] The cited budgets-converter.js proves only the KB->bytes (x1024) conversion. 'budgetsFile cannot be combined with any other assert option' is in lighthouse-ci docs/configuration.md, and 'transfer size' is from the budget.json spec README -- neither is in the cited file.

### Standard, in summary

As of August 2026 the Core Web Vitals set is unchanged and stable: LCP <= 2.5s, INP <= 200ms, CLS <= 0.1, each assessed at the 75th percentile of page loads, segmented across mobile and desktop. FID is fully retired (removed from the web-vitals library in v5.0.0, May 2025). The load-bearing 2026 reality for CI is that field measurement and lab measurement have diverged further, not converged: Google's own guidance says field data (CrUX/RUM) is what you prioritise on, but a login-gated portal is permanently ineligible for CrUX (which requires publicly indexable, sufficiently-visited pages), so field data for this app will exist only if the team ships the web-vitals library themselves. On the lab side, Lighthouse's performance score does not score INP at all (weight 0 in Lighthouse 13.4.1's default config) and Lighthouse 12.0.0 deleted budget.json/LightWallet support outright, while web.dev's article recommending it is still live and undated-as-deprecated. Lighthouse CI is real but quietly aging -- @lhci/cli 0.15.1 shipped 2025-06-25 with no release since, pinning Lighthouse 12.6.1 while Lighthouse itself is at 13.4.1. The sustainable 2026 standard for a small team is therefore: gate merges on deterministic byte-count budgets (Angular's built-in budgets, plus LHCI resource-summary assertions), treat every timing-derived number as an advisory trend, and collect real field data from your own users rather than pretending a CI runner is a user.

### Claims and sources

**A10.1** `[yes]` The Core Web Vitals metric set in 2026 is exactly three metrics -- Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), Interaction to Next Paint (INP), Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS) -- and all three are assessed at the 75th percentile of page loads, segmented across mobile and desktop devices.

- Threshold: `LCP <= 2.5s; INP <= 200ms; CLS <= 0.1; all at p75`
- Source: web.dev -- Web Vitals -- https://web.dev/articles/vitals
- Cross-reference: https://developer.chrome.com/docs/crux/release-notes
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: web.dev/articles/vitals shows last-updated 2024-10-31 and states INP became a stable Core Web Vital in 2024. Cross-checked against the CrUX release notes through July 2026 (published 2026-08-11), which continue to track LCP/CLS/INP as the Core Web Vitals with no new metric and no threshold change. The percentile wording is verbatim: 'the 75th percentile of page loads, segmented across mobile and desktop devices.'

**A10.2** `[yes]` LCP bands: 'good' is 2.5 seconds or less; 'needs improvement' is between 2.5 and 4.0 seconds; 'poor' is greater than 4.0 seconds.

- Threshold: `good <= 2500ms; needs-improvement 2500-4000ms; poor > 4000ms`
- Source: web.dev -- Largest Contentful Paint (LCP) -- https://web.dev/articles/lcp
- Cross-reference: https://web.dev/articles/vitals
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Page last updated 2025-09-04, i.e. still actively maintained. Same thresholds restated on web.dev/articles/vitals.

**A10.3** `[partially]` INP bands: 'good' is 200ms or below; 'needs improvement' is above 200ms and at or below 500ms; 'poor' is above 500ms. INP has fully replaced FID -- FID was deprecated and removed from the web-vitals library in v5.0.0 (May 2025).

- Threshold: `good <= 200ms; needs-improvement 200-500ms; poor > 500ms`
- Source: web.dev -- Interaction to Next Paint (INP) -- https://web.dev/articles/inp
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/GoogleChrome/web-vitals/blob/main/CHANGELOG.md

- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Thresholds are exactly right. The FID/web-vitals sub-claim is NOT on this page -- the page only says INP is 'the successor metric to First Input Delay (FID)'. I independently confirmed the sub-claim is factually true: the web-vitals CHANGELOG lists '**[BREAKING]** Remove the deprecated onFID() function (#519)' under '### v5.0.0 (2025-05-07)', and npm confirms 5.0.0 published 2025-05-07. Cite https://github.com/GoogleChrome/web-vitals/blob/main/CHANGELOG.md for that half.

**A10.5** `[yes]` The web-vitals JavaScript library is the recommended way to collect Core Web Vitals from real users, and it is actively maintained: latest is v6.2.1 published 2026-08-26, with v6.0.0 adding soft-navigation support.

**A10.6** `[yes]` Core Web Vitals do not measure client-side route changes in a Single Page Application by default. Soft-navigation measurement is a separate opt-in capability, shipping unflagged from Chrome 151, and requires the site to explicitly observe soft-navigation entries (via the Performance Observer API or the web-vitals library) to slice LCP/CLS/INP per route change.

The CrUX release notes through July 2026 make no mention of soft-navigation data entering the public dataset, so this is currently self-collected-only.

**A10.7** `[partially]` A page is only eligible for the Chrome UX Report if it is publicly discoverable/indexable and sufficiently popular (a minimum, undisclosed number of visitors), and only from opted-in Chrome users who enable usage-statistic reporting, sync browser history and have no sync passphrase.

Directly load-bearing for this app: a login-gated scheduling portal serving 11 offices will never meet either the indexability or the popularity bar, so PageSpeed Insights field data, Search Console CWV reports and CrUX-based dashboards will be permanently empty for it. Cross-check: CrUX tracked 18.06M origins in July 2026 out of the whole web -- it is a long way from universal coverage.

The claim's prose names only three and omits the fourth: 'Use a supported platform' (desktop Chrome or Android Chrome) -- its own THRESHOLD field already says 'four user opt-in conditions', so claim text and threshold disagree. Corrected claim: four user conditions -- usage-statistic reporting enabled, browser history synced, no Sync passphrase set, and on a supported platform. Page eligibility ('publicly discoverable' via 200 status + no X-Robots-Tag: noindex + no robots noindex meta; 'sufficiently popular' with an undisclosed minimum) is correct as stated.

**A10.8** `[yes]` Field data (CrUX/RUM) is what you should prioritise optimisation work on because it represents real users; lab data (Lighthouse) is for identifying optimisation opportunities and testing lower-end devices. Neither substitutes for the other.

But it is a real risk to name: the 0.15.1 dependency tree pins yargs ^15, inquirer ^6, uuid ^8, tmp ^0.1 and express ^4, all several majors behind, which will surface in whatever dependency scanning the team stands up. Recommendation: pin @lhci/cli@0.15.x, treat it as a CI-only devDependency, and re-evaluate at the next Lighthouse major.

**A10.12** `[yes]` Lighthouse CI assertion syntax: assertions are keyed by Lighthouse audit ID in an eslint-style 'level | [level, options]' form; levels are 'off', 'warn' (prints, does not fail the build) and 'error' (prints, causes non-zero exit); checkable options are minScore, maxLength and maxNumericValue; aggregationMethod is one of median, optimistic, pessimistic, median-run; and when no options are given the defaults are {aggregationMethod: 'optimistic', minScore: 1}.

- Threshold: `levels off|warn|error; minScore/maxLength/maxNumericValue; aggregationMethod median|optimistic|pessimistic|median-run; defaults {aggregationMethod:'optimistic', minScore:1}`
- Source: GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci -- docs/configuration.md -- https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/blob/main/docs/configuration.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/main/docs/configuration.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read the rendered doc and then re-read the raw markdown to confirm exact strings. Presets are exactly three: lighthouse:all, lighthouse:recommended, lighthouse:no-pwa. Category assertions use 'categories:<categoryId>' e.g. 'categories:performance': ['warn', {minScore: 0.9}]. Note the default aggregationMethod is 'optimistic' -- 'the value most likely to pass' -- so out-of-the-box LHCI is deliberately lenient across runs.

**A10.13** `[partially]` budget.json still works under Lighthouse CI despite Lighthouse dropping it, because @lhci/utils converts budget.json client-side into LHCI-native resource-summary assertions; but assert.budgetsFile cannot be combined with any other assert option, and resource-summary sizes are transfer size in kilobytes while LHCI-native maxNumericValue is in bytes.

- Threshold: `budgetsFile is mutually exclusive with other assert options; budget.json sizes in KB (transfer size), maxNumericValue in bytes`
- Source: @lhci/utils 0.15.1 -- src/budgets-converter.js (fetched source) -- https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/@lhci/utils@0.15.1/src/budgets-converter.js
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/blob/main/docs/configuration.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Source confirms convertBudgetsToAssertions() maps resourceSizes -> 'resource-summary:<type>:size' with maxNumericValue: budget * 1024, resourceCounts -> 'resource-summary:<type>:count', and timings -> the metric audit id, all at 'error' level, scoped by matchingUrlPattern derived from budget.path. Because it uses the resource-summary audit (still present and weight 0 in both LH 12.6.1 and 13.0.0) rather than the deleted performance-budget audit, it survives. Practical consequence: prefer writing resource-summary:* assertions directly in lighthouserc so you can mix them with other assertions and control the level per-assertion. The upstream budget.json spec repo GoogleChrome/budget.json was archived 2022-12-29 and is read-only.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Claim is factually correct but the cited file settles only one third of it. budgets-converter.js proves the client-side conversion and the KB->bytes x1024 multiplication (and that @lhci/utils vendors its own src/budget.js, HTTP 200, so it does not depend on Lighthouse's removed module). The mutual-exclusion rule needs docs/configuration.md: 'This option cannot be used in conjunction with any other assert option.' The 'transfer size' wording needs the budget.json spec: 'The budget property is defined in kilobytes and refers to the transfer size of a resource.' Also note the multiplier is 1024, i.e. KiB, not decimal KB.
- Verifier read from the page: "assertions[`resource-summary:${resourceType}:size`] = ['error', {maxNumericValue: maxNumericValue * 1024}];"

**A10.14** `[partially]` Lighthouse CI's default numberOfRuns is 3, and the documented posture is that failing the build on assertions is optional and should be skipped by teams new to performance measurement or with a lot of room for improvement.

- Notes: Tags page shows 12.6.2 and the v12 floating tag both dated Mar 12, 2026, i.e. a release within the last 6 months -- this action is more current than @lhci/cli itself. The public-storage risk is stated by LHCI's own docs: 'This is temporary and public storage. If you're uncomfortable with the idea of your Lighthouse reports being stored on a public URL for anyone to see, skip to the add assertions or Lighthouse CI server steps.' For a HIPAA-regulated portal, set temporaryPublicStorage: false and use uploadArtifacts instead.

**A10.16** `[yes]` Angular 20.3's application builder accepts exactly seven budget types -- all, allScript, any, anyScript, anyComponentStyle, bundle, initial -- with properties type (required), name, baseline, maximumWarning, maximumError, minimumWarning, minimumError, warning, error and no others (additionalProperties: false).

- Threshold: `7 types; 9 properties; additionalProperties false`
- Source: angular/angular-cli 20.3.x -- packages/angular/build/src/builders/application/schema.json (fetched raw) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/builders/application/schema.json
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular/main/adev/src/content/tools/cli/build.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read the definitions.budget enum directly from the 20.3.x schema. The Angular docs source markdown lists the identical set with descriptions: initial = 'The size of JavaScript and CSS needed for bootstrapping the application. This corresponds to the Initial Total value shown in the build output summary'; allScript = 'The size of all scripts'; anyScript = 'The size of any one script'; anyComponentStyle = 'This size of any one component stylesheet'.

**A10.17** `[partially]` Lazy-chunk budgets ARE supported in Angular 20, but only by naming each bundle individually: 'To configure a budget for a lazy-loaded bundle, use type: "bundle" and set name to that bundle's name.' The name matches the builder-reported bundle name, not the emitted filename, and wildcards/regex such as admin.*.js are not supported. A bundle budget whose name matches no chunk evaluates to 0 bytes and silently passes.

- Threshold: `type: 'bundle' + exact name; no wildcard support; unmatched name -> 0 bytes -> always passes`
- Source: Angular docs source -- adev/src/content/tools/cli/build.md (fetched raw) and angular-cli bundle-calculator.ts -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular/main/adev/src/content/tools/cli/build.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/utils/bundle-calculator.ts
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Docs verbatim at lines 99 and 114. The silent-pass behaviour is read from source: BundleCalculator.calculate() does chunks.filter(c => c?.names?.includes(budgetName)).map(...).reduce((l,r)=>l+r, 0) and returns [{size, label}] -- with no match the reduce seed yields 0 and every maximumError comparison passes. Chunk names come from budget-stats.ts as initialRecord?.name ?? getChunkNameFromMetafile(metafile, file), and getChunkNameFromMetafile only returns a name when metafile.outputs[file].entryPoint is set, so esbuild-produced shared/split chunks can carry no name at all. Practical read: per-lazy-chunk budgets are supported on paper and fragile in practice -- use allScript and anyScript for coverage instead.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** First three sub-claims are verbatim-correct on this page. The fourth -- 'unmatched name -> 0 bytes -> silently passes' -- is NOT documented anywhere on this page; it is an inference from source. I verified it holds: BundleCalculator in packages/angular/build/src/utils/bundle-calculator.ts does `this.chunks.filter(chunk => chunk?.names?.includes(budgetName)).map(...).reduce((l, r) => l + r, 0)`, so a non-matching name yields size 0 and any maximumError passes. Cite bundle-calculator.ts for that sentence, and flag it as source-derived rather than documented. Separately, be aware this same build.md is stale on defaults -- it claims anyComponentStyle 'Defaults to warning at 2kb and erroring at 4kb', which contradicts the actual schematic (see claim 19).
- Verifier read from the page: "To configure a budget for a lazy-loaded bundle, use `type: "bundle"` and set `name` to that bundle's name. ... The `name` field matches the bundle name, not the emitted filename, so it does not use wildcard or regular expression patterns such as `admin.*.js`."

**A10.18** `[partially]` Angular budgets are evaluated against RAW uncompressed output bytes, not the 'Estimated transfer size' column printed beside them in the same build summary -- that column is brotli-compressed. Angular's kb/mb units are decimal (1 kb = 1000 bytes, 1 mb = 1,000,000 bytes), not binary.

- Threshold: `Raw bytes; BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE = 1000; transfer-size column uses brotliCompress`
- Source: angular/angular-cli 20.3.x -- tools/esbuild/budget-stats.ts, tools/esbuild/utils.ts, utils/bundle-calculator.ts (fetched raw) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/tools/esbuild/budget-stats.ts
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/tools/esbuild/utils.ts
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: generateBudgetStats() populates stats.assets from outputFiles' raw `size`; the separate calculateEstimatedTransferSizes() calls Node's brotliCompress and feeds only the display table. bundle-calculator.ts line 15 defines BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE = 1000 and calculateBytes multiplies by it for 'kb'/'mb'/'gb' (case-insensitive). Consequence for this repo: the existing 2.5MB error means 2,500,000 raw bytes, which for typical Angular/ABP output is roughly 500-700 KB over the wire with brotli. The scary-looking number is not as scary as it reads, and conversely a raw budget cannot be compared directly against 'ship under 170 KB' compressed guidance without dividing by ~4.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Claim substance is correct but the cited file supports only the 'raw bytes' half. budget-stats.ts contains no BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE and no brotli reference. Add two citations: (a) packages/angular/build/src/utils/bundle-calculator.ts line 15, `export const BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE = 1000;` with 'mb' computed as BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE * BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE -- confirms decimal units; (b) packages/angular/build/src/tools/esbuild/utils.ts, which does `import { brotliCompress } from 'node:zlib'` and stores `result.byteLength` per output file -- confirms the transfer-size column is brotli.
- Verifier read from the page: "stats.assets.push({ name: file, size });  // size taken directly from BuildOutputFile, no compression applied"

**A10.19** `[yes]` Angular CLI 20.3.x generates budgets of initial 500kB warn / 1MB error and anyComponentStyle 4kB warn / 8kB error under --strict, and initial 2MB warn / 5MB error with anyComponentStyle 6kB warn / 10kB error when not strict. Budgets are emitted only into the production configuration.

- Threshold: `strict: initial 500kB/1MB, anyComponentStyle 4kB/8kB. non-strict: initial 2MB/5MB, anyComponentStyle 6kB/10kB`
- Source: angular/angular-cli 20.3.x -- packages/schematics/angular/application/index.ts (fetched raw) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/schematics/angular/application/index.ts
