This directly diagnoses the repo's current 2MB warn / 2.5MB error: it is the NON-STRICT scaffold default (2MB/5MB) with only the error value lowered. It was almost certainly never chosen on evidence. Also note the docs and the code disagree on anyComponentStyle defaults -- see disagreements.

**A10.20** `[partially]` For an enterprise SPA there is no published authoritative initial-bundle number; the defensible practice is to set the error threshold from the measured current build plus a small headroom and ratchet down, using web.dev's ~170 KB compressed critical-path figure as the long-term target (700-850 KB raw at typical brotli ratios) and Angular's strict default of 500kB warn / 1MB error raw as the aspirational ceiling.

- Threshold: `Target: <=1MB raw initial (Angular strict error). Interim: maximumWarning = measured current, maximumError = measured x 1.10, never above 2MB raw. Legacy anchor: <170 KB compressed critical path, <5s TTI on 3G`
- Source: web.dev -- Performance budgets 101, cross-referenced with Angular CLI strict defaults -- https://web.dev/articles/performance-budgets-101
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/schematics/angular/application/index.ts
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: Marked partial deliberately. The 170 KB / 5s-TTI figures come from a web.dev article last updated 2018-11-05, and its headline metric (TTI) was dropped from the Lighthouse performance score in Lighthouse 10 and is now a hidden weight-0 audit in 13.4.1 -- so the article is directionally useful but numerically dated, and I found no current first-party replacement number for 'enterprise SPA initial bundle'. I could not measure this repo's actual initial bundle: the working directory available to me (/home/user/Aditya-gam) contains only a personal README and PDFs, not the portal source, so the 'measured current' input must be filled in by the team from one `ng build --configuration production` run.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The 170 KB compressed / <5s TTI on 3G anchor is confirmed verbatim, including the 3G basis. The rest of the claim is not from this page and should be labelled as such: the 700-850 KB raw equivalent is an unsourced brotli-ratio extrapolation by the author (real brotli ratios on Angular bundles vary roughly 3.5-5x, so the range is a guess, not a derivation), and the Angular strict 500kB/1MB ceiling comes from the schematics source in claim 19. Also note the page frames 170 KB / 5s TTI as a legacy 2019-era default, and TTI is no longer a Core Web Vital and carries weight 0 in Lighthouse 13.4.1 -- so present it as a directional anchor, not a current standard. 'partial' confidence is the right label.
- Verifier read from the page: "Under 5 s Time to Interactive ... Under 170 KB of critical-path resources (compressed/minified) ... These numbers are calculated based on real-world baseline devices and 3G network speed."

### Where sources disagree

- Angular's documentation contradicts the Angular CLI's own code on default budget values. The docs source (adev/src/content/tools/cli/build.md) states anyComponentStyle 'Defaults to warning at 2kb and erroring at 4kb' and initial 'Defaults to warning at 500kb and erroring at 1mb'. The 20.3.x application schematic actually generates anyComponentStyle 4kB/8kB and initial 500kB/1MB under --strict, and anyComponentStyle 6kB/10kB with initial 2MB/5MB when not strict. The initial value matches only in the strict case; the anyComponentStyle value matches neither. Treat the schematic source as authoritative for what you will actually get, and the docs as a statement of intent.
- web.dev actively recommends a feature Lighthouse deleted. 'Use Lighthouse for performance budgets' (web.dev/articles/use-lighthouse-for-performance-budgets, last updated 2019-06-14) walks through budget.json and LightWallet with no deprecation notice, while the Lighthouse v12.0.0 release notes say 'remove budgets (#15950)' and the audit files are verifiably gone from the package from 12.0.0 onward. Google's guidance site and Google's tool disagree, and the guidance site is the one that is wrong. Resolved in favour of the release notes and the package contents.
- Chrome's 'Lighthouse performance scoring' page (developer.chrome.com/docs/lighthouse/performance/performance-scoring) is stamped last-updated 2019-09-19 and presents the weights as 'Lighthouse 10'. The weights it lists happen to still match Lighthouse 13.4.1's default-config.js (FCP 10 / LCP 25 / TBT 30 / CLS 25 / SI 10), but the page never mentions INP, whose weight-0 entry is the single most consequential fact about the score for a 2026 reader. Resolved by reading default-config.js at tag v13.4.1 directly.
- Guidance on whether performance budgets should break the build is genuinely split, including within one document. LHCI's getting-started.md says both 'failing the build based on the audit results is even better!' and 'If you're new to performance measurement or your site has a lot of room for improvement, we recommend skipping this step for now.' web.dev's 'Performance budgets 101' (last updated 2018-11-05) recommends wiring budgets into the build but stops short of saying they should fail it. There is no single authoritative answer; my recommendation -- gate on deterministic bytes, advise on timings -- is a synthesis, not a citation.
- The most widely-cited concrete budget number, web.dev's '<170 KB of critical-path resources, <5s Time to Interactive on 3G' (2018), is anchored to a metric that no longer exists in the scoring model: TTI was dropped from the Lighthouse performance score in Lighthouse 10 and is a hidden weight-0 audit in 13.4.1. The byte figure remains a reasonable directional target; the timing figure should not be quoted as a current standard.
- Angular's docs and Angular's code do not agree on whether lazy-chunk budgets are practically usable. The docs say plainly 'To configure a budget for a lazy-loaded bundle, use type: "bundle" and set name to that bundle's name' -- presented without caveat. The implementation resolves chunk names via metafile entryPoints and fails open (0 bytes, always passes) when no chunk matches, which the docs never mention. Both statements are true; the docs are incomplete in a way that matters.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- Lighthouse's performance score does not include INP at all. In Lighthouse 13.4.1's default-config.js the entry is literally `{id: 'interaction-to-next-paint', weight: 0, ...}` -- same in 12.6.1 and 13.0.0. So a green Lighthouse performance score is silent about the one Core Web Vital most likely to be bad in an ABP data-grid admin app. Total Blocking Time (weight 30, the largest single weight) is the lab proxy, and it is measured during page load only. If the team's mental model is 'Lighthouse 90+ means we're fine on Core Web Vitals', that model is wrong by one whole metric.
- budget.json is dead upstream. Lighthouse 12.0.0's release notes say, verbatim, 'remove budgets (#15950)'; I confirmed by file listing that /core/audits/performance-budget.js, /core/audits/timing-budget.js and /core/config/budget.js exist in lighthouse@11.7.1 and are absent from 12.0.0 onward, and that the `--budget-path` CLI flag is present in 11.7.1 and gone in 13.4.1. Meanwhile web.dev's own article 'Use Lighthouse for performance budgets' -- which walks you through exactly this -- is still live, still shows no deprecation notice, and is stamped last-updated 2019-06-14. Any tutorial-following attempt to add budget.json via the plain Lighthouse CLI produces a silent no-op. It survives only inside @lhci/cli, which converts budget.json client-side into its own resource-summary assertions. The spec repo GoogleChrome/budget.json was archived 2022-12-29.
- Angular budgets measure RAW uncompressed bytes, while the 'Estimated transfer size' column printed immediately beside them in the same build table is brotli-compressed (Node's brotliCompress, in tools/esbuild/utils.ts). The two numbers differ by roughly 4x and it is very easy to read the wrong one. Their 2.5MB error is ~2.5 MB on disk, likely 500-700 KB over the wire -- so the budget is looser than the raw number makes it sound relative to shipping advice, and it cannot be compared to any 'ship under N KB compressed' guidance without converting.
- Their current budget was never chosen. Angular CLI 20.3.x's schematic emits `initial: 2MB warn / 5MB error` for a non-strict `ng new`, and `500kB / 1MB` for `--strict`. The repo's 2MB warn / 2.5MB error is the non-strict scaffold with only the error value nudged down. It is scaffold residue that has been passing for the life of the project, not a threshold anyone defended.
- A `type: "bundle"` budget that matches no chunk fails OPEN. BundleCalculator reduces the filtered chunk list with a seed of 0 and returns `[{size: 0, ...}]`, so a misspelled or drifted bundle name yields 0 bytes and passes every maximumError forever. The budget appears in angular.json, shows up in code review as 'we have lazy budgets', and enforces nothing. This is the worst failure mode on the list because it is invisible.
- This app will never appear in CrUX -- ever. CrUX requires pages that are publicly discoverable and indexable AND meet an undisclosed minimum visitor count, plus opted-in Chrome users. A login-gated portal for 11 offices fails on every axis. That means PageSpeed Insights' field-data panel, Search Console's Core Web Vitals report and every CrUX-backed dashboard will be permanently blank for this origin. Field data is not something the team will 'get later'; it exists only if they instrument it themselves with the web-vitals library. Most teams discover this the week after launch when they open PSI and see nothing.
- Core Web Vitals barely apply to a SPA after the first page load. Chrome's own soft-navigations documentation states plainly: 'The popular Single Page Application architecture has never been fully supported by the Core Web Vitals metrics.' LCP and CLS are scoped to hard navigations, so on a portal where a user logs in once and works for an hour, essentially one navigation out of dozens is measured. Soft-navigation measurement only ships unflagged from Chrome 151 and still requires explicit instrumentation (Performance Observer or web-vitals v6). This is genuinely new -- the doc was updated 2026-07-21 -- and it changes what 'measuring Core Web Vitals' even means for this app.
- `temporaryPublicStorage: true` is the copy-paste default in nearly every Lighthouse CI tutorial, and it uploads the complete Lighthouse report -- including a full-page screenshot and every network request URL -- to a publicly readable address. LHCI's own docs warn: 'This is temporary and public storage. If you're uncomfortable with the idea of your Lighthouse reports being stored on a public URL for anyone to see, skip to the add assertions or Lighthouse CI server steps.' On a HIPAA-regulated portal this is a data-egress path that is harmless today with synthetic data and will still be there, unnoticed, the day real PHI arrives. Set it false at the moment the workflow is written, not later.
- Lighthouse CI is quietly aging and nobody announces it. @lhci/cli 0.15.1 shipped 2025-06-25, the last commit to main is 2025-06-26, and it pins `lighthouse: "12.6.1"` exactly -- while Lighthouse itself is at 13.4.1 (2026-07-20) with daily dev builds. There is no archive banner and no deprecation notice, so it looks healthy. Its dependency tree also pins yargs ^15, inquirer ^6, uuid ^8, tmp ^0.1 and express ^4, which will generate audit noise the moment the team turns on dependency scanning -- worth deciding in advance that this is an accepted CI-only devDependency rather than re-litigating it in a PR.
- LHCI's default aggregationMethod is `optimistic` -- documented as 'the value most likely to pass'. Combined with the default numberOfRuns of 3, the out-of-the-box behaviour is deliberately lenient, and a team assuming they are asserting against a median is measuring something meaningfully more forgiving than they think. Set `aggregationMethod: 'median'` explicitly.
- The `lighthouse:no-pwa` preset is now pointless -- Lighthouse 12.0.0 removed the entire PWA category. Copying the `lighthouse:no-pwa` recipe from a 2023 blog post is harmless but signals the recipe is stale in other ways too.
- Angular's `kb` and `mb` are decimal, not binary: bundle-calculator.ts defines `BYTES_IN_KILOBYTE = 1000`. So `2.5MB` means 2,500,000 bytes, not 2,621,440. A ~5% discrepancy, which matters only when a budget is set right at the edge -- which is exactly where a ratcheting budget lives.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- Standing up a self-hosted Lighthouse CI server (the Node app plus its Postgres/SQLite store) for historical trending, build diffing and GitHub status checks. This is what a larger org does and it genuinely gives better reporting, but it is a service two SDE 1s would have to operate, patch and back up for the rest of the product's life -- and @lhci/cli itself has had no release since 2025-06-25, so you would be operating an unmaintained service. Nightly `uploadArtifacts` retained for 90 days gets you the trend line for a cron block and zero operational surface.
- Gating merges on `categories:performance` with a minScore threshold. Lighthouse's score is 30% Total Blocking Time and 10% Speed Index, both of which swing materially on shared GitHub-hosted runners depending on what else is on the box; Chrome's own scoring documentation advises thinking of performance as 'a distribution of scores, rather than a single number'. A team of two who see a red check they cannot reproduce locally will start merging past it within about three weeks, and the habit generalises to the checks that DO matter. Keep the score visible and advisory.
- Per-lazy-route `type: "bundle"` budgets for every feature area. Angular supports them, but the `name` is the builder-reported bundle name with no wildcard support, esbuild-produced shared/split chunks may carry no name at all, and a budget whose name matches nothing silently computes 0 bytes and always passes. You would be maintaining a list of fragile strings that fail open. `allScript` plus `anyScript` covers the same failure mode with two lines and no rot.
- Commercial synthetic + RUM platforms (SpeedCurve, Calibre, DebugBear and similar) and real-device labs. These are the correct answer at 30 engineers. Setup, dashboard curation and alert tuning are recurring work you do not have, and the specific value they add -- cross-device field distributions -- is muted for an app whose entire population is ~11 offices of staff on managed desktops.
- A full INP/soft-navigation attribution pipeline with a metrics backend, dashboards and alerting before launch. Ship the minimal thing instead: web-vitals v6 with a beacon to one endpoint, so that six months from now you have data. The attribution build, per-route slicing and dashboards are a month of work to analyse a population that does not exist yet.
- Server-side rendering or hydration to improve first-paint metrics. This is a large architectural change to an ABP Angular app, aimed at a metric (LCP on cold navigation) that each user experiences roughly once per working session on a portal they are contractually obliged to use. The payoff structure that justifies SSR -- anonymous visitors who bounce -- does not apply here.
- Backend/API load and latency testing (k6, NBomber, Gatling) as a merge gate. It is real work and it should happen, but with database-per-tenant across 11 to 33 SQL Server databases the interesting performance questions are connection-pool, per-tenant-DB and hosting-shaped, which is the separate infrastructure exercise. A merge gate on API latency measured on a CI runner would measure the runner.
- Chasing a green Lighthouse accessibility or best-practices score inside the performance job. Keep the perf workflow doing one thing; accessibility has its own gate and its own owner, and bundling them means one flaky metric holds the other hostage.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`bundle-budget`** -- tool: Angular CLI 20.3 / @angular-devkit/build-angular:application -- built in, no new dependency

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs `ng build --configuration production` in CI and fails the job on Angular's built-in size budgets. Replace the current single `initial` budget with four entries: `initial` (maximumWarning = today's measured initial total, maximumError = measured x 1.10, capped at 2MB raw), `allScript` (total JS across the whole app including every lazy chunk -- this is the entry that actually catches 'someone added a 3 MB PDF library to a lazy route'), `anyScript` (set ~15% above today's largest single emitted chunk, to catch a runaway individual chunk), and keep `anyComponentStyle`.
- Why at this team size: Zero new tooling, zero new failure modes, and it is the only performance check here that is fully deterministic -- the same commit produces the same bytes on any runner, so it will never flake and therefore will never be bypassed. It also fixes a live defect rather than adding ceremony: the current 2MB/2.5MB is the Angular non-strict scaffold default, so the team has an unowned number guarding nothing, and no coverage at all for lazy chunks. Do NOT try to write per-lazy-chunk `type: bundle` budgets -- the names are builder-derived, unwildcardable, and a mistyped name silently passes; `allScript` + `anyScript` gets 90% of the value for 10% of the maintenance.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Budget implementation read directly from angular/angular-cli branch 20.3.x: packages/angular/build/src/utils/bundle-calculator.ts and tools/esbuild/budget-stats.ts. Angular 20.3.x is the version the repo is already on. https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/utils/bundle-calculator.ts

**`lighthouse-ci`** -- tool: treosh/lighthouse-ci-action@12.6.2 (bundles @lhci/cli 0.15.1 / Lighthouse 12.6.1)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 8-12 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: On every PR that touches the Angular app, builds production, serves dist statically, and runs `lhci autorun` against the four anonymous routes that are about to face the public internet (login, registration, password reset, and the public document-upload entry). Sets `error`-level assertions ONLY on deterministic byte and count facts (`resource-summary:script:size`, `resource-summary:total:size`, `resource-summary:third-party:count`), and `warn`-level on everything score- or timing-derived (`categories:performance`, `largest-contentful-paint`, `cumulative-layout-shift`). numberOfRuns: 3, aggregationMethod: median. temporaryPublicStorage MUST be false; use uploadArtifacts instead.
- Why at this team size: The split is what makes this survivable: the job is a required check, but the only things that can turn it red are byte counts, which are as reproducible as the bundle budget. Timing metrics still get measured and printed on every PR -- the team sees the trend -- but a noisy GitHub-hosted runner cannot block a merge. This is exactly the posture LHCI's own docs recommend for teams new to performance measurement, and it means the check earns trust instead of being routinely overridden. Scope it to anonymous routes only: those are the pages going public for the first time, they need no auth setup, and they are the ones a stranger will actually load.
- Tool maintenance evidence: treosh/lighthouse-ci-action 12.6.2 and the v12 floating tag both dated 2026-03-12 (https://github.com/treosh/lighthouse-ci-action/tags) -- a release within the last 6 months. Caveat to accept knowingly: the underlying @lhci/cli is 0.15.1 from 2025-06-25 with no release in 14 months (https://registry.npmjs.org/@lhci/cli), pinning Lighthouse 12.6.1 while upstream Lighthouse is 13.4.1 (2026-07-20).

**`lighthouse-nightly`** -- tool: treosh/lighthouse-ci-action@12.6.2 with uploadArtifacts: true

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A scheduled workflow (nightly or weekly, on a cron) running the same LHCI config against the deployed non-production environment with numberOfRuns: 5 and aggregationMethod: median, uploading the HTML reports as workflow artifacts with ~90-day retention. Nothing fails; it exists purely to give a trend line that PR-level runs cannot.
- Why at this team size: Reuses the lighthouserc.json already written for the PR job, so the marginal cost is a cron block and an artifact upload. It is the cheap substitute for a self-hosted LHCI server: five runs on a quiet schedule against a stable environment produce a far more meaningful number than three runs on a contended PR runner, and artifacts give you month-over-month comparison without operating a database. Two people can look at this once a month and be done.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Same action as above, 12.6.2 dated 2026-03-12. LHCI's documented default numberOfRuns is 3 and its 'complete experience' recipe uses 5 (https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/blob/main/docs/configuration.md).

**`bundle-analysis (local-only)`** -- tool: Angular CLI `statsJson` option (built in) + esbuild's own analyzer

- Posture: `local-only` | Effort: 1 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A documented one-liner -- `ng build --configuration production --stats-json`, then drop dist/stats.json into https://esbuild.github.io/analyze/ -- run by hand whenever the bundle-budget check goes red or a large dependency is proposed. Written into CONTRIBUTING.md so it is not tribal knowledge.
- Why at this team size: A size gate without a diagnosis tool is a gate people disable. This costs an hour, adds no CI minutes, no dependency and no maintenance, and turns 'the budget check is red and I don't know why' -- the single most common reason budgets get raised instead of fixed -- into a two-minute answer.
- Tool maintenance evidence: `statsJson` confirmed present in the Angular 20.3.x application builder schema with description 'Generates a stats.json file which can be analyzed with https://esbuild.github.io/analyze/' (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/angular/angular-cli/20.3.x/packages/angular/build/src/builders/application/schema.json). Deliberately NOT recommending source-map-explorer: its latest release is 2.5.3 from 2022-09-26 (https://registry.npmjs.org/source-map-explorer), roughly four years stale.

**`lighthouse-authenticated`** -- tool: @lhci/cli puppeteerScript + puppeteer (installed separately)

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 12-20 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Extends the LHCI config with a puppeteerScript that logs into a seeded synthetic tenant and audits two or three of the real post-login screens (the scheduling grid, an examination detail page). Runs on the same nightly schedule, advisory only, never on PRs.
- Why at this team size: This is honestly the phase-2 item -- do it in the second or third month, not before launch. It matters because the anonymous routes are the pages nobody spends time on: real users log in once and then live inside data grids for an hour, which is precisely where an ABP/Angular admin app goes slow. But it is also the most expensive and most fragile thing on this list (auth flow drift, tenant seeding, host-header tenancy resolution making the login URL non-obvious), so it must never be a gate. Defer it consciously rather than half-building it now.
- Tool maintenance evidence: puppeteerScript documented in LHCI docs/configuration.md, which notes puppeteer must be installed independently and that 'the browser is kept open across all URLs, so if you're keeping auth in cookies then everything should be remembered between runs' (https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/blob/main/docs/configuration.md).



## 11. Accessibility

**Verification verdict for this area: `material-errors`** (21 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- CLAIM 10 IS WRONG ON ITS CENTRAL POINT: Cal. Gov. Code 7405 does NOT flow Section 508 / WCAG 2.0 A+AA conformance down to private contractors. 7405(a) binds only 'state governmental entities'; 7405(b) requires a contracting entity merely to 'agree to respond to, and resolve any complaint regarding accessibility of, its products or services.' No conformance standard is imposed on the contractor. The stated threshold ('Section 508 / 36 CFR Part 1194 - i.e. WCAG 2.0 Level A+AA - flowed down by contract') is not in the statute.
- CLAIM 16 STATES A RULE COUNT AND A RULE THAT DO NOT EXIST: the angular-eslint template accessibility preset contains 11 rules, not 12, and does NOT include `no-positive-tabindex`. Verified in packages/eslint-plugin-template/src/configs/accessibility.json at tag v20.7.0 and in the current README: alt-text, click-events-have-key-events, elements-content, interactive-supports-focus, label-has-associated-control, mouse-events-have-key-events, no-autofocus, no-distracting-elements, role-has-required-aria, table-scope, valid-aria. (The rest of claim 16 - config names, 20.7.0 published 2025-11-22, peer range eslint ^8.57.0 || ^9.0.0 - is correct.)
- CLAIM 15 PRESENTS A FABRICATED QUOTE OF SC 3.3.8. The claim says 3.3.8 'requires that Content does not rely on cognitive function test to authenticate unless an alternative authentication method is available.' The actual normative text is: 'A cognitive function test (such as remembering a password or solving a puzzle) is not required for any step in an authentication process unless that step provides at least one of the following: Alternative, Mechanism, Object Recognition, Personal Content.' The claim collapses four exception paths into one and would understate what satisfies the criterion.
- CLAIM 17 OMITS THE FACT THAT MAKES IT ACTIONABLE: axe-core's rule-descriptions.md says of the WCAG 2.2 section, 'These rules are disabled by default, until WCAG 2.2 is more widely adopted and required.' So the single wcag22aa rule (target-size) does not even run unless explicitly enabled. Also, the section counts are 60 (WCAG 2.0 A/AA) + 2 (WCAG 2.1 A/AA) = 62, and the tag list is incomplete - it omits `TTv5` and `wcag2a-obsolete`, and the authoritative tag table lives in doc/API.md, not the cited rule-descriptions.md.
- CLAIM 3's THRESHOLD FIELD CARRIES A WRONG DATE: it states '2028-04-28'. 28 CFR 35.200(b)(2) reads 'Beginning April 26, 2028'. The claim body has it right; the threshold string does not.
- CLAIM 14 CANNOT BE VERIFIED AT ITS CITED URL: https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/section-1557/faqs/index.html returns HTTP 403 to every fetch attempt. Separately, the operative regulation - 45 CFR 92.4's definition of 'Federal financial assistance' - does not name Medicare Part B anywhere in its text. The Part B position and any May 6, 2025 date live in the 2024 final rule preamble (89 FR 37522), which must be cited instead; as written the claim rests on an unreachable page.
- CLAIM 6 OVERSTATES ITS OWN AUTHORITY: the Robles opinion itself distinguishes Weyer v. Twentieth Century Fox, where the Ninth Circuit held an insurance company administering an employer-provided policy was NOT a covered place of public accommodation, and rests its holding on a 'nexus between Domino's website and app and physical restaurants - which Domino's does not contest.' A standalone workers'-comp administration portal with no nexus to a physical place of public accommodation is exactly the Weyer fact pattern. 'Very likely does apply' is not supported; 'contested, nexus-dependent' is.

### Standard, in summary

Build to WCAG 2.2 Level A + AA. WCAG 2.2 is still the current W3C Recommendation (REC 5 Oct 2023, republished 12 Dec 2024); WCAG 3.0 got a fresh Working Draft on 3 March 2026 but W3C says it "is not expected to be a completed W3C standard for a few more years," so it is not a build target. Level AA is the operative level -- every US regulation that names a technical standard names Level A+AA (DOJ Title II and HHS both name WCAG 2.1 AA; Section 508 still names WCAG 2.0 AA), and WCAG 2.2 AA is backward-compatible with all of them. Legally, nothing in the federal web-accessibility rulebook binds this private company directly: the DOJ's April 2024 rule (28 CFR 35.200) covers public entities only, Section 508 covers federal agencies and federal procurement, and Section 1557/504's web rule (45 CFR 84.84, 92.204) covers recipients of HHS federal financial assistance -- which a workers' comp IME scheduling vendor almost certainly is not. What does bind it is ADA Title III (its customers' medical offices are enumerated public accommodations under 42 U.S.C. 12181(7)(F), giving the portal a Robles-style nexus) plus California's Unruh Act, which converts any ADA violation into a state claim carrying a $4,000 statutory minimum per offense plus attorney's fees -- and going onto the public internet is precisely the event that makes an anonymous tester able to reach the registration page. Two indirect routes matter more than the direct ones: both 28 CFR 35.200 and 45 CFR 84.84 cover content a covered organization provides "directly or through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements," and Cal. Gov. Code 7405 binds "any entity that contracts with a state or local entity subject to Section 11135" to Section 508 -- so a single California city, county, school district or state-agency office as a tenant pulls a dated WCAG conformance obligation into the contract. For CI, the sustainable shape is one linter gate with a banked baseline (angular-eslint's templateAccessibility config plus ESLint bulk suppressions) and one axe-core run over the six anonymous-reachable pages, backed by a short manual keyboard/screen-reader pass per release -- because axe-core 4.13 carries exactly one rule tagged wcag22aa, and five of WCAG 2.2's six new A/AA criteria are undetectable by any scanner.

### Claims and sources

**A11.1** `[partially]` WCAG 2.2 is the current W3C Recommendation and is the correct build target in 2026; WCAG 3.0 remains a Working Draft and is not a conformance target. WCAG 2.2 was published as a W3C Recommendation on 5 October 2023 and republished 12 December 2024; a new WCAG 3.0 Working Draft was published 3 March 2026, and W3C states WCAG 3 'is not expected to be a completed W3C standard for a few more years' and 'will not supersede WCAG 2.'

- Threshold: `WCAG 2.2 (W3C Recommendation, 2024-12-12 republication of the 2023-10-05 REC)`

W3C recommends the wide deployment of this specification as a standard for the Web'), the WAI WCAG overview page, the WAI WCAG 3 intro page, and the WAI news item at https://www.w3.org/WAI/news/2026-03-03/wcag3 confirming the 3 March 2026 Working Draft. W3C also confirms WCAG 2.0/2.1/2.2 all remain valid Recommendations -- later versions do not deprecate earlier ones -- and that content conforming to 2.2 also conforms to 2.1 and 2.0. Ignore the trade-press '174 requirements / Candidate Recommendation Q4 2027' timeline chatter: W3C itself publishes no date.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Threshold is correct as stated. But this page settles only the WCAG 2.2 half. The two WCAG 3 quotes are NOT on this page - the only supersession language here is 'The publication of WCAG 2.2 does not deprecate or supersede WCAG 2.0 or WCAG 2.1', which is a different statement about 2.0/2.1, not about WCAG 3. Cite https://www.w3.org/TR/wcag-3.0/ (verified: 'W3C Working Draft 03 March 2026', with 'It is inappropriate to cite this document as other than a work in progress') for the WD status/date, and the WAI WCAG 3 Introduction page for the 'not expected to be a completed W3C standard' / 'will not supersede WCAG 2' quotes.

**A11.11** `[yes]` Section 508 does not reach this application directly. The Revised 508 Standards (36 CFR Part 1194, finalized January 2017) incorporate WCAG 2.0 Level A and AA -- E205.4: 'Electronic content shall conform to Level A and Level AA Success Criteria and Conformance Requirements in WCAG 2.0' -- and apply to federal agencies developing, procuring, maintaining or using ICT. **A11.12** `[partially]` Section 1557 of the ACA applies only to recipients of HHS federal financial assistance, HHS-administered health programs, and Title I entities -- not to vendors as such. 45 CFR 92.2 applies the rule to 'Every health program or activity, any part of which receives Federal financial assistance, directly or indirectly, from the Department', 'Every health program or activity administered by the Department', and 'Every health program or activity administered by a title I entity.' Its ICT provision, 45 CFR 92.204(b), does not itself name WCAG: it says 'A recipient or State Exchange shall ensure that its health programs and activities provided through websites and mobile applications comply with the requirements of section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act, as interpreted consistent with title II of the ADA.'

- Threshold: `Scope = recipients of HHS FFA / HHS programs / Title I entities. 92.204(b) defers to the Section 504 rule; no WCAG version stated in 92.204 itself`
- Source: 45 CFR 92.2 and 45 CFR 92.204 (Cornell LII) -- https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/92.204
- Cross-reference: https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/92.2
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: IMPORTANT PRECISION: a lot of published commentary says '1557 requires WCAG 2.1 AA by May 2026.' The regulation does not say that. 92.204(b) routes to Section 504, whose technical standard and dates live in 45 CFR 84.84 (next entry). A workers' comp IME scheduling vendor is very unlikely to be an HHS FFA recipient -- workers' compensation is state-regulated and is not HHS federal financial assistance -- so 1557 most likely does not bind this company directly. That is a fact question for counsel, not something I can determine.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The 92.204(b) quotation is verbatim and the 'no WCAG version in 92.204' point is confirmed. The scope quotation is from 45 CFR 92.2, not 92.204 - cite https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/92.2 (text verified: 'Every health program or activity, any part of which receives Federal financial assistance, directly or indirectly, from the Department'). Add one qualifier the claim omits: 92.204(a) imposes a general ICT accessibility duty on any 'covered entity' (subject to undue burden / fundamental alteration), so the rule is not limited to the 92.204(b) recipient/State-Exchange subset.
- Verifier read from the page: "(b) A recipient or State Exchange shall ensure that its health programs and activities provided through websites and mobile applications comply with the requirements of section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act, as interpreted consistent with title II of the ADA (42 U.S.C. 12131 through 12165)."

**A11.13** `[yes]` The HHS Section 504 web rule, 45 CFR 84.84, is the provision that actually carries WCAG 2.1 A+AA for health programs, and its compliance dates were extended by one year in May 2026. Current text: recipients with 15 or more employees must comply by May 11, 2027, and recipients with fewer than 15 employees by May 10, 2028. Coverage extends to '(1) Web content that a recipient provides or makes available, directly or through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements; and (2) Mobile apps...'

- Threshold: `WCAG 2.1 Level A+AA; 2027-05-11 (15+ employees) / 2028-05-10 (<15 employees); amendment cites 89 FR 40193 (May 9, 2024) and 91 FR 25507 (May 11, 2026)`
- Source: 45 CFR 84.84 (Cornell LII, current text with amendment citations) -- https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/84.84
- Cross-reference: https://www.hhs.gov/press-room/hhs-extends-mobile-and-web-accessibility-deadline.html
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: HHS OCR's own press release confirms the new dates (May 11, 2027 / May 10, 2028) and the WCAG 2.1 AA standard, and states the extension aligns Section 504 with DOJ's parallel Title II action. The original dates were May 11, 2026 / May 10, 2027 -- anyone working from 2024 or 2025 notes has the wrong dates. HHS also stated the delay 'does not relieve recipients of their other obligations under Section 504.' As with Title II, the 'through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements' language is what pulls a vendor in via its customers.

**A11.19** `[partially]` ESLint has a first-party bulk-suppressions mechanism designed exactly for the 'enable the rule, don't fix 253 things today' problem. Introduced in ESLint v9.24.0 (April 4, 2025), it provides `--suppress-all`, `--suppress-rule <rule>`, `--prune-suppressions` and `--pass-on-unpruned-suppressions`, writing to `eslint-suppressions.json` in the project root (relocatable via `--suppressions-location`). Only rules configured as 'error' are suppressed; new violations of a suppressed rule still fail.

- Threshold: `ESLint >= 9.24.0; default file `eslint-suppressions.json``
- Source: ESLint documentation -- Suppressions -- https://eslint.org/docs/latest/use/suppressions
- Cross-reference: https://eslint.org/blog/2025/04/eslint-v9.24.0-released/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Compatibility check for this stack: angular-eslint 20.7.0 requires eslint ^8.57.0 || ^9.0.0, and ESLint 9.x is still maintained (9.39.5, published 2026-07-10, per https://registry.npmjs.org/eslint). So pin ESLint ^9.39 -- that gives you bulk suppressions AND angular-eslint v20. Do not jump to ESLint 10 until you move to angular-eslint 21+.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Every mechanical detail is confirmed verbatim: all five flags, the default file name and project-root location, the error-only rule, and 'While the rule will be enforced for new code, the existing violations will not be reported.' The one thing the page does NOT state is the introducing version - verify it against the ESLint v9.24.0 release notes rather than this page (npm confirms eslint 9.24.0 published 2025-04-04T19:20:41Z, consistent with the claim). Two practical notes worth adding: the docs 'latest' now tracks ESLint v10.x, and the Node.js API only applies existing suppressions (applySuppressions) - it cannot create or prune them, so the CI ratchet must go through the CLI.
- Verifier read from the page: "Only rules configured as "error" are suppressed. If a rule is enabled as "warn", ESLint will not suppress the violations. ... When you suppress violations, ESLint creates a eslint-suppressions.json file in the root of the project."
