**A11.21** `[partially]` Two first-party baseline/ratchet mechanisms exist for a11y scanning, so a pre-existing violation count never needs to break the build. pa11y-ci's `threshold` option 'permit[s] this number of errors, warnings, or notices, otherwise fail with exit code 2' (default 0), settable globally under `defaults` or per URL. IBM's accessibility-checker supports a baseline directory natively: 'Baselines are a helpful feature of accessibility-checker' where tests fail only when results differ from the established baseline, configured via `baselineFolder` (default 'baselines'), `failLevels` (default ['violation','potentialviolation']), `reportLevels`, `policies` (default ['IBM_Accessibility']) and `ruleArchive` (default 'latest').

- Threshold: `pa11y-ci `threshold` (default 0); accessibility-checker `baselineFolder` (default 'baselines'), `failLevels` default ['violation','potentialviolation']`
- Source: pa11y-ci README and IBM equal-access accessibility-checker configuration README -- https://github.com/pa11y/pa11y-ci/blob/main/README.md
- Cross-reference: https://github.com/IBMa/equal-access/blob/master/accessibility-checker/src/README.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: @axe-core/playwright has NO built-in baseline -- its API is AxeBuilder({page}).withTags([...]).include(...).exclude(...).disableRules(...).analyze(). With axe you implement the ratchet yourself: assert zero violations at impact 'serious'/'critical', and snapshot the sorted list of remaining violation rule-ids per page so a NEW rule id fails but the known set passes. That is ~20 lines and is what I recommend over adopting a second engine. Caution on pa11y: its default runner is HTML_CodeSniffer and its `standard` values are only WCAG2A/WCAG2AA/WCAG2AAA -- that is WCAG 2.0, not 2.1 or 2.2. If you use pa11y at all, set runners to ['axe'].
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The threshold quotation and the default of 0 are exact - but the README documents `threshold` ONLY as a CLI flag. It is not documented under `defaults` or per-URL: the README's 'Default configuration' section says 'Pa11y CI supports two additional options here: concurrency ... useIncognitoBrowserContext', and the URL-object section describes overriding pa11y config (timeout, screenCapture), not threshold. Corrected threshold: 'pa11y-ci --threshold N / -T N (default 0), a CLI flag; the README does not document a defaults-level or per-URL threshold - verify against the source before designing the ratchet around it.' The IBM half is accurate but is on a different repo: 'Baselines are a helpful feature of accessibility-checker' is in IBMa/equal-access accessibility-checker/README.md, and the defaults (ruleArchive: latest, policies: IBM_Accessibility, failLevels: violation + potentialviolation, baselineFolder default 'baselines') are in accessibility-checker/src/README.md. Cite those two URLs, not the pa11y-ci README.
- Verifier read from the page: "-T, --threshold <number>   permit this number of errors, warnings, or notices, otherwise fail with exit code 2 (default: "0")"

### Where sources disagree

- Which WCAG version is 'the standard' depends entirely on who is asking. W3C says WCAG 2.2 (Recommendation, republished 2024-12-12). DOJ's Title II rule (28 CFR 35.200) and HHS's Section 504 rule (45 CFR 84.84) both say WCAG 2.1 Level A+AA. Section 508 (36 CFR Part 1194, E205.4), reachable here via Cal. Gov. Code 7405, still says WCAG 2.0 Level A+AA. There is no single answer. Build to 2.2 AA -- W3C states conformance to 2.2 also satisfies 2.1 and 2.0 -- but a VPAT/ACR must name the specific version being claimed against, and different customers will demand different ones.
- DOJ's own guidance and Ninth Circuit litigation reality point in opposite directions for Title III. ADA.gov (updated 2022-03-18) says businesses 'can currently choose how they will ensure' online accessibility and that 'The Department of Justice does not have a regulation setting out detailed standards' -- and Title III rulemaking is now shelved indefinitely. But Robles v. Domino's (9th Cir. 2019) held that 'the lack of specific regulations, not yet promulgated by the Department of Justice, did not eliminate Domino's statutory duty', and courts routinely order WCAG 2.x AA as the equitable remedy. Reading DOJ's guidance as permission is a mistake; reading WCAG AA as legally mandated for Title III is also technically wrong. Both are true at once.
- Published commentary on Section 1557 is widely inaccurate on the mechanics. Many law-firm alerts and vendor blogs say '1557 requires WCAG 2.1 AA by May 2026.' The regulation does not say that: 45 CFR 92.204(b) contains no WCAG citation at all and instead routes to 'the requirements of section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act, as interpreted consistent with title II of the ADA', with the technical standard and dates living in 45 CFR 84.84. The dates were also extended by one year in May 2026. When this matters for a contract, read 84.84 and 92.204 directly rather than a summary.
- Whether a covered entity's web-accessibility obligation flows down to a software vendor is unsettled. Both 28 CFR 35.200(a) and 45 CFR 84.84(a) reach content provided 'through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements', which clearly obligates the covered entity for vendor-supplied content, but neither regulation makes the vendor itself a regulated party. Sources differ on how aggressively agencies will police flow-down. Practically, the exposure arrives as a contract clause and a VPAT request, not as an enforcement action against this company.
- Whether a website standing alone is a 'public accommodation' splits federal and California state authority. The Ninth Circuit's nexus test (Robles) is favourable to plaintiffs where a physical place of public accommodation exists -- which it does here, since 42 U.S.C. 12181(7)(F) enumerates 'professional office of a health care provider'. California's Court of Appeal reached a narrower result for website-only businesses in Martinez v. Cot'n Wash (2022), which I could NOT open (WebSearch budget exhausted; only a law-firm summary was surfaced) and which likely does not help this portal anyway given its physical-office nexus. Flagging the split rather than picking a side.
- None of the above is legal advice. It is a research summary of primary regulatory and statutory text, assembled by an engineer for engineering planning. The applicability questions that actually decide this company's exposure -- is it an HHS federal financial assistance recipient, will any tenant be a California public entity, does the Robles nexus attach -- are fact-specific legal determinations. Get a California employment/civil-rights attorney to confirm before treating any of it as a compliance position.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The DOJ Title II rule you were told doesn't apply to you can apply to you by contract. 28 CFR 35.200(a) covers web content a public entity provides 'directly or through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements'. California workers' comp is unusually public-sector-heavy: cities, counties, school districts, special districts, CSU and UC are large self-insured employers. The moment one of them is an 'office' on this platform, its injured workers use your portal to access its workers' comp program, and WCAG 2.1 AA by 26 April 2027 (or 2028 for entities under 50,000 population) becomes a contract term. 45 CFR 84.84(a) uses word-for-word the same 'through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements' language for HHS recipients. Both indirect routes are far more likely to bind this company than any direct obligation.
- California Gov. Code 7405 explicitly names contractors, and it points at an OLDER standard. It binds 'any entity that contracts with a state or local entity subject to Section 11135' to Section 508 -- which is still WCAG 2.0 Level A+AA under 36 CFR Part 1194 E205.4, finalized in 2017 and never refreshed. So a single public-sector contract can impose WCAG 2.0 AA (via 7405/508) and WCAG 2.1 AA (via 28 CFR 35.200) simultaneously, with different dates. Building to WCAG 2.2 AA satisfies both because W3C's versions are backward-compatible -- but the VPAT has to say which one you are claiming against.
- Both federal deadlines moved in 2026, and almost every guide still online has the old dates. DOJ extended Title II by one year on 20 April 2026 (91 FR 20912): now 26 April 2027 / 26 April 2028. HHS extended the Section 504 web rule on 11 May 2026 (91 FR 25507): now 11 May 2027 (15+ employees) / 10 May 2028 (under 15). If anyone on the team read a 2024 or 2025 article, they have 'April 2026' and 'May 2026' in their heads -- dates that have already passed. This buys roughly twelve months of headroom on the procurement-driven routes, and it is the single most useful correction in this whole area.
- The ADA is not where the money is -- Unruh is, and going public is the event that switches it on. ADA Title III provides only injunctive relief plus attorney's fees (42 U.S.C. 12188(a)(1), incorporating 42 U.S.C. 2000a-3(a)-(b)); it has no damages. California Civil Code 51(f) makes any ADA violation an Unruh violation, and 52(a) sets a floor of $4,000 per offense plus fees. On an office LAN, no anonymous tester could reach a page. From the day registration, login and password reset are on the public internet, a tester with a screen reader can, and the fee-shifting -- not the $4,000 -- is what drives filing volume in California.
- axe-core has exactly ONE rule tagged wcag22aa: `target-size`. Five of WCAG 2.2's six new Level A/AA criteria are undetectable by any scanner, and they land squarely on the flows going public. The two most likely public-internet hardening moves both CREATE new AA failures while every scanner stays green: adding a CAPTCHA to the registration form breaks 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum) unless a non-cognitive alternative is offered, and adding a drag-and-drop document uploader breaks 2.5.7 Dragging Movements unless there is a click/keyboard path. Also watch 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured -- a sticky header or a toast notification covering the focused form field is an AA failure that looks fine in a screenshot.
- Lighthouse CI is effectively abandoned even though half the '2026 CI accessibility' guides still lead with it. @lhci/cli's last release is 0.15.1 from 25 June 2025 and the last commit on GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci main is 26 June 2025 -- 14 months stale as of today -- while Lighthouse core has moved on to 13.4.1 (20 July 2026). The tools that ARE moving are the ones you'd probably assume were legacy: pa11y shipped 10.0.0 today (28 August 2026) and IBM's equal-access accessibility-checker shipped 4.0.31 a week ago.
- ESLint has a first-party answer to the exact '253 pre-existing violations' problem, and most teams have never heard of it. Bulk suppressions landed in ESLint 9.24.0 (4 April 2025): run `eslint --suppress-all` once, commit `eslint-suppressions.json`, and every existing violation is banked while new ones fail. It beats the usual improvisations -- `--max-warnings 253`, or a scatter of eslint-disable comments -- because `--prune-suppressions` makes the baseline ratchet down automatically as files get touched, and the count can never silently drift upward. Compatibility trap for this stack: angular-eslint 20.7.0 (the right pin for Angular 20.3.x) peers on eslint ^8.57.0 || ^9.0.0, so pin ESLint ^9.39, not ESLint 10.
- The rule the brief called 'label-for-control' does not exist. In angular-eslint v20 it is `@angular-eslint/template/label-has-associated-control`; the older `label-for` name is gone. If anyone wired a config from a pre-v15 blog post, that rule is silently doing nothing right now -- ESLint does not error on an unknown rule inside a plugin namespace in every configuration path, so a dead a11y rule can sit in the config looking like coverage.
- Database-per-tenant plus Host-header tenancy makes contrast failures invisible to a single-host scan. If any of the 11 offices gets custom branding -- a logo colour that becomes a button background, a themed link colour -- axe's `color-contrast` rule will only catch it on the tenant host you happen to scan. This is the one place where the multi-tenant architecture creates an accessibility-specific blind spot rather than just a security one, and the fix costs nothing if you build the nightly job to loop over two or three tenant hosts from day one.
- The '253 violations' number is probably not 253 problems. In an ABP Commercial / Angular Material application, axe findings are typically dominated by two rules -- `color-contrast` (a theme token) and `target-size` (a component-library default). If that holds here, the remediation plan changes from 'fix 253 things over six months' to 'change two theme values and re-scan', which is a day, not a quarter. Before planning any remediation, group the existing violations by rule id and count. Also strip the `best-practice` tag from the count first: `region`, `landmark-*`, `heading-order` and `page-has-heading-one` are not WCAG failures and may be inflating the number substantially.
- WCAG 2.2 deleted a criterion, so some old findings are no longer failures. 4.1.1 Parsing is 'obsolete and removed from WCAG 2.2'. Duplicate `id` attributes and unclosed tags are still real bugs that break assistive technology, but they are no longer a WCAG conformance failure -- and older audit reports, older linters and some vendor scanners still report them as one. Do not spend the one dev-week per month on them.
- No automated result is a conformance claim, and saying otherwise is itself a risk. W3C WAI is explicit: 'Web accessibility evaluation tools can not determine accessibility, they can only assist in doing so.' A green axe run or a Lighthouse accessibility score of 100 is evidence of diligence, not conformance. If an accessibility statement or a VPAT says 'Supports' for a criterion that was only ever checked by a scanner, that written statement becomes the document a plaintiff quotes back. Word the statement as a target plus a method, and have counsel read it.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- A commissioned third-party WCAG 2.2 AA audit with a signed VPAT/ACR (typically $15-40k and 4-8 weeks of remediation follow-up). This is what a larger company does annually. Do it ONCE, and trigger it on a business event -- the first public-agency or large-carrier contract that demands an ACR -- not on a calendar. Until then the money buys a document nobody has asked for, at the cost of roughly two months of the team's entire discretionary capacity.
- Running more than one scan engine. Standard enterprise practice is axe + HTML_CodeSniffer (pa11y) + Lighthouse for coverage breadth. For two people this triples the triage load for heavily overlapping findings, and the engines disagree, which turns every red build into a research task. Pick axe-core and only axe-core. (Extra reason to skip pa11y specifically: its default HTML_CodeSniffer runner's `standard` values are WCAG2A/WCAG2AA/WCAG2AAA -- WCAG 2.0 -- so it silently tests an older standard than the one you are targeting.)
- Automated screen-reader testing in CI (Guidepup, W3C AT Driver, or Deque's paid axe DevTools Watcher tier). Genuinely valuable and genuinely the direction the industry is heading, but the harnesses are slow, OS-pinned, and flaky enough that a two-person team will end up disabling the job. The manual 90-minute pass in check #4 gets most of the same signal at a fraction of the maintenance cost.
- Scanning every page. Enterprise programs crawl the whole app and track a site-wide violation count. Here that turns 253 findings into 900 and produces a number nobody can act on. Scan six public pages as a gate and ~15 authenticated pages nightly; that is where the risk and the traffic are.
- A hosted Lighthouse CI server with historical dashboards and per-PR performance-plus-a11y trend graphs. This is infrastructure to run, and the accessibility signal it produces is a subset of what axe already gives you. It also depends on hosting decisions that are explicitly out of scope for this exercise.
- Component-level a11y unit tests (jest-axe / karma-accessibility-checker) across the shared component library. Sound practice at scale, but for ~27k lines of TS built largely on ABP's supplied Angular components it duplicates the page-level axe run while adding a second set of fixtures to maintain. Revisit only if the team starts authoring genuinely custom interactive widgets (a custom combobox, tree, or date picker) -- those are the components page-level scanning covers worst.
- Targeting WCAG Level AAA. No US or California legal instrument requires it, W3C does not recommend AAA as a general policy for entire sites, and several AAA criteria (e.g. 3.3.9 Accessible Authentication (Enhanced)) are in direct tension with the security hardening this app needs for public exposure.
- Usability testing with disabled users, an accessibility persona program, or a named a11y champion role. All are real practices at companies with an accessibility function. With no QA, no DevOps and one developer-week per month, adopting any of them means dropping the merge gates that actually prevent regressions.
- Turning on `angular.configs.templateAll`, or including axe's `best-practice` tag in the merge gate. Both expand the finding count with items that are not WCAG failures (landmark-*, region, heading-order, page-has-heading-one, stylistic template rules). The whole design of this recommendation is to keep the gated rule set small enough that nobody wants to bypass it.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`lint / angular-eslint`** -- tool: angular-eslint 20.7.0 (`angular.configs.templateAccessibility`) on ESLint ^9.39; bulk suppressions from ESLint 9.24.0

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 3-5 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Runs ESLint over *.ts and *.html with `...angular.configs.templateRecommended` plus `...angular.configs.templateAccessibility` in the HTML block -- 12 accessibility rules including label-has-associated-control, valid-aria, click-events-have-key-events, interactive-supports-focus, role-has-required-aria and alt-text -- all set to 'error'. The 253 pre-existing violations are banked once with `npx eslint --suppress-all` into a committed `eslint-suppressions.json`, so CI fails only on NEW violations. A monthly `--prune-suppressions` run ratchets the baseline down as files get touched.
- Why at this team size: Highest value per hour in the whole area. It is one config block plus one command, it runs in the lint job they almost certainly already have, it adds no new CI service, no browser, no flakiness, and zero runtime. Bulk suppressions mean day one is green -- nobody is asked to fix 253 things -- while every new template written from now on is held to WCAG-relevant rules. Crucially it is a *prevention* gate: at ~27k lines of TS growing weekly, the cheapest a11y strategy is to stop the bleeding rather than remediate. Do NOT also turn on templateAll; that adds stylistic noise and the team will start bypassing.
- Tool maintenance evidence: angular-eslint latest 22.1.0 published 2026-07-12; the v20 line's last release is 20.7.0 on 2025-11-22 and is the correct pin for Angular 20.3.x (peer eslint ^8.57.0 || ^9.0.0) -- https://registry.npmjs.org/angular-eslint . ESLint 9.x still maintained: 9.39.5 published 2026-07-10 -- https://registry.npmjs.org/eslint . Suppressions feature docs: https://eslint.org/docs/latest/use/suppressions

**`a11y / axe-public-flows`** -- tool: @axe-core/playwright 4.13.0 + @playwright/test 1.62.1

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 10-14 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A Playwright spec that visits the six anonymous-reachable pages -- tenant landing, registration (each of the four external roles if the form differs), login, forgot-password, reset-password, document upload -- and runs `new AxeBuilder({page}).withTags(['wcag2a','wcag2aa','wcag21a','wcag21aa','wcag22aa']).analyze()`. Fails the build on any violation with impact 'critical' or 'serious'. For 'moderate'/'minor', it compares the sorted set of violation rule-ids against a committed per-page JSON baseline and fails only when a NEW rule id appears. 'best-practice' tag is deliberately excluded.
- Why at this team size: Scope it to the pages an anonymous plaintiff-side tester can reach, because those are the only pages that generate an Unruh demand letter, and there are about six of them. Six pages is a half-day of test authoring, not a program. The critical/serious-only gate plus a rule-id baseline means the build does not turn red over pre-existing moderate findings, which is the failure mode that gets a11y gates disabled. If they already run Playwright for e2e, this is an additional spec file, not new infrastructure. Deliberately excluding 'best-practice' keeps the finding list to things that are actually WCAG failures.
- Tool maintenance evidence: @axe-core/playwright 4.13.0 published 2026-08-11 with release-candidate builds as recent as 2026-08-28; axe-core 4.13.0 published 2026-08-05 with daily canaries -- https://registry.npmjs.org/axe-core and https://registry.npmjs.org/@axe-core/playwright . @playwright/test 1.62.1 published 2026-07-30 -- https://registry.npmjs.org/@playwright/test . API confirmed at https://github.com/dequelabs/axe-core-npm/blob/develop/packages/playwright/README.md

**`a11y / axe-nightly`** -- tool: @axe-core/playwright 4.13.0 in a GitHub Actions `schedule` workflow

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 4-6 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Same axe configuration run on a scheduled workflow against a broader page list -- the 10-15 highest-traffic authenticated screens (scheduling calendar, appointment detail, document list, admin user management) -- reporting all impact levels to a job summary and opening/updating a single tracking issue. Never blocks a PR. Because tenancy resolves from the Host header, it runs against at least two tenant hosts so that per-office theming (colour contrast especially) is covered.
- Why at this team size: Authenticated screens are behind a login, so they carry far lower drive-by-litigation risk than the public pages and do not justify a merge gate. A nightly advisory run gives visibility and a work queue they can pull from during the one dev-week per month, without ever being the reason a PR is stuck at 6pm. The multi-tenant-host detail matters and costs almost nothing once the first spec exists: contrast failures introduced by one office's theme are literally invisible if you only ever scan one Host header.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Same as above: @axe-core/playwright 4.13.0, 2026-08-11 -- https://registry.npmjs.org/@axe-core/playwright

**`a11y-manual-release-pass`** -- tool: Keyboard + NVDA/VoiceOver + a checklist file in the repo; no CI tooling

- Posture: `local-only` | Effort: 6-8 to write the checklist, then 2-3 per release | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A written 60-90 minute checklist run by one developer before each release, covering only the six public flows: full keyboard-only traversal (no mouse) checking visible focus and no keyboard traps; one screen reader pass (NVDA on Windows or VoiceOver on macOS) through registration and login; and explicit checks of the WCAG 2.2 criteria no scanner can see -- 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (is there a non-cognitive alternative to any CAPTCHA?), 2.5.7 Dragging Movements (is there a click/keyboard alternative to drag-and-drop upload?), 3.3.7 Redundant Entry, 3.2.6 Consistent Help, 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (does a sticky header or toast cover the focused field?).
- Why at this team size: This is the only check that catches the failures most likely to be introduced by going public. Hardening a public registration form usually means adding a CAPTCHA; hardening document upload usually means adding drag-and-drop. Both are WCAG 2.2 Level AA failures that every scanner in this list will report as green. A single keyboard-only pass over six pages also finds focus traps in ABP/Angular Material modals faster than any automated rule. It is deliberately NOT a CI gate -- a two-person team cannot automate screen-reader testing sustainably, and pretending otherwise produces a flaky job that gets deleted in three weeks.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Not a tool. Justified by W3C WAI: 'Web accessibility evaluation tools can not determine accessibility, they can only assist in doing so' -- https://www.w3.org/WAI/test-evaluate/tools/selecting/ ; and by axe-core carrying only one wcag22aa-tagged rule (target-size) -- https://github.com/dequelabs/axe-core/blob/develop/doc/rule-descriptions.md

**`a11y / lighthouse-advisory`** -- tool: @lhci/cli 0.15.1 (or lighthouse 13.4.1 invoked directly)

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Optional Lighthouse run on the public landing and login pages asserting `"categories:accessibility": ["error", {"minScore": 0.9}]` in lighthouserc, with `numberOfRuns: 3` and median aggregation. Advisory only -- reports to the PR, never blocks.
- Why at this team size: Include this ONLY if a Lighthouse job already exists for performance budgets -- then the accessibility category is a free extra assertion. Do not stand it up solely for accessibility. Its engine is axe-core under the hood ('Weighting is based on axe user impact assessments'), so it finds a strict subset of what check #2 already finds, and the runner is 14 months stale. A score of 100 is not a conformance claim and should never be reported to a customer as one.
- Tool maintenance evidence: FAILS THE FRESHNESS BAR: @lhci/cli latest is 0.15.1 published 2025-06-25 (14 months old) -- https://registry.npmjs.org/@lhci/cli -- and the last commit on GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci main is 2025-06-26 -- https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/commits/main . Lighthouse core itself is healthy at 13.4.1, published 2026-07-20 -- https://registry.npmjs.org/lighthouse . Config syntax verified at https://github.com/GoogleChrome/lighthouse-ci/blob/main/docs/configuration.md

**`a11y-conformance-report`** -- tool: ITI VPAT 2.5Rev (April 2025), WCAG or INT edition

- Posture: `local-only` | Effort: 8-12 | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A public /accessibility page stating the conformance target (WCAG 2.2 Level AA), the known gaps, and a monitored contact address for accessibility problems; plus a completed VPAT 2.5Rev (WCAG or INT edition) held internally, ready to hand to procurement. The statement must describe conformance honestly -- 'we target WCAG 2.2 AA and test with axe and manual review' -- not claim conformance the manual pass has not established.
- Why at this team size: Not a CI check, but it is the artifact that actually gets asked for, and the moment it gets asked for it is urgent. Both indirect legal routes run through procurement -- 28 CFR 35.200(a) and 45 CFR 84.84(a) both cover content a covered organization provides 'through contractual, licensing, or other arrangements', and Cal. Gov. Code 7405 binds 'any entity that contracts with a state or local entity subject to Section 11135'. With 11 offices at launch and headroom to 33, the first California city, county, school district or state-agency tenant will send a VPAT request. Writing it after check #4 exists is a day's work; writing it under a contract deadline with no test evidence is a scramble. Have counsel review the wording -- an overstated conformance claim is itself a liability.
- Tool maintenance evidence: VPAT 2.5Rev released April 2025; the WCAG edition covers 'WCAG 2.0 or ISO/IEC 40500..., WCAG 2.1, and WCAG 2.2'; a completed VPAT is an Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) -- https://www.itic.org/policy/accessibility/vpat



## 12. Multi-tenant isolation testing

**Verification verdict for this area: `minor-corrections`** (16 claims checked)

**Material errors the verifier flagged in this area:**

- [2] Citation/threshold overreach: OWASP API1:2023 says "Do not deploy changes that make the tests fail" -- 'deploy', not 'block merges'. The page also says nothing about 'not by manual review'. And the API5:2023 half of the claim is NOT on the cited API1 URL; it needs its own citation (https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa5-broken-function-level-authorization/), where the denial-by-default line is in 'How to prevent' but the 'don't assume administrative based on URL path' line is under 'Is the API Vulnerable?', not 'How to prevent'.
- [6] The cited Kestrel host-filtering page does NOT mention the project template or a wildcard AllowedHosts value. The page's own statement is the opposite framing: 'Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default. To enable the middleware, define an AllowedHosts key'. The template's "AllowedHosts": "*" is a real but separately-sourced fact (dotnet new appsettings.json) and needs its own citation.
- [8] The cited AbpAspNetCoreMultiTenancyModule.cs registers ONLY the four resolvers (QueryString, Route, Header, Cookie). It contains no CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor and no AddDomainTenantResolver. Those live in framework/src/Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/AbpMultiTenancyModule.cs (TenantResolvers.Insert(0, new CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor())) and framework/src/Volo.Abp.AspNetCore.MultiTenancy/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/AbpMultiTenancyOptionsExtensions.cs (InsertAfter(r => r is CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor, ...)). TenantResolverConsts is in Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy.Abstractions, not the cited assembly path. The substance is correct; the single-URL citation is not.
- [10] The cookie write is NOT unconditional on query-string resolution: it sits inside 'if (tenant?.Id != _currentTenant.Id)' and inside 'if (_tenantResolveResultAccessor.Result != null && ...AppliedResolvers.Contains(QueryStringTenantResolveContributor.ContributorName))'. Also, 'persistent' is not shown on the cited file -- the 10-year Expires (DateTimeOffset.Now.AddYears(10), Path=/, HttpOnly=false, IsEssential=true) is in AbpMultiTenancyCookieHelper.cs. Cite both files.
- [13] The cited DistributedCacheKeyNormalizer.cs supports only the key format and the two omission conditions. The TenantConfigurationCacheItem facts ([IgnoreMultiTenancy], CacheKeyFormat "i:{0},n:{1}") are in Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy.Abstractions/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/TenantConfigurationCacheItem.cs, and the FullName-minus-'CacheItem' naming rule is in Volo.Abp.Caching/CacheNameAttribute.cs. All three sub-facts are true but need those citations.
- [14] The cited data-filtering.md does not contain two of the four listed constructs: [IgnoreMultiTenancy] on a DbContext and BlobContainerConfiguration.IsMultiTenant = false appear nowhere in that document. Its AbpDataFilterOptions example also uses typeof(ISoftDelete), not typeof(IMultiTenant), and its Disable<T>() example uses ISoftDelete. BlobContainerConfiguration.IsMultiTenant (default true) is in framework/src/Volo.Abp.BlobStoring/Volo/Abp/BlobStoring/BlobContainerConfiguration.cs.
- [16] 'the only in-database isolation control' is not supported: the same file also builds a database-side variant via AbpEfCoreDataFilterDbFunctionMethods.MultiTenantFilter when UseDbFunction() is true, and database-per-tenant separation via MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver is a second in-database control. The 'raw SQL is outside the filter' caveat is true of EF Core global filters generally but is not stated on this page.

### Standard, in summary

The 2026 standard is that tenant isolation is an *authorization* property that must be proven by executable tests, not by reading the ORM. OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 makes this an explicit, citable requirement: 8.4.1 "Verify that multi-tenant applications use cross-tenant controls to ensure consumer operations will never affect tenants with which they do not have permissions to interact" (Level 2), sitting on top of 8.2.1/8.2.2 (function-level access and BOLA, both Level 1). OWASP API Security Top 10 API1:2023 goes further and requires the tests be a merge gate: "Create tests evaluating authorization mechanism vulnerability; reject changes that cause test failures." Because tenancy here is derived from an attacker-supplied HTTP header, the standard also pulls in WSTG-INPV-17 (Host header injection) and the PortSwigger host-header bypass catalogue -- Host, X-Forwarded-Host, X-Host, X-Forwarded-Server, X-HTTP-Host-Override, Forwarded, duplicate Host lines, absolute-form request targets, line wrapping and port injection -- each of which must have a test asserting the *resolved tenant*, not just the HTTP status. Critically, an ABP unit or integration test proves almost none of this: the ABP startup template runs tests against a single in-memory SQLite database, so connection-string routing, cross-database leakage, Redis key scoping, background-job tenant context and token/claims-vs-host binding are all structurally untestable in the shipped harness. A rigorous standard therefore requires (a) at least two real, separate tenant databases in the test run, (b) HTTP-level tenant-resolution tests through the real middleware pipeline, (c) a static deny-list for the handful of one-line framework switches that disable isolation globally, and (d) out-of-band data-plane audits (per-database TenantId fingerprint, Redis key prefix audit) that catch misrouting the application itself reports as success.

### Claims and sources

**A12.1** `[partially]` OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 contains an explicit, dedicated multi-tenancy requirement: multi-tenant applications must use cross-tenant controls ensuring consumer operations never affect tenants they have no permission to interact with. It is a Level 2 requirement, so it applies to any application handling sensitive data such as PHI.

- Threshold: `ASVS 5.0.0 requirement 8.4.1, Level 2 (chapter V8 Authorization, section V8.4 'Other Authorization Considerations')`
- Source: OWASP Application Security Verification Standard 5.0.0, V8 Authorization (raw source in OWASP/ASVS repo) -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/5.0/en/0x17-V8-Authorization.md
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/ASVS/master/README.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Fetched the chapter file directly and read requirement 8.4.1 verbatim. The repo README confirms 'Latest Stable Version - 5.0.0 ... (dated May 2025)', so 5.0.0 is the current edition to verify against in 2026, not 4.0.3. Note the chapter's own References section points to WSTG Authorization Testing and the OWASP Authorization Cheat Sheet, which is the recommended chain for building the actual test cases. Companion Level 1 requirements that this system must also meet: 8.2.1 (function-level access restricted to consumers with explicit permissions) and 8.2.2 (data-specific access restricted, explicitly naming IDOR and BOLA), plus 8.3.1 (enforce authorization at a trusted service layer, not client-side). ASVS Level 3 items in this chapter (8.1.3, 8.1.4, 8.3.2, 8.3.3, 8.4.2) are out of proportion for this team.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** ID, section title, requirement text and Level 2 are confirmed verbatim -- that part is exactly right. The trailing gloss 'so it applies to any application handling sensitive data such as PHI' is NOT on this page; V8 contains no level definitions. Drop it or cite the ASVS 5.0 levels chapter (0x03-Using-ASVS / verification-levels) separately. Note 8.4.2 (Level 3, admin interfaces) is the only other requirement in V8.4.
- Verifier read from the page: "## V8.4 Other Authorization Considerations ... | **8.4.1** | Verify that multi-tenant applications use cross-tenant controls to ensure consumer operations will never affect tenants with which they do not have permissions to interact. | 2 |"

**A12.2** `[partially]` OWASP API Security Top 10 requires that object-level authorization be covered by automated tests that block merges, not by manual review: prevention guidance explicitly says to create tests evaluating the authorization mechanism and reject changes that fail them. The companion function-level entry requires denial-by-default and warns that endpoints must not be assumed administrative based on URL path alone.

- Threshold: `API1:2023 Broken Object Level Authorization; API5:2023 Broken Function Level Authorization`
- Source: OWASP API Security Top 10 2023 (API1:2023 and API5:2023) -- https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa1-broken-object-level-authorization/
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa5-broken-function-level-authorization/
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Both pages opened. API1 'How To Prevent' includes 'Create tests evaluating authorization mechanism vulnerability; reject changes that cause test failures' -- this is the direct basis for making a tenant-isolation test suite a required CI gate rather than advisory. API5's 'Is the API Vulnerable?' questions map cleanly onto tenant tests: can a user from group X access a function scoped to group Y by guessing the endpoint; can they change HTTP method to reach a sensitive action. Neither page addresses whether scanners can find these flaws; in practice generic DAST cannot, because it has no model of which tenant owns which object.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Corrected: API1:2023 says 'Write tests to evaluate the vulnerability of the authorization mechanism. Do not deploy changes that make the tests fail.' -- the gate is DEPLOY, not MERGE, and the page never contrasts this with manual review. The API5:2023 half is not on this URL; it is verified separately at https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa5-broken-function-level-authorization/ where 'How to prevent' says 'The enforcement mechanism(s) should deny all access by default, requiring explicit grants to specific roles for access to every function', and the URL-path warning ('Don't assume that an API endpoint is regular or administrative only based on the URL path') is under 'Is the API Vulnerable?', not 'How to prevent'.
- Verifier read from the page: "Write tests to evaluate the vulnerability of the authorization mechanism. Do not deploy changes that make the tests fail."

**A12.3** `[yes]` OWASP publishes a specific test for Host header injection that requires substituting an attacker domain into the Host header and, when that is blocked, retrying with X-Forwarded-Host; a vulnerability is indicated when the application's behaviour or generated links reflect the attacker-supplied host. The named exploitation classes include accessing private/internal virtual hosts, password-reset poisoning and cache poisoning.

- Threshold: `WSTG-INPV-17 'Testing for Host Header Injection'`
- Source: OWASP Web Security Testing Guide (latest), Input Validation Testing -- https://owasp.org/www-project-web-security-testing-guide/latest/4-Web_Application_Security_Testing/07-Input_Validation_Testing/17-Testing_for_Host_Header_Injection
- Cross-reference: https://portswigger.net/web-security/host-header
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Test ID and How-to-Test steps read verbatim from the WSTG 'latest' branch. Caveat: I verified the ID in the 'latest' tree only; WSTG stable numbering has historically differed, so cite the version you test against. For this application the 'accessing private virtual hosts' scenario is the exact threat: an unmatched Host does not error, it silently selects the host/landlord context (see the ABP DomainTenantResolveContributor entry below). Password-reset poisoning is also directly in scope because password reset is one of the anonymous endpoints going public.

**A12.4** `[yes]` Host-header validation must be tested against a specific catalogue of bypasses, not just a single spoofed Host: override headers X-Forwarded-Host, X-Host, X-Forwarded-Server, X-HTTP-Host-Override and Forwarded; an absolute-form request target ('GET https://vulnerable-website.com/ HTTP/1.1' with a different Host line); duplicate Host header lines; space-indented line wrapping; and injecting into the port position ('Host: vulnerable-website.com:bad-stuff-here').

- Threshold: `5 override header names, plus absolute-URI, duplicate-Host, line-wrapping and port-injection variants`
- Source: PortSwigger Web Security Academy -- Exploiting HTTP Host header vulnerabilities (secondary/vendor source, but the canonical catalogue) -- https://portswigger.net/web-security/host-header/exploiting
- Cross-reference: https://owasp.org/www-project-web-security-testing-guide/latest/4-Web_Application_Security_Testing/07-Input_Validation_Testing/17-Testing_for_Host_Header_Injection
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Labelled secondary per the evidence rules: PortSwigger is a vendor, not a standards body. I am citing it because it is the most complete public enumeration of the bypass techniques and OWASP WSTG covers only Host and X-Forwarded-Host. The prevention guidance on the parent page is the design rule to test for: 'Don't support Host override headers ... in particular X-Forwarded-Host', and validate Host against an allowlist of permitted domains.

**A12.5** `[yes]` An HTTP origin server MUST ignore the Host header entirely when the request-target is in absolute-form and use the authority from the request-target instead; and a server MUST return 400 to any HTTP/1.1 request lacking a Host field, containing more than one Host field line, or containing an invalid Host value. This means a tenant-resolution test suite must cover absolute-form and duplicate-Host requests, because the value the framework sees may not be the value a proxy or WAF inspected.

- Threshold: `RFC 9112 Section 3.2.2 (absolute-form) and Section 3.2 (Host field rules; 400 Bad Request)`
- Source: RFC 9112 -- HTTP/1.1 (IETF) -- https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9112.html
- Cross-reference: https://portswigger.net/web-security/host-header/exploiting
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Quoted from the RFC text. This is the specification-level reason the absolute-URI test case exists and is not paranoia: it is mandated divergent behaviour. I did not empirically verify how Kestrel 10 resolves absolute-form request targets into HttpContext.Request.Host -- that is exactly why it belongs in the test suite as an assertion about the resolved tenant rather than as an assumption.

**A12.6** `[partially]` Kestrel does not validate the Host header at all, and ASP.NET Core's host-filtering middleware is disabled by default; it only activates when an AllowedHosts key is defined in configuration as a semicolon-delimited list of hostnames without ports. The default ASP.NET Core project template ships AllowedHosts set to a wildcard, which leaves filtering effectively off.

- Threshold: `AllowedHosts configuration key in appsettings.json, e.g. "AllowedHosts": "example.com;localhost"`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Host filtering with ASP.NET Core Kestrel web server (aspnetcore-10.0) -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/servers/kestrel/host-filtering
- Cross-reference: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/host-and-deploy/proxy-load-balancer
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: Verified verbatim: 'Host headers aren't validated' and 'Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default. To enable the middleware, define an AllowedHosts key'. Marked partial because the specific claim that the current .NET 10 web template emits "AllowedHosts": "*" is from my own knowledge of the template, not from a page I opened -- treat it as a thing to check in the repo rather than a cited fact. The actionable, fully-cited part is: filtering is off unless you explicitly set AllowedHosts, and this app's tenancy depends entirely on the Host value.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** First three sub-claims confirmed verbatim. The fourth ('default template ships AllowedHosts set to a wildcard') is NOT on this page -- the page shows only the example "AllowedHosts": "example.com;localhost" and never mentions the template or "*". Cite the template's appsettings.json separately, and note the mechanism is different: with "*" the middleware is added by CreateDefaultBuilder/AddHostFiltering but permits every host, rather than being 'disabled'.
- Verifier read from the page: "Host headers aren't validated. ... Host-filtering middleware is disabled by default. To enable the middleware, define an AllowedHosts key in appsettings.json/appsettings.{Environment}.json. The value is a semicolon-delimited list of host names without port numbers"

**A12.7** `[yes]` If forwarded-headers middleware is enabled with the XForwardedHost forwarder, HttpContext.Request.Host is overwritten from the client-supplied X-Forwarded-Host header. ForwardedHeadersOptions.AllowedHosts, which restricts acceptable X-Forwarded-Host values, defaults to an empty list meaning all hosts are allowed; ForwardedHeaders itself defaults to None so the middleware does nothing until explicitly configured. Microsoft links this behaviour to an elevation-of-privilege advisory where a proxy fails to restrict Host headers to known-good values.

- Threshold: `ForwardedHeadersOptions.AllowedHosts default = empty IList<string> (all hosts allowed); ForwardedHeadersOptions.ForwardedHeaders default = ForwardedHeaders.None; KnownNetworks default = loopback /8; KnownProxies default = IPv6 loopback; ForwardLimit default = 1; CVE-2018-0787`
- Source: Microsoft Learn -- Configure ASP.NET Core to work with proxy servers and load balancers (aspnetcore-10.0) -- https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/host-and-deploy/proxy-load-balancer
- Cross-reference: https://portswigger.net/web-security/host-header/exploiting
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read the options table verbatim. Two traps worth a test each: (1) the same Microsoft page shows a sample that calls options.KnownNetworks.Clear(); options.KnownProxies.Clear(); which removes the only spoofing protection, and this snippet is very widely copy-pasted; (2) setting the environment variable ASPNETCORE_FORWARDEDHEADERS_ENABLED=true enables forwarding with cloud-oriented settings and, per the page, 'doesn't enable features such as the KnownProxies option to restrict which IPs forwarders are accepted from'. Whether a reverse proxy sits in front is an infrastructure decision and out of scope here; the in-scope, code-level control is that the app must not accept X-Forwarded-Host from an untrusted peer, and the test asserts the resolved tenant when that header is spoofed.

**A12.9** `[yes]` ABP's domain tenant resolver reads httpContext.Request.Host.Value and sets context.Handled = true whenever the Host has any value at all -- including when the host does not match the configured domain format. Because the resolver chain stops at the first contributor where Handled is true or a tenant was found, a request bearing an unrecognised Host is not rejected: it resolves to a null tenant, i.e. the host/landlord context, and the chain never reaches the __tenant resolvers.

- Threshold: `DomainTenantResolveContributor.GetTenantIdOrNameFromHttpContextOrNullAsync sets context.Handled = true unconditionally after the Host check; TenantResolveContext.HasResolvedTenantOrHost() returns 'Handled || TenantIdOrName != null'`
- Source: ABP Framework source -- DomainTenantResolveContributor.cs, TenantResolveContext.cs, TenantResolver.cs -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/framework/src/Volo.Abp.AspNetCore.MultiTenancy/Volo/Abp/AspNetCore/MultiTenancy/DomainTenantResolveContributor.cs
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/framework/src/Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy.Abstractions/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/TenantResolveContext.cs
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read all three files. This is the single most important behaviour to test in this application. It means Host-header spoofing does not produce an error page -- it produces a silent downgrade to the host context, where the tenant list, tenant connection strings and landlord admin identity live. Anonymous endpoints (registration, login, password reset, document upload) are the ones that will execute in that context. The correct assertion is not 'returns 400' but 'ICurrentTenant.Id for this request equals the expected tenant', which requires an HTTP-level test with an inspectable resolved-tenant signal (a test-only endpoint or a log/response header emitted in the test environment).

Test case: assert no Set-Cookie for __tenant is ever emitted in production configuration, and that a request carrying a forged __tenant cookie resolves to the host-derived tenant, not the cookie's.

**A12.11** `[yes]` ABP's multi-tenant connection-string resolver falls back silently to the application's Default connection string when the current tenant has no connection string configured. There is no exception and no warning: a tenant whose connection string is missing or blank reads and writes the shared/host database, and the EF Core tenant filter then makes the result look merely empty rather than wrong.

- Threshold: `MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver.ResolveAsync: 'if (tenant == null || tenant.ConnectionStrings.IsNullOrEmpty()) { //Tenant has not defined any connection string, fallback to default logic; return await base.ResolveAsync(connectionStringName); }'`
- Source: ABP Framework source -- MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver.cs -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/framework/src/Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver.cs

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read the resolver source and the connection-strings doc, which confirms the resolver 'fallbacks to the base logic if no connection string specified for the current tenant'. With 11 offices at launch and headroom to 33, tenant onboarding is the highest-risk repeated operation in this system, and this is its failure mode. Compounding it: UnitOfWorkDbContextProvider.GetDatabaseApiKey keys cached DbContext instances as '{DbContextType.FullName}_{SHA256(connectionString)}', so two tenants that both fall back to Default share one DbContext inside a unit of work, leaving only the EF query filter between them. Also note ResolveConnectionStringAsync forces the HOST connection string for any DbContext type marked [IgnoreMultiTenancy].

**A12.12** `[yes]` Background job tenant context in ABP is opt-in per job-args class. The executor derives the tenant from the job arguments only if that args type implements IMultiTenant; otherwise it falls back to the ambient ICurrentTenant.Id, which in a background worker process is normally null -- so the job executes in the host context and, under database-per-tenant, against the host database.

- Threshold: `BackgroundJobExecuter.GetJobArgsTenantId: 'jobArgs switch { IMultiTenant multiTenantJobArgs => multiTenantJobArgs.TenantId, _ => CurrentTenant.Id }', wrapped in using(CurrentTenant.Change(...))`

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Read the executor source verbatim. Cross-checked the second source and found the opposite of confirmation, which is itself the finding: ABP's background-jobs documentation page contains zero occurrences of the word 'tenant', as does the background-workers page. The framework behaviour is real but undocumented, so a developer adding a job has no prompt to implement IMultiTenant on the args class. This yields a two-hour reflection test with very high value: enumerate all IBackgroundJob<T>/IAsyncBackgroundJob<T> implementations in the solution and assert every T implements IMultiTenant.

**A12.13** `[partially]` Redis keys are tenant-scoped only when data goes through ABP's typed IDistributedCache<TCacheItem>, which prefixes 't:{tenantId},' via DistributedCacheKeyNormalizer. Injecting Microsoft's raw IDistributedCache bypasses the normalizer entirely and produces unprefixed, cross-tenant keys, and the [IgnoreMultiTenancy] attribute on a cache-item class deliberately removes the prefix. ABP's own tenant configuration cache -- which carries each tenant's connection string -- is marked [IgnoreMultiTenancy] and is therefore stored under a global, unprefixed key.

- Threshold: `Key format: 't:{CurrentTenant.Id},c:{CacheName},k:{KeyPrefix}{Key}'; prefix omitted when args.IgnoreMultiTenancy is true or CurrentTenant.Id is null. TenantConfigurationCacheItem is decorated [IgnoreMultiTenancy]; its cache name defaults to the type FullName minus the 'CacheItem' suffix, i.e. 'Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy.TenantConfiguration', with key format 'i:{id},n:{name}'.`

- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified

Define IgnoreMultiTenancy attribute on the cache item class to disable this'. TenantConfiguration carries a ConnectionStrings property and is serialised into the cache item, so every office's SQL Server connection string -- credentials included -- sits in Redis under a globally-readable, non-tenant-prefixed key. Redis hardening is infrastructure and out of scope; the in-scope controls are (a) an automated audit that every Redis key either carries a t:{guid} prefix or is on an explicit allowlist, and (b) a decision about encrypting tenant connection strings at rest in the tenant store.

**A12.14** `[partially]` Tenant isolation can be switched off globally or per-artifact by several single-line changes, each of which is invisible in a code review that is not looking for it: DataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>() in a using block; AbpDataFilterOptions.DefaultStates[typeof(IMultiTenant)] = new DataFilterState(isEnabled: false) which disables the filter application-wide; [IgnoreMultiTenancy] on a DbContext which forces the host connection string; and BlobContainerConfiguration.IsMultiTenant = false which shares stored documents across all tenants.

The multi-tenancy page's only note on disabling the tenant filter is a *functionality* caveat -- 'this approach won't work if your tenants have separate databases since there is no built-in way to query from multiple database in a single database query' -- not a warning about leakage. In other words, ABP does not document DataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>() as a security-critical operation, so the guardrail has to come from your own CI. With one SQLite database, CurrentTenant.Change() exercises only the EF Core global query filter; connection-string routing, cross-database queries, the DbContext-per-connection-string cache key and the Default-connection-string fallback are all invisible. Any tenant-isolation suite must therefore run against at least two genuinely separate databases.

Also note MongoDB tests use EphemeralMongo, not SQLite.

**A12.16** `[partially]` The EF Core global query filter is the only in-database isolation control ABP applies, it is applied per entity type at model-build time, and its correctness depends on a compiled-query cache key that includes the current tenant id and the filter states. It is applied only to entities implementing IMultiTenant and only to LINQ queries -- raw SQL execution paths are outside it.

- Threshold: `AbpDbContext.CreateFilterExpression: 'e => !IsMultiTenantFilterEnabled || EF.Property<Guid>(e, multiTenantPropertyName) == CurrentTenantId'; AbpDbContext.GetCompiledQueryCacheKey(): '{CurrentTenantId ?? "Null"}:{IsSoftDeleteFilterEnabled}:{IsMultiTenantFilterEnabled}'`
- Source: ABP Framework source -- AbpDbContext.cs -- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/framework/src/Volo.Abp.EntityFrameworkCore/Volo/Abp/EntityFrameworkCore/AbpDbContext.cs
- Cross-reference: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/docs/en/framework/infrastructure/data-filtering.md
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: The filter expression and cache-key code were read directly and are verified. Marked partial on one sub-claim: I did not open Microsoft's EF Core documentation on whether global query filters compose over FromSqlRaw, so treat 'raw SQL bypasses the filter' as the reason to test rather than as a cited fact -- ExecuteSqlRaw and any Dapper usage certainly bypass it, FromSqlRaw composition is the uncertain case. Two testable consequences either way: (1) an entity that forgets to implement IMultiTenant gets no filter at all and ShouldFilterEntity will not flag it; (2) a row written while the tenant context is null lands with TenantId = null inside a tenant database and becomes permanently invisible to that tenant -- silent data loss rather than a leak, which no exception will surface.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** Both quoted thresholds match the file verbatim, and the per-entity-type / IMultiTenant-only scoping is confirmed (the filter is built inside 'if (typeof(IMultiTenant).IsAssignableFrom(typeof(TEntity)))' and applied via entityTypeBuilder.HasAbpQueryFilter). Two corrections: (1) drop 'the only in-database isolation control' -- the same method emits a DB-function variant, 'AbpEfCoreDataFilterDbFunctionMethods.MultiTenantFilter(((IMultiTenant)e).TenantId, CurrentTenantId, true)', when UseDbFunction() is true, and database-per-tenant separation via MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver is a second, independent control; (2) the 'raw SQL execution paths are outside it' caveat is correct EF Core behaviour but is not stated anywhere on this page -- cite EF Core docs or mark it as reasoning.
- Verifier read from the page: "Expression<Func<TEntity, bool>> multiTenantFilter = e => !IsMultiTenantFilterEnabled || EF.Property<Guid>(e, multiTenantPropertyName) == CurrentTenantId; ... public virtual string GetCompiledQueryCacheKey() { return $"{CurrentTenantId?.ToString() ?? \"Null\"}:{IsSoftDeleteFilterEnabled}:{IsMultiTenantFilterEnabled}"; }"

### Where sources disagree

- ABP contradicts itself on tenant resolver order. The multi-tenancy documentation and ABP's own first-party AI skill file (.claude/skills/abp-multi-tenancy/SKILL.md) both list resolution as: user claims, query string, route, header, cookie, then domain/subdomain LAST. The source disagrees: AddDomainTenantResolver calls TenantResolvers.InsertAfter(r => r is CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor, ...), placing the domain resolver SECOND. This materially changes whether ?__tenant= can override the hostname, so trust the source and pin the behaviour with a test. Docs: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/docs/en/framework/architecture/multi-tenancy/index.md vs source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/abpframework/abp/dev/framework/src/Volo.Abp.AspNetCore.MultiTenancy/Volo/Abp/MultiTenancy/AbpMultiTenancyOptionsExtensions.cs
- ABP and OWASP disagree on whether disabling the tenant filter is a security matter. ABP's strongest wording anywhere is hygiene -- 'Always use the Disable and Enable methods it inside a using block to guarantee that the filter is reset' -- and its only note specific to disabling the multi-tenancy filter is a functionality caveat about separate databases, not a leakage warning. OWASP ASVS 8.4.1 and API1:2023 treat any path that removes a cross-tenant control as an authorization defect requiring an explicit test. I did not find an ABP page that warns about DataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>() in security terms; if one exists I did not open it. Follow OWASP here: treat every disable site as a reviewed exception with a test.
- ABP's multi-tenancy documentation never mentions Host header spoofing at all, despite shipping a resolver that derives tenancy from Request.Host and despite OWASP (WSTG-INPV-17), Microsoft ('Host headers aren't validated') and PortSwigger all treating host-derived trust as a distinct vulnerability class. The absence is not a contradiction so much as a gap, but it means an ABP-only reading of the problem will miss the entire attack surface.
- Microsoft's own proxy documentation is internally inconsistent about forwarded-header trust. The options table states that requests must match KnownProxies or KnownNetworks before forwarded headers are processed, and that this 'limits header spoofing by not accepting forwarders from untrusted proxies' -- while a code sample on the same page shows options.KnownNetworks.Clear(); options.KnownProxies.Clear();, which removes exactly that protection. The page also notes that setting ASPNETCORE_FORWARDEDHEADERS_ENABLED=true 'doesn't enable features such as the KnownProxies option'. Both snippets are widely copy-pasted. Whichever pattern is in your code, assert the resulting behaviour with a spoofed X-Forwarded-Host test rather than reading the config.
- WSTG test-ID numbering differs between the 'latest' and 'stable' trees. I verified Host header injection as WSTG-INPV-17 in the latest tree and the authorization-bypass test as WSTG-ATHZ-02 in the stable tree; I did not cross-check either ID across both trees. Cite the version you actually test against.
- Semgrep's language-support page advertises C# at 'Generally available' maturity with 170+ Pro rules, but does not state on that page which capabilities require the commercial product versus the OSS CLI. My recommendation relies only on custom rules in the OSS CLI, which is well established, but I could not verify the OSS/paid boundary from the page I opened -- if you plan to lean on Semgrep's bundled C# rulesets rather than your own six rules, confirm licensing first.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The premise 'tenancy resolves from the HTTP Host header ONLY' is very likely false in the running application. ABP's AbpAspNetCoreMultiTenancyModule unconditionally registers QueryString, Route, Header and Cookie resolvers for __tenant, and AddDomainTenantResolver does not replace them -- it inserts the domain resolver after the current-user resolver, leaving all four behind it. Unless the code explicitly clears AbpTenantResolveOptions.TenantResolvers, ?__tenant=<other office's guid> is a live input on every anonymous endpoint. This is the first thing to check in the repo and the first test to write.
- A spoofed or unrecognised Host does not produce an error -- it produces the HOST (landlord) context. DomainTenantResolveContributor sets context.Handled = true whenever Request.Host has any value, including when the domain format does not match, and the resolver chain stops at the first Handled contributor. So Host: attacker.com yields a null tenant, which under database-per-tenant means the host database: the tenant registry, the connection strings, the landlord admin identity. The endpoints most exposed to this are exactly the ones going public for the first time -- registration, password reset, document upload.
- One crafted link can pin a victim's browser to another office. MultiTenancyMiddleware writes a persistent __tenant cookie whenever the query-string resolver was the one that applied. That converts a one-shot ?__tenant= parameter into durable tenant confusion for that browser, and it is emitted by the framework with no application code involved.
- A tenant with no connection string silently uses the shared/host database. MultiTenantConnectionStringResolver falls back to base resolution when tenant.ConnectionStrings is empty -- no exception, no log. With 11 offices at launch and headroom to 33, tenant onboarding is the most-repeated high-risk operation in the system, and its failure mode is invisible: the EF Core filter makes wrong-database results look merely empty. Assert connection-string distinctness on every onboarding.
- Two tenants that both fall back to the Default connection string share a DbContext instance. UnitOfWorkDbContextProvider keys cached DbContexts as {DbContextType}_{SHA256(connectionString)}, so identical connection strings collapse to one context -- leaving only the EF Core query filter between two offices in a system whose whole isolation story is 'separate databases'.
- Background jobs run as HOST unless the args class opts in. BackgroundJobExecuter takes the tenant from job args only if that type implements IMultiTenant, otherwise it inherits the ambient tenant, which in a worker is null. ABP's background-jobs and background-workers documentation pages contain zero occurrences of the word 'tenant', so nothing prompts a developer to get this right. A two-hour reflection test covers every job you will ever add.
- Every office's SQL Server connection string -- credentials included -- is cached in Redis under a global, non-tenant-prefixed key. TenantConfigurationCacheItem is decorated [IgnoreMultiTenancy], so the key normalizer omits the t:{tenantId} prefix, and TenantConfiguration serialises its ConnectionStrings property. The cache name resolves to 'Volo.Abp.MultiTenancy.TenantConfiguration'. Anyone who can read Redis holds the keys to all 11 databases.
- Redis tenant scoping only happens through ABP's typed IDistributedCache<T>. Inject Microsoft's raw IDistributedCache and you get unprefixed, cross-tenant keys with no warning from the compiler, the framework or code review. This is an easy and natural thing for an SDE 1 to write, and it is invisible in a diff.
- For authenticated requests the Host header is irrelevant -- the token wins. CurrentUserTenantResolveContributor is inserted at index 0 and short-circuits the chain, so the tenantid claim overrides the hostname. ABP never verifies that the token's tenant matches the host-resolved tenant. A tenant-A token replayed against tenantB.example.com is served tenant A's data. That is not a data leak, but it means your mental model of the system is wrong, and it means token/claims binding is a separate control from host resolution that needs its own test.
- ABP's own documentation is wrong about resolver order. Both the multi-tenancy doc and ABP's first-party AI skill file list the domain resolver LAST (position 6, after the cookie resolver). The source inserts it SECOND, immediately after the current-user resolver. If you reason from the docs about whether __tenant can override the hostname, you will reach the wrong conclusion.
- ABP's shipped test harness structurally cannot test your architecture. The startup template runs integration tests against a single in-memory SQLite database with a fresh database per test case, and ABP's three testing documentation pages mention tenants zero times. Every green test in that harness is compatible with completely broken connection-string routing. This is the concrete answer to 'what can unit tests against an ORM not prove'.
- Kestrel does not validate the Host header at all -- Microsoft states this plainly -- and host-filtering middleware stays disabled until you define AllowedHosts. In an application where the Host header IS the authorization boundary between eleven medical offices, that default is the whole ballgame, and it is one configuration key.
- A single line shares PHI documents across every office: BlobContainerConfiguration.IsMultiTenant = false. ABP documents it neutrally as a feature for sharing blobs between tenants. For a workers' comp document store on MinIO it is the highest-severity one-liner in the codebase, and it looks like ordinary configuration in a pull request.
- One line disables the tenant filter application-wide: AbpDataFilterOptions.DefaultStates[typeof(IMultiTenant)] = new DataFilterState(isEnabled: false). ABP's only comment on this is 'Carefully change defaults for global filters'. There is no framework-level guard, no analyzer warning, and no test in the default template that would fail.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- A dedicated third-party tenant-isolation penetration test per release. A larger org buys one every quarter or before each major release. Buy exactly ONE, scoped tightly to tenant resolution and cross-tenant access, timed for the window when the app is on the public internet but still holds only synthetic data -- that window is a genuine gift and it closes permanently the day real PHI lands. Recurring engagements are not defensible on two developers' budget or calendar.
- Authenticated multi-tenant DAST in CI (OWASP ZAP or Burp Enterprise crawling as tenant A and diffing against tenant B). This is standard at a company with an AppSec team. Here it is the wrong tool: a generic scanner has no model of which object belongs to which office, so it will not recognise a cross-tenant read as a finding, and maintaining authenticated scan sessions for 11 tenants is a permanent part-time job. Targeted xUnit assertions on the resolved tenant find strictly more, for less.
- A custom Roslyn analyzer NuGet package for the forbidden-construct rules. It is the 'proper' engineering answer and gives editor squiggles, but it is a separate project to build, version, publish and keep compiling against each .NET SDK bump. Six Semgrep rules -- or six ripgrep patterns -- get you the same CI enforcement this year for a tenth of the effort and no ongoing maintenance surface.
- Property-based or fuzz testing of the tenant resolver (generating random Host values and header combinations). The interesting inputs here are a small, enumerable, well-documented set -- the PortSwigger bypass catalogue is about a dozen cases. A hand-written table-driven test covers them exhaustively and readably. Fuzzing adds flaky runtime and a triage burden for no additional coverage.
- Provisioning all 11 (or 33) tenant databases in the test environment. Isolation is a pairwise property: every routing and leakage rule that can be violated between 33 tenants can be violated between 2. Two tenants plus the host proves the mechanism; 11 just multiplies test runtime, fixture complexity and CI cost. Scale-out belongs in the onboarding runbook, not the test matrix.
- Chaos or failure-injection testing on connection-string routing (killing a tenant database mid-request, corrupting the tenant cache). Valuable at a company with an on-call rotation that can act on what it learns. With two SDE 1s and no DevOps, the realistic outcome is a suite that breaks, gets muted, and teaches nothing.
- Database-level Row-Level Security in SQL Server as defence in depth behind the EF Core filter. This is a common recommendation and it is genuinely strong -- for SHARED-database multi-tenancy. You are database-per-tenant, so RLS defends against a threat your architecture has already eliminated, while adding migration complexity to 11 databases and a second place where isolation logic can drift out of sync with the first.
- ASVS Level 3 authorization requirements (8.1.3, 8.1.4, 8.3.2, 8.3.3, 8.4.2) -- documented environmental and contextual decision attributes, immediate propagation of authorization changes through self-contained tokens, delegated-identity token chaining, and layered device-posture controls on admin interfaces. Level 2 is the correct target for this application, and 8.4.1 (the multi-tenancy requirement) is itself Level 2. Reaching for L3 dilutes attention across requirements that will not be met rather than deepening the ones that must be.
- Continuous production leak detection via per-tenant honeytoken records and alerting. This is real practice at scale and it is the right eventual answer, but it presumes someone is watching an alert channel and can respond. Build the nightly tenant-data-fingerprint check first; it is the same idea at a cadence two people can actually sustain, and it works without a monitoring stack.
- A formal written threat model document satisfying ASVS 8.1.1/8.1.2's authorization-documentation requirements. The documentation requirements are Level 1 and you will eventually want them for HIPAA evidence anyway -- but as a first move, a one-page table mapping each of the four external roles to what they may touch is worth more than a document nobody re-reads. Do not spend a developer-week on documentation before the spoofing tests exist.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`tenant-resolution-spoofing`** -- tool: xUnit v3 + Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing (WebApplicationFactory), already present in the ABP template test stack

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 10-14 to build the harness and first 15 cases; ~1 per new case thereafter | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: HTTP-level xUnit tests through the real ASP.NET Core middleware pipeline (WebApplicationFactory) that fire a matrix of crafted requests at the four anonymous endpoints -- registration, login, password reset, document upload -- and assert the RESOLVED TENANT, not the status code. Matrix: valid Host for tenant A; Host for tenant B; unknown/attacker Host; no Host; duplicate Host lines; absolute-form request target with a conflicting Host; X-Forwarded-Host, X-Host, X-Forwarded-Server, X-HTTP-Host-Override and Forwarded set to tenant B while Host is tenant A; ?__tenant=<B guid>; __tenant header; __tenant cookie; and a tenant-A bearer token replayed against tenant B's hostname. Each case asserts an expected ICurrentTenant.Id, exposed in the Test environment only via a minimal diagnostic endpoint or a test-only response header. Also asserts no Set-Cookie for __tenant is ever emitted.
- Why at this team size: This is the highest value-per-hour check in the whole area and the only one that directly tests the stated top risk. It needs no Docker, no new tooling and no infrastructure decisions -- it runs in the existing test project against the existing in-memory database, because it asserts tenant RESOLUTION, not tenant DATA. It is also the check most likely to fail immediately: ABP registers the __tenant query-string, route, header and cookie resolvers by default, so the premise 'tenancy resolves from the Host header only' is probably false in the running app right now. Fifteen assertions that take an afternoon to write will tell you that on day one.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Microsoft.AspNetCore.Mvc.Testing 10.0.11 is current, and the 11.0.0-preview.7.26381.103 preview published 2026-08-11 (NuGet registration index, https://api.nuget.org/v3/registration5-gz-semver2/microsoft.aspnetcore.mvc.testing/page/10.0.11/11.0.0-preview.7.26381.103.json). xunit.v3 4.0.0 published 2026-08-15 (https://azuresearch-usnc.nuget.org/query?q=packageid:xunit.v3). Volo.Abp.TestBase 10.6.0 stable, 10.7.0-rc.3 published 2026-08-18 (https://api.nuget.org/v3/registration5-gz-semver2/volo.abp.testbase/page/10.0.0-rc.1/10.7.0-rc.3.json).

**`tenant-db-routing-tests`** -- tool: Testcontainers.MsSql with xUnit (replacing the template's single in-memory SQLite for this one test project). Cheap fallback if Docker in CI proves painful: two SQLite FILE databases with per-tenant connection strings -- this still proves routing, fallback and DbContext-cache-key behaviour, just not SQL Server semantics.

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 16-24 for the two-database harness and ~10 tests; the SQLite-file fallback is 4-6 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Integration tests running against TWO genuinely separate tenant databases plus a host database, proving the things a single-database ORM test cannot: that a query executed under tenant A's context physically hits A's database; that data written under A is absent from B's database at the SQL level (not merely filtered out); that a tenant configured with no connection string does NOT silently fall back to the Default/host database; that switching CurrentTenant inside one unit of work yields a different DbContext and a different physical connection; and that a DbContext marked [IgnoreMultiTenancy] is routed to the host. Assertions read the actual connection string and query the target database directly, so a wrong-database result cannot masquerade as an empty result.
- Why at this team size: ABP's own testing docs are silent on multi-tenancy and its template gives you one in-memory SQLite database, so without this the team has literally zero coverage of the mechanism their entire isolation model rests on. Cap it hard: about ten tests, one test project, running on PR. Do NOT try to provision all 11 offices -- two tenants plus the host proves every routing rule that 33 would. If Docker-in-Actions becomes a maintenance tax, take the two-SQLite-files version; it is worse fidelity but it still catches the silent Default-connection-string fallback, which is the specific bug most likely to bite during office onboarding.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Testcontainers.MsSql 4.14.0 published 2026-08-14, 32.4M total downloads (https://azuresearch-usnc.nuget.org/query?q=packageid:Testcontainers.MsSql and https://api.nuget.org/v3/registration5-gz-semver2/testcontainers.mssql/index.json). Core Testcontainers 4.14.0, 109M downloads.

**`background-job-tenant-context`** -- tool: xUnit + System.Reflection (no new dependency)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 2-3 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: A single reflection-driven test that enumerates every IBackgroundJob<T> and IAsyncBackgroundJob<T> implementation in the solution and asserts each argument type T implements IMultiTenant with a non-null TenantId at enqueue time. Optionally extends to distributed event ETOs and to asserting that every periodic background worker explicitly establishes a tenant scope rather than inheriting the ambient (host) context.
- Why at this team size: Two hours of work that closes a failure mode the team cannot reasonably be expected to know about: ABP's BackgroundJobExecuter derives tenant from job args only when the args class implements IMultiTenant, and otherwise falls back to the ambient tenant, which in a worker is null -- so the job runs against the host database. ABP's background-jobs documentation never mentions tenants, so nothing in the developer's normal workflow prompts them to get this right. A reflection test is self-maintaining: it automatically covers every job added in future without anyone remembering to write a test.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Uses only xunit.v3 4.0.0, published 2026-08-15 (https://azuresearch-usnc.nuget.org/query?q=packageid:xunit.v3) -- no additional tool to maintain.

**`tenancy-forbidden-constructs`** -- tool: Semgrep OSS with ~6 custom rules (C# support is Generally Available). A ripgrep-based shell step in the workflow is a legitimate 1-hour substitute if Semgrep feels heavy.

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 4-6 for the Semgrep rules; 1-2 for the ripgrep version | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Static deny-list scan for the small set of one-line changes that disable isolation: DataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>(); any write to AbpDataFilterOptions.DefaultStates for IMultiTenant; [IgnoreMultiTenancy] on entities, DbContexts or cache-item classes; BlobContainerConfiguration IsMultiTenant = false; constructor injection of Microsoft's raw IDistributedCache instead of ABP's IDistributedCache<T>; and FromSqlRaw/ExecuteSqlRaw/Dapper usage on multi-tenant entities. Findings require an inline allowlist annotation with a justification comment, so each one becomes a deliberate, reviewed decision.
- Why at this team size: With no senior reviewer on the team, code review will not reliably catch a single added line like container.IsMultiTenant = false -- especially since ABP's documentation presents these as ordinary configuration options and never flags DataFilter.Disable<IMultiTenant>() as security-critical. Six rules over about six lines of forbidden syntax is the cheapest durable guardrail available, and the allowlist-with-justification pattern means it produces near-zero recurring noise: a clean repo stays green, and the only time it speaks is when someone is about to do the exact thing you are afraid of.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Semgrep 1.175.0 published 2026-08-26 (https://pypi.org/pypi/semgrep/json). C# is listed at the 'Generally available' maturity tier on https://docs.semgrep.dev/supported-languages.
