- Threshold: `45 CFR 164.306(d)(3)(ii)(B)(1) and (2)`
- Source: GPO govinfo, CFR Title 45 Vol 2,  164.306 Security standards: General rules -- https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-306.xml
- Cross-reference: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/06/2024-30983/hipaa-security-rule-to-strengthen-the-cybersecurity-of-electronic-protected-health-information
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Cross-referenced against the NPRM preamble, which states HHS is 'concerned that some regulated entities proceed as if compliance with an addressable implementation specification is optional' and that this is a misreading. 164.306(b)(2) also lets you weigh size, capabilities, technical infrastructure and cost -- the explicit statutory basis for a two-person team scaling down.

**A14.3** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.312(b) Audit controls is a standard with NO implementation specifications: 'Implement hardware, software, and/or procedural mechanisms that record and examine activity in information systems that contain or use electronic protected health information.' OCR/CMS guidance states plainly that 'the Security Rule does not identify data that must be gathered by the audit controls or how often the audit reports should be reviewed.' FOR CODE: you choose the event schema, but it must cover ePHI access, not just writes. FOR CI: assert that read paths to ePHI emit audit records, since ABP/EF Core change-tracking captures mutations by default but reads are the ones that get missed.

- Threshold: `n/a -- no prescribed data set, no prescribed review frequency`
- Source: HHS/CMS HIPAA Security Series, Vol. 2 Paper 4, 'Security Standards: Technical Safeguards' (5/2005, rev. 3/2007) -- https://www.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/ocr/privacy/hipaa/administrative/securityrule/techsafeguards.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-312.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Downloaded the PDF and extracted the text directly (17 pages) rather than relying on a summarizer. This paper is still the document NIST SP 800-66r2 cites as 'Security Standards: Technical Safeguards'. It is old (2007) but has not been superseded and is still hosted by HHS.

**A14.4** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) Information system activity review is REQUIRED: 'Implement procedures to regularly review records of information system activity, such as audit logs, access reports, and security incident tracking reports.' Writing audit logs is not sufficient -- somebody must regularly review them and that review must be documented. FOR CODE: build a queryable review surface (anomalous cross-tenant access attempts, failed logins, bulk document downloads) rather than raw log dumps. FOR CI: this is the one obligation that CI cannot discharge; it needs a scheduled human ritual with a retained artifact.

- Threshold: `164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) -- Required; frequency is 'regularly', undefined by the rule`
- Source: GPO govinfo, CFR Title 45 Vol 2,  164.308 Administrative safeguards -- https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-308.xml
- Cross-reference: https://www.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/ocr/privacy/hipaa/administrative/securityrule/techsafeguards.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Extracted verbatim from the CFR XML. This is the most commonly missed code-adjacent requirement: teams build audit logging to satisfy 164.312(b) and never build the review step that 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) separately requires.

**A14.5** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.312(d) Person or entity authentication is a bare standard with no implementation specifications and names no method. Multi-factor authentication is NOT required by the current Security Rule. OCR's June 2023 cybersecurity newsletter frames it as a recommendation: regulated entities 'should consider implementing multi-factor authentication solutions, including phishing-resistant multi-factor authentication, where appropriate.' FOR CODE: with OpenIddict, MFA is a risk-management choice you document, not a regulatory checkbox -- but see the NPRM entry below, which would make it mandatory.

- Threshold: `n/a -- no method prescribed; MFA is 'consider', not required`
- Source: HHS OCR Cybersecurity Newsletter, June 2023, 'HIPAA and Cybersecurity Authentication' -- https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/security/guidance/cybersecurity-newsletter-june-2023/index.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-312.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: The newsletter cites NIST SP 800-63, SP 800-63-4 (then draft) and SP 1800-17. OCR's Q1 2022 newsletter separately ties weak authentication to 164.312(d) and 164.312(a)(1), stating 'Weak password rules and single factor authentication are among the practices that can contribute to successful attacks' -- so the regulator's practical expectation is stronger than the rule text.

**A14.6** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.312(e) Transmission security is a Required standard, but BOTH of its implementation specifications -- Integrity controls (e)(2)(i) and Encryption (e)(2)(ii) -- are Addressable. OCR guidance states 'The Security Rule allows for EPHI to be sent over an electronic open network as long as it is adequately protected' and that where risk analysis shows significant risk of unauthorized access, 'a covered entity must encrypt those transmissions under the addressable implementation specification for encryption.' FOR THIS APP: moving from an office LAN to the public internet is exactly the risk-analysis change that converts 'addressable' into 'you must'. FOR CI: assert HSTS, secure/HttpOnly/SameSite cookie flags, HTTPS redirect, and that no ePHI or tenant identifier appears in query strings or logs.

- Threshold: `164.312(e)(2)(i) Addressable; 164.312(e)(2)(ii) Addressable; standard (e)(1) itself Required`
- Source: HHS/CMS HIPAA Security Series Vol. 2 Paper 4 + GPO CFR 45  164.312 -- https://www.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/ocr/privacy/hipaa/administrative/securityrule/techsafeguards.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-312.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Whether your TLS termination uses a FIPS-validated module is a hosting decision and out of scope here; the code-side obligation is to refuse plaintext and to fail CI if the app will serve over HTTP.

**A14.7** `[partially]` Encryption is the breach safe harbor, and it is decided in code and config. 45 CFR 164.402 defines 'unsecured protected health information' as PHI not rendered unusable/unreadable/indecipherable by a technology or methodology the Secretary specifies. HHS's guidance names NIST SP 800-111 for data at rest and NIST SP 800-52, SP 800-77 or SP 800-113 (or others that are FIPS 140-2 validated) for data in motion, and requires decryption keys be stored separately from the encrypted data. FOR CODE: ePHI encrypted to that standard that is compromised is not a reportable breach -- the single largest consequence-reducer available to this team, and it lives in the MinIO/SQL Server/TLS configuration, not in a policy document.

- Threshold: `NIST SP 800-111 (at rest); NIST SP 800-52 / 800-77 / 800-113 or FIPS 140-2 validated (in motion); NIST SP 800-88 for media destruction`
- Source: HHS, Guidance to Render Unsecured Protected Health Information Unusable, Unreadable, or Indecipherable to Unauthorized Individuals -- https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/breach-notification/guidance/index.html
- Cross-reference: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-402.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Note the guidance still says FIPS 140-2, not 140-3 -- it dates from 74 FR 42740 (2009) and has not been updated to the current FIPS. The key-separation requirement is the part teams get wrong: encrypting a MinIO bucket with a key stored in the same database defeats the safe harbor.
- **VERIFIER CORRECTION:** The NIST publication list (800-111 at rest; 800-52/800-77/800-113 or FIPS 140-2 validated in motion; 800-88 for destruction) is correct. But separate key storage is a 'should' in the guidance, not a requirement -- replace 'requires decryption keys be stored separately' with 'guidance says decryption tools should be stored on a device or at a location separate from the data'. Safe-harbor eligibility turns on the encryption meeting the named processes and the confidential process or key not having been breached. Separately: the guidance's FIPS 140-2 reference is unrevised since 2013; for a 2026 standard target FIPS 140-3, since FIPS 140-2 certificates move to CMVP's historical list on 21 Sept 2026.
- Verifier read from the page: "To avoid a breach of the confidential process or key, these decryption tools should be stored on a device or at a location separate from the data they are used to encrypt or decrypt."

**A14.8** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.308(a)(8) Evaluation is a Required standard with no implementation specifications: 'Perform a periodic technical and nontechnical evaluation, based initially upon the standards implemented under this rule and, subsequently, in response to environmental or operational changes affecting the security of electronic protected health information, that establishes the extent to which a covered entity's or business associate's security policies and procedures meet the requirements of this subpart.' Going from office LAN to public internet IS an environmental/operational change that triggers a fresh evaluation. Automated testing is explicitly a valid *input* but cannot satisfy the standard alone: it is silent on the nontechnical half.

- Threshold: `164.308(a)(8) -- Required; frequency is 'periodic' plus event-triggered, undefined`
- Source: GPO govinfo, CFR Title 45 Vol 2,  164.308 Administrative safeguards -- https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-308.xml
- Cross-reference: https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-66r2.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Extracted verbatim from the CFR XML. The 'in response to environmental or operational changes' clause is the load-bearing one for this project right now.

**A14.9** `[yes]` NIST SP 800-66r2 5.1.8 (Evaluation) explicitly contemplates automated tooling as an evaluation collection method: 'Collection methods may include the use of interviews, surveys, and the outputs of automated tools, such as access control auditing tools, system logs, and the results of penetration testing,' and asks 'Has the organization explored the use of automated tools to support the evaluation process?' It also directs entities to 'Implement tools that can provide reports on the level of compliance' and to 'Document Results', and points to NIST SP 800-53A Rev. 5 assessment procedures. So: CI output is admissible evidence for part of 164.308(a)(8); a green pipeline is not the evaluation.

- Threshold: `NIST SP 800-66r2, February 2024, 5.1.8, Table 15`
- Source: NIST SP 800-66r2, Implementing the HIPAA Security Rule: A Cybersecurity Resource Guide -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-66r2.pdf
- Cross-reference: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-308.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Downloaded the 122-page PDF and extracted the full text; quotes are verbatim from the extraction. SP 800-66r2 also says penetration testing should be conducted 'if reasonable and appropriate' and that senior-management written approval should be obtained first -- i.e. it is discretionary under the current rule.

**A14.10** `[yes]` NIST SP 800-66r2 contains NO guidance on secure software development. A full-text search of the extracted publication returns zero occurrences of 'SSDF', 'SP 800-218', 'Secure Software Development', 'secure coding', 'SDLC', 'system development life cycle', 'code review' and 'web application'. It is an operations-and-governance guide, not an application-security guide. IMPLICATION: you cannot cite the authoritative NIST HIPAA implementation guide as a reason to run SAST or to gate on code quality; those must be justified as risk-management measures under 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B).

- Threshold: `0 occurrences of each of: 800-218, SSDF, secure coding, SDLC, code review, web application`
- Source: NIST SP 800-66r2 (Feb 2024) -- full-text extraction -- https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/SpecialPublications/NIST.SP.800-66r2.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verified by programmatic term counting over a pypdf text extraction of all 122 pages. Caveat: PDF text extraction can miss text rendered in figures; I saw no figures in the relevant sections. 'static' appears twice, both unrelated to static analysis. This is a negative finding, so I flag the method rather than claim certainty about images.

**A14.11** `[yes]` 45 CFR 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires SIX YEARS of documentation retention (Required): 'Retain the documentation required by paragraph (b)(1) of this section for 6 years from the date of its creation or the date when it last was in effect, whichever is later,' where (b)(1)(ii) covers 'a written (which may be electronic) record of the action, activity, or assessment' for anything the subpart requires to be documented. FOR CI: any scan report, test result or evaluation output you intend to cite as compliance evidence must survive six years -- 2,190 days.

- Threshold: `6 years (2,190 days) from creation or last-in-effect, whichever is later`
- Source: GPO govinfo, CFR Title 45 Vol 2,  164.316 Policies and procedures and documentation requirements -- https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2024-title45-vol2/xml/CFR-2024-title45-vol2-sec164-316.xml
- Cross-reference: https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/45/164.312
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Verbatim from CFR XML. 164.316(b)(2)(iii) additionally requires documentation to be reviewed and updated in response to environmental or operational changes -- the same trigger as the 164.308(a)(8) evaluation.

**A14.12** `[yes]` GitHub Actions CANNOT hold six years of compliance evidence. GitHub's documentation states 'By default, the artifacts and log files generated by workflows are retained for 90 days before they are automatically deleted,' and the configurable maximum is 90 days for public repositories and 400 days for private repositories. 400 days is roughly 18% of the 2,190 days that 45 CFR 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires. FOR CI: evidence you plan to rely on must be exported out of Actions into durable storage at generation time.

- Threshold: `Default 90 days; maximum 400 days (private/internal), 90 days (public) -- vs. 2,190 days required`
- Source: GitHub Docs -- Configuring the retention period for GitHub Actions artifacts and logs in your organization -- https://docs.github.com/en/organizations/managing-organization-settings/configuring-the-retention-period-for-github-actions-artifacts-and-logs-in-your-organization
- Cross-reference: https://docs.github.com/en/actions/how-tos/manage-workflow-runs/remove-workflow-artifacts
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Both GitHub pages fetched; the second confirms the 90-day default independently. GitHub also warns custom retention applies only to new artifacts going forward, not retroactively. Where you put the exported evidence is a hosting question and out of scope here.

**A14.13** `[yes]` THE NPRM IS NOT FINAL AND HAS NOT BEEN WITHDRAWN. RIN 0945-AA22 ('HIPAA Security Rule to Strengthen the Cybersecurity of Electronic Protected Health Information') has exactly ONE Federal Register document: the NPRM at 90 FR 898, published January 6, 2025, comments closed March 7, 2025. The most recent Unified Agenda entry (Publication ID 2026, reginfo pubId 202510) lists Agenda Stage of Rulemaking = 'Long-Term Actions', Priority = 'Economically Significant', Major = Yes, and a Timetable of NPRM 01/06/2025 -> Final Action 07/00/2027. HHS's own NPRM landing page still says 'While the Department is undertaking this rulemaking, the current Security Rule remains in effect' and is marked 'Content last reviewed December 27, 2024'.

- Threshold: `90 FR 898 (Jan 6, 2025); comments closed 2025-03-07; Unified Agenda projected Final Action 07/00/2027; stage = Long-Term Actions`
- Source: OMB/OIRA Unified Agenda of Regulatory and Deregulatory Actions, RIN 0945-AA22 (Publication ID 2026) -- https://www.reginfo.gov/public/do/eAgendaViewRule?pubId=202510&RIN=0945-AA22
- Cross-reference: https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents.json?conditions%5Bregulation_id_number%5D=0945-AA22&order=newest
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Three independent confirmations: (1) the Federal Register API returns count=1 for RIN 0945-AA22 -- no final rule, no withdrawal, no comment-period extension; (2) the Unified Agenda entry above; (3) HHS's own page at hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/security/hipaa-security-rule-nprm/. 'Long-Term Actions' in OIRA parlance means the agency does not expect action within the next 12 months. A projected date in the Unified Agenda is planning data, not a commitment.

**A14.14** `[yes]` IF FINALISED AS PROPOSED, automated vulnerability scanning becomes mandatory at a defined cadence. Proposed 45 CFR 164.312(h)(2)(i)(A): 'Conduct automated vulnerability scans to identify technical vulnerabilities in the covered entity's or business associate's relevant electronic information systems in accordance with the covered entity's or business associate's risk analysis required by  164.308(a)(2) or at least once every six months, whichever is more frequent.' Proposed (h)(2)(i)(B) additionally requires reviewing and testing the effectiveness of the scanning tooling itself at least once every 12 months. Proposed (h)(2)(ii) requires monitoring authoritative sources (HHS names the NIST National Vulnerability Database and CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog) for known vulnerabilities on an ongoing basis, with no minimum interval specified.

- Threshold: `Automated vulnerability scans: at least once every 6 months, or more often if risk analysis says so. Scanner effectiveness review: at least every 12 months.`
- Source: HIPAA Security Rule To Strengthen the Cybersecurity of ePHI, 90 FR 898 (Jan 6, 2025) -- proposed regulatory text -- https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/06/2024-30983/hipaa-security-rule-to-strengthen-the-cybersecurity-of-electronic-protected-health-information
- Cross-reference: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/xml/2025/01/06/2024-30983.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Quoted verbatim from the Federal Register full-text XML (1.46 MB) which I downloaded and searched directly, not from a summary. HHS explicitly requested comment on 'whether once every six months is the appropriate frequency'.

**A14.15** `[yes]` IF FINALISED AS PROPOSED, penetration testing becomes mandatory annually with NO small-entity exemption. Proposed 45 CFR 164.312(h)(2)(iii): 'Perform penetration testing of the covered entity's or business associate's relevant electronic information systems by a qualified person... (B) Penetration testing must be performed at least once every 12 months or in accordance with the covered entity's or business associate's risk analysis required by  164.308(a)(2), whichever is more frequent.' A 'qualified person' is defined as one 'with appropriate knowledge of and experience with generally accepted cybersecurity principles and methods'. HHS explicitly CONSIDERED and REJECTED exempting small and rural providers, stating that 'setting different requirements for cybersecurity for small and rural health care providers would lead such health care providers to believe that they can limit their investment in cybersecurity.'

- Threshold: `Penetration testing: at least once every 12 months, by a qualified person. No small/rural carve-out.`
- Source: 90 FR 898, proposed 45 CFR 164.312(h)(2)(iii) and the Regulatory Impact Analysis -- https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/xml/2025/01/06/2024-30983.xml
- Cross-reference: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/06/2024-30983/hipaa-security-rule-to-strengthen-the-cybersecurity-of-electronic-protected-health-information
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: HHS costed this at an average of 3 hours per regulated entity (low 2, high 10) at $119.94/hr for an information security analyst -- roughly $360 per entity per year. That figure is not credible for an external test of an internet-facing multi-tenant portal, and HHS itself solicited comment on it ('For regulated entities that have conducted penetration tests, the amount of time and costs of such tests'). See disagreements.

**A14.16** `[partially]` IF FINALISED AS PROPOSED, the addressable/required distinction disappears and encryption + MFA become mandatory. The NPRM proposes to 'remove the distinction between required and addressable implementation specifications and make all implementation specifications required, with specific, limited exceptions.' Proposed 164.312(b)(2): 'Encrypt all electronic protected health information at rest and in transit' except under enumerated exceptions. Proposed 164.312(f)(2)(ii)(A): 'Deploy multi-factor authentication to all technology assets... to verify that a person seeking access to the relevant electronic information system(s) is the user that the person claims to be,' and (B) MFA for any privilege-changing action. Also proposed: 164.308(a)(14) Compliance audit -- 'Perform and document an audit at least once every 12 months of... compliance with each standard and implementation specification in this subpart.'

- Threshold: `Encryption of all ePHI at rest and in transit; MFA on all technology assets and all privilege changes; annual compliance audit of every standard and implementation specification`

**A14.17** `[yes]` IF FINALISED AS PROPOSED, patch timeliness gets hard deadlines that a dependency-scanning SLA can be built against: critical risks within 15 calendar days of identifying the need (or of a patch becoming available, if none exists at identification), and high risks within 30 calendar days on the same terms. Everything else is governed by the entity's own written patch management policy under proposed 164.308(a)(4).

- Threshold: `Critical: 15 calendar days. High: 30 calendar days. Others: per written policy.`
- Source: 90 FR 898 preamble discussion of proposed 45 CFR 164.308(a)(5)(ii)(D) / 164.312(h)(2)(iv) -- https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/xml/2025/01/06/2024-30983.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: verified
- Notes: Quoted verbatim from the NPRM full text. These are preamble-stated interpretations of 'reasonable and appropriate period of time' rather than numerals in the proposed regulatory text itself, which matters if the final rule moves them. Even unfinalised, 15/30 days is a defensible, cheap SLA to adopt now for NuGet/Yarn advisories.

**A14.18** `[yes]` NEITHER the current Security Rule NOR the 2025 NPRM regulates how you write code. A full-text search of the 90 FR 898 NPRM (949,067 characters extracted) returns ZERO occurrences of: 'secure coding', 'software development', 'SDLC', 'OWASP', 'application security', 'code review', 'input validation', 'SQL injection', 'static analysis', 'SBOM', 'software bill of materials', 'SSDF' and '800-218'. 'Web application' appears once, and only inside a description of CISA's free Cyber Hygiene Services. HIPAA regulates the properties of the deployed system and the documentation you keep about them -- not your development process.

- Threshold: `0 occurrences of each of 12 application-security terms across the full NPRM text`

By contrast 'asset inventory' appears 24 times and 'Cybersecurity Framework' 25 times -- HHS's attention is on inventory, configuration and operations, not on the build.

**A14.19** `[yes]` 42 U.S.C. 17941 (HITECH  13412, added by Pub. L. 116-321, Jan. 5, 2021) requires the Secretary to consider whether an entity 'had, for not less than the previous 12 months, recognized security practices in place' when setting fines, deciding whether to terminate an audit early, and agreeing remedies. 'Recognized security practices' means practices developed under 15 U.S.C. 272(c)(15) (the NIST Cybersecurity Framework), the approaches promulgated under section 405(d) of the Cybersecurity Act of 2015 (HHS 405(d) / HICP), and other programs developed under other statutory authorities. Crucially:  17941(b)(3) says nothing subjects an entity to liability for electing NOT to engage in them, and (b)(4) says they do not substitute for Security Rule compliance.

- Threshold: `'not less than the previous 12 months' of demonstrated recognized security practices`

Cross-referenced against the NPRM preamble, which cites 'Public Law 116-321, 134 Stat. 5072, adding sec. 13412 (Jan. 5, 2021) (codified at 42 U.S.C. 17941)'. This is the strongest cost/benefit argument available to a two-person team: the 12-month clock is the cheap part, and it only starts when you begin.

**A14.20** `[yes]` IF FINALISED AS PROPOSED, the CI/CD pipeline itself is arguably in regulatory scope. Proposed 45 CFR 164.304 would define 'relevant electronic information system' as one 'that creates, receives, maintains, or transmits electronic protected health information OR THAT OTHERWISE AFFECTS the confidentiality, integrity, or availability of electronic protected health information,' and 'technology asset' as 'the components of an electronic information system, including but not limited to hardware, software, electronic media, information, and data.' A pipeline that can deploy code into the ePHI-handling application plainly affects its integrity and availability. That would pull the CI system into the MFA, audit-logging, vulnerability-scanning and asset-inventory requirements.

- Threshold: `Proposed 164.304 definitions of 'relevant electronic information system' and 'technology asset'`
- Source: 90 FR 898, proposed 45 CFR 164.304 definitions -- https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/xml/2025/01/06/2024-30983.xml
- Accessed: 2026-08-28 | Researcher confidence: partial
- Notes: The definitional text is verbatim and verified; the INFERENCE that CI/CD falls inside it is mine, not HHS's -- HHS nowhere discusses build systems. I mark this partial because no source states the conclusion. It is a foreseeable-risk flag, not a citable requirement. Even so, applying MFA and least privilege to the GitHub org costs almost nothing and forecloses the question.

### Where sources disagree

- HHS's own cost estimate for penetration testing is contested by HHS. The Regulatory Impact Analysis assumes an average of 3 hours per regulated entity (low 2, high 10) at $119.94/hr for an information security analyst -- roughly $360/year. That is far below the market cost of a qualified external test of an internet-facing, multi-tenant, database-per-tenant portal. HHS itself flagged the uncertainty by requesting comment on 'the amount of time and costs of such tests'. Do not plan a budget from the NPRM's figure.
- Is MFA required? The letter and the practice diverge. 45 CFR 164.312(d) names no method, and OCR's June 2023 newsletter frames MFA as something entities 'should consider... where appropriate' -- a recommendation. But OCR's Q1 2022 newsletter treats 'weak password rules and single factor authentication' as contributing causes of breaches under the same section, and the NPRM would make MFA mandatory on all technology assets. So the rule says optional, the regulator's published expectation says do it, and the proposal says you will have to. For an internet-facing portal with four self-registering external roles, treat MFA as effectively expected and document the decision either way.
- 'Addressable' means different things to industry and to the regulator. Industry overwhelmingly reads it as optional. HHS states in the NPRM that this is a misreading -- that it 'remains concerned that regulated entities believe that flexibility overrides the need for them to protect all ePHI' and that OCR's enforcement experience shows 'addressable' is 'misunderstood to be optional' -- and proposes deleting the concept for that reason. The safe reading is HHS's: addressable means implement it or produce a written justification plus an equivalent alternative.
- Whether the NPRM will be finalised at all is genuinely open. The Unified Agenda's 'Long-Term Actions' classification and July 2027 projected date are OIRA/HHS planning data, not commitments, and agenda dates slip routinely. Meanwhile HHS's own NPRM page has not been updated since December 2024 and still says only that 'the current Security Rule remains in effect'. Anyone telling this team the six-month scanning requirement is 'coming in 2026' is over-reading the record; anyone calling the NPRM dead is under-reading it. Plan for the cadence, do not budget for the certainty.
- NIST SP 800-66r2 (Feb 2024) and the Jan 2025 NPRM point in different directions on testing. SP 800-66r2 treats penetration testing as discretionary -- do it 'if reasonable and appropriate', with written senior-management approval -- while the NPRM would make it an annual mandate with no small-entity exemption. Both are current documents from the federal government about the same rule. The reconciliation is that SP 800-66r2 describes the rule as it exists and the NPRM describes the rule HHS wants; but a team reading only one of them will reach the wrong conclusion about what is expected.

### Surprises (things a two-person team would not know to look for)

- The controls everyone assumes HIPAA mandates are the ones it does not. Today, encryption at rest, encryption in transit, automatic logoff, integrity-authentication of ePHI and transmission integrity controls are ALL 'Addressable' under 45 CFR 164.312 -- and MFA is not in the rule at all. What is unconditionally Required is narrower and less glamorous: unique user identification, an emergency access procedure, audit controls, authentication, the transmission security standard itself, regular review of information system activity, periodic evaluation, and six-year documentation. Teams over-invest in encryption checkboxes and under-invest in the audit-log review obligation, which is the one that is actually Required and has no product feature.
- Writing audit logs does not satisfy HIPAA. 164.312(b) requires mechanisms that 'record AND EXAMINE' activity, and 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) separately and unconditionally requires procedures to 'regularly review records of information system activity, such as audit logs'. ABP's audit logging module gives you the recording for free and gives you nothing at all for the reviewing. There is no CI check that can discharge this -- it needs a recurring calendar event, a query, and a dated artifact saying who looked and what they found. That artifact is what an investigator asks for.
- GitHub Actions physically cannot hold your compliance evidence. Artifacts and logs default to 90 days and max out at 400 days for private repositories. 45 CFR 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires six years -- 2,190 days. Every DAST report, dependency audit and test result you generate and leave in Actions silently expires at roughly 18% of the required lifetime. Nothing fails when this is wrong; you discover it in year three when the evidence for year one is gone. This is the single most concrete, most checkable, least-anticipated CI consequence of being HIPAA-regulated.
- The January 2025 NPRM is neither law nor dead, and the distinction is worth money. Federal Register data shows exactly one document under RIN 0945-AA22 -- no final rule, no withdrawal, not even a comment-period extension. The current Unified Agenda places it in 'Long-Term Actions' with a projected Final Action of July 2027, and HHS's own NPRM landing page has not been touched since 27 December 2024. So the six-month scan and annual pen test cadences are planning targets, not present obligations. But adopt the cadence NOW anyway, because 42 U.S.C. 17941 only credits you for 'not less than the previous 12 months' of recognized security practices -- the clock is the expensive part and it only starts when you begin.
- HIPAA has essentially nothing to say about how you write code. Zero occurrences of 'secure coding', 'SDLC', 'OWASP', 'code review', 'input validation', 'SQL injection', 'static analysis', 'SBOM' or 'SSDF' across the entire 949,000-character NPRM -- and zero in NIST SP 800-66r2 as well. It regulates the deployed system's properties and your documentation of them. Practical consequence: you cannot defend a SAST or lint gate to an auditor by saying 'HIPAA requires it', because it does not. Defend it as risk management under Required 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B), and be honest internally that its justification is engineering quality, not compliance.
- 'Automated testing satisfies the periodic evaluation' is half true, and the half that is false is the half that gets cited. NIST SP 800-66r2 5.1.8 explicitly names 'the outputs of automated tools' as a valid collection method and asks whether the organisation has 'explored the use of automated tools to support the evaluation process' -- so CI output is admissible evidence. But 164.308(a)(8) requires a TECHNICAL AND NONTECHNICAL evaluation with documented results. A green pipeline is an input to the evaluation, never the evaluation itself, and presenting it as such is how a defensible position becomes an indefensible one.
- If the NPRM finalises as written, your CI/CD pipeline is probably in scope. Proposed 164.304 would define a 'relevant electronic information system' as one that 'otherwise affects the confidentiality, integrity, or availability' of ePHI. A pipeline with deploy rights to the PHI-handling application affects all three. HHS never discusses build systems, so this is inference rather than a stated requirement -- but MFA and least privilege on the GitHub org cost almost nothing and close the question permanently. Most teams assume CI is categorically outside the regulated boundary.
- HHS considered exempting small and rural providers from penetration testing and explicitly refused, on the reasoning that a carve-out 'would lead such health care providers to believe that they can limit their investment in cybersecurity'. There is no small-team relief mechanism in the proposal. The only scaling lever that survives is 164.306(b)(2), which requires HHS to take into account your size, capabilities, technical infrastructure and cost -- which is a reason to write those factors explicitly into every decision record you produce, not a reason to skip requirements.
- The breach safe harbor is an engineering decision, not a policy one, and it is the largest single consequence-reducer available. Under 164.402, ePHI rendered unusable per HHS's guidance is not 'unsecured PHI', so its compromise is not a reportable breach. The guidance names NIST SP 800-111 for data at rest and SP 800-52 / 800-77 / 800-113 or FIPS 140-2 validated methods in motion, AND requires that decryption keys be stored separately from the encrypted data. That last clause is where implementations fail: encrypting MinIO objects with a key held in the same SQL Server instance forfeits the safe harbor while looking fully compliant on a checklist.
- Even the strengthened rule refuses to name a TLS version or cipher suite. The NPRM uses 'prevailing cryptographic standards' deliberately and states that an entity 'using an encryption algorithm that is known to be insecure would not be in compliance'. That converts crypto configuration from a one-time setup task into a standing review obligation tied to your risk analysis. Whatever TLS policy the app ships with needs a calendar reminder attached to it, not just a config file.
- The most common OCR finding is not a missing scanner -- it is a missing or inadequate risk analysis under Required 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(A). Everything in this document, including every merge gate, is downstream of that one document: proposed 164.312(h)(2)(i)(A) sets scan frequency 'in accordance with the risk analysis... or at least once every six months, whichever is more frequent', so the risk analysis literally sets your CI schedule. A two-person team that builds excellent CI and never writes a risk analysis has optimised the wrong end. HHS's free Security Risk Assessment Tool exists for exactly this size of organisation, though NIST cautions it 'cannot identify technical vulnerabilities' and must be paired with actual scanning.

### Standard elsewhere, deliberately NOT recommended here

- An external third-party penetration test before launch. A larger company books one annually as a matter of course, and the NPRM would require one every 12 months by a 'qualified person'. But the NPRM is in 'Long-Term Actions' with a July 2027 projected final date, and there is no current regulatory requirement -- NIST SP 800-66r2 says only to conduct one 'if reasonable and appropriate' and to get written senior-management approval first. HHS costed it at 3 hours; real market cost for an internet-facing multi-tenant portal is one to two orders of magnitude higher. Do the ZAP baseline scan now, budget for one external test in the first year AFTER launch (once there is real PHI and once the NPRM's shape is clearer), and write down that decision as your risk-analysis record.
- HITRUST CSF certification, SOC 2 Type II, or any third-party attestation. None of these is required by HIPAA -- not by the current rule, not by the NPRM. They are commercial trust signals demanded by enterprise customers. Eleven offices in one workers' comp network are not going to ask, and a two-person team cannot absorb the evidence-collection burden. If a customer does ask later, 42 U.S.C. 17941 recognized security practices (NIST CSF or HHS 405(d) HICP) is the cheaper answer and has actual statutory weight with the regulator that HITRUST does not.
- A GRC or continuous-compliance platform (Vanta/Drata class). These automate evidence collection against control frameworks, which sounds exactly like what 164.316 demands. But they charge per-integration and are calibrated for organisations with dozens of systems and a compliance owner. Here the entire evidence set is: a monthly CI bundle, a handful of Markdown decision records, one risk analysis, and one log-review log. That fits in the repository. Buying a platform would add a vendor, a recurring cost, and a second place where the truth lives -- and it would still not write the decision records for you.
- Real-time security monitoring and alerting on audit logs. Proposed 164.312(d)(2)(i) would require deploying controls that 'monitor in real-time all activity' and alert workforce members. That is not required today, and 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(D) asks only for 'regularly review'. A two-person team with no on-call rotation cannot answer a 3am alert, and an alerting system nobody answers is worse evidence than no alerting system -- it documents that you saw the event and did nothing. Do a documented weekly or monthly review with a retained artifact instead, and revisit if the NPRM finalises.
- A NIST SP 800-218 SSDF implementation or a CISA secure-software attestation. SSDF is the right framework for a software producer selling to the US federal government; it is not a HIPAA obligation. Neither the current Security Rule nor the NPRM mentions SSDF, secure coding or the SDLC anywhere, and NIST SP 800-66r2 -- the authoritative HIPAA implementation guide -- does not either. Adopting SSDF's ~40 practices would consume the entire one-developer-week-per-month budget for a year and would not answer a single question OCR asks.
- A full authenticated DAST crawl or an IAST agent, as opposed to the unauthenticated baseline scan. Authenticated scanning of an ABP/Angular SPA requires maintaining a session-replay script that breaks every time the login flow changes, and the findings skew heavily toward false positives on framework-generated markup. The anonymous attack surface -- registration, login, password reset, document upload -- is where the newly-internet-facing risk actually is, and that is exactly what an unauthenticated baseline scan covers. Revisit after the first year.
- A formal annual compliance audit of every standard and implementation specification. Proposed 164.308(a)(14) would require exactly this, and a larger company runs it as a project with an external assessor. It is not required today. What IS required today is the 164.308(a)(8) evaluation, which is narrower and which the LAN-to-internet migration triggers regardless. Do that one properly and honestly, once, and let the compliance audit wait for the final rule.

### Recommended checks from this area

**`tenant-isolation`** -- tool: xUnit + ABP test host + Testcontainers (or a fixed pair of seeded SQL Server databases); no third-party licence required

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 16-24 to build the harness and 8-12 tests; ~1-2h/month to extend as endpoints are added | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: An integration-test suite that boots the API against at least two seeded tenant databases and asserts that a token minted for tenant A can never read, write, list or enumerate any entity, blob or audit row belonging to tenant B -- including via ID-guessing, ABP's IRepository/IQueryable surfaces, the Host header, and any endpoint that bypasses the data filter. Should include at least one test that deliberately sets the Host header to another tenant mid-session and asserts rejection.
- Why at this team size: This is the one check that maps to the stated highest-consequence failure mode AND to a Required obligation: 45 CFR 164.312(a)(1) access control plus 164.308(a)(4) information access management. Tenancy resolving from the Host header alone means a single missed data-filter attribute is a multi-tenant PHI breach across 11 offices. It is also the check most likely to catch a real defect per hour spent, and unlike SAST it produces zero false positives, so a two-person team will not learn to bypass it. Note it does not depend on any hosting decision -- it runs entirely in CI.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party: xUnit and the ABP test host ship with the framework the team already runs (ABP Commercial 10.0.2). No external tool to go stale.

**`audit-trail-coverage`** -- tool: xUnit + ABP Audit Logging + NetArchTest or a reflection-based convention test

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 10-16 initial; near-zero ongoing once the convention test exists | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Tests asserting that every endpoint touching ePHI emits an audit record carrying a non-null, non-system user identity, a tenant identifier, the action, and a timestamp -- and specifically that READ paths are covered, not just writes. Plus an architecture test that fails the build if a new controller or application-service method handling a PHI-bearing entity is added without audit coverage.
- Why at this team size: 45 CFR 164.312(b) audit controls is a Required standard with no implementation specifications, and 164.312(a)(2)(i) unique user identification is Required -- meaning attributability is not negotiable. ABP/EF Core change tracking gives you writes almost free; reads are the gap and reads are what OCR asks about. The convention test is the part that makes this sustainable: it means the two developers do not have to remember, the build remembers for them.
- Tool maintenance evidence: ABP Audit Logging is a first-party module of ABP Commercial 10.0.2, already a dependency. NetArchTest is optional and replaceable with ~40 lines of reflection if it goes stale.

**`dependency-audit`** -- tool: NuGet Audit (built into .NET 10 SDK / NuGet 7.0) + 'dotnet list package --vulnerable --include-transitive' + Yarn 4 'yarn npm audit'

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 3-5 total, most of it deciding the suppression policy | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Fails the build on high and critical known vulnerabilities in .NET and JS dependencies, including transitive ones, checked against the GitHub Advisory Database at restore time. On .NET 10 this is on by default with NuGetAuditMode defaulting to 'all' (transitive included); set <WarningsAsErrors>$(WarningsAsErrors);NU1903;NU1904</WarningsAsErrors> to gate on high/critical while leaving low/moderate as warnings. Pair with 'yarn npm audit' for the Angular side.
- Why at this team size: Zero new tooling, zero licence, zero maintenance -- it is already in the SDK they run. It is the cheapest possible discharge of proposed 164.312(h)(2)(ii) ('monitor authoritative sources for known vulnerabilities on an ongoing basis'), and today it is defensible risk management under Required 164.308(a)(1)(ii)(B). Adopt the NPRM's 15-day-critical / 30-day-high remediation SLA now as written policy; it costs nothing and it is the number HHS has already put in writing. Microsoft documents a pattern for a dedicated audit pipeline so that new advisories do not block unrelated bug fixes -- use it, because a two-person team that gets blocked by a 3am advisory will disable the gate.
- Tool maintenance evidence: First-party Microsoft/NuGet feature. The Microsoft Learn doc 'Auditing package dependencies for security vulnerabilities' carries ms.date 2025-10-01 and updated_at 2026-07-07, and documents NuGet 7.0 / .NET 10 SDK behaviour including the new 'dotnet package update --vulnerable' command. https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/nuget/concepts/auditing-packages

**`dast-baseline-scan`** -- tool: OWASP ZAP via the zaproxy/action-baseline GitHub Action

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 8-16 to stand up (most of it getting a seeded staging target and tuning out the initial noise); ~1h/month to triage | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A scheduled (not per-PR) OWASP ZAP baseline scan against a deployed staging instance seeded with synthetic data, covering the newly-anonymous surfaces: registration, login, password reset and document upload. Publishes an HTML/JSON report as a retained artifact. Run it monthly, which beats the proposed six-month floor with margin and gives you twelve dated reports a year.
- Why at this team size: This is the single check that directly produces the artifact proposed 164.312(h)(2)(i)(A) will demand ('automated vulnerability scans... at least once every six months'), and it is admissible today as an input to the Required 164.308(a)(8) evaluation -- NIST SP 800-66r2 names 'the outputs of automated tools' as a valid collection method. Deliberately scheduled and NOT a merge gate: a DAST tool wired into every PR is exactly the kind of slow, noisy, occasionally-wrong check that a two-person team learns to bypass. Monthly and advisory keeps it alive. Whether staging is reachable from CI is a hosting question and out of scope here.
- Tool maintenance evidence: ZAP core is very actively maintained: weekly releases, latest w2026-08-26 published 2026-08-26 (https://github.com/zaproxy/zaproxy/releases). The wrapper Action zaproxy/action-baseline last released v0.15.0 on 2025-10-24 -- about 10 months old, inside the 12-month window but slowing; if it stalls, the same scan runs from the ZAP Docker image in three lines of YAML.

**`transport-and-session-security`** -- tool: xUnit integration tests + ZAP baseline rules (both already in the stack above)

- Posture: `required-gate` | Effort: 6-10 | Launch-blocking: **yes**
- What it does: Automated assertions against the running app that HTTPS is enforced, HSTS is present, auth cookies carry Secure/HttpOnly/SameSite, session inactivity timeout is enforced server-side, and that no ePHI, tenant identifier or token ever appears in a URL query string or in an application log line. The log-leakage half is a unit/integration test over the logging pipeline; the header half is an assertion in the same job as the DAST scan.
- Why at this team size: Going from an office LAN to the public internet is precisely the risk-analysis change that converts 164.312(e)(2)(ii) encryption-in-transit from 'addressable' to 'you must, and here is why' -- OCR's own guidance says where risk analysis shows significant risk 'a covered entity must encrypt those transmissions'. Automatic logoff (164.312(a)(2)(iii)) is addressable, but an internet-facing PHI portal that never times out is a hard sentence to write in a risk-analysis document. The log-leakage test is disproportionately valuable: ePHI in an application log is a disclosure that no amount of database encryption saves you from, and it is a mistake two developers under feature pressure will make.
- Tool maintenance evidence: No new tooling beyond what checks 1 and 4 already establish.

**`compliance-evidence-export`** -- tool: A GitHub Actions workflow + whatever durable store the team already has; SBOM generated by 'dotnet sbom'/CycloneDX or Trivy

- Posture: `scheduled` | Effort: 5-8, plus ~15 min/month to confirm it ran | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: A scheduled job that collects the month's compliance-relevant CI output -- DAST report, dependency-audit output and resolved SBOM, tenant-isolation and audit-trail test results, the commit SHA and timestamp -- into a single dated, hashed bundle and writes it somewhere that survives six years. Also emits a short index file listing what was produced, so the annual evaluation has a table of contents.
- Why at this team size: 45 CFR 164.316(b)(2)(i) requires six years -- 2,190 days -- of retention for records of any action, activity or assessment the Security Rule requires to be documented. GitHub Actions caps private-repo artifact retention at 400 days and defaults to 90. Every scan you run and then let expire is money spent producing evidence you will not have when OCR asks. This is the highest evidence-value-per-hour item on the list and the one most likely to be missed, because nothing fails when it is absent -- you simply discover in year three that year one is gone. Where the bundle lands is a hosting decision and out of scope; that it leaves Actions is not.
- Tool maintenance evidence: Trivy is very actively maintained: v0.74.0 released 2026-08-14, v0.73.0 on 2026-08-03 (https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/releases). OWASP Dependency-Check is also current if preferred -- v13.0.0 published 2026-08-03 per its releases Atom feed -- though it is redundant with NuGet Audit here and adds NVD API-key friction.

**`addressable-decision-register`** -- tool: A ~60-line script in the existing CI (Python or a dotnet tool), plus Markdown ADRs in the repo

- Posture: `advisory` | Effort: 4-6 to build; 1-3h per decision record to actually write, spread over months | Launch-blocking: **no**
- What it does: Not a scanner: a CI check that fails if a listed addressable implementation specification (automatic logoff, encryption at rest, ePHI integrity authentication, transmission integrity controls, transmission encryption) has no corresponding decision record in /docs/security/ carrying a date, the risk-analysis reference, the decision, and -- where the answer is 'not implementing' -- the equivalent alternative measure. A simple file-presence-and-schema check over Markdown front-matter.
- Why at this team size: 45 CFR 164.306(d)(3)(ii)(B) does not let you silently skip an addressable specification -- you must document why it is not reasonable and appropriate AND implement an equivalent alternative where that is reasonable. This is the obligation with the highest ratio of legal exposure to engineering effort, and it is invisible to every scanner on the market. Keeping the records in the repo means they are versioned, dated, diffable and survive staff turnover -- which is what 164.316(b)(2)(iii)'s 'review and update in response to environmental or operational changes' actually needs. Advisory rather than a gate, because the failure mode is a stale document, not a broken build, and blocking merges on prose is how a two-person team starts bypassing CI.
- Tool maintenance evidence: No third-party dependency; the check is a shell/Python script the team owns. Deliberately chosen over a GRC SaaS product for exactly that reason.
